# Lilavati <note>original: "Līlāvatī." This title translates to "The Beautiful" or "The Playful One." Legend says Bhaskara II named the book after his daughter to console her after a failed wedding arrangement, though it serves as a foundational text on arithmetic and geometry.</note>

This indicates that the book includes a "ṭīkā" (commentary) in "bhāṣā" (the local spoken language, likely Hindi in this context) along with the "anvaya" (the prose sequence of the original Sanskrit verses) to make the complex poetry easier for students to understand.</note><-

<note>This leaf contains no historical handwriting or mathematical diagrams. In the study of historical manuscripts, these blank pages—often referred to by the Latin term <term>vacat</term>—are significant as they indicate the original volume of the paper and protect the inscribed pages from the friction and environmental damage associated with the manuscript's binding. The presence of these pages suggests the manuscript was handled with care, intended to preserve the technical knowledge of Bhaskara II for future generations.</note>

# Letter of Thanks

---

Many thanks are due to the eminent scholar, Shri Ram Swarup Shastri, a resident of Moradabad and an ornament of the Gauda lineage <note>The Gauda Brahmins are a traditional community of scholars</note>. He is a master of Logic <note>original: *Nyaya*</note> and other traditional sciences, having studied at the Government Sanskrit College in Kashi <note>Modern-day Varanasi, the historic center of Indian learning</note>. It is this scholar who, with great effort, has composed a lucid Hindi commentary on the chapter concerning Arithmetic <term>Vyaktaganita: the science of working with known quantities, as opposed to algebra</term> titled "**Lilavati**." This work is a primary part of the great treatise *Siddhanta Shiromani*, authored by the venerable master Bhaskaracharya.

This Hindi commentary was created for the benefit of all students, ensuring a very clear understanding of the text’s meaning. Within this commentary, the mathematical rules, examples, and procedures can be understood without difficulty. The composition of this commentary by the said Pandit is a great favor to those modern students who are eager to learn the science of mathematics.

At our encouragement, this Pandit has adorned the book *Lilavati* with this Hindi commentary and sent it to us with great respect. We, in turn, have published it with great enthusiasm, printing it at our own "**Shri Venkateshwar**" Press. We offer our thanks to those students who shall acquire and study this book. Because a book such as this, graced with such a detailed Hindi explanation, has never been printed or published until now, we hope that people will certainly acquire this volume and find success through the labors of this eminent scholar.

-> Your well-wisher, <-

-> **Khemraj Shrikrishnadas**, <-
-> Manager of the "**Shri Venkateshwar**" Steam Press, Mumbai. <-

<summary>A formal acknowledgment by publisher Khemraj Shrikrishnadas thanking Pandit Ram Swarup Shastri for creating a clear Hindi commentary on Bhaskaracharya’s famous mathematical work, the Lilavati.</summary>
<keywords>Khemraj Shrikrishnadas, Shri Venkateshwar Press, Lilavati, Bhaskaracharya, Ram Swarup Shastri, Hindi commentary, Indian mathematics history, Mumbai publishing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory front matter of the Lilavati commentary. The previous page introduced the scholar Ram Swarup Shastri and the publisher Khemraj Shrikrishnadas. This page serves as the formal "Bhumika" (Preface), establishing the scriptural and cultural importance of mathematics and the specific motivation for this new edition.</meta>

# Preface

---

-> Astronomy is known as the eye of the Vedas. <note>original: "Jyotisham nayanam smritam." In the Vedic tradition, the six auxiliary sciences are compared to parts of the body; astronomy/mathematics is the sight that allows for the correct timing of rituals.</note> <-

Dear Readers! It will be well known to all you distinguished gentlemen that the divine book which describes the educational system for the four classes <note>original: *varnas*, the traditional social divisions of ancient India</note> is the Veda. It has six limbs <term>Vedangas: the six auxiliary disciplines used to understand and apply the Vedas</term>, namely: Phonetics <note>Shiksha</note>, Ritual <note>Kalpa</note>, Grammar <note>Vyakarana</note>, Etymology <note>Nirukta</note>, Prosody <note>Chhanda</note>, and Astronomy/Mathematics <note>Jyotisha</note>. The study of these six limbs of the Veda is the religious duty of the three classes, from the Brahmins down to the Vaishyas.

Within that same Astronomy <note>Jyotisha</note>, which is a limb of our most revered Veda, there are two divisions: predictive astrology <note>Phalita</note> and mathematics <note>Ganita</note>. Of these, the mathematical division holds a place of supreme honor to this day, not only on this island <note>referring to Jambudvipa/India</note> but also in other lands. Although the number of people in India who understand this eternal mathematics is currently very small, millions of thanks are due to that God who, in His mercy, manifested the Great Scholar <note>Mahamahopadhyaya</note> Shri Sudhakarji—the best among the learned—within the supremely holy city of Kashi <note>Varanasi</note>. Like a moon in the sky of mathematical science, he has increased the flow of the ocean of mathematics through his skillful arts. He serves as the Professor of Mathematics at the Government Sanskrit College in Kashi. It is because of him that the science of mathematics is also promoted in the land of Mithila.

However, if one looks at other regions, it becomes exceedingly difficult to find those who fully understand our eternal mathematical sciences. Even if some clever mathematicians are found, they generally do not focus on teaching. For this reason, the desires of those wishing to learn traditional mathematics arise only to vanish within their hearts. To remove this terrible state of affairs, I have been commissioned by the honorable Seth Khemraj Shrikrishnadasji to compose a commentary on the **Lilavati**. 

Dear friends! The **Lilavati** is a book known by name to all wise men, from children to the elderly, not only on this island but also in foreign lands. Nowadays, this book is the first step <term>Sopana: literally a staircase or ladder; a foundational text</term> into traditional mathematics. For this reason, to promote it everywhere and according to the letter sent by the aforementioned Sethji, I have created this commentary in the common language <note>Hindi</note> titled **"Swarupaprakash"** (The Light of the True Form), following the prose order of the text...

<summary>The author explains the status of mathematics (Ganita) as a sacred science within the Vedic tradition and notes the decline of its study. He credits the scholar Sudhakara Dvivedi with its revival in Kashi and introduces his own Hindi commentary, "Swarupaprakash," commissioned to make the foundational text "Lilavati" accessible to modern students.</summary>

<keywords>Lilavati, Vedanga, Ganita, Jyotisha, Sudhakara Dvivedi, Khemraj Shrikrishnadas, Kashi, mathematics, astronomy, Swarupaprakash commentary</keywords>

This page concludes the personal preface and begins a historical section regarding the author, Bhaskaracharya.</meta>

<page-num>( 2 )</page-num>
<header>Preface</header>

...and by the grace of God, it has now been printed and prepared. I have surrendered all rights for the reprinting and distribution of this book to Seth Khemrajji <note>Seth Khemraj Shrikrishnadas, a famous publisher of Sanskrit and Hindi texts in Mumbai</note>. I now hope that appreciative gentlemen will examine this work and make my labor successful. For followers of the Vedic religion, studying this work is extremely necessary, because the science of astronomy is considered the eye of the Vedas: "Astronomy is known as the eye." <note>original Sanskrit: "Jyotisham nayanam smritam." The metaphor suggests that while the Vedas provide the body of knowledge, astronomy/mathematics provides the vision to navigate time and ritual.</note>

I hope that gentlemen will set aside any malice and forgive whatever mistakes I may have made, as is the nature of being human.

# Determination of the Author’s Time and Other Details

Shri Bhaskaracharya, the creator of "Lilavati," resided in a city named Vijjadavid (which is famous nowadays by the name Bijapur) near the Sahyadri mountains. He was born to Shri Maheshwaropadhyaya of the Shandilya lineage <term>gotra: a paternal lineage tracing back to a common rishi or sage</term> in the Shaka year 1036 <note>equivalent to 1114 CE</note>. Bhaskaracharya himself has written this fact in the "Prashnadhyaya" [Chapter on Questions] of his work "Goladhyaya" [Book on the Spheres]. He was a Karnataka Brahmin and a follower of the Vaishnava tradition <note>worshippers of Vishnu</note>. His composed works include "Lilavati," "Bijaganita" [Algebra], "Goladhyaya" [Spheres], "Ganitadhyaya" [Mathematics], "Karanakutuhala," and others.

Just as the work "Siddhanta Shiromani" by Bhaskaracharya is widely popular at this time, the "Lalla Siddhanta" <note>an earlier astronomical treatise by the scholar Lalla</note> was popular during Bhaskaracharya's own era. Bhaskaracharya also attained his scholarship by first studying the "Lalla Siddhanta," but later, accepting the views of Brahmagupta <note>a famous 7th-century mathematician and astronomer</note>, he refuted many topics found in Lalla’s system.

On this text "Lilavati," there are commentaries by great scholars such as Gangadhara, Ganesha Daivajna, Suryadasa, Lakshmidasa, Munishvara, Ramakrishna, and Kripanatha. Furthermore, the notes of Shri Bapudeva Shastri and the printed notes prepared by the Great Scholar <term>Mahamahopadhyaya: a title given to prestigious scholars</term> Shri Sudhakar Dwivediji—the professor of mathematics at the Kashi [Varanasi] State Sanskrit College—have also been published. In the year 1587 AD, by the command of the Emperor Akbar, Faizi translated this "Lilavati" into Persian. Later, in 1816 AD, Mr. J. Taylor and in 1817 AD, Mr. Henry Thomas Colebrooke translated it into English. Some say...

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>Preface</header>

# Preface

It is said that Bhaskaracharya, upon seeing an astrological alignment <term>yoga: a specific combination of planets in a horoscope</term> for early widowhood in the birth horoscope of his daughter Lilavati, did not marry her. To ensure her name would remain famous throughout the world, he composed this book on *Patiganita* <term>arithmetic: literally 'mathematics of the board'</term> in her name. Some also say that Bhaskaracharya had no children and, being very saddened by this, he composed this work in the name of his wife, Lilavati, so her name would endure for a long time. However, from a copper plate found by Dr. Bhau Daji <note>a famous 19th-century physician and archaeologist</note> near the Nashik region, it appears that Bhaskaracharya had sons, grandsons, and a full lineage. A copy of that copper plate is written here for the pleasure of those interested in history.

---

## Copy of the Copper Plate

1. Salutations to the Lord of the Ganas <note>Ganesha</note>. [The inscription begins with prayers for] success, the nectar of the earth, and protection... from the inhabitants of the Cherevara region.

**Verse—** He whose intellect is formidable in the *Bhatta* school <note>the Purva Mimamsa philosophy of Kumarila Bhatta</note>, who is expert in *Sankhya* philosophy, and whose mind is independent in the *Tantras* <note>technical scientific treatises</note>.
He possesses faultless knowledge of the *Vedas* and is highly skilled in the mechanical arts and crafts. || 1 ||

He is a master of prosody <note>the study of poetic meters</note>, a specialist in the *Vaisheshika* school of philosophy, and an expert in the *Prabhakara* school of philosophy, appearing like the sun <note>Prabhakara</note> himself; a poet among poets. || 2 ||

In the three branches of the many-virtued science of mathematics and astronomy <note>the 'three branches' or *skandhatraye* are Siddhanta (theory), Samhita (omens), and Hora (horoscopy)</note>, he is like the three-eyed Lord Shiva.
Glory be to Shri Bhaskaracharya, whose feet are worshipped by the learned. || 3 ||

May there be prosperity for the illustrious Yadu dynasty, together with all its possessions, in which Vishnu himself was born as a free agent to protect the universe. || 4 ||

The King, the illustrious Bhillama, was the crest-jewel of kings—a lion against the masses of fierce elephants of the thundering Gurjara army;
He was skilled in breaking the gate-like chests of the Latas, and a thorn in the hearts of the Karnatas. After him came the illustrious Jaitrapala, who took away the happiness of the husbands of the trembling and distressed women of the Andhra city. || 5 ||

<summary>This page discusses the romanticized legends of how the "Lilavati" was named—either after the author's daughter or wife—and counters them with historical evidence from a copper plate inscription detailing Bhaskaracharya's immense scholarship and his royal patrons.</summary>
<keywords>Bhaskaracharya, Lilavati, arithmetic, copper plate, Dr. Bhau Daji, Yadu dynasty, King Bhillama, King Jaitrapala, Sanskrit philosophy, mathematics history</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the transcription of the copper plate inscription regarding Bhaskaracharya’s lineage and the royal patronage his family received. It specifically traces the family from the time of King Bhoja through the Yadava kings Jaitrapala and Singhana.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>Preface</header>

Singhana, the ruler of the earth, who was born from the illustrious Jaitrapala and possesses a fragment of the glory of Vishnu <note>original: "Lakshmikanta-lavah"</note>, has dispelled the cycle of worldly existence. He accumulated great wealth on the battlefield and acted as the punisher of the world. He overthrew the Lord of Mathura and the King of Kashi at the front of the battle; even the brave Hammira was defeated by his mere servant boy. || 6 ||

Formerly, the Supreme Being <note>Purushottama/Vishnu</note> took an incarnation in the Yadu clan for the welfare of the world. Glory to King Singhana <note>referred to here as Siddhamahipati</note>, who now protects this entire earth and protects me as well. || 7 ||

In the lineage of the sage Shandilya, there was a king among poets named Trivikrama. His son was Bhaskarabhatta, who was given the title "Lord of Knowledge" <term>Vidyapati: a title of high academic or literary honor</term> by King Bhoja <note>the famous 11th-century polymath King of Dhar</note>. || 8 ||

From him was born Govinda, the "All-Knowing," who was like the god Govinda <note>Krishna</note> himself. His son was Prabhakara, who was like a second sun <note>original: "Prabhakara" also means 'sun'</note>. || 9 ||

From him was born Manoratha, who fulfilled the desires <note>original: "manoratha"</note> of the virtuous. From him was born the illustrious Maheshvaracharya, a lord among poets. || 10 ||

His son was the illustrious and learned Bhaskara, whose feet were worshiped by crowds of poets, who was the root of the vine of sacred Vedic knowledge, and who resided at the feet of Lord Krishna <note>original: "Kansari," the enemy of Kansa</note>. He was a treasury of Brahminical wisdom. No disputant, however skilled, could ever succeed in a debate against his disciples. He was possessed of holy and virtuous fame. || 11 ||

Bhaskara's son was named Lakshmidhara, the chief among all scholars and a master of the meaning of the *Vedas*. He was a "Crowning Lion" <note>original: "Chakravarti," indicating supremacy</note> among logicians, skilled in discerning the essence of the sacrificial rituals <term>Kratu-kriya: the performance of Vedic sacrifices</term>. || 12 ||

Having recognized that Lakshmidhara was expert in the meaning of all the scriptures, King Jaitrapala formerly brought him from his home and made him the leader of the wise men in his court. || 13 ||

**Verse—** From him was born a son, Changadeva, the chief astrologer to the Emperor Singhana. It is he who establishes this college <term>matha: a center for higher learning or a monastery</term> for the purpose of spreading the scriptures composed by the illustrious master Bhaskaracharya. || 14 ||

The works composed by Bhaskara, such as the *Crest-Jewel of Systems* <term>Siddhanta Shiromani: Bhaskara's magnum opus on astronomy and mathematics</term> and others, as well as the works written by others in his lineage, must be studied and explained in my college regularly. || 15 ||

<summary>This page records the illustrious lineage of the mathematician Bhaskaracharya, noting that his grandson Changadeva served as the royal astrologer to King Singhana and founded a college dedicated to teaching Bhaskara's scientific works.</summary>
<keywords>Singhana, Jaitrapala, Bhaskaracharya, Changadeva, Siddhanta Shiromani, genealogy, Yadava dynasty, mathematics college</keywords>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>Introduction</header>

# Introduction

> This gift was given to the monastery by the illustrious Sonhadeva, along with Hemadri and others. All this land and property must be protected by future kings for the increase of their great merit. || 16 ||

Hail! In the Shaka year 1128 <note>approximately 1206 CE</note>, in the year named Prabhava, in the month of Shravana, on the full moon day during a lunar eclipse, the illustrious Sonhadeva—in the presence of all the people and with the ritual pouring of water <note>original: "hastodaka-purvakam," a gesture symbolizing the irrevocable transfer of a gift</note>—granted the primary place to the monastery built by his own Guru. The details are as follows:

In this market town <note>original: "Patani"</note>, whatever is brought for sale, the tax of red lead <note>original: "sindura"</note> which was formerly held by the official <note>original: "Raula"</note> from the customers is now given to the monastery. By the decision of the Brahmins, the tax for the maintenance of the religious grant <term>Brahmottara: land or assets granted to Brahmins for religious purposes</term> was given from the customers. A twentieth part <note>original: "bisova"</note> of the pulse/grain from the customers at the threshing floor; a tax on betel nuts from the customers; and for every oil mill that functions, a portion of oil is to be maintained. Furthermore, land was granted, measured as 306 units, purified on all four sides, located within the cultivated fields of the village, including the field of the scholar Dhamoji... <note>The text here contains several specific local place names and agricultural terms in Old Marathi/Prakrit which refer to the boundaries of the gifted land.</note>

***

Some say that when Bhaskaracharya was studying in his teacher's home <term>Gurukul: a traditional residential school where students live with their mentor</term>, his teacher, seeing him as a leader among the handsome, well-born, and masters of all scriptures, decided to marry his daughter to him. The daughter also wished to marry him. However, after completing his education, when Bhaskaracharya prepared to return to his own home, the teacher proposed the marriage. Bhaskaracharya, considering her his teacher's daughter <note>and therefore spiritually like a sister</note>, did not marry her and returned home. Consequently, his teacher's daughter refused to marry any other man and spent her life in contemplation. To ensure her name remained famous in the world, Bhaskaracharya composed this book, *Lilavati*, in her name. Although such legends <note>original: "kimbadanti"</note> exist and books are often written for specific reasons, it is simply the nature of learned men to act for the welfare of the world.

->Pandit Ramswarup Shastri — Moradabad<-

<summary>This page records a 13th-century land and tax grant to a monastery by Sonhadeva and recounts the popular legend that Bhaskaracharya named his mathematical masterpiece 'Lilavati' after his teacher's daughter to immortalize her.</summary>

<meta>This page marks the formal beginning of the mathematical treatise after the table of contents. It transitions from the structural outline of the book to the traditional invocation (Mangala) and the author's introduction to his work.</meta>

# Auspiciousness!

## Lilavati

### With Prose Order and Commentary.

---

-> I bow to the elephant-faced one, whose lotus feet are worshipped by hosts of gods, <-
-> who, when remembered, generates joy for his devotees by destroying obstacles. <-
-> I compose the *Lilavati*, a clear work on good mathematics that gives pleasure to the clever, <-
-> with its concise syllables and tender, pure words. || 1 ||

**Commentary—** It is an ancient tradition that scientific treatises which have auspicious beginnings, middle sections, and endings will flourish, and their students will become heroic men. Therefore, at the start, the author composes an auspicious invocation <note>original: "Mangala"</note> in the form of a salutation, beginning with the words "He who generates joy." The meaning is: He who, when remembered, destroys <note>original: "vinighnan"; meaning to completely remove</note> obstacles—those sins that act as barriers to the work being undertaken—and generates joy in the devotee who has placed his heart in Him. To Him, whose lotus feet are worshipped by the groups <note>original: "vinda"; a multitude or host</note> of gods <note>original: "vrundaraka"</note>; to the elephant-faced one <note>original: "matanganana"; having the face of a majestic elephant</note>, meaning Lord Ganesha; having bowed to Him with body, speech, and mind—this is the intent. 

I, Bhaskaracharya <note>A renowned 12th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note>, am declaring <note>original: "vachmi"; I speak or reveal</note> the system <term>Patiganita: A branch of mathematics dealing with arithmetic and measurement, literally "the mathematics of the board"</term> of good mathematics, as defined by wise scholars. This work is called the *Lilavati* <note>The title translates to "The Beautiful" or "The Playful One," often personified as a graceful woman</note>, which possesses great charm <note>original: "lalitya"</note>. It is very clear and provides mental satisfaction to the clever—those whose intellect has been sharpened by the study of grammar and other sciences. It is composed with concise syllables that convey vast meanings, and with words that are both tender and pure.

**Interpretation regarding Lord Rama:** In the context of Rama, "the obstacle" <note>original: "vighna"</note> refers to Ravana, the killer of the bird Jatayu. The "elephant-faced one" refers to Kumbhakarna, who had a massive face like a great elephant. After bowing to the husband of Janaki (Rama), who destroyed these figures and brought joy to his devotee Vibhishana, the rest of the meaning is as before.

**Interpretation regarding Lord Krishna:** In the context of Krishna, the "obstacle" refers to the demonic forces, and the "elephant-faced one" refers to the elephant demon Kuvalayapida, whom Krishna destroyed. After bowing to the son of Nanda (Krishna), who brought joy to his devotee Ugrasena, the rest of the meaning is as before. <note>The author uses a poetic device called "shlesha" or double-entendre, allowing the same verse to praise Ganesha, Rama, and Krishna simultaneously.</note> || 1 ||

**Prose Order—** He who, when remembered, generates joy for the devotee by destroying obstacles—having bowed to that elephant-faced one whose feet are worshipped by the hosts of gods, I compose this *Lilavati*, a clear [system] of good mathematics, which gives pleasure to the clever through concise syllables and tender, pure words. || 1 ||

<summary>This page contains the opening invocation of the Lilavati, where Bhaskaracharya seeks blessings from Ganesha, Rama, or Krishna through a multi-layered verse before introducing his mathematical treatise.</summary>
<keywords>Bhaskaracharya, Lilavati, Ganesha, arithmetic, Mangala, Patiganita, Sanskrit mathematics, Shlesha</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues from the introductory invocation. It completes the author’s preface and moves directly into the standard definitions of currency and weights used in 12th-century India, which are essential for the mathematical problems that follow.</meta>

(2)

# Lilavati

**Meaning:** Having bowed to the elephant-faced Lord Ganesha—who destroys obstacles the moment he is remembered, who inspires love in his devotees, and whose feet are saluted by the hosts of gods—I, Bhaskaracharya, am now bringing to light this mathematical treatise <term>Patiganita: A system of arithmetic and measurement performed on a board</term> named *Lilavati*. This work is filled with the beauty of clear, pure words and concise phrasing, and it provides great insight to bring joy to those learned in the mathematical sciences. || 1 ||

**Two decades of cowries are what make a Kakini; four of those are a Pana.**
**Sixteen of those are known here as a Dramma, and sixteen Drammas make a Nishka. || 2 ||**

**Prose Order:** Two decades of cowries <note>original: "Varataka"; small sea shells used as the lowest denomination of currency</note> are a Kakini. Four of those are a Pana. Sixteen of those are a Dramma. Likewise, in this context, a Nishka is to be understood as sixteen Drammas. || 2 ||

**Meaning:** Twenty (20) cowries are called 1 Kakini. 4 Kakinis make one Pana. 16 of those Panas make one Dramma. In this mathematical science, 16 Drammas make one Nishka. || 2 ||

**A Gunja is said here to be equal to two barley grains; a Valla is three Gunjas, and eight of those are a Dharana.**
**Two of those are a Gadyanaka; and one Ghataka is declared to be fourteen Vallas. || 3 ||**

**Prose Order:** Here, a Gunja <note>original: "Gunja"; the red and black seed of the *Abrus precatorius* plant, used as a seed-weight</note> is said to be equal to two barley grains <note>original: "Yava"</note>. A Valla is said to be three Gunjas. Eight of those are called a Dharana. Two of those are called a Gadyanaka. Likewise, one Ghataka is declared to be equal to fourteen Vallas. || 3 ||

**Meaning:** In this mathematical science, one (1) Gunja is equal to two (2) barley grains. 3 Gunjas make 1 Valla. 8 Vallas make 1 Dharana. 2 Dharanas are called a Gadyanaka. 14 Vallas are called a Ghataka. || 3 ||

**Those who know weights call half-of-ten Gunjas a Masha; sixteen of those called Mashas are a Karsha.**
**Four Karshas are a Pala; and a Karsha of gold is known by the name Suvarna. || 4 ||**

**Prose Order:** Those who know the scales call half-of-ten <note>original: "Dashardha"; five</note> Gunjas a Masha. Sixteen of those called Mashas are a Karsha. Four Karshas are a Pala. A Karsha of gold is known by the name Suvarna. || 4 ||

**Meaning:** Experts in weighing call 5 Gunjas 1 Masha. 16 Mashas are called 1 Karsha. 4 Karshas are called 1 Pala. A Karsha-weight of gold is itself called a Suvarna. || 4 ||

<meta>CC-0. Late Pt. Manmohan Shastri Collection Jammu. Digitized by eGangotri</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the mathematical treatise *Lilavati* and defines the fundamental units of currency and weight used in the text, ranging from small cowrie shells and barley seeds to gold coins.</summary>
<keywords>Bhaskaracharya, Lilavati, Ganesha, Varataka, Kakini, Pana, Dramma, Nishka, Gunja, Masha, Karsha, Indian metrology</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the definitions from the introductory chapter. While the previous page focused on currency and small weights (cowries, gunjas), this page defines linear measurements, units of area, and volumes for dry goods like grain.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Definitions.</header>

# Chapter on Definitions.

**Eight barley grains, measured by their width, make a finger-breadth; twenty-four fingers make a hand.**
**Four hands make a staff in this system, and two thousand of these make a Krosha. || 5 ||**

Prose Order: Eight <note>original: "ashta-sankhyaih"</note> barley-widths <note>original: "yavodaraih"; literally "the bellies of barley grains"</note> make a finger-breadth <note>original: "angulam"</note>. Twenty-four <note>original: "shad-gunitaih chaturbhih"; literally "six times four"</note> fingers make a hand <note>original: "hastah"; often called a cubit</note>. In this context, four hands make a staff <note>original: "dandah"</note>. Two thousand of those make a Krosha <note>original: "kroshah"; a standard distance measurement</note>. || 5 ||

Meaning: In this science of mathematics, eight (8) barley grains placed side-by-side make one <term>Angula (finger-breadth)</term>. Twenty-four (24) Angulas make one <term>Hasta (hand/cubit)</term>. 4 Hastas make one <term>Danda (staff)</term>, and 2000 (two thousand) Dandas make one <term>Krosha</term>. || 5 ||

---

**A Yojana consists of four Kroshas; likewise, ten hands make a Vamsha.**
**A square field bounded by four sides of twenty Vamshas each is a Nivartana. || 6 ||**

Prose Order: A Yojana <note>original: "yojanam"</note> is made of four Kroshas. Likewise, ten hands <note>original: "karanam"; a synonym for hasta</note> make a Vamsha <note>original: "vamshah"; literally "bamboo pole"</note>. A field enclosed by four sides, each measuring twenty Vamshas, is a Nivartana <note>original: "nivartanam"</note>. || 6 ||

Meaning: Four Kroshas make one <term>Yojana</term>. Ten hands make one <term>Vamsha</term>. A square area of land that is twenty Vamshas long and twenty Vamshas wide is called a <term>Nivartana</term>. || 6 ||

---

**A twelve-edged figure with a width, length, and depth of one hand is called a Cubic Cubit.**
**For grain and such, the measure of a Cubic Cubit is called the Magadha-Khari in the scriptures. || 7 ||**

Prose Order: A twelve-edged <note>original: "dvadashashram"; referring to a cube's edges</note> figure that measures one hand in width, length, and thickness <note>original: "vistriti-dairghya-pindaih"</note> is known as a Cubic Cubit <note>original: "ghana-hasta"</note>. For measuring grain and other commodities, the volume of a Cubic Cubit is called the Magadha-Khari <note>original: "magadha-kharika"; a standard bushel from the Magadha region</note> in the scriptures. || 7 ||

Meaning: A pit or container with twelve edges that is 1 (one) hand wide, 1 (one) hand long, and 1 (one) hand deep is called a <term>Ghanahasta (Cubic Cubit)</term>. In the measurement of grain and similar goods, the weight or volume contained in a Ghanahasta is known in the scriptures as the <term>Khari</term> of the Magadha country. || 7 ||

---

**A Drona is the sixteenth part of a Khari; an Adhaka is the fourth part of a Drona.**
**A Prastha is the fourth part of an Adhaka; a fourth of a Prastha is declared to be a Kudava. || 8 ||**

Prose Order: Indeed, a sixteenth part of a Khari <note>original: "kharyah shodashamshah"</note> is a Drona. A fourth part of a Drona is an Adhaka. In this context, the fourth part of an Adhaka is declared to be a Prastha. The fourth part of a Prastha is declared by the ancients <note>original: "adyaih"; literally "by the first/earliest ones"</note> to be a Kudava. || 8 ||

Meaning: A 16th (sixteenth) part of the Khari mentioned above is called a <term>Drona</term>. A 4th (fourth) part of a Drona is called an <term>Adhaka</term>. In this mathematical science, a 4th (fourth) part of an Adhaka is a <term>Prastha</term>, and a 4th (fourth) part of a Prastha is called a <term>Kudava</term>. || 8 ||

<meta>CC-0. Late Pt. Manmohan Shastri Collection Jammu. Digitized by eGangotri</meta>
<meta>Original Sanskrit vocabulary: Yava, Angula, Hasta, Danda, Krosha, Yojana, Vamsha, Nivartana, Ghanahasta, Magadha-kharika, Drona, Adhaka, Prastha, Kudava</meta>

<summary>This page defines the traditional Indian units of linear distance, land area, and dry volume used in Bhaskara's calculations. It establishes the hierarchy of measures from the width of a barley grain up to the Yojana (a long distance) and the Khari (a large volume of grain).</summary>
<keywords>linear measurement, units of area, volume measures, Angula, Hasta, Yojana, Nivartana, Khari, Magadha units, Indian mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the definitions of measurements from the previous page. While the previous page focused on linear measurements (hands, spans, and distances like the Krosha), this page transitions into units of weight used for trade and concludes with a prayer to Lord Ganesha to mark the formal beginning of the mathematical treatise.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

### Now, the Supplement —

**A Ser is said here to be equal to seventy-two Tanks, each Tank being equal to three-quarters of a Gadyanaka.**
**A weight of forty Sers is called a Man; these are the Turkic definitions for weighing grain and other goods. || 1 ||**

Prose Order: In this context, for the weighing of grain and other goods, a <term>Ser</term> <note>original: "serah"</note> is said to be equal to seventy-two <note>original: "dvi-sapta-tulyaikh"; literally "two and seventy"</note> <term>Tanks</term> <note>original: "takai-h"</note>, which are themselves equal to a three-quarter <note>original: "padona"; literally "less one quarter"</note> <term>Gadyanaka</term>. A <term>Man</term> <note>original: "manabhidhanam"; a maund</note> is said to be forty <note>original: "kha-yugai-h"; a numerical code where 'kha' (space/zero) and 'yuga' (four) represent 40</note> Sers. This is the Turkic nomenclature <note>original: "turushka-samjna"</note>. || 1 ||

Meaning: A three-quarter Gadyanaka—meaning 36 (thirty-six) <term>Rattis</term> (the weight of a Gunja seed)—constitutes 1 (one) **Tank**. Seventy-two (72) Tanks make 1 (one) **Ser** in the weighing of grain and other commodities. Forty (40) Sers make 1 (one) **Man**. This is the system of nomenclature established by the "Yavanas" <note>A term used here for the Mughal or Islamic administrative authorities</note>. || 1 ||

**A Ser consists of one hundred and ninety-two Ghatakas; five of those make a Ghatika, and eight of those—**
**Make a Man; this is the nomenclature established by Shah Alamgir for use in his own kingdom and cities. || 2 ||**

Prose Order: Here follows the nomenclature established by <term>Alamgir Shah</term> <note>The Mughal Emperor Aurangzeb, reigned 1658–1707</note> for use in his own kingdom and cities. A Ser consists of weights numbering "two, nine, and one" <note>original: "dvy-anke-ndu"; a symbolic number code: dvi (2), anka (9), indu (1), read right-to-left as 192</note> **Ghatakas**. Five Sers make a **Ghatika**. Eight of those Ghatikas make a **Man**. || 2 ||

Meaning: In the weight system prevalent during the reign of Emperor Alamgir, 192 (one hundred and ninety-two) Ghatakas make 1 (one) Ser. 5 (five) Sers make 1 (one) <term>Dhadi</term> <note>original: "ghatika" in Sanskrit, explained as "dhadi" in Hindi; a regional unit</note>. 8 (eight) Dhadis make 1 (one) Man. This nomenclature is still prevalent today in the "Middle Country" <note>Madhya-desha, referring to the central Ganges plain of India</note>. || 2 ||

**The remaining definitions regarding time and other measures should be understood as they are famous in worldly usage.**

Meaning: The remaining definitions of time and other units should be known through common public usage. For example: 60 (sixty) seconds make 1 minute. 60 minutes make 1 hour. 24 (twenty-four) hours make 1 day and night. 15 (fifteen) days and nights make 1 **Paksha** (fortnight). 2 Pakshas make 1 month. 12 (twelve) months make a year. Also, in traditional units: 60 **Palas** make 1 **Ghadi**. 2.5 (two and a half) Ghadis make 1 hour. 12 hours make 1 day. 7 (seven) days make 1 week, and so on.

->Thus ends the chapter on definitions.<-

> **Salutations to Ganesha, who possesses the pure luster of a blue lotus,**
> **And who sports with the restless black serpent dangling playfully from his neck. || 1 ||**

Prose Order: Salutations to Ganesha, who sports with the restless black serpent <note>original: "kala-vyala"; the dark snake is a traditional ornament of the divine family</note> dangling playfully from his neck <note>original: "lila-gala-lulu-lallola"</note>, and whose complexion has the pure luster of a blue lotus <note>original: "nila-kamala-amala-kantaye"</note>. || 1 ||

Meaning: I bow to Lord Ganesha, who plays with the flickering serpent hanging around his neck and whose body shines with the clean, oily radiance of a blue lotus flower. || 1 ||

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>The Eight Basic Operations</header>

# The Eight Basic Operations

> **One, ten, hundred, thousand, ten-thousand, hundred-thousand, million, and ten-million are the names in order. || 2 ||**
> **Then come hundred-million, billion, ten-billion, hundred-billion, trillion, and ten-trillion.**
> **Then the "ocean," the "last," the "middle," and the "beyond-half." These names for the places of numbers, each ten times greater than the last, were created by the ancients for the purpose of practical calculation. || 3 ||**

**Prose Order:** The names—unit, ten, hundred, thousand, ten-thousand <note>original: "ayuta"</note>, hundred-thousand <note>original: "laksha"</note>, million <note>original: "prayuta"</note>, and ten-million <note>original: "koti"</note>; then hundred-million <note>original: "arbuda"</note>, billion <note>original: "abja"</note>, ten-billion <note>original: "kharva"</note>, hundred-billion <note>original: "nikharva"</note>, trillion <note>original: "mahapadma"</note>, and ten-trillion <note>original: "shanku"</note>; following that, the "ocean" <note>original: "jaladhi"; 10^14</note>, the "last" <note>original: "antya"; 10^15</note>, the "middle" <note>original: "madhya"; 10^16</note>, and the "beyond-half" <note>original: "parardha"; 10^17</note>—were established by earlier teachers in sequence, each being ten times the previous, to facilitate the use of numbers. || 2 || 3 ||

**Meaning:** Unit, ten, hundred, thousand, ten-thousand, lakh, ten-lakh, crore, etc.—for the sake of mathematical transactions, the ancient teachers designated these names so that each subsequent place is ten times the value of the one before it. For example, ten is ten times one; a hundred is ten times ten; a thousand is ten times a hundred, and so on. || 2 || 3 ||

---

## Now, the rule for Addition and Subtraction in half a verse:

->The method for performing addition and subtraction is described in the following half-stanza:<-

**(Rule 1) The sum of the digits, or their difference, should be calculated according to their places, either in order or in reverse order.**

**Prose Order:** The addition <note>original: "yogah"</note> or the difference <note>original: "antaram"</note> should be performed according to the specific place <note>original: "yathasthanakam"</note>, either in direct order <note>from right to left</note> or in reverse order <note>from left to right</note>.

**Meaning:** Whether adding or subtracting, one must operate digit-by-digit according to their place value—that is, adding or subtracting the units to units, tens to tens, hundreds to hundreds, and so forth. This can be done starting from the first digit (units) or from the last digit (the highest place value).

**The Problem:** An example regarding addition and subtraction—

> **O dear child Lilavati, you who possess a clever mind, tell me the sum of these numbers: two, five, thirty-two, one hundred and ninety-three, eighteen, and ten.**
> **Add a hundred to this sum, and then tell me the result after subtracting ten thousand, if you are skilled in the method of addition and subtraction.**

**Prose Order:** O child! O wise Lilavati! If you are skilled in the logic of addition and subtraction <note>original: "vyakte yukti-vyavakalana-marge"</note>, then tell me the sum of these: 2, 5, 32, 193, 18, and 10, increased by 100. Also, tell me the result when 10,000 <note>original: "ayuta"</note> is subtracted from it. || 1 ||

<page-num>(6)</page-num>
<header>LILAVATI</header>

**Meaning:** O Lilavati! You who possess the pride of a sixteen-year-old’s intellect! If you are clever in arithmetic <note>original: *pāṭī-gaṇita*, referring to mathematics performed on a board or slate</note> specifically in addition and subtraction, then tell me: what is the total of 2 (two), 5 (five), 32 (thirty-two), 193 (one hundred ninety-three), 18 (eighteen), 10 (ten), and 100 (one hundred)? And when all these are added together, how much remains when the sum is subtracted from 10,000 (ten thousand)? || 1 ||

**Statement:** 2 | 5 | 32 | 193 | 18 | 10 | 100
Result of addition <note>original: *samyojanājjātam*</note>: 360.

**Explanation:** According to the previously mentioned rule, following the sequence, first sum all the digits in the units place:

| |
| :--- |
| 2 |
| 5 |
| 32 |
| 193 |
| 18 |
| 10 |
| 100 |
| --- |
| 360 |

When added—that is, 2 (two) and 5 (five) make 7 (seven), and 2 (two) make 9 (nine), and 3 (three) make 12 (twelve), and 8 (eight) make 20 (twenty). From this twenty, the unit digit 0 (zero) is placed in the units column, beneath the other unit digits. Then, the remaining 2 (two) from the tens place is held in memory <note>a "carry" in modern terms</note>, and the digits in the tens place are summed: 3 (three) and 9 (nine) make 12 (twelve), and 1 (one) makes 13 (thirteen), and 1 (one) makes 14 (fourteen). To this, the previously carried 2 (two) is added, making 16 (sixteen). From this, the 6 (six) is placed to the left of the zero in the tens column, resulting in 60. The remaining 1 (one) from the sixteen is held in memory, and the digits in the hundreds place are counted: 1 (one) and 1 (one) make 2 (two). To this, the previous 1 (one) is added, making 3 (three). This is placed to the left of the six in the hundreds column, resulting in 360. Thus, the sum is three hundred sixty; in this same manner, addition should be performed elsewhere.

Subtracted from ten thousand <note>original: *ayuta*</note> (10,000), the result is 9,640.

<gloss>9910</gloss> <note>This interlinear annotation appears to be a correction or an alternative calculation note in the manuscript.</note>

**Explanation:** 10,000 was subtracted according to the previously mentioned rule. That is, in the units place:

| |
| :--- |
| 10000 |
| 360 |
| --- |
| 9640 |

When the unit-place zero was subtracted from the unit-place zero, only zero remained. That 0 was placed beneath the unit digits. Next, the digit in the tens place is also zero. Since 6 (six) cannot be subtracted from it, one hundred is taken from the hundreds place; however, since the hundreds and thousands places are also zero here, one ten-thousand <note>original: *ayuta*</note> is taken from the ten-thousands place. This is converted into ten thousands, of which 9 (nine) are left in the thousands place. From the remaining 1 (one) thousand, ten hundreds are made, of which 9 (nine) are left in the hundreds place. From the remaining one hundred, ten tens were made, from which 6 (six) tens were subtracted...

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>Types of Multiplication ( 7 )</header>
<meta>image quality: excellent, footer metadata: CC-0. Late Pt. Manmohan Shastri Collection Jammu. Digitized by eGangotri</meta>

...the remaining 4 (four) were placed in the tens column to the left of the 0 (zero) that was already there. Next, 3 (three) was subtracted from the 9 (nine) in the hundreds place, leaving 6 (six); this was placed in the hundreds column to the left of the 4. Since there were no more digits left to subtract from, the remaining digits from the original number were brought down to their respective places on the left—that is, the digit in the thousands place stayed in the thousands place. Thus, subtracting 360 (three hundred sixty) from 10,000 (ten thousand) leaves a remainder of 9640 (nine thousand six hundred forty). This method should be understood for other problems as well.

->Thus ends the section on Addition and Subtraction.<-

# Now, the Rule for Multiplication in two and a half verses—

The method of multiplication is now explained in two and a half verses. This operation of <term>multiplication</term> <note>original: *guṇana*</note> is of five types:
1. <term>Multiplication by Form</term> <note>original: *rūpaguṇā* - standard multiplication using the multiplier as it is</note>
2. <term>Multiplication by Place</term> <note>original: *sthānaguṇā* - multiplication based on the decimal position</note>
3. <term>Multiplication by Division</term> <note>original: *vibhāgaguṇā* - breaking the multiplier into factors</note>
4. <term>Multiplication by Parts</term> <note>original: *khaṇḍaguṇā* - breaking the multiplier into additive parts</note>
5. <term>Multiplication by a Desired Number</term> <note>original: *iṣṭaguṇā*</note>.

The number by which we multiply is called the <term>multiplier</term> <note>original: *guṇaka*</note> and the number being multiplied is called the <term>multiplicand</term> <note>original: *guṇya*</note>.

> (Rule 2) Multiply the last digit of the multiplicand by the multiplier; 
> then, shifting the multiplier, do the same for the penultimate digit, 
> and so on for the preceding ones. || 4 ||

**Word-for-word connection:** Multiply the last digit of the multiplicand with the multiplier. In this way, by shifting the multiplier, multiply the penultimate digit. In this same way, multiply all the preceding digits in order. || 4 ||

**Meaning:** Multiply the final digit of the multiplicand by the multiplier; then, move the multiplier over and multiply the next digit beside it. Continue this process, using the same multiplier to multiply all the digits from the end to the beginning in sequence. Because the multiplier is used in its natural form <note>its "rūpa"</note> to perform the multiplication, this is called "Multiplication by Form" <note>rūpaguṇā</note>. || 4 ||

# Here is the Example—An illustration regarding multiplication:

> O girl! O Lilavati, whose eyes are as restless as those of a young fawn! 
> O auspicious one! Tell me: if you are skilled in the methods of 
> multiplication by form, place, division, and parts, 
> what would be the result of the digits 135 <note>original: *pañca-try-eka* (five, three, one). In ancient Indian math, numbers are often listed from units place upwards, so 5-3-1 represents 135</note> 
> multiplied by 12 <note>original: *divākara* (the Sun). In Sanskrit poetic math, "Sun" is a code for 12, representing the twelve solar months or aspects of the sun</note>? 
> And tell me, what would those multiplied digits be if they were then 
> divided by that same multiplier? || 2 ||

**Word-for-word connection:** O girl! O you with restless eyes like a young deer! O Lilavati! O auspicious one! If you are capable in multiplication by form, place, division, and parts, then tell me: how much would the digits measured as five-three-one (135) be when multiplied by twelve? Furthermore, tell me how much they would be when those product digits are divided by that same multiplier. || 2 ||

<vocab>multiplication types (guṇaprakāra), addition (saṅkalita), subtraction (vyavakalita), multiplier (guṇaka), multiplicand (guṇya), multiplication by form (rūpaguṇā), multiplication by place (sthānaguṇā), multiplication by division (vibhāgaguṇā), multiplication by parts (khaṇḍaguṇā), multiplication by choice (iṣṭaguṇā), Lilavati, multiplication by twelve (divākaraguṇā), prose order (anvaya)</vocab>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

# Meaning:
O girl! You with eyes like a young fawn! O mine of cleverness! Auspicious Lilavati! If you know the methods of multiplication by Form, Place, Division, and Parts, then tell me: what is the result if 135 (one hundred thirty-five) is multiplied by 12 (twelve)? Tell me this using all the various methods. Also, tell me what the result is when that same product is divided by 12. // 2 //

## Setup:
<term>Multiplicand</term> <note>original: *guṇya*</note>: 135
<term>Multiplier</term> <note>original: *guṇaka*</note>: 12

### Rule: Multiply the last digit of the multiplicand by the multiplier.
By performing this, the result is 1620.

**Explanation:** According to the previously mentioned rule of multiplication, take the 5 at the end of the multiplicand 135 and multiply it by the multiplier 12.

| 135 | Multiplying by twelve gives 60 (sixty). From this sixty, the zero |
| :--- | :--- |
| 12 | is placed in the units place below the multiplicand and multiplier, and the remaining 6 (six) |
| --- | is kept in memory. Then, multiply the 3 (three) next to the end by the multiplier; |
| 1620 | |

3 times 12 is 36 (thirty-six). To this, add the 6 from the previous sixty, which makes 42 (forty-two). From this, the last digit 2 is placed in the tens place to the left of the previous zero, and the remaining 4 (four) is kept in memory. Then, multiply the third digit, 1 (one), by the multiplier—meaning 12 times 1 is 12. To this twelve, add the 4 from the previous forty-two, which results in 16 (sixteen). These digits are placed to the left of the previously placed digits. Thus, the product is 1620 (one thousand six hundred twenty). This method is commonly used everywhere.

# And "Numbers move from right to left"—
<note>original: *aṅkānāṃ vāmato gatiḥ* — a fundamental rule in Indian mathematics stating that while we read from left to right, the place value increases as we move toward the left.</note>

| 1 2 3 5 | Multiplication of the last digit. | Numbers are counted from the right side; according to this method, the last digit in the multiplicand is 1 (one). |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1 5 6 5 | Multiplication of the second digit. | Multiplying it by 12 (twelve) results in 12. <note>The text here describes placing the product 12 starting at the appropriate place value.</note> |
| 1 6 2 0 | Multiplication of the third digit. | Multiplying the second digit, 3 (three), by the multiplier 12 gives 36 (thirty-six). |
| | This is the result. | Of this, 6 is placed in the position of the multiplicand 3, and 3 is written below the 2 in the hundreds place and added. |

This makes 1565 <note>The text is demonstrating the step-by-step accumulation of products.</note>. Then, multiplying the third digit, 5 (five), by the multiplier 12 gives 60 (sixty). From this, the zero is written in the place of the multiplicand 5, and the 6 (six) is added to the 6 in the tens place, which equals 12 (twelve). The 2 (two) is written in the tens place, and the remaining 1 (one) is carried to the hundred-

<header>Methods of Multiplication!</header>

Added 5 to the 1 <note>This likely refers to a carry operation from the previous page's calculation</note>, making 6; the result then became 1620 (one thousand six hundred twenty).

### Now, the Method of Multiplication by Parts—

> (Verse 3) **The multiplicand should be written repeatedly, as many times as there are parts of the multiplier. Multiplied by those parts and added, the result is found.** <note>original Sanskrit: *guṇyastvadho'dho guṇakhaṇḍatulyas-taiḥ khaṇḍakaiḥ saṅguṇito yuto vā*</note>

**Prose order:** Or, the <term>multiplicand</term> <note>the number being multiplied</note>, placed one below the other as many times as there are parts of the <term>multiplier</term>, is multiplied by those parts and then added; the result is the product.

*Meaning:* Alternatively, imagine any number of parts (segments) that make up the multiplier. Place the multiplicand in as many spots as there are parts. Multiply the multiplicand separately by each part of the multiplier placed below it. When these individual products are added together, the final product is obtained.

**Setup: Or, when the multiplier is divided into the parts 8 and 4.**

By multiplying the multiplicand separately by these two, the same result of 1620 is produced.

**Expansion:** In this case, the multiplier 12 (twelve) was split into two parts: 8 (eight) and 4 (four).

| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 135 | 135 | The multiplicand 135 was placed in two positions. The two parts of the multiplier were placed in two spots separately below the multiplicand and multiplied. That is, the multiplicand 135 (one hundred thirty-five) was multiplied by the multiplier part 8 (eight), yielding 1080 (one thousand eighty); and the same multiplicand 135 was multiplied by the second part, 4 (four), yielding 540 (five hundred forty). |
| 8 | 4 | |
| --- | --- | |
| 1080 | 540 | |
| --- | --- | |
| 1080 | | |
| 540 | | |
| --- | | |
| 1620 | Product. | |

When these were added together, the result was the same 1620 (one thousand six hundred twenty).

### Now, the Method of Multiplication by Division—

> (Verse 4) **If the multiplier is divisible by a number, the multiplicand multiplied by that divisor and then by the resulting quotient yields the product.** <note>original Sanskrit: *bhakto guṇaḥ śuddhyati yena tena labdhyā ca guṇyo guṇitaḥ phalaṃ vā*</note> // 5 //

**Prose order:** Or, if the multiplier is divided by a number such that it leaves no remainder, the multiplicand multiplied by that divisor and then by the <term>quotient</term> <note>the result of the division</note> becomes the result. // 5 //

*Meaning:* Alternatively, if the multiplier can be divided by any digit without leaving a remainder, then the product is also obtained by multiplying the multiplicand by that divisor, and then multiplying that result by the quotient. // 5 //

<meta>CC-0. Late Pt. Manmohan Shastri Collection Jammu. Digitized by eGangotri</meta>

(10)

# Lilavati.

**Setup: Or, the <term>multiplier</term> <note>the number being multiplied by</note> is divided by three, resulting in a <term>quotient</term> <note>the result of division</note> of 4. By multiplying the <term>multiplicand</term> <note>the number to be multiplied</note> by these three and four, the same result of 1620 is produced.**

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| 3 ) 12 ( 4 | Quotient from dividing the multiplier |
| 12 | Multiplication of the quotient |
| --- | |
| 135 | |
| 4 | Then, by the divisor of the multiplier |
| --- | |
| 540 | |
| | obtaining the result by multiplying |
| 3 | |
| --- | |
| 1620 | the same result. ||

**Expansion:** Or, according to the method mentioned above, the multiplier 12 (twelve) was divided by 3 (three), which gave a quotient of 4 (four), leaving no remainder. The multiplicand 135 was multiplied by this quotient 4, resulting in a product of 540 (five hundred forty). Then, this was multiplied by the 3 (three) that had been used to divide the multiplier. The result was the same 1620 (one thousand six hundred twenty). In this method, the multiplication is performed by dividing the multiplier; therefore, it is called <term>Division Multiplication</term> <note>original: *vibhāga-guṇā*</note>.

---

->Now, the method for Multiplication by Place Value<-

### (Verse 5) The division of form is twofold: [the multiplier] multiplied separately by the digits of the places and then added. <note>original Sanskrit: *dvidhā bhavedrūpavibhāga evaṃ sthānaiḥ pṛthagvā guṇitaḥ sametaḥ*</note>

**Prose order:** Or, the product is obtained by multiplying separately by the digits of the places and then adding them. Thus, the division of form is twofold.

**Meaning:** Alternatively, first multiply by the unit-place digit of the multiplier, then by the tens-place digit, and so on for as many digits as are in the multiplier. When these are multiplied separately in order and then added together, the product is obtained.

**Setup: Or, in the division by place, using the parts 1 and 2. When the multiplicand is multiplied separately by these two and placed according to their positions and added, the same result of 1620 is produced.**

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| 135 | 135 |
| 2 | 1 |
| --- | --- |
| 270 | 135 |
| 270 | |
| 135 | |
| --- | |
| 1620 result. | |

**Expansion:** Or, according to the method stated above, the place division was performed. That is, first the multiplicand 135 was multiplied by the unit-place digit 2 (two) of the multiplier, which gave 270 (two hundred seventy). Then, the multiplicand 135 was multiplied by the tens-place digit 1 (one), which gave the same 135 (one hundred thirty-five). The digits multiplied by the tens-place digit were written by shifting them one place to the left and then added. This resulted in the same 1620 (one thousand six hundred twenty).

<note>This method is essentially the modern long multiplication algorithm, where each partial product is shifted to represent its place value (units, tens, etc.).</note>

<summary>This page explains two further methods of multiplication from the Lilavati: "Division Multiplication," where the multiplier is factored, and "Place Value Multiplication," which mirrors the modern long multiplication algorithm.</summary>

<keywords>Lilavati, Bhaskara II, multiplication, division multiplication, place value multiplication, mathematics, Indian mathematics, arithmetic, Sanskrit, Hindi</keywords>

( 11 )

# Multiplication Method.

### ->Method for multiplying by assuming a desired number<-

** (Verse 6) Alternatively, multiply by the <term>multiplier</term> <note>the number you multiply by</note> as increased or decreased by a desired number; then subtract or add the product of the <term>multiplicand</term> <note>the number to be multiplied</note> and that same desired number. <note>original Sanskrit: "iṣṭonayuktena guṇena nighno'bhīṣṭaghnaguṇyānvita-varjito vā"</note>**

**Prose order:** Alternatively, the <term>multiplicand</term> multiplied by the <term>multiplier</term> as decreased or increased by a desired number, and then [respectively] added to or subtracted from the product of the <term>multiplicand</term> and that desired number, gives the result. || 6 ||

**Meaning:** Alternatively, one may subtract from or add to the <term>multiplier</term> a certain digit that makes the multiplication easier. Multiply the <term>multiplicand</term> by this modified multiplier. If you subtracted a digit from the <term>multiplier</term> originally, then multiply the <term>multiplicand</term> by that same digit and add the resulting <term>product</term> <note>original: "labdhi" (literally "attainment")</note> to the first result. Conversely, if you added a digit to the <term>multiplier</term>, multiply the <term>multiplicand</term> by that same digit and subtract the result from the first product. The remainder will be the final <term>product</term> <note>original: "guṇan-phala"</note>. || 6 ||

**Setup:** Or, using the <term>multiplier</term> decreased by two (which is 10) and then multiplying the <term>multiplicand</term> separately by 10 and 2, the result is the same: 1620.

**Expansion:** Or, according to the method mentioned above, 2 was subtracted from the <term>multiplier</term>.

| 135 | 135 |
| :---: | :---: |
| 10 | 2 |
| --- | --- |
| 1350 | 270 |
| 1350 | Addition. |
| 270 | |
| --- | --- |
| 1620 | Result. |

The remaining 10 (ten) was multiplied by the <term>multiplicand</term>, resulting in 1350 (one thousand three hundred fifty). Then, the <term>multiplicand</term> 135 was multiplied by the 2 (two) that was previously subtracted, resulting in 270 (two hundred seventy). By adding these two products together, the result is the same 1620 (one thousand six hundred twenty). || 6 ||

---

**Or, the <term>multiplicand</term> is multiplied by the <term>multiplier</term> increased by eight (which is 20), and then the <term>multiplicand</term> multiplied by 8 (eight) is subtracted; the result is the same: 1620.**

**Expansion:** Or, according to the method mentioned above, if we take 8 (eight) as our <term>desired number</term> <note>original: "iṣṭa"</note> and add it to the <term>multiplier</term> 12 (twelve), it becomes 20 (twenty). Then, the <term>multiplicand</term> 135 is multiplied by this <term>multiplier</term> of 20, which results in 2700 (two thousand seven hundred). Next, the <term>multiplicand</term> 135 is multiplied by the 8 (eight) that was previously chosen as the <term>desired number</term>, resulting in 1080 (one thousand eighty). When this is subtracted from the amount produced by multiplying by 20, the remainder is 1620. This is the result.

| 135 | 135 | Subtraction |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 20 | 8 | 2700 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2700 | 1080 | 1080 |
| | | --- |
| | | 1620 |
| | | Final Result |

<summary>This page explains a flexible multiplication technique where the multiplier is rounded to a nearby convenient number (an "assumed" or "desired" number). The final product is then adjusted by adding or subtracting the difference, demonstrating the distributive property of multiplication.</summary>
<keywords>multiplication methods, iṣṭa-kalpanā, multiplicand, multiplier, arithmetic rules, Lilavati, distributive property</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the methods of multiplication discussed on page 11 to the inverse operation: division. It uses the result of the previous multiplication example (1620) to demonstrate the step-by-step long division process.</meta>

( 12 )

# Līlāvatī

## Now, Division.

(The method of division.) (a) That which is divided is called the **dividend** <note>original: bhājya</note> and that which divides it is called the **divisor** <note>original: bhājaka</note>.

### A verse on the rule for division—

->A verse regarding the method of division<-

> (Sūtra 7) **The multiple of the divisor which can be subtracted from the dividend, starting from the last place, is truly the result in division.** <note>original Sanskrit: "bhājyāddharaḥ śuddhyati yadguṇaḥ syādantyātphalaṃ tatkhalu bhāgahāre"</note>

**Prose order:** Starting from the final digit of the dividend, the number of times the divisor can be subtracted is indeed the result in division.

**Meaning:** Starting from the final digit of the dividend, the number of times (the multiple) the divisor can be distributed into the dividend is, with certainty, the result obtained in the division.

**Here, using the previous example, we arrange the calculated digits and factors for the sake of division—Dividend: 1620. Divisor: 12. The quotient obtained from the division: 135.**

| Divisor | Dividend | Result |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 12 ) | 1620 | ( 0135 |
| | 12 | |
| | 42 | |
| | 36 | |
| | 060 | |
| | 60 | |
| | 00 | |

**Expansion:** To demonstrate the division of the numbers multiplied in the previous example, the layout of the division is shown here using that same case. The <term>dividend</term> <note>the total to be divided</note> is 1620 (one thousand six hundred twenty) and the <term>divisor</term> <note>the number we divide by</note> is 12 (twelve). According to the rule stated above, when trying to divide the last digit, 1 (one), by twelve, no digit is <term>obtained</term> <note>original: "labdha"</note>, so the result is a zero; this is written to the right of the dividend. Next, 16 (sixteen) is divided; one is obtained as the quotient and 4 (four) remains. This quotient of one is placed to the right of the 0 (zero). The digit 2 (two) is then brought down to the remainder 4 (four), making it forty-two. The divisor goes into that three times; then, the divisor multiplied by three, which is 36 (thirty-six), is subtracted from 42 (forty-two), leaving 6 as the remainder. This quotient of three is placed to the right of the first quotient digit. Then, the 0 (zero) is brought down to the remainder 6, making it 60 (sixty). The divisor goes into that 5 times; then, the divisor multiplied by five, which is 60 (sixty), is subtracted from 60, resulting in <term>no remainder</term> <note>original: "niḥśeṣa", meaning the division is complete</note>. This quotient of 5 (five) is placed to the right of the previous quotient digits. Thus, the total **quotient is 135 (one hundred thirty-five).**

## Another Method—The Second Way—

> (Sūtra 8) **Or, if possible, by reducing both the divisor and the dividend by some common factor, the result remains the same.** <note>original Sanskrit: "samena kenāpyapavartya hārbhājyau bhavedvā sati sambhave tu"</note> ॥ 7 ॥

**Prose order:** Alternatively, if possible, after reducing both the <term>divisor</term> <note>original: hāra</note> and the <term>dividend</term> <note>original: bhājya</note> by any equal number, the result is obtained. ॥ 7 ॥

**Meaning:** Alternatively, if it is possible, divide both the dividend and the divisor by a common number to simplify them; then, by dividing the <term>quotient</term> <note>original: labdhi</note> of the dividend by the <term>quotient</term> of the divisor, the resulting value is the final answer. ॥ 7 ॥

Or, the dividend and divisor reduced by three <gloss>540</gloss> <gloss>4</gloss> or by four <gloss>405</gloss> <gloss>3</gloss>; when divided by their respective new divisors, the result is that same 135 ॥

**Expansion:** Alternatively, according to the method mentioned above, both the dividend and the divisor are divided by three. That is, when the dividend 1620 is divided by three, the quotient is 540 (five hundred forty); and when the divisor 12 is divided by three, the quotient is 4 (four). Subsequently, when the dividend's quotient 540 is divided by the divi-

### Now, the Rule for Squaring in two verses—
Now, the method for performing a square is stated in two verses:

( Sūtra 9 ) **The product of two equal numbers is called the square.** <note>original Sanskrit: "samadvighātaḥ kṛtirucyate"</note>
**Prose order:** The product of two equals is called a <term>square</term> <note>original: kṛti, literally "doing" or "action," used here as a mathematical term for a square</note>.
**Meaning:** The result obtained by multiplying two identical numbers together is called a square.

( Sūtra 10 ) **Now, set down the square of the last digit; then the remaining digits multiplied by twice the last digit should be placed above their respective places. Then, discarding the last digit, move the remaining number and repeat the process.** ॥ 8 ॥ <note>original: "atha sthāpyo’ntyavargo dviguṇāntyanighnāḥ..."</note>

**Prose order:** Now, the square of the last digit is to be set down; likewise, the other digits, multiplied by twice the last digit, are to be placed above their own positions. Then, having discarded the last digit and shifted the remaining number, the square of the new last digit is to be set down. This should be done until no digits remain.

**Meaning:** (If there are multiple digits) Square the <term>last digit</term> <note>original: antya, the digit in the highest place value, usually the leftmost</note> and place it above that last digit. Then, multiply the remaining digits by twice that last digit and place those results above their respective digits. After this, delete the last digit that was just squared, shift the remaining numbers, and perform the same operation (squaring the new "last" digit, etc.) as before. Continue this process until all digits are exhausted. Finally, arrange all the obtained numbers by shifting each one place further and add them together; the result is the square. ॥ 8 ॥

### Here is the Illustration—Example regarding Squaring:
**O friend! If you know the method of squaring, tell me the square of nine, of fourteen, of three hundred less three, and also of ten thousand plus five.** ॥ 3 ॥

**Prose order:** O friend! If you know the path of the rule for squaring, then tell me the square of nine, of fourteen, of three hundred diminished by three, and of ten thousand increased by five. ॥ 3 ॥

**Meaning:** O dear Līlāvatī! If you know the method for squaring, then tell me the individual squares of 9 (nine), 14 (fourteen), 297 (two hundred ninety-seven), and 10005 (ten thousand five). ॥ 3 ॥

**Layout:** 9 | 14 | 297 | 10005. By performing the operation as stated, the squares are produced: 81 | 196 | 88209 | 100100025.

**Expansion:** (a) Following the aforementioned rule, when the digit 9 (nine) was multiplied by its equal digit nine, the square became 81.

( b ) According to (Sūtra 10), the square of 14 (fourteen) was calculated. # The Third Method of Squaring

->This method is particularly simple for squaring two-digit numbers.<-

> (Sūtra 13) **The product of the two parts doubled,**
> **combined with the sum of the squares of those parts, is the square.**
> <note>original Sanskrit: "khaṇḍa-dvayasyābhihati-dvinighnī tat-khaṇḍa-varga-aikya-yutā kṛtir vā"</note>

**Prose Order:** Alternatively, the product of the two parts doubled, and then added to the sum of the squares of those parts, becomes the <term>square</term> <note>original: kṛti; literally "action" or "making," used here to mean the result of multiplying a number by itself</note>.

**Meaning:** Alternatively, whatever number you wish to square, divide it into two parts. Multiply those two parts together and then double the result. Then, calculate the squares of both parts individually. Adding these squares to the previously doubled product yields the square of the original number.

Example:—
( a ) According to the method described above,
the number 9 (nine) is divided into two parts: 5 (five) and 4 (four).

| Original Number | Two Parts | Mutually Multiplied | Doubled | Square of Both | Addition |
| :--- | :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 9 | 5, 4 | 5<br>4<br>———<br>20 | 20<br>2<br>———<br>40 | 5 \| 4<br>5 \| 4<br>——— ———<br>25 \| 16 | 40<br>25<br>16<br>——— |
| | | | | | **Square Result 81** |

Then, 5 (five) and 4 (four) were multiplied together, resulting in 20 (twenty). This was doubled to make 40 (forty). Next, the squares of the two parts were calculated separately: the square of 5 (five) is 25 (twenty-five), and the square of 4 (four) is 16 (sixteen). When these are added to the 40 (forty), the sum is 81 (eighty-one). This is the square result for 9 (nine).

( b ) Alternatively, for 14 (fourteen), two parts 6 (six) and 8 (eight) were made.
Following this, the two parts 6 and 8 were multiplied together to get 48 (forty-eight); this was doubled to get 96 (ninety-six). Then the squares of the two parts were calculated separately: the square of 6 (six) is 36 (thirty-six) and the square of 8 (eight) is 64 (sixty-four). When these two square results are added to 96, the sum is 196 (one hundred ninety-six). This is the square result.

| Original Number | Two Parts | Mutually Multiplied | Doubled | Square of both parts | Addition |
| :--- | :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 14 | 6, 8 | 6<br>8<br>———<br>48 | 48<br>2<br>———<br>96 | 6 \| 8<br>6 \| 8<br>——— ———<br>36 \| 64 | 96<br>36<br>64<br>——— |
| | | | | | **Square Result 196** |

## The Fourth Method of Squaring.

**The product of the number decreased and increased by an arbitrary amount, combined with the square of that arbitrary amount, is the square. || 9 ||**
<note>original Sanskrit: "iṣṭonayugmāśivadhaḥ kṛtiḥ syādiṣṭasya vargēṇa samanvitō vā"</note>

**Prose Order:** Alternatively, the product of the number decreased and increased by the <term>arbitrary number</term> <note>original: iṣṭa; literally "desired" or "chosen"</note>, when combined with the square of that arbitrary number, becomes the square. || 9 ||

**Meaning:** Alternatively, choose any number as the "arbitrary number" <note>iṣṭa</note>. Subtract it from the original number in one place and add it to the original number in another. Then, multiply these two resulting numbers together. Calculate the square of the arbitrary number you chose and add it to the product of the two numbers. The result is the square of the original number.

### Alternatively, the number is 297. This is decreased and increased by 3.
### 294 and 300. Their product is 88200. With the square of three (9) added,
### the square is produced as 88209. Thus it is everywhere.

<margin>

hundred ninety-four)<br>
remained, and when<br>
the arbitrary 3 (three)<br>
was added to the<br>
number, it became<br>
300 (three hundred).<br>
Multiply these together.
</margin>

Explanation—Alternatively, according to the rule above, subtract the assumed arbitrary number 3 (three) from the number 297 (two hundred ninety-seven).

| Original Number | Assumed Arbitrary | Number minus Arbitrary | Number plus Arbitrary |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |

When multiplied, the result was 88200 (eighty-eight thousand two hundred). Then, the square of the arbitrary 3 (three) was calculated, which is 9 (nine). When this was added to the previous product, the square result 88209 was obtained, just as before. It is to be understood in this manner everywhere.

<page-num>१८</page-num>
<header>Līlāvatī</header>

### Formula for Extracting the Square Root — Verse
#### Rule for Extracting the Square Root, Verse 10

(Verse 14) Having subtracted the square from the last <term>odd digit</term> <note>original: viṣama; the 1st, 3rd, 5th, etc., places in a number</note>, double the resulting root. When the <term>even digit</term> <note>original: sama; the 2nd, 4th, 6th, etc., places</note> is divided by that doubled root, subtract the square of the quotient from the preceding odd digit. Place the quotient, doubled, in a line. When the even digit is divided by that line, subtract the square of the result from the last odd digit and place the doubled result in the line. Repeat this process; half of the final line will be the square root. || 10 ||

**Prose Order:** The calculator, after subtracting the square from the last odd digit, should double the root. When the even digit is divided by that, and the square of the quotient is subtracted from the preceding odd digit, the quotient should be placed in the line doubled. When the even digit is divided by the line, and the square of the result is subtracted from the last odd digit, that result should be placed in the line doubled. By doing this repeatedly, half of the line becomes the square root. || 10 ||

**Meaning:** The calculator should first identify the odd digits in the square number. From the last odd digit, subtract the largest possible square. Take the root of that square, double it, and place it in a separate row called the <term>line</term> <note>original: paṅkti</note>. 

Next, divide the even digit adjacent to that odd digit by this doubled root. Take the resulting quotient, square it, and subtract that square from the next odd digit. Now, double this quotient and place it in the line (shifting one position). 

Then, divide the next even digit by the values in the line. Take that quotient, subtract its square from the next odd digit, and add the doubled quotient to the line. Continue this process until the digits are exhausted. Finally, by adding all the digits in the line and dividing by two (taking half), the square root is obtained. || 10 ||

---

### Here is the Problem: Examples of Square Roots.

> O friend! Find the square roots of four and of nine, and also of those squares calculated previously. Tell them to me separately if your intellect has grown in this subject. || 4 ||

**Prose Order:** O friend! if your intellect has grown in this subject, then find the square root of four and nine, as well as the square roots of the squares previously calculated, each one separately. || 4 ||

**Meaning:** O dear Līlāvatī! If your mind is sharpened in the method of extracting square roots, then tell me the square root of 4 and 9, and also the square roots of the squares we calculated in the previous lessons.

**Expansion:** Digits are counted starting from the first place <note>the units place</note> moving upwards. The first, third, fifth, and so on, are called **odd digits** <note>viṣama</note>. The second, fourth, sixth, eighth, and so on, are called **even digits** <note>sama</note>. When extracting the square root, for the sake of memory... <note>The text ends mid-sentence here</note>

<summary>This page introduces the mathematical algorithm for extracting square roots by identifying odd and even decimal places, a method involving successive division and subtraction. It concludes with a challenge to Līlāvatī to calculate the roots of 4, 9, and previously derived squares.</summary>
<keywords>square root, vargamūla, algorithm, odd and even digits, viṣama, sama, Līlāvatī, Bhāskara II, arithmetic</keywords>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>The Method of Square Root Extraction</header>

### The Method of Square Root Extraction

For this reason, a mark such as ( ' ) should be placed above the <term>odd digits</term> <note>original: viṣama aṅka; digits in the 1st, 3rd, 5th positions from the right</note> and a mark such as ( — ) should be placed above the <term>even digits</term> <note>original: sama aṅka; digits in the 2nd, 4th, 6th positions</note>. In extracting a square root, the number of digits in the resulting root is exactly equal to the number of odd digits present in the original quantity.

**Examples:**
Quantities: 4 | 9 | 81 | 196 | 88209 | 100100025
Roots obtained respectively: 2 | 3 | 9 | 14 | 297 | 10010005

(a) According to the aforementioned method, the square root of 4 is 2, because only the square of 2 can be subtracted from it, after which the digits are exhausted.
(b) Similarly, the square root of 9 is 3, because the quantity is exhausted once the square of 3 is subtracted.
(c) To find the square root of 81: Here, the final odd digit position is the entire number 81. By subtracting the square of 9, the quantity is exhausted; therefore, the square root is 9.
(d) For 88209: Following the previous rule, we subtract the square of 2 from the last odd digit 8. That is, we subtract 4. A remainder of 4 is left. Above this, the even digit 8 is brought down, making the even-position number 48. The root 2, which was subtracted from the odd digit, is doubled to 4 and placed in a separate column; this is called the <term>line</term> <note>original: paṅkti; a working column of divisors</note>. Then, using that "line" digit...
