### Three Verses on the Method for Cubing

Three verses providing the formula for calculating a <term>cube</term> <note>original: ghana; the result of a number multiplied by itself twice, e.g., x³</note>.

->**A cube is declared to be the product of three equal numbers.**<-
<note>original: samatrigātaśca ghanaḥ pradiṣṭaḥ</note>

**Connection:** The product of three equal numbers is declared to be a cube.
**Meaning:** The quantity obtained by multiplying three identical digits together is called a cube.

**Place the cube of the last; then the square of the last**
**multiplied by three and the first; then the square of the first**
**multiplied by three and the last; then the cube of the first—**
**all [11] summed by shifting positions becomes the cube.**
**Having designated those two parts [as 'last' and 'first'], then**
**this method is to be performed repeatedly as the square and cube**
**are established, or the process may be done starting from the first digit. [12]**

**Meaning:** Take the cube of the <term>last digit</term> <note>original: antya; refers here to the digit in the highest place value, such as the tens place in a two-digit number</note> and place it in a position. Then, square that last digit, multiply it by the <term>first digit</term> <note>original: ādi; the digit in the lower place value, such as the units place</note>, and multiply that by 3; place this result below the first, shifted one position to the right. Next, square the first digit, multiply it by 3, and then multiply it by the last digit; write this in the same row, shifted one position further. Then, take the cube of the first digit and write it in the row, shifted one position further. 

By adding all these values together, the cube of a two-digit number is found. If there are more digits, treat the two digits whose cube was just calculated as the "last" part and the next single digit as the "first" part; then repeat the operation according to the rules mentioned above. Continue this method repeatedly for as long as digits remain. When the quantity is exhausted, sum the lines. That will be the cube. Alternatively, the result is also obtained if one performs the calculation starting from the side of the first digit <note>the units place</note>.

<vocab>Square root, Cube, Rule of operation, Product of three equals, Last digit, First digit, Positional shifting, Total quantity</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the extraction of a large square root and introduces the algebraic method for cubing numbers, specifically the formula (a+b)³ = a³ + 3a²b + 3ab² + b³ expressed in positional arithmetic.</summary>
<keywords>cube, ghana, square root, vargamūla, Lilavati, Bhaskara, arithmetic algorithm, positional notation</keywords>

### Here is an illustration:
->Example regarding the process of cubing.<-

> Tell me, O friend, what is the cube of nine, and the cube of the cube of three,
> as well as the cube of the cube of five. Then, tell me the <term>cube root</term> <note>original: ghanapada</note>
> of those cubes, if your intellect is <term>dense</term> <note>original: ghanā; a mathematical pun where 'ghana' means both a geometric cube and 'thick' or 'expert'</note> in the matter of cubing. [5]

**Prose Order:** O friend! If your mind is expert in cubing, then tell me the cube of 9; the cube of 27 (which is the cube of three); and the cube of 125 (which is the cube of five). Furthermore, tell me the cube root of these results. [5]

**Meaning:** O friend! If your intellect is adept in performing the cubing process, then tell me the cube of 9, the cube of 27 (the cube of three), and the cube of 125 (the cube of five). Also, state the cube roots of these quantities after they have been cubed. [5]

**Statement of values:** 9 | 27 | 125

**The resulting cubes in order are:** 729 | 19,683 | 1,953,125

**Elaboration:** According to the previously mentioned rule, when 9 is multiplied by 9 twice, the result is seven hundred twenty-nine.

(B) Now, we must calculate the cube of twenty-seven (27). Here, according to the second method <note>referring to the digit-by-digit algebraic expansion (a+b)³</note>, the cube of the <term>last digit</term> <note>original: antya; the digit in the tens place, here '2'</note> is calculated as 8. This is placed in the first position. 

Then, the square of the last digit (2) is 4. This is multiplied by the <term>first digit</term> <note>original: ādi; the digit in the units place, here '7'</note>, resulting in 28. This is then multiplied by three, yielding 84. These eighty-four are placed below the 8, shifted by one position. 

Then, the square of the first digit (7) is 49. This is multiplied by three, which yields 147. This is then multiplied by the last digit (2), resulting in 294. These two hundred ninety-four are written in the working line, shifted further by one position. 

Finally, the cube of the first digit (7) is 343. These three hundred forty-three are added to the line.

| **27** | **8** | **8** |
| Square of the last (2²) multiplied by 3 and the first (7) | | **84** |
| Square of the first (7²) multiplied by 3 and the last (2) | | **294** |
| Cube of the first digit (7³) | | **343** |

### The Third Method of Cubing

->**"Alternatively, the quantity multiplied by its two parts, then multiplied by three, and added to the sum of the cubes of those two parts [is the cube]."** <note>original Sanskrit: khaṇḍābhyāṃ vā hato rāśistrighnaḥ khaṇḍaghanaikyayuk</note><-

**Prose Construction:** Alternatively, the quantity multiplied by its two parts, multiplied by three, and then joined with the sum of the cubes of the parts, becomes the cube.
**Meaning:** Or, divide the <term>quantity</term> <note>original: rāśi</note> you wish to cube into two parts. Multiply the quantity by these two parts, then multiply that result by three. Finally, calculate the cube of each part separately and add them to the first result. The resulting sum is called the cube.
**Example:** Quantity: 9. Its parts: 4 and 5. Multiplying the quantity by these [9 × 4 × 5] equals 180. Multiplied by three, it becomes 540. Adding the sum of the cubes of the parts (189), the cube becomes 729.

**Expansion:** According to the above rule, the quantity 9 (nine) was divided into two parts: 4 (four) and 5 (five). First, the quantity 9 was multiplied by the first part, 4, which resulted in 36 (thirty-six). This was multiplied by the second part, 5, making 180 (one hundred eighty). This was multiplied by 3 (three), resulting in 540 (five hundred forty). Then, the cube of each part was calculated separately—the cube of 4 is 64 (sixty-four) and the cube of 5 is 125 (one hundred twenty-five). These...

| <gloss>Cube of the first part</gloss> | | Quantity: 9 | Two parts: 4, 5 |
| :--- | :---: | :--- | :--- |
| 4 | | 9 | By the first part |
| 4 | | 4 | |
| 16 | | 36 | Multiplication of the quantity |
| 4 | | 5 | |
| 64 | | 180 | Multiplication by the second part |
| <gloss>Cube of the second part</gloss> | | 3 | Multiplication by three |
| 5 | | 540 | |
| 5 | | 540 | |
| 25 | | 64 | |
| 5 | | 125 | |
| 125 | | **729** | **Sum** |

...were added to the previously multiplied quantity, and the result is the cube.

# Methods of Cubing
<page-num>25</page-num>

Alternatively, take the <term>quantity</term> <note>original: rāśi</note> 27. Its parts are 20 and 7. Multiplied by these and then by three, the result is 11,340. When joined with the sum of the cubes of the parts (8343), the <term>cube</term> <note>original: ghana</note> becomes 19,683.

**Exposition:** According to the rule mentioned above, the quantity 27 (twenty-seven) is divided into two parts: 20 (twenty) and 7 (seven). First, the quantity 27 is multiplied by the first part, 20, which results in 540 (five hundred forty). Then, this is multiplied by the second part, 7, which results in 3780 (three thousand seven hundred eighty). That result is multiplied by 3 (three), yielding 11,340 (eleven thousand three hundred forty). Next, the first part 20 is cubed, resulting in 8000 (eight thousand), and the second part 7 is cubed, resulting in 343 (three hundred forty-three). When the cubes of both these parts are added to the previously calculated figure (the one multiplied by three), the final result of the cubing is obtained.

### Another Method for Cubing

> **"The cube of the square root, multiplied by itself, becomes the cube of the square quantity."** || 13 ||

**Prose Construction:** The cube of the square root, multiplied by itself, becomes the cube of the square quantity. || 13 ||

**Meaning:** The cube of a <term>square root</term> <note>original: vargamūla</note> multiplied by itself (i.e., squared) results in the cube of the <term>square quantity</term> <note>original: vargarāśi</note>.

**Example:** Take the quantity 4. Its square root is 2. The cube of this root is 8. This cube multiplied by itself (8 × 8) becomes 64, which is the cube of 4.

**Exposition:** According to the method above, the square quantity is 4 (four). Its root is 2 (two). The cube of this root is 8 (eight). When 8 is multiplied by its own value (8), the result is 64 (sixty-four). This is the final result.

Alternatively, take the quantity 9. Its square root is 3. The cube of this root is 27. The square of 27 is 729, which is the cube of 9. Thus, the cube of a square is the same as the square of the cube of its square root. This is utilized in <term>Algebra</term> <note>original: bījagaṇita; literally "seed mathematics" or the science of calculation with unknown elements</note>.

Thus ends the section on cubing.

# Lilavati
<page-num>26</page-num>

Expansion: and the <term>squared quantity</term> <note>original: varga-rāśi</note> is 9 (nine). Its root is three. When that root is cubed, it becomes 27 (twenty-seven). This was then multiplied by its equal digit, 27, resulting in <margin>27<br/>729</margin> 729 (seven hundred twenty-nine). This is the <term>cube</term> <note>original: ghana</note> of 9. Whatever is the cube of a square is also the square of the cube of the root; this is of great assistance in <term>algebra</term> <note>original: bīja gaṇita</note>. Thus ends the section on Cubing.

->### Now, the Formula for the Cube Root in Two Verses—<-

->Two verses regarding the method of extracting the cube root.<-

The first place is a cubic place; then follow two non-cubic places. Subtract the cube from the last cubic place and set down the root separately. Divide the next non-cubic place by three times the square of that root. || 14 || Place the quotient in the line of the root. Subtract the square of that quotient, multiplied by the previous root and by three, from the next place. Then subtract the cube of the quotient from its following cubic place. Thus the line of the root is formed, and the process is repeated. || 15 ||

**Prose Order:** The first place shall be the cubic place. Then there shall be two non-cubic places. Again, in the same way, after subtracting the cube from the final cubic place, the root should be placed separately. One should divide the place preceding that by three times the square of this root. The quotient should be placed in the line. Subtract its square, multiplied by the last digit and by three, from the first non-cubic place. From the place preceding that, subtract the cube of the quotient. Thus the line is formed. From this, the process is repeated again. || 14 || 15 ||

**Meaning:** In the quantity for which the cube root is to be extracted, the first place is a <term>cubic place</term> <note>original: ghana-sthāna</note>, indicated by this mark: **।**. Then follow two <term>non-cubic places</term> <note>original: aghana-sthāna</note>, indicated by this mark: **००**. Then another cubic place, then two non-cubics; in this manner, identify the cubic and non-cubic places until the end. Then, from the final cubic place, subtract the cube of an assumed digit. Write down the digit whose cube was subtracted in a separate place. Then, square that digit, multiply it by 3 (three), and use this as a divisor to divide the next non-cubic place. Write the resulting quotient in the line, extending it by one place. Then, square this quotient, multiply it by the previous digit and by three, and subtract that from the second non-cubic place. Then, subtract the cube of the quotient from the adjacent cubic place. If digits remain, repeat this method until the quantity is exhausted. || 14 || 15 ||

Here is the setup for finding the roots of the cubes mentioned in the previous examples: 729; 19,683; 1,953,125. The roots obtained in order are 9, 27, and 125.

# Method for Calculating the Cube Root

Expansion—Following the rules mentioned previously, marks for the <term>cubic</term> <note>original: ghana; the units, thousands, and millions places</note> and <term>non-cubic</term> <note>original: aghana; the tens/hundreds and ten-thousands/hundred-thousands places</note> positions were placed upon the quantity 729 (seven hundred twenty-nine). By subtracting the cube of 9 (nine) from the final cubic position, the quantity is exhausted. Therefore, the cube root of this quantity is simply 9 (nine).

->-|-|-<-

Likewise, marks for cubic and non-cubic places were placed upon the quantity 19,683 (nineteen thousand six hundred eighty-three). Then, from the final cubic place of 9, the cube of 2 (which is 8) was subtracted. <note>In the number 19,683, the "19" is treated as the final cubic group; 2 cubed is 8, which is the largest cube less than 19.</note>

| Quantity |
| :---: |
| \| - - \| <note>Marks indicating non-cubic and cubic places</note> |
| 19683 |
| 8 |
| --- |
| 11683 |
| 84 |
| --- |
| 3283 |
| 294 |
| --- |
| 343 |
| 343 |
| 000 |
| --- |
| **Line of the Root** |
| 2 |
| 07 |
| --- |
| 27 |

After subtracting 8, 11,683 (eleven thousand six hundred eighty-three) remained. The root 2 (two) was written separately; this became the "line." Then, the root 2 (two) was squared and multiplied by 3 (three), which resulted in twelve. This was used to divide the first non-cubic place of the cube. Then 84 (eighty-four) was subtracted, and a <term>quotient</term> <note>original: labdhi</note> of 7 (seven) was obtained. This was written in the line. Then 3,283 (three thousand two hundred eighty-three) remained. Then that quotient of 7 (seven) was squared, resulting in 49 (forty-nine). This was multiplied by the previous digit of the line, 2 (two), which made 98 (ninety-eight). That was then multiplied by 3 (three), resulting in 294 (two hundred ninety-four). This was subtracted from the second non-cubic place, leaving 343 (three hundred forty-three) remaining. Finally, the cube of the quotient 7 (seven), which is 343, was subtracted, and the quantity was exhausted.

->|-|-|-|<-

Third example: Using this same method, the cube root of the quantity 1,953,125 is 125 (one hundred twenty-five). || Thus ends the Cube Root ||

### Now, the Eight Operations of Fractions

**First, the reduction of fractions to a common denominator. Here, the formula for the "Division Class" is given in one verse:**
In the eight operations of fractions, we first describe the <term>reduction of fractions</term> <note>original: aṃśasavarṇanam; making the denominators uniform</note>. Among the various types—Division Class, Sub-division Class, Addition of a Fraction, and Subtraction of a Fraction—the formula for the "Division Class" <note>original: bhāgajāti; simple fractions</note> is written in this verse:

> The numerators and denominators of two quantities are multiplied by each other's denominators. This is the method for determining a common denominator. Alternatively, a wise person may multiply the numerator and denominator by the denominators reduced by a common factor. || 1 ||
> <note>original: anyonyahārābhihitau harāṃśau rāśyoḥ samacchedavidhānam-evam...; this is the classic rule for finding a common denominator by cross-multiplication.</note>

<summary>This page demonstrates the step-by-step extraction of cube roots for the numbers 729, 19,683, and 1,953,125, then begins a new section on the arithmetic of fractions.</summary>
<keywords>cube root extraction, ghanamūla, arithmetic table, common denominator, bhinna-parikarma, fractions, Bhaskara II, Lilavati</keywords>

<page-num>28</page-num>
## Lilavati

**Construction:** The numerator and denominator should be multiplied by the other denominators. In this way, the procedure for finding a <term>common denominator</term> <note>original: samaccheda; making the "divisors" or denominators equal so fractions can be combined</note> of the quantities is accomplished. Alternatively, a wise mathematician may first reduce the denominators by a common factor and then multiply the numerators and denominators crosswise. || 1 ||

**Rule:** Multiply the denominator and the numerator of one quantity by the denominator of the other quantity. Then, multiply the denominator and numerator of that second quantity by the denominator of the first. By doing so, the quantities will have a common denominator. Alternatively, a wise person may divide the denominators of the quantities by a common number to reduce them, and then multiply the denominators and numerators of the quantities by these reduced denominators. This also results in a common denominator. This process is called the <term>Reduction of Fractions</term> <note>original: bhagajati; specifically the "class of fractions" related to simple addition and subtraction</note>. || 1 ||

# Example Problem:

->An example regarding the reduction of fractions.<-

O friend! Tell me the common denominators for the purpose of addition of three wholes <note>original: rupa-trayam; the integer 3</note>, one-fifth <gloss>$\frac{1}{5}$</gloss>, and one-third <gloss>$\frac{1}{3}$</gloss>. Also, find the common denominators for the purpose of subtraction of one-sixty-third <gloss>$\frac{1}{63}$</gloss> and one-fourteenth <gloss>$\frac{1}{14}$</gloss>. || 1 ||

**Construction:** O friend! Speak the common denominators for 3, 1/5, and 1/3 for addition. Likewise, speak the common denominators for 1/63 and 1/14 for subtraction. || 1 ||

**Meaning:** O friend! For the purpose of addition, take the integer 3, one-fifth part <gloss>$\frac{1}{5}$</gloss> of a unit, and one-third part <gloss>$\frac{1}{3}$</gloss> of a unit, and express them all with a single common denominator. Furthermore, for the purpose of finding the difference (subtraction), take one-sixty-third part <gloss>$\frac{1}{63}$</gloss> of a unit and one-fourteenth part <gloss>$\frac{1}{14}$</gloss> of a unit and express both with a single common denominator.

**Arrangement:** $\frac{3}{1}$ $\frac{1}{5}$ $\frac{1}{3}$ result in common denominators: $\frac{45}{15}$ $\frac{3}{15}$ $\frac{5}{15}$

The result of the addition is $\frac{53}{15}$

**Expansion—** Following the rule mentioned above: for $\frac{3}{1}$ $\frac{1}{5}$ $\frac{1}{3}$, first we multiply the denominators and numerators of the other two quantities by the first denominator, 1. This results in $\frac{3}{1}$ $\frac{1}{5}$ $\frac{1}{3}$. Then, we multiply the denominators and numerators of the other two quantities by the second denominator, 5. This results in $\frac{15}{5}$ <unclear>$\frac{1}{5}$</unclear> $\frac{5}{15}$. Then, we multiply the other quantities by the third denominator, 3. This results in $\frac{45}{15}$ $\frac{3}{15}$ $\frac{5}{15}$. Now, because all the denominators are the same, the reduction to a common denominator is complete. Since the denominators are now identical, we add all the numerators together, which results in $\frac{53}{15}$.

# Method for Calculating Cube Roots

Now, the calculation for the second example (subtraction of fractions):
| 2 | 1 |
|---|---|
| 63 | 14 |

By dividing the denominators by the common factor of seven <note>original: sattapavartitabhyam; using the number 7 to reduce the denominators</note>:
| 2 | 1 |
|---|---|
| 9 | 2 |

After cross-multiplying to find the <term>common denominator</term> <note>original: samaccheda; literally "equal divisors"</note>:
| 4 | 9 |
|---|---|
| 126 | 126 |

The result after subtraction is <gloss>5/126 = 5/126</gloss>

->Thus ends the reduction of simple fractions.<-

Regarding the example of the difference (subtraction): Given the fractions <term>2/63 and 1/14</term>. Here, a <term>reduction</term> <note>original: apavartana; dividing by a common factor</note> of 7 can be applied to the denominators of both quantities. For this reason, after dividing the denominators by 7, the quantities became <term>2/9 and 1/2</term>. To find the common denominator, the numerator and denominator of each fraction are multiplied by the reduced denominator of the other. Thus, multiplying the numerator and denominator of the first quantity by the second reduced denominator (2) results in <term>4/126</term>. Then, multiplying the numerator and denominator of the second quantity by the first reduced denominator (9) results in <term>9/126</term>. Now, to find the difference, we subtract 4 from 9, which results in <term>5/126</term>. This value can also be expressed by multiplying back by the common factor of 7, resulting in <term>35/882</term>.

## Now, the rule-verse for the reduction of sub-fractions in half a stanza:

The class of sub-fractions <note>original: prabhaga-jati</note> is defined as taking a "fraction of a fraction." The method for solving these is described in the following half-verse:

> Numerators multiplied by numerators and denominators by denominators—this is the reduction of sub-fractions.

**Prose interpretation:** In the class of sub-fractions, the numerators should be multiplied by one another, and the denominators should be multiplied by one another to achieve the simplified form <note>original: savarnanam; making them "of the same color" or uniform</note>.

### An Example Here:
->An example regarding the class of sub-fractions.<-

> O wise one! If one-third of half a <term>drumma</term> <note>a silver coin</note> is taken, and of that, three-quarters is taken; and of that, one-fifth, one-sixteenth, and one-quarter are requested by a beggar and given by a miser—tell me, if you know the mathematical class known as sub-fractions, exactly how many <term>cowries</term> <note>original: varataka; small shells used as the lowest denomination of currency</note> did he give? || 2 ||

**Prose Order—** O wise one! When a miser was asked for a gift, he gave a certain amount. He gave a fifth part of a sixteenth part of a fourth part of three-quarters of two-thirds of half of a <term>Dramma</term> <note>A silver coin of medieval India, equivalent here to 1280 cowries.</note>. O child! If you understand the class of calculations known as <term>sub-fractions</term> <note>original: prabhaga-jati; literally "the class of parts of parts," where fractions are multiplied together</note>, then tell me: how many <term>Varatakas</term> <note>Cowry shells, used as the smallest unit of currency</note> did he actually give? || 2 ||

**Meaning—** O intelligent one! A miser was asked for charity. He gave the fourth part <gloss>1/4</gloss> of the sixteenth part <gloss>1/16</gloss> of the fifth part <gloss>1/5</gloss> of the three-quarters <gloss>3/4</gloss> of the two-thirds <gloss>2/3</gloss> of the half <gloss>1/2</gloss> of one <term>Dramma</term>. If you know the science of sub-fractions in mathematics, then O son! Say how many cowries that miser gave to the beggar.

### Layout—
| <gloss>1</gloss> | <gloss>1</gloss> | <gloss>2</gloss> | <gloss>3</gloss> | <gloss>1</gloss> | <gloss>1</gloss> | <gloss>1</gloss> |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| — | — | — | — | — | — | — |
| <gloss>1</gloss> | <gloss>2</gloss> | <gloss>3</gloss> | <gloss>4</gloss> | <gloss>5</gloss> | <gloss>16</gloss> | <gloss>4</gloss> |

After reduction <note>original: savarnite; the process of multiplying all numerators and all denominators</note>:

The result is <gloss>6/7680</gloss>. When simplified by dividing both by six, the result is: <gloss>1/1280</gloss>.

->One single cowry was given. || Thus ends the class of sub-fractions. ||<-

**Explanation—** For any quantity that does not have a denominator written below it, a denominator of one is assumed. For this reason, since there is 1 Dramma, we imagine a denominator of one <gloss>1/1</gloss> below it. Then, following the rules, we multiply the numerators of all the quantities: <gloss>1/1</gloss>, <gloss>1/2</gloss>, <gloss>2/3</gloss>, <gloss>3/4</gloss>, <gloss>1/5</gloss>, <gloss>1/16</gloss>, and <gloss>1/4</gloss>. Multiplying the numerators (1 × 1 × 2 × 3 × 1 × 1 × 1) equals 6. Then, multiply all the denominators together: 2 times 3 is 6; 6 times 4 is 24; 24 times 5 is 120; 120 times 16 is 1920; and 1920 times 4 is 7680. This product of all denominators results in the fraction <gloss>6/7680</gloss>. Reducing this by dividing by 6, we get the form <gloss>1/1280</gloss>. This means the miser gave the 1,280th part of one Dramma. Since the question asks for the answer in cowries, we convert the Dramma to cowries. One Dramma equals 1280 cowries. When we divide this by the denominator 1280, the quotient is 1. Thus, one cowry was given.

### Now, the rule for "Fractional Association" and "Fractional Dissociation" in one and a half verses—
The method for performing <term>Association</term> <note>Adding a fraction of a quantity to itself</note> and <term>Dissociation</term> <note>Subtracting a fraction of a quantity from itself</note>:

> Multiply the whole number by the denominator and add or subtract the numerator, if the parts of a unit are increased or decreased. || 2 || In cases where a quantity is increased or decreased by its own parts, as in "Association" or "Dissociation" of fractions, multiply the denominator of the lower fraction by the denominator above it, and multiply the upper numerators by that same increased or decreased value. || 3 ||

<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from the multiplication of sub-fractions (Prabhaga-jati) to the methods of Bhagānubandha (association of fractions) and Bhāgāpavāha (dissociation of fractions). These rules allow a mathematician to combine whole numbers with fractions or adjust a fraction by a portion of itself.</meta>

<page-num>( 31 )</page-num>
<header>The Method for Classes of Fraction Association and Dissociation</header>

**Prose Order—** If the parts of a single unit are to be added or subtracted, then the numerators should be made positive <note>added</note> or negative <note>subtracted</note> after multiplying the whole units by the denominator. || 2 ||

In cases of "fraction association" <note>original: bhāgānubandha; adding a fraction to a quantity</note> or "fraction dissociation" <note>original: bhāgāpavāha; subtracting a fraction from a quantity</note> where a part of a fraction is added to or subtracted from that fraction:
Multiply the upper denominator by the lower denominator. Then, multiply the upper numerator by that same lower denominator, increased or decreased by its own numerator. || 3 ||

**Meaning—** If a part of a whole unit <term>Rupa</term> is added or subtracted: first, multiply the whole number by the denominator. If the part is to be added, add that result to the numerator and place it in the numerator's position while keeping the denominator the same. If the part is to be subtracted, subtract the numerator from that multiplied result and place it in the numerator's position, keeping the same denominator. This is the method for associating and dissociating fractions with whole numbers.

Furthermore, in cases of association or dissociation where a fraction is increased or decreased by a portion of *itself*, follow this: multiply the top denominator by the bottom-most denominator. To find the new numerator, if it is an association <note>addition</note>, add the bottom numerator to its denominator and multiply the top numerator by this sum. If it is a dissociation <note>subtraction</note>, subtract the bottom numerator from its denominator and multiply the top numerator by the remainder. This results in the simplified fraction. || 3 ||

->**Here is the Problem:**<-

An example regarding the association and dissociation of fractions:

### O friend! If you understand the association of parts and the dissociation of parts, 
### Tell me what "two with a quarter" and "three minus a quarter" 
### Become when they are simplified into a common fraction. || 3 ||

**Prose Order—** O friend! If you know the association and dissociation of fractions, then tell me: what do "two with a quarter" <note>original: sānghri dvayam</note> and "three minus a quarter" <note>original: vyānghri trayam</note> become when simplified? || 3 ||

**Meaning—** O friend! If you understand these mathematical rules, tell me the result of simplifying two units plus one-quarter of a unit <gloss>2 + 1/4</gloss> and three units minus one-quarter of a unit <gloss>3 - 1/4</gloss>. || 3 ||

**Formal Layout—** 2 $\frac{1}{4}$ and 3 <gloss>-</gloss>$\frac{1}{4}$
When simplified, they become: $\frac{9}{4}$ and $\frac{11}{4}$

**Expansion—** According to the first rule mentioned above, for "two with a quarter" <gloss>2 + 1/4</gloss>, the whole number 2 is multiplied by the denominator 4, which equals 8. Since this is an association <note>addition</note>, the numerator 1 is added to 8, resulting in 9. This is placed over the original denominator 4. Thus, the association of the quantity results in $\frac{9}{4}$.

For "three minus a quarter" <gloss>3 - 1/4</gloss>, the whole number 3 is multiplied by the denominator 4, which equals 12. Since this is a dissociation <note>subtraction</note>, according to the rule, we subtract the numerator...

## Second Example

**The Problem:—** An example concerning this very subject of fraction association and dissociation—

> Oh friend! If you understand the methods of fraction association and dissociation, then tell me: what does a quarter <note>original: "anghri", meaning a foot or one-fourth part</note> become when increased by its own third part, and then increased by half of that sum? Similarly, what do two-thirds become when diminished by their own eighth part, and then further diminished by three-sevenths of that remainder? And what does a half become when diminished by its own eighth part and then increased by nine-sevenths of that remainder? Speak! || 4 ||

**Prose Order:** Oh friend! If you know fraction association and dissociation, then tell me: here, what would a quarter increased by its own third part and then increased by half [of that sum] be? Likewise, what would two-thirds diminished by their own eighth part and then diminished by three-sevenths of that become? And what would a half, diminished by its own eighth part and then increased by nine-sevenths of its own [remainder], be? Tell me this. || 4 ||

**Meaning:** Oh friend! If you know fraction association <note>adding a fraction to itself</note> and dissociation <note>subtracting a fraction from itself</note>, then according to those rules, tell me the following: What is the result of 1/4 <gloss>one-fourth</gloss> joined with its own 1/3 <gloss>one-third</gloss>, and that resulting figure then joined with its own 1/2 <gloss>one-half</gloss>? Furthermore, if two-thirds <gloss>2/3</gloss> are diminished by their own 1/8 <gloss>one-eighth</gloss>, and the resulting figure is then diminished by its own three-sevenths <gloss>3/7</gloss>, what is the result? And if one-half <gloss>1/2</gloss> is diminished by its own eighth part, and the remaining figure is then increased by its own nine-sevenths <gloss>9/7</gloss>, what will be the final form? || 4 ||

# Now, the rule for the addition and subtraction of fractions in half a verse—
->Now, the method for adding and subtracting fractions in half a verse:<-

> The sum or difference is found of parts having equal denominators. For an integer, unity is assumed as the denominator.

Prose order: The sum <term>yoga: addition</term> of numerators with equal denominators <term>hara: literally 'the divisor'</term> is to be made. Likewise, the difference <term>antara: subtraction</term> is to be made. For a quantity without a denominator <note>original: "ahārarāśeḥ"</note>, unity <term>rūpa: the number 1</term> is assumed to be the denominator.

Meaning: After making the denominators of fractional quantities equal <term>samaccheda: common denominator</term>, one should add or subtract them. For any quantity below which no denominator is written, the number one (1) should be imagined as its denominator.

---

### Here is the illustration:
->An example regarding the addition and subtraction of fractions.<-

> Friend, tell me the sum of these parts: a fifth, a quarter, a third, a half, and a sixth. Also, tell me quickly the remainder when three is diminished by these parts. (5)

Prose order: O friend! Tell me the sum of these: a fifth, a quarter, a third, a half, and a sixth. Furthermore, tell me quickly what the remainder <gloss>śeṣa</gloss> would be of three when diminished by these parts. (5)

Meaning: O friend! Add together one-fifth ($\frac{1}{5}$), one-fourth ($\frac{1}{4}$), one-third ($\frac{1}{3}$), one-half ($\frac{1}{2}$), and one-sixth ($\frac{1}{6}$) and tell me the result. Then, tell me quickly what the remainder will be when these parts are subtracted from three (3). (5)

Numerical Statement: $\frac{1}{5}$ $\frac{1}{4}$ $\frac{1}{3}$ $\frac{1}{2}$ $\frac{1}{6}$. The sum produced is $\frac{137}{60}$.

Expansion: According to the aforementioned rule, first find the common denominator of $\frac{1}{5}$ $\frac{1}{4}$ $\frac{1}{3}$ $\frac{1}{2}$ $\frac{1}{6}$. That is, taking the denominator 5 of the first quantity, excluding its own numerator and denominator, multiply the numerators and denominators of all other quantities. Then the form became $\frac{1}{5}$ $\frac{24}{120}$ $\frac{30}{120}$ $\frac{40}{120}$ $\frac{60}{120}$. <note>This describes an algorithm for finding a common denominator by successive multiplication.</note> Then, taking the denominator 4 of the second quantity, excluding its own numerator and denominator, multiply the denominators and numerators of the other quantities. Then the form became $\frac{4}{20}$ $\frac{5}{20}$ $\frac{20}{60}$ $\frac{30}{60}$ $\frac{20}{60}$. Then, taking the denominator 3 of the third quantity, according to the previously stated method, multiply the denominators and numerators; it became $\frac{12}{60}$ $\frac{15}{60}$ $\frac{20}{60}$ $\frac{60}{420}$ — $\frac{60}{360}$. Then, taking the denominator 2 of the fourth quantity, according to the previously stated method, multiply the other quantities' denominators and numerators; then $\frac{24}{120}$...

<header>Method of Multiplying Fractions</header>

### Method of Multiplying Fractions

$\frac{30}{120}$ $\frac{50}{120}$ $\frac{60}{120}$ $\frac{120}{720}$ — such was the form. Then, following the previously mentioned method, the denominators and numerators of the other quantities were multiplied by 6 (the denominator of the last quantity). Then the form became: $\frac{144}{720}$ $\frac{180}{720}$ $\frac{240}{720}$ $\frac{360}{720}$ $\frac{120}{720}$. That is, they now have a common denominator <term>samaccheda: a "shared divisor"</term>. Now, adding all the numerators together, the sum is one thousand forty-four: $\frac{1044}{720}$. By applying a reduction <term>apavartana: dividing by a common factor</term> of thirty-six (36) to this, the result became $\frac{29}{20}$.

<margin>
| 144 | 180 |
|:---:|:---:|
| 720 | 720 |
| 144 | |
| 180 | |
| 240 | |
| 360 | |
| 120 | |
| --- | |
| 1044 | |
</margin>

**Statement:—** Now, the remainder of three minus those [fractions] is $\frac{29}{20}$.

Explanation—The previously mentioned parts <note>the sum</note> $\frac{29}{20}$ were subtracted from 3. That is, according to the rule, a denominator of 1 was imagined under the whole quantity <term>āhāra rāśi</term> three (3) to create a common denominator. Then it became: $\frac{3}{1}$ and $\frac{29}{20}$ — which transforms to $\frac{60}{20}$ and $\frac{29}{20}$. Their difference was taken, meaning 29 (twenty-nine) was subtracted from the numerator 60 (sixty), leaving a remainder of $\frac{31}{20}$.

->Thus ends the addition and subtraction of fractions.<-

### Now, the rule for the multiplication of fractions in half a verse:—

Now, the method for multiplying fractions is written in half a verse:—

> The product of the numerators divided by the product of the denominators is the result in the multiplication of fractions. || 4 ||

**Prose order:—** The product of the numerators <term>aṃśāhati</term> should be divided by the product of the denominators <term>chedavadha</term>; the result obtained is the product <term>phala</term> in fraction multiplication. || 9 || <note>The verse is numbered 4 in the text but referenced as 9 in the prose commentary.</note>

**Meaning:—** One should multiply the numerators of the fractional quantities with each other, and then multiply the denominators with each other. By dividing the product of the numerators by the product of the denominators, the resulting quotient is the product of the multiplication. || 9 ||

### Here is the illustration:—

->An example regarding the multiplication of fractions<-

> What would be the product of two and one-third multiplied by two and two-sevenths? Also, find the product of a half multiplied by a third, if you are skilled in the method of multiplying fractions. || 6 ||

**Prose order:—** O friend! If you are skilled in the method of multiplying fractions, then what would two and one-third <note>original: "satryamśarūpadvitayena"</note> multiplied by two and two-sevenths <note>original: "samsaptamāmśadvitayam"</note> be? And what would a half multiplied by a third be? Know this and tell me. || 6 ||

**Meaning:—** O friend! If you are somewhat clever in performing fraction multiplication, then what is the result of $2 \frac{1}{3}$ <gloss>two with a third part</gloss> multiplied by $2 \frac{2}{7}$ <gloss>two with two-sevenths</gloss>? And what will be $\frac{1}{2}$ <gloss>a half</gloss> multiplied by $\frac{1}{3}$ <gloss>a third</gloss>? Tell me. || 6 ||

<vocab>bhinnaguṇanaprakāraḥ: the method of multiplying fractions, samaccheda: common denominator, apavartana: reduction/simplification, nyāsaḥ: numerical statement/layout, saṅkalitavyavaklite: addition and subtraction, karaṇasūtram: rule/formula, aṃśāhatiḥ: product of numerators, chedavadha: product of denominators, labdhi: quotient/result, satryamśarūpadvitayena: with the number two and a third part, saptamāmśadvitayam: two with two-seventh parts, tribhāgena: by a third part</vocab>

| **Statement:—** | 2 | 2 |
| | 1 | 1 |

**Result of multiplication: 5**

->Multiplier. Multiplicand.<-

Expansion—2 1/3 and 2 1/7. Here, in both places, the process of <term>bhāgānubandha: "addition of a fraction," referring to mixed numbers</term> was used to perform <term>savarṇana: "homogenization" or reduction to a common fraction form</term>. The result was 7/3 and 15/7. That is: the denominator 3 of the first quantity was multiplied by the whole number 2, resulting in 6. The numerator 1 was added to it while the denominator remained the same, resulting in 7/3 as the conversion of the first quantity. Then, following the same method for the second quantity, the denominator 7 was multiplied by the whole number 2, resulting in 14. The numerator 1 was added to this, resulting in the form 15/7. Thus, the multiplier and multiplicand took the forms 7/3 and 15/7. Now, according to the rule mentioned earlier, both numerators and both denominators were multiplied together, resulting in 105/21. Now, the numerator 105 was divided by the denominator 21, resulting in a quotient <term>labdhi</term> of 5. This is the final result.

->Multiplier. Multiplicand.<-

| | | | | |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :--- | :---: |
| **Statement:—** | 1 | 1 | **Result of multiplication** | 1 |
| | — | — | | — |
| | 2 | 3 | | 6 |

Expansion—1/2 and 1/3. Here, according to the rule mentioned above, the numerators and denominators were multiplied together, resulting in the form 1/6. Since the denominator cannot be divided into the numerator in this case <note>meaning it cannot be simplified into a whole number</note>, this 1/6 is the final answer.

### Now, the procedural rule for the division of fractions in half a verse:—

->The method for dividing fractions in half a verse:—<-

> Swap the denominator and the numerator of the divisor;
> then, in the operation of division, the method of multiplication should be performed.
> <note>original: "chedam lavañca parivartya harasya śeṣaḥ kāryo'tha bhāgaharaṇe guṇanāvidhiśca"</note>

Prose order:— Now, in the division of fractions, after swapping the denominator and the numerator of the divisor, the remaining procedure of multiplication should be performed.

Meaning:— To divide fractions, write the denominator in the place of the numerator and the numerator in the place of the denominator of the divisor <note>invert the divisor</note>. Then, follow the rest of the multiplication rules. That is, by multiplying the numerators together and the denominators together, and then dividing the product of the numerators by the product of the denominators, the resulting quotient is the result of the fraction division.

<vocab>Nyāsa: setup/statement; Savarṇana: reduction to a common form; Guṇaka: multiplier; Guṇya: multiplicand; Bhāgānubandha: mixed number addition; Labdhi: quotient/result; Bhinnabhāgahāra: division of fractions; Karaṇasūtra: procedural rule; Cheda: denominator; Lava: numerator</vocab>

<header>Method for Squaring Fractions</header>

# Method for Squaring Fractions

## Here is the example—

An example regarding the division of fractions:

> If your intellect is as sharp as the tip of the interior of a blade of <term>darbha grass: a sacred grass used in rituals, known for its extremely sharp points</term> and is capable of dividing fractions, then tell me, friend, the result of dividing five by two increased by one-third, and also the result of dividing one-sixth by one-third. ॥ 7 ॥

**Word-for-word connection:** O friend! If your <note>original: "darbhīya-garbhāgra-sutīkṣṇa-buddhiḥ"</note> intellect, sharp as the point of a blade of darbha grass, is capable of dividing fractions, then tell me the result of dividing five by two and a third, and one-sixth by one-third. ॥ 7 ॥

**Meaning:** O friend! If your subtle intellect, as sharp as the tip of a blade of <term>kusha: another name for darbha grass</term>, is capable of performing the division of fractions, then tell us: what is the result of dividing five by two units and one-third (2 1/3)? And what is the result of dividing one-sixth (1/6) by one-third (1/3)? ॥ 7 ॥

**Statement:** 
| | | | | |
| :--- | :---: | :--- | :--- | :---: |
| 7/3 | 5/1 | | 1/3 | 1/6 |

**By performing the method described:**
**The results are:** 
| | | | | |
| :--- | :---: | :--- | :--- | :---: |
| 15/7 | | | 1/2 | |

**Expansion:** Regarding 2 1/3 and 5/1—here, the first quantity underwent <term>bhāgānubandha: the process of adding a fraction to a whole number</term>. That is, the denominator 3 was multiplied by the whole number 2, resulting in 6. The numerator 1 was added to this, resulting in the form 7/3. Thus, it became 7/3 and 5/1. Then, according to the rule mentioned previously <note>the rule of inverting the divisor</note>, the denominator 3 of the divisor was moved to the numerator's place, and the numerator 7 was moved to the denominator's place. This resulted in 3/7 and 5/1. Then the multiplication rule was applied—multiplying numerator by numerator and denominator by denominator—which resulted in 15/7. Now, dividing the numerator by the denominator, the resulting quotient <term>labdhi</term> is the answer.

Furthermore, for 1/3 and 1/6—here, in the divisor, the positions of the denominator and numerator were swapped, resulting in 3/1 and 1/6. The multiplication method was performed, resulting in 3/6. After reducing this by a factor of 3, the result is 1/2. This is the answer.

->Thus ends the division of fractions.<-

## Now, the rule for squaring fractions, etc., in half a verse:

The rule for finding the square, cube, and so on, of a fraction is given in half a stanza—

> To find the square, the squares of the denominator and numerator should be calculated; to find the cube, their cubes should be calculated. To find the roots, the roots of the denominator and numerator should be calculated for the sake of obtaining the result. ॥ 5 ॥

**Word-for-word connection:** In the square of a fraction, the squares of the denominator and numerator should be made. In the method for the cube of a fraction, the cubes should be made. Now, for the sake of establishing the roots, the roots of the denominator and numerator should be made. ॥ 5 ॥

**Meaning:** If you wish to square a fraction, square both the denominator and the numerator. If you wish to cube a fraction, cube both the denominator and the numerator. To find the square root or cube root of fractional quantities, take the square root or cube root of both the denominator and the numerator respectively. ॥ 5 ॥

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

### Here is the example—

> O friend! Tell me quickly the square of three and a half, and from that square, its square root. Then tell me its cube and the cube root from that cube, if you understand the squares and cubes of fractions. ॥ 8 ॥

<term>Word-for-word connection: an analysis of the Sanskrit sentence structure (Anvaya)</term>—O friend! If you understand the distinct <term>varga-ghana: squares and cubes</term> [of fractions], then quickly state the square of three and a half, and from that square, the <term>varga-pada: square root</term>. Also, state the <term>ghana: cube</term>, and from that cube, state the cube root. ॥ 8 ॥

**Meaning:** O friend! If you understand how to calculate the squares, square roots, cubes, and cube roots of fractions, then state the <term>varga: square</term> and the <term>varga-mula: square root</term> of the mixed number three and a half (3 ½). Also, quickly state the <term>ghana: cube</term> of that same quantity and the root of the resulting cube.

**Statement:** 3 ½. When converted into a <term>cheda-sadrisha-rupa: a form where the whole number is integrated with the denominator (an improper fraction)</term>, it becomes 7/2.
The square of this is 49/4. Its root is 7/2. The cube is 343/8. Its [cube] root is 7/2.

->Thus ends the eight operations on fractions.<-

<term>Expansion</term>—First, the <term>bhaganubandha: addition of a fraction to a whole number</term> was performed on the quantity 3 ½. That is, the denominator 2 was multiplied by the whole number 3, resulting in 6. The numerator 1 was added to this, making it 7/2. Now, to find the square, the numerator and denominator were squared according to the previously mentioned rule, resulting in 49/4. When the root of this squared quantity was taken, it returned to the original quantity, 7/2. Then, the original quantity 7/2 was cubed, resulting in the form 343/8. When the root of this cubed quantity was taken, it returned to the original 7/2.

->Thus ends the eight operations on fractions.<-

->Example regarding the squares, cubes, etc., of fractions.<-

### Now, two verses in the Arya meter regarding rules for operations with zero—

The method for addition, multiplication, and other operations involving zero in two <term>Arya: a specific meter of Sanskrit poetry</term> verses:

> In addition, zero is equal to the quantity added. In squaring and other operations, the result is zero. A quantity divided by zero is called "khahara" (divided by zero). A quantity multiplied by zero becomes zero, but this "multiplied by zero" quantity should be kept as is for further operations. ॥ 6 ॥
> If a quantity has zero as its multiplier and then zero becomes its divisor, the quantity should be known as unchanged. Similarly, it remains unchanged when zero is subtracted from or added to it. ॥ 7 ॥

<term>Word-for-word connection</term>—In addition, zero is equal to the quantity being added. In squaring and similar operations, the result is zero. A quantity divided by zero becomes <term>khahara: literally "zero-divisor," interpreted in Indian mathematics as a value similar to infinity</term>. A quantity multiplied by zero becomes zero. In subsequent operations, the <term>khaguna: multiplied by zero</term> state must be considered. And if zero becomes the multiplier and then zero is the divisor, the quantity is understood to be unchanged. In the same way, when zero is subtracted or added, it is known to be unchanged. ॥ 6 ॥ 7 ॥

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>Procedure for Operations with Zero</header>

# Procedure for Operations with Zero

**Meaning:** In addition, zero becomes equal to the other quantity <note>i.e., x + 0 = x</note>. The square, square root, cube, and cube root of zero, when calculated, result only in zero. When a quantity is divided by zero, zero remains in the place of the divisor <note>This creates the mathematical expression of infinity in this system, known as 'khahara' or 'zero-divided'</note>. When multiplied by zero, the result is zero. However, if after multiplying by zero, a further division or subtraction remains to be done, one should keep the "zero-multiplied quantity" in mind <note>original: "khaguna-rashiko chintana kare"</note> and write it as it is. This is because if a quantity is multiplied by zero and then subsequently divided by zero, the quantity remains unchanged. This occurs because the multiplier and the divisor are equal; just as a quantity remains the same when multiplied and then divided by the same number, it remains "as it was" in this specific operation involving zero. Similarly, a quantity added to zero or subtracted from zero remains unaltered. ॥ 6 ॥ 7 ॥

## Here is the example—

### Example for addition, squaring, etc., with zero—

> O friend! Tell me, what does zero plus five become? Tell me the square of zero, its root, its cube, its cube root, and five multiplied by zero. What is ten divided by zero? And what is the quantity which, when multiplied by zero, increased by half of itself, multiplied by three, and then divided by zero, becomes sixty-three? ॥ 9 ॥
<note>original: "खं पंचयुग्भवति किं वद खस्य वर्गं मूलं घनं घनपदं खगुणाश्च पञ्च ॥ खेनोद्धृता दश च कः खगुणो निजार्द्धयुक्तस्त्रिभिश्च गुणितः खहृतस्त्रिषष्टिः ॥ ९ ॥"</note>

**Word-for-word connection:** O friend! Tell me, what is <term>pancha-yuk kham: five added to zero</term>? Likewise, what is the square, square root, cube, and cube root of zero? What is five <term>kha-gunah: multiplied by zero</term>, and ten <term>khenoddhritah: divided by zero</term>? (Furthermore), tell me what is the quantity which, multiplied by zero, joined with half of itself, multiplied by three, and divided by zero, equals sixty-three? ॥ 9 ॥

**Meaning:** O friend! What is the result of five added to zero? What is the square, square root, cube, and cube root of zero? How much is five multiplied by zero? What is the quotient when ten is divided by zero? Finally, if a certain number is multiplied by zero, then half of that product is added to it, then the whole is multiplied by 3, and then divided by zero, the result is 63—tell me, what was that original number? ॥ 9 ॥

**Statement of results:** 0. This, when five is added, becomes: 5.
Square of zero: 0.
Root: 0. Cube: 0. Cube root: 0.

**Statement:** 5. These, multiplied by zero, become: 0.

**Statement:** 10. These, divided by zero, become: <gloss>10/0</gloss> <note>This represents the 'khahara' or infinite quantity</note>.

The unknown quantity <note>Let it be x</note> is multiplied by 0. Added to half of itself <gloss>1/2</gloss>.
Multiplied by 3. Divisor: 0. Result: 63.
Then, by using the <term>viloma-vidhi: the method of inversion</term> or <term>ishta-karmana: the method of working with an assumed value</term>, the quantity obtained is: 14.
<note>Calculation: If we treat the multiplication and division by zero as canceling out, the equation is (x + x/2) * 3 = 63. This simplifies to 1.5x * 3 = 63, or 4.5x = 63, which means x = 14.</note>
This mathematical operation is of great use in <term>Graha-ganita: planetary calculations (astronomy)</term>.

<summary>This page defines the rules for mathematical operations involving zero, including the concept that multiplication and division by zero cancel each other out in specific algebraic contexts, followed by a practice puzzle.</summary>
<keywords>zero, Shunya, square root, cube root, khahara, multiplication by zero, division by zero, Bhaskara II, Lilavati, inverse operations, planetary mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page which posed a series of mathematical riddles regarding operations with zero and an algebraic problem involving an unknown quantity, this page provides the step-by-step calculations and the final solution.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

**Calculation—** Following the previously mentioned rule, when zero (0) is added to five (5), the result is five. When zero (0) is squared, the result is zero. When the square root of zero (0) is taken, it is also zero. Furthermore, taking the cube or the cube root of zero (0) results only in zero (0).

Multiplying five (5) by zero (0) results in zero (0), according to the prescribed method. ॥

When ten (10) is divided by zero (0), according to the rule, the zero (0) becomes the denominator <term>denominator: "hara"</term> below the ten, expressed as $\frac{10}{0}$.

Although the "Method of Inverse Operation" <term>inverse operation: "viloma-vidhi"</term> will be explained later, it is required for this example, so the subject is introduced here. Namely, if one must perform an inverse calculation, one should treat the divisor as a multiplier and the multiplier as a divisor; consider the square as a square root and the square root as a square; consider the cube as a cube root and the cube root as a cube. Where an addition was requested, perform a subtraction, and where a subtraction was requested, perform an addition. All these operations should be performed on the "known quantity" <term>known quantity: "drishya-rashi"</term> <note>literally "the visible quantity" provided in the problem</note> to discover the original "unknown quantity" <term>unknown quantity: "agyat-rashi"</term>. Furthermore, regarding fractions, if a part <term>part/numerator: "ansha"</term> is to be added or subtracted: if it was added in the original problem, subtract the numerator from the sum of the numerator and denominator; if it was subtracted, add it. Then proceed with the remaining steps as before. According to this logic, the multiplier is treated as a divisor, the addition as subtraction, the multiplier as a divisor, and the divisor as a multiplier. Applying this method to the known quantity, sixty-three (63): first, (conceptually) multiply by zero (0).

<insert>
-> ( Assumption ) <-

| Multiplier | 0 | Divisor |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Added | 1 | Difference |
| Multiplier | 3 | Divisor |
| Divisor | 0 | Multiplier |
| Known Quantity | 63 | |
| | 63 x 0 | |

3 ) 63 ( 21 x 0
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;6 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 7
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;--- &nbsp;&nbsp; ---
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;3 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 14-0
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;3 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 0
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;--- &nbsp;&nbsp; ---
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;0 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 14 Unknown
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Quantity
</insert>

When this was done, according to the rule mentioned earlier, although multiplying by zero results in zero, there are still remaining steps in the calculation. For this reason, one must keep the known quantity in mind as "63 x 0" <note>original: "khaguna-rashiko chintana kiya"</note>. Then, it is divided by three (3), resulting in the form "21 x 0." Now, at this stage, we must subtract the "part" <note>this refers to the inverse of the problem's "increased by half of itself"</note>. For this reason, the numerator of two (2) is added to the denominator of one (1), making three (3). This three (3) is divided into the quantity twenty-one (21), yielding a quotient <term>quotient: "labdhi"</term> of seven (7). When this seven (7) is subtracted from twenty-one (21), the form becomes "14 x 0." Now, the final step is to divide by zero (0). Since a multiplication by zero has been carried through the calculation and we have now reached a division by zero, according to the "Rule of Operations with Zero" <note>shunya-parikarma-sutra</note>, the multiplier and divisor cancel each other out. The quantity remains exactly as it was. Thus, fourteen (14) is the unknown quantity. ॥

<note>This concludes the solution to the riddle: $(x \cdot 0 + x/2) \cdot 3 / 0 = 63$. Working backward: $63 \cdot 0 / 3 = 21 \cdot 0$. Then reversing the "add half" (which is multiplying by 3/2) by multiplying by 2/3: $21 \cdot (2/3) = 14$. The final division by zero "restores" the number multiplied by zero earlier.</note>

<summary>This page explains the practical application of mathematical rules for zero, specifically focusing on how multiplication and division by zero cancel each other out in complex algebraic problems. It introduces the "Method of Inverse Operation" (Viloma-vidhi) to solve for an unknown quantity starting from a known result.</summary>
<keywords>Zero (Shunya), Inverse Operation (Viloma-vidhi), Denominator (Hara), Numerator (Ansha), Known Quantity (Drishya-rashi), Unknown Quantity (Agyat-rashi), Lilavati, Bhaskara II, Algebra</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the explanation of zero in the previous page, this section concludes the "Eight Operations with Zero" and introduces the "Method of Inverse Operation" (Vyastavidhi). The previous page ended while explaining how to handle fractions in reverse calculations.</meta>

<page-num>४१</page-num>
<header>The Method of Inverse Operation</header>

According to the questioner's request, the calculation was performed on the known quantity 14, and the result remains sixty-three. This is because although multiplying 14 by zero should technically make the quantity zero, the calculation is still in progress; therefore, the quantity was contemplated as 14 x 0. Then, when its own half was added to it, the form became 21 x 0. Then it was multiplied by three (3), resulting in the form 63 x 0. Finally, it was divided by zero (0); according to the previously mentioned rule, the quantity remained exactly as it was <note>The rule states that if zero is both a multiplier and a divisor, the value remains unchanged</note>, because where zero is the multiplier, if zero also becomes the divisor, there is no change in the quantity. For this reason, 63 is the "seen quantity" <term>seen quantity: "drishta-rashi," the result provided in a math problem</term>. This same quantity is also obtained by the "Method of Desired Operation" <term>desired operation: "ishta-karma," a method of trial and error or algebraic substitution</term>. These Eight Operations with Zero <term>eight operations: "shunya-parikarmashtaka"</term> are very useful in planetary mathematics.

->This concludes the Eight Operations with Zero.<-

### Now, the formula for the Method of Inverse Operation in two verses—

Now, the method for performing the Inverse Operation <term>inverse operation: "vyastavidhi" or "viloma-vidhi"</term> is described in two verses:

> Make the divisor a multiplier, and the multiplier a divisor; make the square a root, and the root a square.
> Make a negative into a positive, and a positive into a negative, to determine the unknown quantity from the seen result. ॥ 8 ॥
> <note>original Sanskrit: "chedam gunam gunam chedam vargam mulam padam kritim / rinam swam swamrinam kuryad drishye rashiprasiddhaye"</note>

**Prose Order:** In the inverse method, to determine the quantity: treat the divisor as a multiplier, treat the multiplier as a divisor, treat the square as a square root, treat the square root as a square, treat the subtracted amount as an addition, and perform the operation on the seen result. ॥ 8 ॥

**Meaning:** To know the original quantity in an inverse calculation, imagine the denominator to be a multiplier and the multiplier to be a denominator. Imagine the square to be a square root and the square root to be a square. Similarly, imagine the number to be subtracted as a number to be added, and the number to be added as a number to be subtracted. By performing the calculation in this way, the original "seen quantity" is successfully established.

### If the inverse operation involves fractional parts—

> Now, in the case of increasing or decreasing by one's own parts, the denominator is increased or decreased by the numerator.
> The numerator remains unchanged; in the inverse, the rest is as previously stated. ॥ 9 ॥
> <note>original Sanskrit: "atha swamshadhikone tu lavadhyono haro harah / amshastvavikritastatra vilome sheshamuktavat"</note>

**Prose Order:** Now, if there is an increase or decrease by its own part, the denominator should be the denominator plus or minus the numerator. However, the numerator should be known as unchanged. The rest of the inverse operation should be done as previously stated. ॥ 9 ॥

**Meaning:** If a quantity is increased or decreased by a fraction of itself: if it was decreased, add the numerator to the denominator to form a new denominator; if it was increased, subtract the numerator from the denominator to form a new denominator. Keep the numerator exactly as it is. Then, perform the rest of the inverse steps as described before. ॥ 9 ॥

### Here is the example:

#### Example regarding the Method of Inverse Operation:

> A number is multiplied by three, increased by three, and its [result] is divided by seven; 
> then decreased by its own third part, then multiplied by itself, then decreased by fifty-two...
> <note>This verse poses a riddle where the reader must work backward from a final result to find the starting number.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the rules for calculating with zero and introduces the Method of Inverse Operation (working backward from a result). It provides mathematical verses (sutras) for inverting operations like multiplication, squaring, and fractional adjustments.</summary>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

> Tell me, O restless-eyed girl, that quantity which—after being multiplied by three, increased by three-quarters of itself, divided by seven, diminished by one-third of the quotient, squared, reduced by fifty-two, then the square root taken, and eight added, and finally divided by ten—results in two. Do you know the pure inverse operation, O maiden? || 10 ||

**Prose Order (Anvaya):** — O girl! O restless-eyed one! If you know the pure inverse operation, then tell me that quantity which is multiplied by three, increased by its own three quarters, then divided by seven, diminished by its own third part, squared, diminished by fifty-two, the square root of that being increased by eight, and even after being divided by ten, the result is two. Tell me that quantity. || 10 ||

**Interpretation (Artha):** — O sixteen-year-old girl! O restless-eyed one! If you know the correct method of the inverse operation, then what is that quantity which was multiplied by 3, then increased by its own three parts <note>original: 'swacharana' literally means feet or quarters</note>, thereafter divided by 7; then from the resulting number, its <term>third part</term> <gloss>1</gloss>/<gloss>3</gloss> was subtracted; then that resulting number was <term>squared</term> and 52 was subtracted; then of the remainder, the <term>square root</term> was taken and 8 was added; thereafter, even after dividing by 10, the <term>quotient</term> <term>quotient: "labdhi," the result of a division</term> is 2—then say, what is that quantity which, when the aforementioned process is applied, results in a quotient of 2? || 10 ||

**Statement (Nyasa):** — Multiplier 3; Addition <gloss>3</gloss>/<gloss>4</gloss>.
Divisor 7; Subtraction <gloss>1</gloss>/<gloss>3</gloss>; Square; Subtraction 52.
Square root; Addition 8; Divisor 10.
Known Result 2; by performing the operations as stated, the resulting quantity is 28.

->This is the Inverse Method.<-

**Step-by-Step Expansion:** — Here the "seen quantity" <note>the final result given in the problem</note> is 2. This was multiplied by 10, so it became 20. From that, 8 was subtracted, leaving 12. That was squared, resulting in 144. To those, 52 was added, making 196. The square root of these was taken, which became 14. To this, its own third part must be added; for this reason, the numerator 1 was subtracted from the denominator 3 <note>To reverse the subtraction of a fraction of the whole, one divides by the remaining portion</note>, leaving 2. These [14] were divided by [2], resulting in 7 as the quotient. This was added to 14, becoming 21. This was then multiplied by 7, which became 147. Now, from this quantity, its own <term>three-fold quarter</term> <note>original: "trigunita chaturthansha," or 3/4ths</note> must be [subtracted]...

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>The Method of Desired Operation</header>
<meta>CC-0. Late Pt. Manmohan Shastri Collection Jammu. Digitized by eGangotri</meta>

Because it must be reduced, three (3) was added to every four (4), making the divisor seven (7); the numerator was left unchanged, resulting in the form $\frac{147}{7}$ and $\frac{3}{7}$. Then, after performing the <term>reduction of the fraction</term><note>original: "bhagapavaha," the process of simplifying or clearing fractions</note> on $\frac{147}{7}$, the form became $\frac{588}{7}$. Now, the numerator was divided by the divisor |$\frac{3}{7}$|, which resulted in 84 (eighty-four). This is the remainder when three-quarters of 147 is subtracted from itself. Now, dividing by three, the <term>quotient</term><note>original: "labdhi"</note> of 28 (twenty-eight) was obtained. This is the unknown quantity.

<margin>28 is the unknown quantity.</margin>
<insert>
( Statement of operations )

Divisor: 7
Difference: $\frac{3}{4}$
Square—
Difference: 52

Divisor: 10

By performing the multiplication and other operations on the unknown quantity according to the questioner's instruction, the <term>given result</term><note>original: "drishya rashi," literally the 'seen quantity' or the target number provided in the riddle</note> of 2 (two) is obtained. For example: the known quantity 28 (twenty-eight) was multiplied by three (3), which became 84 (eighty-four). Now, its own three-quarters must be added to it; therefore, three-quarters ($\frac{3}{4}$) of eighty-four—which is 21 (twenty-one) tripled—was added to eighty-four, making 147 (one hundred forty-seven). This was divided by seven (7), giving a quotient of 21 (twenty-one). From this, its own <gloss>1/3</gloss> third part, 7 (seven), was subtracted, leaving 14 (fourteen). This was squared, making 196 (one hundred ninety-six). From this, 52 (fifty-two) was subtracted, leaving 144 (one hundred forty-four). The square root of this was taken, yielding 12 (twelve). To this, 8 (eight) was added, making 20 (twenty). By dividing this by 10 (ten), the same given result of 2 (two) was obtained as the quotient.

->Thus ends the Method of Inverse Operation.<-

### Now, the Rule-Verse for the Desired Operation—
### The method for performing the "Desired Operation" is stated in one verse:

> Any assumed quantity, treated according to the statement of the question—multiplied, divided, or reduced or increased by its own parts—is the <term>divisor</term>. The given result, multiplied by the assumed quantity and divided by this divisor, yields the required quantity. This is called the Desired Operation. || 10 ||

**Prose Order (Anvaya):** — An assumed quantity (*ishtarashih*) should be multiplied, divided, decreased by parts, or increased by parts according to the statement of the question (*uddeshakalapavat*). By this result, the given quantity (*drishtam*) multiplied by the assumed quantity (*ishtahatam*) should be divided (*bhaktam*). The resulting quantity (*rashih*) is produced. Thus, the Desired Operation (*ishtakarma*) is proclaimed. || 10 ||

**Meaning:** — In the Desired Operation <note>This is the "Rule of False Position," where you guess a number and then adjust it</note>, one should imagine any "desired" number and multiply it, divide it, or subtract/add its parts according to the questioner's instructions. Whatever value is thus produced, use it to divide the product of the given result and the assumed number. The resulting quotient will be the actual quantity sought. The teachers have described the "Desired Operation" in this manner. || 10 ||

<vocab>Desired Operation (Ishtakarma), Unknown quantity (Ajnata rashi), Quotient (Labdhi), Three-quarters (Chaturthansha-trigunita), Reduction of fractions (Bhagapavaha), Inverse Method (Vyastavidhi), Rule-verse (Karana-sutra), Prose order (Anvaya), Statement of operations (Alapa)</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a complex inverse mathematical problem to find the number 28 and introduces Verse 10, which defines the 'Ishtakarma' (Rule of False Position) for solving equations by assuming a value.</summary>
<keywords>arithmetic, inverse operation, Ishtakarma, Lilavati, Bhaskara, fractions, square root, algebra</keywords>

<header>Lilavati</header>

### Here is an example:
->Example regarding the Method of Supposition<-

> A quantity is multiplied by five, diminished by its own third part, and then divided by ten. To this result, the third, the half, and the quarter of the original quantity are added. If the resulting sum is sixty-eight, what is that quantity? || 11 ||
<note>original: "ūnasaptati" literally means 'one less than seventy' (69), but the commentary treats the target value as 68 (eight and sixty).</note>

**Prose Order:** What is that quantity which, when multiplied by five, diminished by its own third part, divided by ten, and increased by the third, half, and quarter parts of the original quantity, becomes sixty-eight? || 11 ||

**Meaning:** What is that quantity which—after being multiplied by five, having its own third part subtracted, then being divided by ten, and finally having the third, half, and quarter parts of the assumed quantity added to it—results in the number 68 (sixty-eight)? || 11 ||

**Statement of values:** 
Multiplier: 5; Its own third part: $\frac{1}{3}$; Divisor: 10;
Parts of the quantity: $\frac{1}{3}$ $\frac{1}{2}$ $\frac{1}{4}$; Given result: 68

In this case, let the <term>assumed quantity</term><note>original: "kalpita-rāśi"; a trial number used to test the operations</note> be 3. Multiplied by five, it becomes 15. Diminished by its own third part (which is 5), it becomes 10. Divided by ten, the result is 1. Now, adding the third, half, and quarter parts of the assumed quantity (3)—which are $\frac{1}{3}$, $\frac{1}{2}$, and $\frac{1}{4}$—to that 1, the divisor-sum becomes $\frac{17}{4}$. Now, the given result (68) is multiplied by the assumed quantity (3), making 204. This is then divided by the divisor-sum ($\frac{17}{4}$), which results in the quantity 48. 

Thus, in all such examples: when a quantity is multiplied or divided by something, or increased or diminished by a portion of itself, one should assume a desired quantity and perform the operations as instructed. Whatever result is produced by the assumed quantity should be used to divide the product of the given result and the assumed quantity; the quotient is the true quantity.

**Detailed Explanation:** Here the multiplier is 5, and its own third part $\frac{1}{3}$ is subtracted, and the divisor is 10. The third, half, and quarter parts ($\frac{1}{3}$ $\frac{1}{2}$ $\frac{1}{4}$) of the quantity are then added, and the <term>given result</term><note>original: "dr̥śya rāśi"; the target number specified in the problem</note> is 68. 

Now, according to the rule mentioned above, an <term>assumed quantity</term><note>original: "iṣṭa-rāśi"</note> of 3 is imagined. According to the questioner’s instructions, this was first multiplied by 5, which resulted in 15. From this, its own third part (which is 5) was subtracted, leaving a remainder of 10. This was divided by 10, resulting in a <term>quotient</term><note>original: "labdhi"</note> of 1. 

Now, the third, half, and quarter parts of the assumed quantity (3) must be added to this quotient. For this reason, first a <term>common denominator</term><note>original: "samaccheda"; the process of finding a shared denominator for fractions</note> was established. That is to say, leaving aside its own denominator, the numerator and denominator of the first quantity were multiplied by the denominators of the other quantities. In this manner, for as many quantities as there are, each denominator...

<meta>The text ends mid-sentence. The author is beginning to explain the arithmetic of adding fractions with different denominators (1 + 1 + 1.5 + 0.75) to reach the 17/4 used in the Sanskrit summary above.</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates the "Method of Supposition" (Ishtakarma) through a mathematical riddle. By assuming the number 3 and following the operations, the author demonstrates how to scale the result to find the true answer, 48.</summary>
<keywords>Ishtakarma, Method of Supposition, Bhaskara II, Lilavati, arithmetic, fractions, common denominator, assumed quantity</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues from the mathematical demonstration on page 44. It concludes the verification of the first problem—finding a hidden quantity using the Method of Supposition—and introduces a new problem involving a herd of elephants, categorized as "Drishtajati" (Method of the Known Category).</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>Method of the Known Category</header>

When the numerators and denominators were multiplied, it resulted in:
$\frac{2}{1} \times \frac{3}{2} \times \frac{4}{3} \times \frac{5}{4} = \frac{120}{24} = \frac{5}{1}$

<note>The scribe is showing the intermediate steps of fraction multiplication and reduction.</note>

The values $\frac{9}{1}$ and $\frac{18}{24}$ were transformed into a common denominator format: $\frac{24}{24}, \frac{24}{24}, \frac{24}{24}, \frac{36}{24}, \frac{17}{24}$. When these numerators were added, the form became $\frac{102}{24}$. Here, a reduction by a factor of 6 was applied, resulting in $\frac{17}{4}$.

Then, the **assumed quantity** 3 was multiplied by the **known result** 68, which produced 204. This was then divided by the first fraction $\frac{17}{4}$. Specifically:
$\frac{204}{1} \div \frac{17}{4}$

In this division, the numerator and denominator of the divisor were swapped:
$\frac{204}{1} \times \frac{4}{17}$

Now, multiplying the numerators together and the denominators together, the form became $\frac{816}{17}$. When the numerator was divided by the denominator, the <term>quotient</term><note>original: "labdhi"</note> was 48.

This 48 is the actual quantity which, when the previously stated mathematical operations are performed, results in 68. To verify:
When 48 is multiplied by five, it becomes 240. Subtracting its own third part (80) leaves a remainder of 160. Dividing this by ten results in 16. To this 16, if we add the third part of the original quantity 48 (which is 16), the half (24), and the quarter (12), the sum becomes 68. Thus, in all such examples, the result obtained is the desired quantity.

<note>Verification: $16 + 16 + 24 + 12 = 68$. The math is consistent.</note>

## Another Example:

Another example—in this, one male elephant and three female elephants, totaling 4, are the "known" <margin>quantities</margin>. For this reason, this is called an example of the **Known Category** (<term>Drishtajati</term><note>A problem where a specific whole number remains after various fractional parts are removed.</note>).

> Half a herd of elephants, increased by a third part [of that half], was seen in a cave. One-sixth of the herd and one-seventh [of that sixth] were drinking water in the river. One-eighth of the herd together with its ninth part was playing with passion in a lotus pond. One king elephant was seen followed by three female elephants. What is the total number of the herd? || 1 || This is the additive term.

**Prose Order:** Half the herd of elephants together with a third part was seen in a cave. A sixth part and a seventh part mixed together drink water in the river. Similarly, an eighth part together with its ninth part drinks water in a lotus pond. Also, a king elephant (nagendra) follows three female elephants and plays with passion. Then, what would the total number of the herd be? || 1 ||
