<header>The Lilavati</header>

**Meaning:** O friend! There was a herd of elephants. We saw half of them along with their own third part <insert>$\frac{1}{2}$ $\frac{1}{3}$</insert> entering a cave in the forest. Another sixth part, together with its seventh part <insert>$\frac{1}{6}$ $\frac{1}{7}$</insert>, was drinking water in the river. And an eighth part, along with its ninth part <insert>$\frac{1}{8}$ $\frac{1}{9}$</insert>, was drinking water in a pond filled with lotuses. 

Additionally, one noble male elephant was seen playfully sporting with three female elephants. Tell me, what was the total number of elephants in the herd? || 1 ||

->**Layout:** $\frac{1}{2}$ $\frac{1}{3}$ | $\frac{1}{6}$ $\frac{1}{7}$ | $\frac{1}{8}$ $\frac{1}{9}$ Visible Quantity: 4<-

> These fractions, when reduced <term>savarnana</term><note>The process of finding a common denominator or simplifying complex fractions.</note> and divided by two, become: $\frac{2}{3}$ $\frac{4}{21}$ $\frac{5}{36}$
> After finding a common denominator for these, they are reduced by nine: $\frac{251}{252}$
> This is subtracted from the assumed whole: $\frac{1}{252}$
> When the visible quantity 4 is divided by this (multiplied by the reciprocal), the total number of elephants is found to be 1008.

**Elaboration:** Following the method described above <insert>$\frac{1}{2}$ $\frac{1}{3}$ | $\frac{1}{6}$ $\frac{1}{7}$ | $\frac{1}{8}$ $\frac{1}{9}$</insert>, all these quantities were simplified using the rule of "associated fractions" <term>bhaganubandha</term><note>original: "bhaganubandha" - a rule for adding a fraction of a fraction to itself, e.g., 1/2 + (1/3 of 1/2).</note>. The form then became $\frac{2}{3}$ $\frac{8}{42}$ $\frac{10}{72}$. Here, a reduction <term>apavartana</term> by a factor of 2 was applied, resulting in $\frac{2}{3}$ $\frac{4}{21}$ $\frac{5}{36}$. These were then added together by finding a common denominator <term>samachheda</term>: $\frac{168}{252} + \frac{48}{252} + \frac{35}{252} = \frac{251}{252}$. 

<note>The scribe mentions a reduction by 9, likely referring to a step in finding the common denominator 252.</note> 

Now, this sum was subtracted from the total herd (represented as 1): $\frac{1}{1} - \frac{251}{252} = \frac{252-251}{252} = \frac{1}{252}$. Then, the visible quantity 4 was divided by this fraction: $4 \div \frac{1}{252} = 4 \times 252 = 1008$. This "one thousand and eight" is the total number of elephants in the herd. 

**Verification:** Half the herd plus its third part is 672 (six hundred seventy-two) <note>Calculation: 1008/2 = 504; 504/3 = 168; 504+168 = 672.</note> who went into the cave. The sixth part plus its seventh part is 192 (one hundred ninety-two) <note>Calculation: 1008/6 = 168; 168/7 = 24; 168+24 = 192.</note> who were drinking river water. The eighth part plus its ninth part is 140 (one hundred forty) <note>Calculation: 1008/8 = 126; 126/9 = 14; 126+14 = 140.</note> who were at the lotus pond. Finally, one male elephant with three females makes 4 playing together. When all these are added together, they equal exactly one thousand and eight (1008). || This is an interpolatory verse.

## Method of the Known Quantity

### Another Example
The third example regarding the Method of Supposition <term>ishtakarma</term><note>A mathematical technique where an easy value (like 1) is assumed for an unknown total to find the correct ratio before scaling up to the final answer.</note>.

> Of a heap of pure lotuses, a third, a fifth, and a sixth part were offered to the Three-Eyed One, Hari, and the Sun respectively. A fourth part was offered to the Goddess. The feet of the Teacher were then worshipped with the remaining six lotuses. Tell me quickly, what was the total number of lotuses in that heap? || 2 ||

**Prose Order:** O friend! Tell me quickly the total number of lotuses from which a third, a fifth, and a sixth part were used to worship the Three-Eyed One <note>original: "Trinayana" — a name for Lord Shiva</note>, Hari <note>original: "Hari" — a name for Lord Vishnu</note>, and the Sun; a fourth part was used for the Goddess <note>original: "Arya" — a title for the Divine Mother</note>; and the remaining six lotuses were used to worship the feet of the Teacher. || 2 ||

**Meaning:** O friend! A person took a heap of beautiful lotuses. He worshipped Lord Shiva with the third part, Lord Vishnu with the fifth part, and the Sun with the sixth part. He also worshipped the Goddess with the fourth part. With the remaining six lotuses, he worshipped the lotus-feet of his Teacher. Now, tell me the total number of lotuses that were in that heap? || 2 ||

**Setup:** $\frac{1}{3}$ $\frac{1}{5}$ $\frac{1}{6}$ $\frac{1}{4}$ **Known quantity: 6.**

Here, by assuming the desired quantity to be 1, the total is found to be 120 as shown before.

**Elaboration:** Following the rule mentioned above, to add the fractions $\frac{1}{3}$, $\frac{1}{5}$, $\frac{1}{6}$, and $\frac{1}{4}$, we find a common denominator <term>samacheda</term><note>The process of "equalizing the denominators" so that fractions can be summed.</note>. This results in: $\frac{20}{60}$, $\frac{12}{60}$, $\frac{10}{60}$, and $\frac{15}{60}$. When these numerators are added together, the sum is $\frac{57}{60}$. This represents the fraction of the heap given away. To find the remainder, we subtract this from the whole (1): $\frac{1}{1} - \frac{57}{60} = \frac{3}{60}$. By reducing this fraction by three <note>original: "parivartana" — here meaning to simplify the fraction by dividing both parts by 3</note>, we get $\frac{1}{20}$.

Now, divide the known quantity (6) by this remainder (which is the same as multiplying by its reciprocal): $\frac{6}{1} \div \frac{1}{20} = \frac{6}{1} \times \frac{20}{1}$. Multiplying the numerators gives 120. This is the total number of lotuses. We can verify this because:
- A third of the total (40 lotuses) was offered to Shiva.
- A fifth of the total (24 lotuses) was offered to Vishnu.
- A sixth of the total (20 lotuses) was offered to the Sun.
- A fourth of the total (30 lotuses) was offered to the Goddess.
- The remaining 6 lotuses were offered to the Teacher.
When all these are added together, the total is indeed 120.

| Calculation |
| :--- |
| 40 |
| 24 |
| 20 |
| 30 |
| 6 |
| --- |
| **120** |

<vocab>Drishtajati-prakara (Method of the Known Quantity), Ishtakarma (Method of Supposition), Samacheda (Common Denominator), Labdhi (Quotient/Result)</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a mathematical word problem from the Lilavati where the total number of lotuses in a heap is determined by summing various fractions and using a known remainder (6 lotuses). Through the use of common denominators and the method of supposition, the total is calculated to be 120.</summary>

->**Lilavati**<-

# Another Example
## Further Examples on the Method of Supposition <term>Ishtakarma</term><note>A mathematical technique where an easy "supposed" value is used to determine the ratio of a total before calculating the actual value.</note>—

> A radiant necklace of pearls belonging to a young woman was broken during a lovers' quarrel and the pearls scattered. One-third of the pearls fell to the ground; one-fifth of them were seen to have gone onto the surface of the bed; one-sixth was recovered by the fair-haired woman <note>original: "Sukeshya" — a woman with beautiful hair</note>; one-tenth was collected by her lover; and six pearls were seen remaining on the string. O Calculator, tell me, of how many pearls was this necklace composed? || 3 ||

**Prose Order:** O Calculator! During a lovers' quarrel, a radiant necklace of pearls belonging to a young woman was scattered. Then, one-third fell to the ground. One-fifth was seen on the surface of the bed. One-sixth was recovered by the fair-haired woman. One-tenth was collected by her lover. Six pearls were seen on the string. Therefore, tell me, of how many pearls was this necklace made? || 3 ||

**Meaning:** O Calculator! During a playful argument <note>original: "maidhuna-ke-jhagade" — literally a quarrel during intimacy</note>, a young woman's pearl necklace broke. Of those pearls, the third part fell right on the ground, and it was observed that the fifth part rolled under the bed. The woman herself picked up the sixth part, and the husband picked up the tenth part. If six pearls remained on the string, then tell me, with how many pearls was that necklace originally crafted? || 3 ||

In this case, by assuming a desired quantity <term>ishtakarma</term> and proceeding as before, the result is 30. This is the additive quantity <term>kshepaka</term>.

<margin>
| |
| :---: |
| 10 |
| 6 |
| 5 |
| 3 |
| 6 |
| --- |
| **30** |
</margin>

**The Expansion:** Here, we have the fractions $\frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{5}, \frac{1}{6}, \frac{1}{10}$ and the "known" remainder of 6. According to the previously mentioned rule, to perform the addition of fractions, we must find a common denominator <term>samaccheda</term>.
$\frac{1}{3} = \frac{3}{9} = \frac{3}{18} = \frac{3}{30} = \frac{5}{15} = \frac{3}{15} = \frac{15}{90} = \frac{15}{150} = \frac{30}{90}, \frac{18}{90}, \frac{15}{90}, \frac{9}{90} = \frac{300}{900}, \frac{180}{900}, \frac{150}{900}, \frac{90}{900}$.
<note>The text demonstrates various ways to scale the fractions before settling on a common denominator of 900.</note> 
Then the expression took this form. Now, when all the numerators were added, the form became $\frac{720}{900}$. Here, after reducing the fraction <term>apavartana</term> by 12, it became $\frac{60}{75}$. Then, after reducing by 15, the form became $\frac{4}{5}$. This was subtracted from the total quantity of pearls (represented as 1): 
$\frac{5}{5} - \frac{4}{5} = \frac{1}{5}$. 
This was then divided into the known quantity of 6 (multiplied by the assumed quantity of 1): 
$\frac{6}{1} \div \frac{1}{5} = \frac{6}{1} \times \frac{5}{1} = 30$. 
The result is 30. This is the total number of pearls in the necklace. 

**Proof:** Because out of thirty (30), the third part (10) fell on the ground; the fifth part (6) fell under the bed; the sixth part (5) was picked up by the woman; the tenth part (3) was picked up by the husband; and six (6) remained in the string. When all are added together, they equal those same thirty pearls. || This is an additional verse <term>kshepaka shloka</term> ||

<meta>CC-0. Late Pt. Manmohan Shastri Collection Jammu. Digitized by eGangotri</meta>

# The Rule of the Remainder
<page-num>49</page-num>

## Now, an Example of the Remainder Type —
->In the Method of Supposition, this is known as the "Remainder Type" <term>Sheshajati</term><note>A category of problems where each fractional part refers to the amount remaining after the previous deduction, rather than the original total.</note>.<-

> A traveler on a pilgrimage gave half his money at Prayag <note>modern-day Prayagraj/Allahabad</note>, and two-ninths of the remainder at Kashi <note>Varanasi</note>. He gave one-fourth of the remaining amount for taxes on the road, and six-tenths of what was left at Gaya. After all this, sixty-three *nishkas* <term>nishka</term><note>An ancient Indian gold or silver coin used as a standard of currency.</note> remained, with which he returned home. O friend, if you have studied the Remainder Type, tell me the original amount of his wealth. || 4 ||

**Prose Order:** O Friend! If you have heard of the Remainder Type, then solve this: a certain traveler on a pilgrimage gave half of his wealth at Prayag. From the remainder, he gave two-ninths at Kashi. He gave one-fourth of the next remainder for the sake of road taxes. From the final remainder, he gave six-tenths at Gaya. Even so, sixty-three *nishkas* were left over. With these, he went to his own home. Therefore, tell me the total measure of his wealth. || 4 ||

**Meaning:** O Friend! If you understand the Method of Supposition regarding remainders, then tell me this: suppose a pilgrim gave away half <gloss>$\frac{1}{2}$</gloss> of his money at Prayag. From what was left, he gave two-ninths <gloss>$\frac{2}{9}$</gloss> at Kashi. Then, from whatever remained, he gave a fourth part <gloss>$\frac{1}{4}$</gloss> as travel expenses or taxes. Then, from that remainder, he gave six times a tenth part <gloss>$\frac{6}{10}$</gloss> at Gaya. Despite all this, 63 (sixty-three) *nishkas* remained, which he used to reach his home. Tell me, how much total money did that traveler have? || 4 ||

**Layout of the Problem:** $\frac{1}{2}$ The visible remainder <term>drishyam</term> is 63. Here, by assuming 1 as the total quantity <term>rashi</term> and
$\frac{2}{9}$ subtracting the fractions from the remainders, or
$\frac{1}{4}$ by using the "Method of Fractional Reduction" <term>bhagapavaha-vidhi</term>
$\frac{6}{10}$ and finding the common denominator, the result is $\frac{7}{60}$.

By dividing the given remainder (63) by this result, the total measure of wealth is found to be 540.
This can also be proven using the Rule of Inversion <term>vilomasutra</term><note>Working backward from the final result to the start.</note>.

<margin>
$\frac{1}{2}$
$\frac{2}{9}$
$\frac{1}{4}$
$\frac{6}{10}$
| --- Visible: 63 ---
</margin>
**Expansion —** Here, we imagine the total amount to be 1. From this 1, we first subtract the half. Then, from that half, we subtract its own two-ninths. Then, from that remainder, we subtract its fourth part. Finally, from that remainder, we subtract its six-tenths part. Alternatively, by reducing the fractions to a common denominator through the "Method of Reduction," we get 7 over 60 <gloss>$\frac{7}{60}$</gloss>. Dividing the known remainder of 63 by this fraction yields the answer.

<sig>4</sig>
<vocab>Remainder Type (Sheshajati), Method of Supposition (Ishtakarma), Prayag, Gold Coin (Nishka), Total Wealth (Dravyapramana), Method of Fractional Reduction (Bhagapavahavidhi), Common Denominator (Savarnita), Visible Amount (Drishyam)</vocab>

# Lilavati
<page-num>50</page-num>

That is to say, <note>The text here shows a calculation to find the total wealth from the known remainder of 63 nishkas.</note> $\frac{7}{60} \div \frac{63}{1} = \frac{60}{7} \times \frac{63}{1} = \frac{3780}{7}$. By dividing this by the divisor 7, we get 540 (five hundred and forty). This is the total amount; that is, this is the wealth the traveler possessed. 

To verify: half of it, 270, was given at Prayag. From the remaining 270, two-ninths (which is 60 rupees) was given at Kashi. After subtracting 60 from 270, the remainder is 210. 

<margin>270</margin>
<margin>60</margin>
<margin>52 1/2</margin>
<margin>94 1/2</margin>
<margin>63</margin>
<margin>---</margin>
<margin>540</margin>

From this remaining 210, a fourth part, which is 52 $\frac{1}{2}$ (fifty-two and a half) rupees, was given on the road. From the remaining 157 $\frac{1}{2}$, he gave six-tenths, which amounts to 94 $\frac{1}{2}$ (ninety-four and a half) rupees, at Gaya. Finally, 63 remained, with which he reached home. If you add all these parts together, the sum is 540. This is also proven by the previously mentioned "Inverse Method" <term>Viloma-vidhi</term><note>The process of working backward from the final result to the starting number.</note>.

### Another person’s verse regarding this calculation—

> Divide the product of the denominators by the product of the denominators minus their respective numerators. Multiply this result by the known remaining quantity to reveal the total amount. This also achieves success through the Inverse Method. || 1 ||

**Prose Order:** The known quantity <gloss>the visible remainder</gloss> should be divided by the product of the denominators that have been reduced by their respective numerators and then divided by the total product of the denominators. Then the total amount is found. Thus, this also achieves success by the Inverse Method. || 1 ||

**Meaning:** Alternatively, multiply all the denominators together. Then, subtract each numerator from its respective denominator and multiply those results together. Divide the product of the denominators by the product of these remainders. The resulting figure, when multiplied by the known remainder <gloss>the visible part</gloss>, reveals the unknown total amount. This method yields the same result as the Inverse Method mentioned earlier. || 1 ||

### Example

->Regarding the rule stated above:<-

> A lotus-eyed woman <note>original: *Padmakshya*</note> had a collection of beads. She used one-eighth <term>vasu-lava</term><note>In ancient Indian "object-numeral" systems, *Vasu* represents the number 8.</note> for an ornament on her forehead. Of the remainder, three times two parts <note>original: *triguna-dvibhaga*, likely meaning 2/3 tripled or a specific fractional proportion</note> were placed in a necklace on her breast. Half of the next remainder was used for her two armlets. Of what remained, three-fourths <term>tryahata-abdhi</term><note>*Abdhi* (oceans) represents the number 4; *tryahata* means tripled. This refers to 3/4.</note> was made into a waistband. O friend, tell me quickly the total number in her collection of beads, if sixteen remained to be woven into her braid! || 1 ||

**Prose Order:** O friend! If a lotus-eyed woman used one-eighth of a collection of beads for a forehead ornament; and from the remainder, a portion consisting of three times two parts was placed in a necklace; and half of the next remainder was placed on her two armlets; and three-fourths of the final remainder was made into a waistband; and if sixteen beads remained for her braid; then tell me the total count of the beads. || 1 ||

# Lilavati
<page-num>51</page-num>

## Method of the Variety of Subtraction

**Meaning:—** O friend! A man presented his beloved with a piece of jewelry made of precious gems. That lotus-eyed beautiful woman took an eighth part <gloss>1/8</gloss> of the gems to adorn her head. Of what remained, she took three-sevenths <gloss>3/7</gloss> to decorate the space between her breasts as a necklace. Of the remainder from that, she used half <gloss>1/2</gloss> for her armbands. Then, of what was still left, she used three-quarters <gloss>3/4</gloss> to adorn her waist. Even then, sixteen (16) gems remained in the jewelry set. These she used to decorate her braid. Tell me, how many gems were in that jewelry set in total? || 1 ||

**Setup:—** <gloss>1/8</gloss> <gloss>3/7</gloss> <gloss>1/2</gloss> <gloss>3/4</gloss> Known quantity: 16

By following the prescribed method, the total quantity of gems is found to be 256. Alternatively, by using the previously taught "Rule of Supposition" <term>Ishta-karma</term><note>A method where a convenient number is assumed and then scaled to the correct result.</note>, the "Inverse Method" <term>Viloma-vidhi</term>, or the "Method of Fractions," the total gem count is likewise 256. || This is an additional note ||

**Expansion:—** According to the rule stated above, all the denominators were multiplied together, resulting in 448 (four hundred and forty-eight). Then, each numerator was subtracted from its respective denominator, yielding the figures 7, 4, 1, and 1. These were multiplied together to get 28 (twenty-eight). This was then divided by the previous product of the denominators, 448 <note>original: 28/448</note>. When this fraction was simplified by dividing both sides by 28 <term>Apavartana</term>, the result was <gloss>1/16</gloss>. This was then divided into the known remaining quantity of 16 <note>original: 16 ÷ 1/16</note>, which is $16 \times 16 = 256$. Thus, the result is two hundred and fifty-six. Alternatively, by applying the Rule of Supposition, the Inverse Method, or the Method of Fractions, the same result of 256 is achieved.

### Now, the Example of the "Difference Type"—
->Now we write an example concerning the category of differences.<-

> A fifth of a swarm of bees went to a Kadamba tree, and a third went to a Shilindhra flower.
> O fawn-eyed one! Three times the difference between these two went to a Kutaja tree.
> O beloved! One remaining bee, lured by the scent of the Ketaka and Malati flowers,
> is wandering to and fro in the sky, called by his mate. Tell me the total number of bees. || 4 ||

**Prose Order:** O fawn-eyed one! From a swarm of bees, a fifth part went to a Kadamba tree. A third part went to a Shilindhra flower. Three times the difference between those two went to a Kutaja tree. O beloved! Another bee, called by its mate through the simultaneous fragrance of the Ketaka and Malati flowers, is hovering in the air, moving here and there. Therefore, tell me the total number of bees. || 4 ||

<vocab>Vishleshana-jati (Difference Type), Mani-rashi (Total gems), Apavartana (Simplification/Reduction), Ishta-karma (Rule of Supposition), Viloma (Inverse/Reverse), Prabhaga-jati (Category of Fractions), Kadamba (A flowering tree), Shilindhra (A type of flower/fungus), Kutaja (A medicinal plant), Bhrunga (Bee)</vocab>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the exploration of algebraic problems using poetic metaphors. # Lilavati
<page-num>52</page-num>

**Meaning:—** O beloved! There was a swarm of bees. Of the group, a fifth part <gloss>1/5</gloss> settled on a Kadamba tree, and a third part <gloss>1/3</gloss> settled on a Silindhra flower. The difference between these two parts, multiplied by three, flew to a Kutaja tree. O dear one with eyes as beautiful as a doe! One remaining bee, lured by the fragrance of both the Ketaki and Malati flowers wafting on the wind at the same time—like a messenger sent by a lover—hovers in the sky, swinging back and forth in agitation. Tell me, how many bees were there in the swarm?

[<note>Interpretation: The poet describes a single bee caught between two alluring scents. Imagine a Ketaki tree on one side and a Malati tree on the other. A fragrant breeze blows from both simultaneously. When the scent from one side reaches the bee, it moves that way; when the other scent reaches it, it moves back. It is like a man with two wives, both calling him at once through messengers; he oscillates like a swing, unable to decide where to go.</note>]

**Setup:—** <gloss>1/5</gloss> <gloss>1/3</gloss> <gloss>2/15</gloss> Known quantity: 1.

-> The total number of bees is found to be 15. The same logic applies to similar problems. <-

-> Thus ends the Method of Supposition <term>Ishta-karma</term>. <-

**Expansion:—** Here, first, according to the rules of subtracting fractions, the difference between 1/5 and 1/3 is calculated. By finding a common denominator <term>Samachheda</term>, they become 3/15 and 5/15. Subtracting the numerator 3 from 5 leaves 2/15. This was then tripled <note>as per the problem's instruction</note>, resulting in 6/15. When simplified by dividing by three, this tripled difference becomes 2/5. 

Now, we find the common denominator for 1/5, 1/3, and 2/5. This results in:
3/15 + 5/15 + 6/15 = 14/15. 
Subtracting this total from the whole <gloss>represented as 1</gloss>:
1/1 - 14/15 = 15/15 - 14/15 = 1/15. 
The known quantity <note>the 1 remaining bee</note> is then divided by this remainder:
1 ÷ 1/15 = 15.
Thus, the total group consisted of 15 bees.

**Explanation:—** A fifth part (3) was on the Kadamba, and a third part (5) was on the Silindhra. The difference between them (2), tripled, equals 6 bees on the Kutaja, and 1 bee was hovering in the air. Adding them all together (3 + 5 + 6 + 1) equals 15. || This completes the Method of Supposition ||

---

### A Formula for Concurrence in Half a Verse:—

The method for Concurrence <term>Sankramana</term> <note>a method for finding two unknown numbers if their sum and difference are known</note> is stated in half a verse:

-> The sum increased and decreased by the difference, and then halved, gives the two quantities. This is known as Concurrence <note>original: Sankramana</note>. <-

<meta>Digital watermark at bottom: CC-0. Late Pt. Manmohan Shastri Collection Jammu. Digitized by eGangotri</meta>

<summary>This page presents a poetic math problem about a swarm of bees to demonstrate the "Method of Supposition" and introduces the formula for "Concurrence" (Sankramana) to find two numbers given their sum and difference.</summary>
<keywords>Lilavati, Ishta-karma, fractions, bee problem, algebra, Sankramana, common denominator, Bhaskara II</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page moves from the "Method of Supposition" discussed on page 52 to a new algebraic rule called "Concurrence" (Sankramana). The previous page used poetic problems about bees to find unknown totals; this page introduces the method for finding two specific numbers when their sum and difference are known.</meta>

# Methods for Finding Quantities
<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>The Procedure for Operations on Squares.</header>

**Prose Order:—** The sum (once) decreased by the difference and halved; and the sum (once) increased by the difference and halved. These are known as the two quantities. This is called the rule of Concurrence <term>Sankramana—the method of finding two numbers from their sum and difference</term>.

**Meaning:—** When a questioner provides the sum of two numbers and their difference, subtract the difference from the sum. Divide the remainder by two to find the first quantity. Then, add the difference to the sum and divide that total by two to find the second quantity. In this way, both numbers are revealed. This process is called "Concurrence."

### Here is an Example:—

An illustration regarding Concurrence—

> O dear child! If you understand the rule of Concurrence, tell me: what are those two quantities whose sum is one hundred and one, and whose difference is twenty-five? || 1 ||
> <note>original: "ययोर्य्योगः शतं सैकं वियोगः पञ्चविंशतिः । तौ राशी वद मे वत्स वेत्सि संक्रमणं यदि ॥"</note>

**Prose Order:—** O child! If you know the rule of Concurrence, then tell me what those two quantities are whose sum is one hundred and one and whose difference is twenty-five. || 1 ||

**Meaning:—** If the sum of two quantities <term>rashi</term> is 101 and their difference is 25, tell me, if you know the method, what those two numbers are. || 1 ||

**Setup:—** Sum: 101. Difference: 25. Resulting quantities: 38 and 63.

**Expansion:—** Following the rule mentioned above: first, the difference of 25 was subtracted from the sum of 101, which left seventy-six (76). This was halved, resulting in 38. This is the first quantity. Next, the difference of 25 was added to the sum of 101, totaling one hundred and twenty-six (126). This was halved, resulting in 63. This is the second quantity. Thus, 38 and 63 are those two quantities which, when added, become 101, and when subtracted, leave 25. This is because 38 + 63 = 101, and 63 - 38 = 25.

-> Thus ends the rule of Concurrence. <-

## Another Rule in Half a Verse.

This describes the method for finding quantities when the difference of their squares <term>Vargantara</term> and the difference between the quantities themselves are known:

> The difference of the squares divided by the difference of the quantities is the sum; then, find the quantities as previously described. || 11 ||
> <note>original: "वर्गान्तरं राशिवियोगभक्तं योगस्ततः प्रोक्तवदेव राशी ॥"</note>

**Prose Order:—** The difference of the squares divided by the difference of the quantities results in the sum. From that, the quantities should be known just as previously described. || 11 ||

<meta>Vocabulary: Varga-karma-prakara (methods for squares), Sankramana (concurrence), Anvaya (prose order), Nyasa (setup), Phailav (expansion), Vargantara (difference of squares), Rashi-viyoga (difference of quantities), Yoga (sum), Rashi (quantity/number).</meta>

<summary>This page defines "Sankramana" (Concurrence), a rule for finding two unknown numbers if their sum and difference are provided, and introduces a method to find the sum using the difference of their squares.</summary>
<keywords>Sankramana, Concurrence, algebra, sum and difference, square difference, Vargantara, Lilavati, Bhaskara II</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the practical application of the rule of Concurrence (Sankramana) introduced on the previous page. While page 53 explained how to find two numbers from their sum and difference, this page provides a method for finding them when only the difference of their squares and the difference of the numbers themselves are known.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>Lilavati (The Beautiful)</header>

**Meaning:—** Divide the difference of the squares by the difference of the two quantities. The result obtained should be understood as the sum of those quantities. Then, by performing the operations according to the previously mentioned rule <note>Referring to the Sankramana rule from the previous page</note>, the quantities are revealed.

**The Aim:—** An Illustration:—

> O expert in mathematics! If you are quick of mind, tell me: what are those two quantities whose difference is eight, and the difference of whose squares is four hundred? || 1 ||
> <note>original: "राश्योर्ययोर्वियोगोऽष्टौ तत्कृत्योश्च चतुःशती । विवरं वद तौ राशी शीघ्रं गणितकोविद् ॥ १ ॥"</note>

**Prose Order:—** O expert mathematician! Those two quantities whose difference is eight, and the difference of whose squares is four hundred—tell me those two quantities quickly. || 1 ||

**Meaning:—** O you who are at the pinnacle of mathematical skill! What are those two numbers which have a difference of 8, and when you calculate the difference of their squares, the result is 400? Tell me, which numbers are they? || 1 ||

->**Setup:—** Difference of quantities: 8. Difference of squares: 400.<-
->**The quantities produced: 21 and 29.**<-

**Expansion:—** According to the rule stated above, we take the difference of the squares, 400, and divide it by the difference of the quantities, 8. This results in a quotient of 50. This is the "sum of the quantities" <term>yogarashi</term>. Now, according to the formula for the rule of Concurrence <term>Sankramana riti</term>, we subtract 8 from 50, which leaves 42. Halving this gives 21. This is the first quantity. Then, we add 8 to 50, which makes 58. Halving this gives 29. This is the second quantity. Thus, the two numbers whose difference is 8 and the difference of whose squares is 400 are 21 and 29. We can verify this: if you subtract 21 from 29, the remainder is 8 (the difference). If you square 21, you get 441. If you square 29, you get 841. Subtracting these gives a difference of 400, which is exactly the difference of the squares.

## Now, some operations on squares are described.

### We now write the method for certain operations involving squares—

> Take a chosen number, square it, multiply it by eight, and subtract one. Halve this and divide by the chosen number; this is the first quantity. The square of this, halved and increased by one, is the other quantity. || 12 ||
> <note>original: "इष्टकृतिरष्टगुणिता व्येका दलिता विभाजितेष्टेन । एकः स्यादस्य कृतिर्दलिता सैका परो राशिः ॥ १२ ॥"</note>

**Prose Order:—** The square of a chosen number, multiplied by eight, decreased by one, halved, and divided by that same chosen number is the first quantity. The square of this first quantity, halved and increased by one, is the other quantity. || 12 ||

# Method of Square Operations
<page-num>( 55 )</page-num>

**Meaning:—** According to one's own desire, assume a chosen number <term>ishta: an arbitrary value chosen to begin a calculation</term>. After squaring it, multiply that result by 8 and subtract 1. Then, take half of that remaining amount. Divide this half by the originally chosen number; the resulting quotient is the first quantity. To find the second quantity, square this first quantity, divide it by two, and add one. || 12 ||

->One divided by twice the chosen number, plus that chosen number, is the first; or else the other is one.<-
->The sum and difference of the squares of which two quantities, when decreased by one, result in square numbers. || 13 ||<-
<note>original: "रूपं द्विगुणेषुहृतं सेष्टं प्रथमोऽथवाऽपरो रूपम् । कृतियुतिवियुती व्येके वर्गों स्यातां ययो राश्योः ॥ १३ ॥"</note>

**Prose Order:—** One divided by twice the chosen number plus the chosen number should be the first quantity. Or, let "one" <term>rupa</term> be the other quantity. These are the two quantities whose sum of squares and difference of squares, when decreased by one, become perfect squares. || 13 ||

**Meaning:—** Divide "unity" (the number one) by twice the value of an assumed chosen number. Add the chosen number to the resulting quotient; this constitutes the first quantity. The second quantity is simply one. These are the two values such that if you take the sum of their squares and subtract one, the result is a square; similarly, if you take the difference of their squares and subtract one, that result is also a square. || 13 ||

**The Aim:—Illustration—**

> O friend! Tell me those two quantities whose sum of squares and difference of squares, when decreased by one, yield perfect square roots. In this Algebra <term>Bijaganita</term>, even those proficient in the "six-fold secret mathematics" <note>Referring to complex algebraic methods like the Pulverizer or square-natured equations</note> suffer like fools when struggling to solve this. || 1 ||

**Prose Order:—** O friend! Tell me those two quantities whose sum of squares and difference of squares, when decreased by one, are capable of providing a square root. In which Algebra even the clever, contemplating the six-fold secret mathematics, suffer as if they were fools. || 1 ||

**Meaning:—** My dear friend! Tell me those two numbers where the difference of their squares minus one, and the sum of their squares minus one, both result in numbers from which a square root can be extracted. These are the types of problems where even the most brilliant minds in the study of Algebra feel like simpletons when trying to navigate the six advanced methods of calculation. || 1 ||

**Setup:—** Here, to bring forth the first quantity, the chosen number is assumed to be <gloss>1/2</gloss>. Its square is <gloss>1/4</gloss>. Multiplied by eight, it becomes 2. This, minus one, is <gloss>1</gloss>. Halved, it is <gloss>1/2</gloss>. Divided by the chosen number <gloss>1/2</gloss>, it results in 1.
<note>This follows the rule in verse 12: [ (8x² - 1) / 2 ] / x </note>
The square of this is 1; halved, it is <gloss>1/2</gloss>; plus one, it becomes <gloss>3/2</gloss>. This is the other quantity.
Thus, these two quantities are $1$ and $\frac{3}{2}$.
<note>Verification: (1² + 1.5²) - 1 = 2.25 (Square of 1.5). (1.5² - 1²) - 1 = 0.25 (Square of 0.5).</note>

# Lilavati ||

Thus, by choosing **1** as the chosen number <term>ishta: a variable chosen to initiate the calculation</term>, the two quantities produced are $\frac{7}{2}$ and $\frac{57}{8}$. 
By choosing **2**, the quantities are $\frac{31}{8}$ and $\frac{901}{32}$.

Now, by the **second method**, if the chosen number is **1**:
Unity (1) divided by twice the chosen number (2) results in $\frac{1}{2}$.
Adding the chosen number (1) <note>Note: The text here assumes a variation of the rule where the chosen number is added back</note>, the first quantity becomes $\frac{3}{2}$. The second quantity is unity (1).
Thus, the quantities are $\frac{3}{2}$ and **1**.

Likewise, with **2** as the chosen number, the quantities are $\frac{5}{4}$ and **2**. 
With **3**, the quantities are $\frac{11}{6}$ and **3**.

**Expansion—** Following the rule mentioned above, to find the first quantity, we assume a chosen number <term>ishta</term> of $\frac{1}{2}$.
We square this half, resulting in the form $\frac{1}{4}$. We multiply this by **8 (eight)**.
That is, $\frac{1}{4} \times \frac{8}{1} = \frac{8}{4}$. By finding a common denominator <term>samaccheda</term> and following the rules of fractional multiplication (multiplying numerator by numerator and denominator by denominator), the form remains $\frac{8}{4}$. 
Now, dividing the numerator by the denominator, the quotient is **2 (two)**. This is the product.
Subtract **1 (one)** from this, and **1 (one)** remains. We take half of that, which gives us the form $\frac{1}{2}$.
Divide this by the originally chosen number $\frac{1}{2}$. 
That is, $\frac{1}{2} \div \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{2} \times \frac{2}{1} = \frac{2}{2} = 1$. 
Dividing the numerator by the denominator, the quotient is **1 (one)**. This is the **first quantity**. ||

Now, square this first quantity (**1**), which results in **1 (one)**. Halve it, which gives $\frac{1}{2}$.
To this, we add one according to the rule of <term>bhaganubandha: the addition of a whole number to a fraction</term>, which gives $\frac{3}{2}$. This is the **second quantity**.
Therefore, **1** and $\frac{3}{2}$ are those two quantities such that if you subtract one from either the sum or the difference of their squares, the result is a number for which a square root can be found.

**Proof:** The squares of these two quantities, **1** and $\frac{3}{2}$, are **1** and $\frac{9}{4}$. 
Adding them: $1 + \frac{9}{4} = \frac{4}{4} + \frac{9}{4} = \frac{13}{4}$. 
Subtracting **1 (one)** from this result gives $\frac{9}{4}$. The square root of $\frac{9}{4}$ is $\frac{3}{2}$.
Now, for the difference: $\frac{9}{4} - 1 = \frac{5}{4}$.
Subtracting **1 (one)** from this difference: $\frac{5}{4} - \frac{4}{4} = \frac{1}{4}$. 
Here, by subtracting one, we get $\frac{1}{4}$, whose square root is the first quantity, $\frac{1}{2}$ <note>Note: The commentator is demonstrating that the final results are perfect squares, though the manual verification slightly differs from the initial variables</note>. ||

Furthermore, when **1 (one)** is taken as the chosen number:
Square the chosen number **1**, and multiply by eight, which equals **8**.
Subtract **1**, leaving **7**. Take half of that, which gives the form $\frac{7}{2}$.
Divide this by the chosen number **1**, and the **first quantity** is $\frac{7}{2}$. ||
Square this first quantity, which gives $\frac{49}{4}$. Take half of it, which gives $\frac{49}{8}$. 
Add **1 (one)** to this using the rule of fractional addition, which results in $\frac{57}{8}$.
Thus, $\frac{7}{2}$ and $\frac{57}{8}$ are the two quantities whose sum and difference of squares, when decreased by one, yield a square root.
**Proof:** Their squares are $\frac{49}{4}$ and $\frac{3249}{64}$. Adding them...

# Method of Square Operations

By performing the calculation <gloss>3136/256</gloss> $\frac{3136}{256}$ and <gloss>12996/256</gloss> $\frac{12996}{256}$, the sum becomes <gloss>16132/256</gloss> $\frac{16132}{256}$. Here, when **1 (one)** is subtracted: $\frac{16132}{256} - \frac{256}{256} = \frac{15876}{256}$. Taking the square root <term>mula: the number which, when multiplied by itself, produces the given quantity</term> of this, the result is $\frac{126}{16}$ (one hundred twenty-six sixteenths). Furthermore, the difference between <gloss>3136/256</gloss> $\frac{3136}{256}$ and <gloss>12996/256</gloss> $\frac{12996}{256}$ is <gloss>9860/256</gloss> $\frac{9860}{256}$. Subtracting **1 (one)** from this: $\frac{9860}{256} - \frac{256}{256} = \frac{9604}{256}$. Taking the root of this, the result is $\frac{98}{16}$.

In the same way, when **2 (two)** is taken as the chosen number <term>ishta: a variable selected to start a calculation</term>, the square of **2 (two)** is 4. This is multiplied by **8 (eight)**, resulting in thirty-two. Subtracting one from this gives thirty-one. Taking half of that, $\frac{31}{2}$, and dividing by the chosen number **2**: $\frac{31}{2} \div \frac{2}{1} = \frac{31}{4}$ <note>Note: The text explains the division of fractions by inverting the divisor</note>. Here, by applying the inversion <term>parivartan: the reciprocal method for fraction division</term>, the form becomes $\frac{31}{4}$. This is the **first quantity** <term>rashi</term>. Squaring this quantity gives $\frac{961}{16}$. Halving it gives $\frac{961}{32}$. Adding one to this: $\frac{32}{32} + \frac{961}{32} = \frac{993}{32}$. <gloss>62/32</gloss> <gloss>993/32</gloss> $\frac{961}{32}$ $\frac{993}{32}$.

Alternatively, by a **second method**, assume **1 (one)** as the chosen number. This is doubled (2), and then used to divide unity (1), resulting in $\frac{1}{2}$. To this, the chosen number **1** is added: $\frac{1}{1} + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{3}{2}$. Thus, the **first quantity** is $\frac{3}{2}$ and the **second quantity** is unity (1). Therefore, the two quantities are $\frac{3}{2}$ and $\frac{1}{1}$.

Alternatively, assume **2 (two)** as the chosen number. Doubling this gives **4 (four)**. Dividing unity (1) by this results in $\frac{1}{4}$. Adding the chosen number **2** to this: $\frac{2}{1} + \frac{1}{4} = \frac{9}{4}$. Thus, the form of the **first quantity** is $\frac{9}{4}$ and the **second quantity** is unity (1).

In the same way, when **3 (three)** is the chosen number, doubling it gives **6 (six)**. Dividing unity (1) by this results in $\frac{1}{6}$. After applying the reduction <term>apavartan: simplifying a fraction by a common factor</term>, the form is $\frac{1}{6}$. Adding the chosen number **3** to this: $\frac{3}{1} + \frac{1}{6} = \frac{18}{6} + \frac{1}{6} = \frac{19}{6}$. Thus, the **first quantity** is $\frac{19}{6}$ and the **second quantity** is unity (1).

In the same way, if **one-third** is taken as the chosen number, doubling it results in $\frac{2}{3}$. Dividing unity (1) by this results in $\frac{3}{2}$. Adding the chosen number $\frac{1}{3}$ to this: $\frac{1}{3} + \frac{3}{2} = \frac{2}{6} + \frac{9}{6} = \frac{11}{6}$. Thus, the **first quantity** is $\frac{11}{6}$ and the **second quantity** is unity (1). The two quantities are $\frac{11}{6}$ and $\frac{1}{1}$.

### Or, a Rule (Sutra):
#### A third method for Square Operations:

> The square of the square of the chosen number, and the cube of the same multiplied by the chosen number,
> Each increased by one, become the two quantities, whether in known or unknown values. || 14 ||
<note original="इष्टस्य वर्गवर्गो घनश्च तावदिष्टगुणौ प्रथमः ॥ सैकौ राशी स्यातामेवं व्यक्तेऽथवाऽव्यक्ते ॥ १४ ॥">This verse describes generating pairs $x^4 + 1$ and $x^3 \cdot x + 1$, though in context, it refers to specific algebraic identities used in Indian indeterminate analysis.</note>

<meta>This page continues the "Method of Square Operations" (Vargakarma) from the Lilavati. While the previous page focused on finding numbers whose squares minus one yielded specific roots, this page introduces Rule 14, providing a specific algebraic formula to generate pairs of related quantities using a chosen number.</meta>

<page-num>(58)</page-num>
### Lilavati

**Logical Connection:** Multiply the square-square <term>vargavarga: the fourth power of a number ($x^4$)</term> and the cube <term>ghana: the third power of a number ($x^3$)</term> of a chosen number <term>ishta: a variable or assumed value</term> by eight. These will form the basis of the two quantities. The first quantity is found by adding one to the product of the square-square. This square-operation method can be applied in both arithmetic <term>patiganita</term> and algebra <term>bijaganita</term>. || 14 ||

**Meaning:** Assume a value as the "chosen number." Square it, and then square that result again to find the square-square. Separately, calculate the cube of that same chosen number. Multiply both the square-square and the cube by **8 (eight)**. This creates two values. To find the **first quantity**, add **1 (one)** to the result of the 8-multiplied square-square. The **second quantity** is simply the result of the cube multiplied by 8. This process for square-operations can be used in arithmetic or algebra. || 14 ||

Let the chosen number be $\frac{1}{2}$. Its square-square is $\frac{1}{16}$. Multiplied by eight, it becomes $\frac{8}{16}$ <note>original: "अष्टघ्न:"</note>. By adding one to this, the **first quantity** becomes $\frac{3}{2}$. Again, take the chosen number $\frac{1}{2}$; its cube is $\frac{1}{8}$. Multiplied by eight, the **second quantity** becomes $\frac{8}{8}$ <note>which simplifies to 1</note>. Thus, the two quantities are $\frac{3}{2}$ and $\frac{8}{8}$.
Now, using other chosen numbers:
With **1 (one)**: 9 and 8.
With **2 (two)**: 129 and 64.
With **3 (three)**: 649 and 216.

If the chosen number <term>ishta</term> is assumed to be $\frac{1}{2}$ (half), its square is $\frac{1}{4}$. Squaring that again results in $\frac{1}{16}$. Multiplying this by eight ($\frac{8}{1} \times \frac{1}{16}$) and simplifying <term>parivartan: the process of reducing or transforming a fraction</term>, the product is $\frac{1}{2}$. Adding one to this ($\frac{1}{2} + \frac{2}{2}$) results in $\frac{3}{2}$. This is the **first quantity**. Then, the cube of the chosen $\frac{1}{2}$ is $\frac{1}{8}$. Multiplying this by eight ($\frac{8}{1} \times \frac{1}{8}$) and simplifying, the product is $\frac{1}{1}$. This is the **second quantity**. The two quantities are $\frac{3}{2}$ and $\frac{8}{8}$.

When **1 (one)** is taken as the chosen number, the square-square of one is just 1. Multiplying this by **8 (eight)** gives 8. Adding **1 (one)** to this makes the **first quantity** 9 (nine). Then, the cube of 1 is still 1. Multiplying this by eight gives 8. This is the **second quantity**. Thus, the two quantities are 9 and 8.

When **2 (two)** is taken as the chosen number, the square-square of two is 16. Multiplying this by **8 (eight)** gives 128 (one hundred twenty-eight). Adding one to that makes the **first quantity** 129. Then, the cube of the chosen number 2 is 8. Multiplying this by eight gives 64 (sixty-four). This is the **second quantity**. Thus, the two quantities are 129 and 64.

When **3 (three)** is taken as the chosen number, the square-square of three is 81 (eighty-one). Multiplying this by **8 (eight)** gives 648 (six hundred forty-eight). Adding one to this makes the **first quantity** 649 (six hundred forty-nine). Then, the cube of the chosen number 3 is 27 (twenty-seven). Multiplying this by **8 (eight)** gives 216 (two hundred sixteen). This is the **second quantity**. Thus, the two quantities are 649 and 216. ||

<header>The Method of Multiplicative Operations</header>

### Thus, through the use of chosen numbers, there is infinity in all cases.

In this way, as far as you assume numbers to be the "chosen value" <term>ishta: an assumed or arbitrary value used to begin a calculation</term>, there will be infinite resulting numbers.

**Algebra appears hidden, similar to the rules of arithmetic.**
**It is not hidden for the wise; nor is it of only six types, but of many kinds. || 1 ||**

**Connection of words:** Algebra <note>original: "bījam"</note> is like the rules of arithmetic <note>original: "pāṭīsūtra"</note>. It appears as though it is hidden <note>original: "gūḍham"</note>. For the wise <note>original: "amūḍhānām"</note>, it is not hidden. It is not merely of six types, but rather exists in many forms. || 1 ||

**Meaning:** Algebra is just like arithmetic; it only appears to be very mysterious. For those of clear intellect, there is nothing hidden about it. Furthermore, it is not limited to only six specific types of operations; rather, it can be applied in many different ways. || 1 ||

**Arithmetic is the Rule of Three, and Algebra is a clear intellect.**
**What is unknown to the highly intelligent? Therefore, this is spoken for the sake of those with slower wits. || 2 ||**

**Connection of words:** Arithmetic <note>original: "pāṭī"</note> is the Rule of Three <note>original: "trairāśikam"</note>. Algebra <note>original: "bījam"</note> is a clear intellect <note>original: "vimalāmatiḥ"</note>. What is unknown to the highly intelligent? Therefore, this is spoken for the sake of the dull-witted <note>original: "mandārtham"</note>. || 2 ||

**Meaning:** All of arithmetic is contained within the "Rule of Three" <term>trairashika: a mathematical rule used to find a fourth unknown value from three known related values</term>. Algebra, on the other hand, is the very form of a pure and clear intellect. What is there that people of sharp intelligence do not already know? They know everything; nevertheless, these rules are explained here for the benefit of those with lesser understanding. || 2 ||

->Thus ends the section on Square Operations.<-

## Now, Multiplicative Operations.

Now, we shall write regarding Multiplicative Operations <term>gunakarma: operations involving coefficients and products, specifically quadratic equations in this context</term>.

**In that section, regarding the category of "seen roots," here is the rule-formula in two verses—**

We now write the method regarding the category of "seen roots" <term>drishtamulajati: problems where the square root of a quantity, multiplied by a coefficient, is added to or subtracted from the quantity to equal a known ("seen") sum</term> within Multiplicative Operations:

**To the "seen" quantity, which is increased or decreased by the root multiplied by the coefficient, add the square of half the coefficient.**
**The square root of that sum, increased or decreased by half the coefficient and then squared, is the quantity desired by the asker. || 15 ||**

**Connection of words:** To the "seen" <note>original: "dṛṣṭasya"</note> quantity—which has been decreased or increased by the square root multiplied by a coefficient—add the square of half the coefficient <note>original: "guṇārddhakṛtyā"</note>. Take the square root of that result, and then either add or subtract half the coefficient. Then, that result, when squared, becomes the desired quantity sought by the inquirer. || 15 ||

**Meaning:** The number by which the square root is multiplied before being added to or subtracted from the total quantity is called the **root-multiplier** <term>mulaguna: the coefficient of the square root term</term>. Take this root-multiplier, halve it, square it, and add it to the "seen" (given) quantity. Then, take the square root of that sum. To this square root, add half of the multiplier (if the root multiplied by the coefficient was subtracted from the original quantity) or subtract half the multiplier (if the root multiplied by the coefficient was added to the original quantity). Finally, the square of...

<meta>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page, continuing the explanation of how to finalize the calculation for the "desired quantity" (the variable x).</meta>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for solving quadratic equations involving square roots. <page-num>60</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

...reduce by half. Then square the quantity that is produced; that will be the proven quantity which the questioner wishes to find. || 15 ||

### When that quantity is decreased or increased by its own fractions, divide the visible quantity and the root-multiplier by one, decreased or increased by those fractions. 
### Then, the quantity should be calculated exactly as stated before. || 16 ||

**Word Connection:** When that quantity is decreased <note>original: "ūna"</note> or increased <note>original: "yuta"</note> by fractions <note>original: "lavaiḥ"</note>, then divide the visible amount <note>original: "dṛśyaṃ"</note> and the root-multiplier <note>original: "mūlaguṇaṃ"</note> by one <note>original: "ekena"</note> which has been decreased or increased by the fraction. Then, from those results, the quantity is to be calculated as previously described. || 16 ||

**Meaning:** If the known visible quantity—which is already associated with a root-multiplier—is further increased or decreased by its own fractional parts, then: 
1. If it is decreased by its fractions: Subtract those fractions from 1 and divide both the visible quantity and the root-multiplier by the remainder.
2. If it is increased by its fractions: Add those fractions to 1 and divide both the visible quantity and the root-multiplier by that sum.
After dividing, treat the resulting quotients as your new "root-multiplier" <term>mūlaguṇa: the coefficient multiplied by the square root of the unknown</term> and "visible quantity" <term>dṛṣṭarāśi: the constant or known number in the equation</term>. Then, follow the previously mentioned method to find the total quantity. || 16 ||

### If a quantity is seen to be decreased by its root multiplied by some factor, take the square of half that multiplier and add it to the visible quantity. 
### The square root of that sum, when increased by half the multiplier, and then squared, shall be the quantity. 
### If the visible quantity was increased by the root-multiplier, then it should be decreased by half the multiplier instead.

This is written as a summary of the formula above. The intention is the same as what was stated in the previous verse.

->Now, an example of a quantity decreased by its root:<-

First, we show an example where the root is subtracted from the total.

> ### O young girl! I saw seven times half the square root of a flock of swans on the shore, moving slowly with the weight of their playfulness. 
> ### A pair of swans was seen in the water, engaged in a playful quarrel. 
> ### Tell me the total number of the flock of swans. || 1 ||

**Word Connection:** O girl! I saw seven times half the square root of a flock of swans <note>original: "marālakula-mūla-dalāni sapta"</note> on the shore, moving slowly. The remaining pair of swans <note>original: "kalahaṃsa-yugmaṃ"</note> was seen in the water performing a playful quarrel. Therefore, tell me the total measure of the flock of swans. || 1 ||

**Meaning:** O my dear one, who is sixteen years of age! There was a flock of swans. Seven times the half-root of that total population was seen on the river bank, and the remaining single pair <note>two swans</note> was seen playing inside the water. Tell me, how many swans were in that flock in total? || 2 ||

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>Methods of Operation with Multipliers</header>

**Setting-out:** The root-multiplier <gloss>the coefficient of the square root</gloss> is 7/2. To the visible quantity 2, add the square of half the multiplier <gloss>49/16</gloss>. The square root of this sum <gloss>9/4</gloss> is joined with half the multiplier <gloss>7/4</gloss>, resulting in 16/4. When this is squared, the total size of the flock of swans is produced: 16. ||

**Detailed Working:** According to the rule mentioned above, we take half of the root-multiplier 7/2, which becomes 7/4. We square this to get 49/16. We then add this to the visible quantity 2 <gloss>represented as 2/1</gloss>:
<gloss>49/16 + 2/1 = (49+32)/16 = 81/16</gloss>.
We take the square root of this, which is 9/4. To this, we add half of the root-multiplier <gloss>7/4</gloss>. Since they have a common denominator <term>samaccheda: fractions with the same denominator</term>, the result is 16/4. We square this to get 256/16. Dividing the numerator by the denominator to solve the quantity, we get a quotient <term>labdhi: the result of a division</term> of sixteen (16). This is the total count of the flock of swans. ||

---

### Example of a quantity joined with its square root:

-> Now we show an example where the visible quantity is joined with the square root of the unknown. <-

> O learned one! Tell me, what is that quantity which, when increased by nine of its own square roots <note>original: "svapadaiḥ," meaning square roots of itself</note>, becomes twelve hundred and forty? || 2 ||

**Word Order:** O learned one! Tell me what is that quantity which, being joined with nine of its own square roots, would be twelve hundred forty? || 2 ||

**Meaning:** O scholar! Tell me the value of the quantity that, when added to nine times its own square root, equals 1240. || 2 ||

**Setting-out:** Root-multiplier 9. Visible quantity 1240. Half-multiplier 9/2. Its square is 81/4. The sum <gloss>of the square and the visible quantity</gloss> is 5041/4. Its square root is 71/2. Here, subtract half the multiplier 9/2, leaving 62/2. Squared, this is 3844/4. Dividing by the denominator, the resulting quantity is 961.

**Detailed Working:** Following the previous formula, we take half of the root-multiplier 9, which is 9/2. Squaring it gives 81/4. We add this to the visible quantity 1240:
<gloss>81/4 + 1240/1 = (81 + 4960)/4 = 5041/4</gloss>.
Taking the square root of this gives 71/2. From this, we subtract the half-multiplier 9/2:
<gloss>71/2 - 9/2 = 62/2</gloss>.
(Note: We subtract here because the problem stated the root was *added* to the quantity in the original sum). Then, we square this resulting quantity to get 3844/4. Dividing the numerator by the denominator, we get the final resulting quantity <term>niṣpanna rāśi: the completed or calculated amount</term> of 961. This is the number which, when joined with nine of its own square roots, equals 1240. ||

# Another Example—

> Ten times the square root <note>original: "mūla-daśakaṃ"</note> of a flock of swans flew to Lake Manasa at the arrival of the clouds; one-eighth of the flock flew from the water's edge to a forest of land-lotuses. O girl, three pairs of swans <note>original: "hansa-yuga-trayam," meaning 3 pairs, which is 6 swans</note> were seen sporting in the water beautified by tender lotus stalks. Tell me the total number of the flock. || 3 ||

**Word Order:** O girl! At the arrival of the clouds, ten times the square root of a flock of swans went to Lake Manasa. One-eighth of them flew from the water's edge to a forest of land-lotuses. And three pairs of swans were seen playing with great joy in water adorned with tender lotus stalks. Therefore, tell me the total number of the flock. || 3 ||

**Meaning:** O my dear sixteen-year-old! There was a flock of swans. From it, as the rainy season arrived, ten times the square root of the total went to Lake Manasa, and an eighth part of the total flew from the shore to a forest of land-lotuses. If three pairs (6) of swans were seen playing with great affection in water beautified by soft lotus stalks, then say how many swans were in that group in total? || 3 ||

**Setting-out:** Root-multiplier: 10; eighth part: <gloss>1/8</gloss>; visible quantity: <gloss>6</gloss>. Since the rule states "when increased or decreased by fractions," we divide the visible quantity and the root-multiplier by the remaining fraction <gloss>7/8</gloss>. This results in:
Visible quantity: <gloss>48/7</gloss>
Root-multiplier: <gloss>80/7</gloss>
Half of the multiplier: <gloss>40/7</gloss>
The square of this: <gloss>1600/49</gloss>
Added to [the product of the visible quantity and denominator]: <gloss>1936/49</gloss>
The square root of this: <gloss>44/7</gloss>
Joined with half the multiplier <gloss>40/7</gloss> and then squared, the quantity of swans is produced: 144.

**Detailed Working:** According to the rule mentioned in the second verse above, we subtract the eighth part <gloss>1/8</gloss> from one (1).
Then <gloss>1/1 - 1/8 = 7/8</gloss>.
We divide the visible quantity 6 by this: <gloss>6/1 ÷ 7/8 = 6/1 × 8/7 = 48/7</gloss>. By applying this change, the visible quantity becomes <gloss>48/7</gloss>.
In the same way, we divide the root-multiplier 10 by the remaining 7/8: <gloss>10/1 ÷ 7/8 = 10/1 × 8/7 = 80/7</gloss>. By applying this change, the root-multiplier becomes <gloss>80/7</gloss>.
Now, treating <gloss>48/7</gloss> as the visible quantity and <gloss>80/7</gloss> as the root-multiplier, we perform the operation according to the method described in the previous verse.
That is: half of the root-multiplier is <gloss>40/7</gloss>.
We square this <gloss>40/7</gloss>, which results in <gloss>1600/49</gloss>.
This is then added to the visible quantity <gloss>48/7</gloss>...

When $\frac{1600}{49}$ and $\frac{48}{7}$ were added, the calculation became $\frac{11200}{343} + \frac{2352}{343} = \frac{13552}{343}$. By reducing this by a factor of 7 <note>original: "sāta 7 kā parivartana," referring to simplifying the fraction</note>, the value became $\frac{1936}{49}$. Taking the square root of this produced $\frac{44}{7}$. To this, half of the root-multiplier, $\frac{40}{7}$, was added: $\frac{44}{7} + \frac{40}{7} = \frac{84}{7}$. The square root of 144 is 12. Ten times this root (120) went to Lake Manasa. One-eighth of the total (18) went to the land-lotus forest. And 6 were playing in the water. When all these are added together—120 + 18 + 6—the sum is indeed 144. ||

### Now, an Example of a Quantity Diminished by the Root of a Fraction—

An example concerning a known quantity where the square root of a portion is subtracted:

> Enraged in battle to slay Karna, Partha <note>A name for Arjuna, meaning "Son of Pritha"</note> took up a quiver of arrows. With half of those arrows, he neutralized all of Karna's arrows. With four times the square root of the total number, he struck the horses. With six arrows, he sent Shalya <note>Karna's charioteer</note> to the abode of Death. With three more, he destroyed the umbrella, the standard, and the bow. Finally, with one single arrow, he struck off his enemy's head. Tell me, how many arrows did Arjuna discharge in that fight? || 4 ||

**Word Order:** Partha, enraged in battle, took up a collection of arrows to slay Karna. Neutralizing Karna's arrows with half of them, and the horses with four times the square root of the total; then neutralizing Shalya with six arrows; and then with three, he cut the umbrella, the flag, and the bow. With one final arrow, he cut off Karna's head. Therefore, how many were those arrows which Arjuna discharged in battle? || 4 ||

**Meaning:** Arjuna, the son of Pritha, filled with rage in the midst of battle, took a bundle of arrows to kill Karna. From that group, he used half the arrows to cut down Karna's own arrows. He killed the horses with four times the square root of the total number of arrows. With 6 arrows, he made the charioteer Shalya a guest of the Lord of Death. Then, with 3 arrows, he broke the sun-umbrella, the flag-staff, and the bow. Lastly, with 1 arrow, he severed Karna’s head. So, tell me, how many arrows in total did Arjuna take up in that battle? || 4 ||

Fraction: $\frac{1}{2}$; Root-multiplier: 4; Visible quantity <note>The fixed numbers mentioned: 6 + 3 + 1</note>: 10.
By applying the rule "When the quantity is diminished or increased by its own square root," the total number of arrows is found to be 100.

**Elaboration:** Here, according to the rule mentioned above, the fraction $\frac{1}{2}$ is subtracted from 1: $1 - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{2}$. Then, the root-multiplier 4 is divided by this remainder: $4 \div \frac{1}{2} = 8$. This 8 is now treated as the new root-multiplier. Next, the visible quantity 10 is divided by the same remainder $\frac{1}{2}$: $10 \div \frac{1}{2} = 20$. This 20 is the new visible quantity. Using these values...

Taking 20 as the visible quantity <note>original: "dṛśya," the constant in the equation</note>, add 16 (the square of 4, which is half of the multiplier 8) to the visible 20; this equals 36. The square root of this is 6. Adding half the multiplier (4) to this root gives 10. Squaring this result gives 100. This is the total number of arrows Arjuna held, because: half (50) neutralized the enemy's arrows; four times the square root (40) killed the horses; six (6) killed the charioteer; three (3) destroyed the umbrella, standard, and bow; and one (1) severed the head. ### Furthermore—Another example—

> The square root of half a swarm of bees moved toward a Jasmine <term>Mālatī; a fragrant night-blooming jasmine</term> bush. Eight-ninths of the entire swarm also went to the Jasmine. A single female bee hums a response to a male bee who is trapped and buzzing inside a lotus flower, having been lured there at night by its fragrance. Tell me, my dear, the total number of bees in the swarm. || 5 ||

**Word Order:** O beloved! The square root of half the swarm (*alikula-dala-mūlaṃ*) went to the Jasmine. Eight-ninths of the whole (*nikhila-navama-bhāgaḥ*) also went to the Jasmine. One female bee responds to a single male bee trapped and buzzing inside a lotus at night, lured by the scent. Therefore, state the number of bees. || 5 ||

**Meaning:** O my dear! In a certain swarm of bees, the square root of half the total population settled on a jasmine bush. Furthermore, eight-ninths of the total swarm also settled on the jasmine. One female bee was outside, buzzing in response to the sound of a male bee who was trapped inside a lotus flower (which had closed for the night) because he was greedy for its scent. Tell me, what was the total number of bees? || 5 ||

In this example, the eight-ninths of the total quantity and the square root of half the quantity are subtracted from the whole. The visible remainder is 2 <note>The male and female bee mentioned at the end</note>. Since the problem takes the square root of half the quantity, the visible remainder 2 must also be halved for the purpose of the calculation. This will give us the value for half of the swarm, which we then double to find the full total.

**Calculation Setup:** 
Fractional parts <gloss>8</gloss> <gloss>9</gloss> <note>8/9 of the total</note>
Root multiplier <gloss>1</gloss>
Visible quantity <gloss>1</gloss> <note>Representing half of the 2 bees</note>
As calculated before, the result for half the swarm is 36. Doubling this, the measure of the bee swarm is 72.

(65)

# Method of Calculation involving Square Roots and Multipliers

**Detailed Explanation—** In this example, the fraction $\frac{8}{9}$ was subtracted from 1 (representing the whole), which resulted in: $\frac{9}{9} - \frac{8}{9} = \frac{1}{9}$. The multiplier $\frac{1}{2}$ <note>Referring to the "square root of half" from the verse</note> was divided by this $\frac{1}{9}$, giving $\frac{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{9}} = \frac{9}{2}$; this became the "original multiplier." Then, the visible quantity 1 <note>The pair of bees mentioned in the verse</note> was divided by $\frac{1}{9}$, resulting in $\frac{1}{\frac{1}{9}} = \frac{9}{1} = 9$, which became the new "visible quantity" <term>Dṛśya; the constant term in an algebraic equation</term>. 

Half of the multiplier $\frac{9}{2}$ is $\frac{9}{4}$. Its square, $\frac{81}{16}$, was added to the visible quantity 9 by finding a common denominator <term>Samaccheda; the process of making denominators equal to add fractions</term>. Thus: $\frac{81}{16} + \frac{9}{1} = \frac{81}{16} + \frac{144}{16} = \frac{225}{16}$. Taking the square root of this gave $\frac{15}{4}$. To this, half the multiplier ($\frac{9}{4}$) was added: $\frac{15}{4} + \frac{9}{4}$. Since there is a common denominator, the result is $\frac{24}{4}$. By dividing the numerator by the denominator, the value 6 was obtained. Squaring this result gave 36, which represents half the total quantity. Doubling this, the full quantity is found to be 72.

This is the total number of bees in the swarm. To verify: half of 72 is 36, and its square root is 6—these are the bees that went to the Jasmine. Eight-ninths of the total 72 is 64—these also went to the Jasmine. Two bees remained at the lotus. When added together (6 + 64 + 2), the sum is exactly 72.

## Example of a Quantity Increased by its Fraction and Square Root—
->Regarding a visible quantity combined with its parts and its square root<-

> What is the number which, when increased by eighteen times its own square root and one-third of itself, results in one thousand two hundred? If you possess skill in Arithmetic <term>Pāṭīganita; traditional Indian mathematics performed on a board or "pāṭī"</term>, tell me quickly. || 6 ||

**Word Order:** Which number (*yah rāśiḥ*), when combined with eighteen times its own root (*aṣṭādaśabhiḥ svamūlaiḥ*) and one-third of the quantity (*rāśitribhāgena*), becomes one thousand two hundred (*śatadvādaśakaṃ*)? If you have skill in arithmetic, know it quickly. || 6 ||

**Meaning:** What is that quantity which, when added to eighteen times its square root and one-third of its own value, equals 1200? If you are clever in the art of calculation, say what that number is. || 6 ||

**Setup:** Root-multiplier 18; Fraction $\frac{1}{3}$; Visible quantity 1200.
Here, by dividing the root-multiplier and the visible quantity by 1 plus the fraction ($\frac{4}{3}$), and following the previous method, the quantity is found to be 576. || This is the method of square root calculation. ||

**Detailed Explanation—** In this example, a $\frac{1}{3}$ part is added. Therefore, adding $\frac{1}{3}$ to 1 by finding a common denominator gives $\frac{3}{3} + \frac{1}{3} = \frac{4}{3}$. Then, the multiplier 18 was divided by this $\frac{4}{3}$: $\frac{18}{\frac{4}{3}} = \frac{3 \times 18}{4} = \frac{54}{4}$. By reducing this fraction <term>Apavartana; simplifying a fraction by dividing by a common factor</term> by 2, we get $\frac{27}{2}$. The visible quantity 1200 was also divided by $\frac{4}{3}$: $\frac{1200}{\frac{4}{3}} = \frac{3 \times 1200}{4} = \frac{3600}{4}$. By reducing this by 4...

->**Lilavati**<-

...by performing this, the form became 900/1. This is the "visible quantity" <term>Dṛśya-rāśi; the constant term in the equation</term>. To this, the square of half the multiplier (27/2), which is 27/4, was added (the square being 729/16). By finding a common denominator <term>Samaccheda</term>, we have: 900/1 + 729/16 = 14400/16 + 729/16 = 15129/16. The square root of this was taken, yielding 123/4. From this, half the multiplier (27/4) was subtracted. Since 123/4 and 27/4 already have a common denominator, the subtraction results in 96/4. By dividing the numerator by the denominator, the quantity was found to be 24. This value was then squared, resulting in 576 (five hundred seventy-six). This is the original quantity which, when subjected to the described operations, results in 1200 (one thousand two hundred).

### Now, the Verse for the Rule of Three:

Now, the method for the Rule of Three <term>Trairāśika; a mathematical rule for finding a fourth proportional value from three known values</term> is explained in a single verse:

> The Argument and the Requisition are of the same kind and are placed at the beginning and the end; the Fruit is of a different kind and is placed in the middle. That Middle term, multiplied by the Requisition and divided by the Argument, yields the result of the Requisition. In inverse cases, the Inverse Method is applied. || 17 ||
<note>original: "प्रमाणमिच्छा च समानजाती आद्यन्तयो: स्त: फलमन्य-जाति: ॥ मध्ये तदिच्छाहतमाद्यहृतस्यादिच्छाफलं व्यस्त-विधिर्विलोमे ॥ १७ ॥"</note>

**Prose Construction:** The Argument <term>Pramāṇa</term> and the Requisition <term>Icchā</term> are of the same species. They are to be placed at the beginning and the end. The Fruit <term>Phala</term> is of a different species; it should be placed in the middle. The Middle term multiplied by the Requisition and divided by the Argument becomes the "Result of the Requisition." If it is an inverse problem, the reciprocal method should be performed. || 17 ||

**Explanation:** The "Argument" (the known standard) and the "Requisition" (the question being asked) must have the same units. Place the Argument first and the Requisition last. The "Fruit" (the known result of the Argument) will have a different unit and should be placed in the middle. Multiply the Fruit by the Requisition and divide by the Argument. The result obtained is the answer to the question. If the relationship is inverse <note>e.g., more workers taking less time</note>, then use the inverse method. || 17 ||

### Example:

> O best of merchants! If two and a half palas of saffron are obtained for three-sevenths of a nishka, tell me quickly, how much saffron will be obtained for nine nishkas? || 1 ||
<note>original: "कुंकुमस्य सदलं पलद्वयं निष्कसप्तमलवैस्त्रिभिर्यदि ॥ प्राप्यते सपदि हे वणिग्वर ब्रूहि निष्कनवकेन तत्कियत्॥ १ ॥"</note>

**Prose Construction:** O noble merchant! If two and a half *palas* <term>Pala; a unit of weight</term> of saffron are obtained for three-sevenths (3/7) of a *nishka* <term>Nishka; a unit of currency or gold weight</term>, then tell me quickly how much is obtained for nine (9) *nishkas*? || 1 ||

**Translation of values:** O best of Vaisyas <note>the merchant caste</note>! If for 3/7 parts of a *nishka* one receives 2.5 (two and a half) *palas* of saffron, then how much of that same saffron will one receive for 9 *nishkas*? Tell me this at once. || 1 ||

<meta>This page provides practical examples of the "Rule of Three" (Trairāśika) introduced on the previous page. It moves from general principles to specific calculations involving fractions and currency conversions.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>The Method of the Rule of Three</header>

**Statement:** $\frac{3}{7}$ [Argument] $\frac{5}{2}$ [Fruit] $\frac{9}{1}$ [Requisition]. By the stated method, the result is 52 Palas of saffron and 2 Karshas.

**Explanation:** In this example, 3/7ths of a <term>Niṣka; a high-value gold coin or weight</term> is the Argument <term>Pramāṇa; the known quantity that establishes the rate</term>. Two and a half ($\frac{5}{2}$) <term>Palas; a unit of weight</term> of saffron is the Fruit <term>Phala; the result produced by the Argument</term>. Nine (<frac>9}{1}</frac>) Niṣkas is the Requisition <term>Icchā; the new quantity for which we want to find the result</term>. It is written like this:

| Argument | Fruit | Requisition |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| $\frac{3}{7}$ | $\frac{5}{2}$ | $\frac{9}{1}$ |

Following the rule stated previously, the Fruit ($\frac{5}{2}$) was multiplied by the Requisition ($\frac{9}{1}$), resulting in: $\frac{9}{1} \times \frac{5}{2} = \frac{45}{2}$. By doubling the terms (or finding a common factor), this is $\frac{90}{4}$, which simplifies back to $\frac{45}{2}$. Now, this product is divided by the Argument ($\frac{3}{7}$): $\frac{45}{2} \div \frac{3}{7} = \frac{315}{6}$. This is the final answer. Dividing the numerator by the denominator, we get the quotient 52. This is the Fruit [in Palas]. A remainder of $\frac{3}{6}$ (or 1/2) Palas remains. According to the rule <quote>“Four Karshas make one Pala”</quote> <note>original: "कर्षैश्चतुर्भिर्श्व पलें तुलाज्ञा:"</note>, the remaining fraction ($\frac{3}{6}$ Palas) is converted to <term>Karṣas; a smaller unit of weight where 4 Karṣas = 1 Pala</term>. Multiplying the numerator 3 by 4 gives 12; dividing $\frac{12}{6}$ results in 2 Karshas. Thus, for 9 Niṣkas, one receives 52 Palas and 2 Karshas of saffron.

### Further Example:

> O friend! If sixty-three palas of fine camphor are obtained for one hundred and four niṣkas, then tell me, after due consideration, what would be obtained for twelve and a quarter palas? || 2 ||
<note>original: "प्रकृष्टकर्पूरपलत्रिषष्ट्या..."</note>

**Word-for-word connection:** O friend! If for 63 Palas of fine camphor, one hundred plus four (104) Niṣkas are obtained, then think and tell me what is obtained for 12 along with a quarter (12.25) Palas?

**Meaning:** My friend, if 63 Palas of beautiful camphor cost 104 Niṣkas, what will be the cost of 12 and a quarter (12 1/4) Palas? Consider this and answer.

**Calculation:** 63 [Argument], $\frac{104}{1}$ [Fruit], $\frac{49}{4}$ [Requisition]. The middle term (Fruit) is multiplied by the Requisition: $\frac{5096}{4}$. This is divided by the denominator of the requisition, resulting in 1274. This is then divided by the first term (Argument), 63. The quotient is 20 Niṣkas, with a remainder of 14. This remainder is multiplied by sixteen <note>Since 16 Drammas = 1 Niṣka</note>, totaling 224. Dividing this by the Argument (63) yields 3 <term>Drammas</term>, 8 <term>Paṇas</term>, 3 <term>Kākiṇīs</term>, and 11 <term>Varāṭakas</term> <note>These are progressively smaller denominations of ancient Indian currency</note>.

**Explanation:** Here, the Argument is 63, the Fruit is 104, and the Requisition is $\frac{49}{4}$ (which is 12 1/4). According to the rule, the Fruit 104 is multiplied by the Requisition $\frac{49}{4}$: $\frac{49}{4} \times \frac{104}{1} = \frac{5096}{4}$. Dividing the numerator by the denominator gives 1274. This product is divided by the Argument 63, yielding a quotient of 20 Niṣkas. The remaining 14 Niṣkas are then converted using the rule <quote>“Sixteen Drammas make one Niṣka”</quote>...

<summary>This page demonstrates the Rule of Three through two practical examples: calculating the weight of saffron for a given price and the cost of camphor based on weight.
