<meta>This page continues the specific calculation for the price of camphor from page 67 and introduces a new word problem involving the weight of rice using the "Rule of Three" (Trairāśika). The author demonstrates how to convert between different denominations of currency and volume.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
<header>Līlāvatī</header>

Multiplying [the remainder] by 16 to convert to <term>Drammas; a silver coin, 16 of which make one Niṣka</term>, we get 224. Dividing this by the Argument [63], the quotient is 3 Drammas, with a remainder of 35. Following the rule <quote>“Those [Drammas] are known here to be sixteen [to a Paṇa]”</quote> <note>original: "ते षोडश द्रम्म इहावगम्यः"</note>, we multiply the remainder 35 by 16 to get 560 <term>Paṇas; a copper coin</term>. Dividing this by the Argument 63, the quotient is 8 Paṇas, with 56 remaining. According to the rule <quote>“And those [Paṇas] are four [to a Kākiṇī]”</quote> <note>original: "ताश्च पणश्वतस्रः"</note>, multiplying 56 by 4 gives 224 <term>Kākiṇīs; a small copper coin</term>. Dividing this by 63, the quotient is 3 Kākiṇīs, with 35 remaining. By the rule <quote>“Twenty cowries make one Kākiṇī”</quote> <note>original: "वराटकानां दशकद्वयं यत् सा काकिणी"</note>, multiplying 35 by 20 gives 700 <term>Varāṭakas; cowrie shells used as small change</term>. Dividing this by 63, the quotient is 11 Varāṭakas, and the remainder is $\frac{7}{63}$. Reducing this fraction by its common factor of 7, we get $\frac{1}{9}$. Thus, for 12 and a quarter Palas of camphor, one receives 20 Niṣkas, 3 Drammas, 8 Paṇas, 3 Kākiṇīs, and 11 $\frac{1}{9}$ Varāṭakas.

->And another example:<-

> If one and one-eighth <term>Khārīs; a large unit of dry measure/volume</term> of husked rice can be obtained for two Drammas, tell me quickly, how much would be obtained for seventy Paṇas? || 3 ||

**Connection:** If two Drammas buy a <term>Śāli-taṇḍula-khārikā; a Khārī of husked rice</term> plus an eighth part ($1 \frac{1}{8}$), then what is obtained for seventy Paṇas? Tell this immediately. || 3 ||

**Meaning:** If one and one-eighth Khārīs of rice are obtained for two Drammas, how many will be obtained for 70 Paṇas? Speak quickly. || 3 ||

**Layout:** 
| Argument | Fruit | Requisition |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| $\frac{32}{1}$ | $1 \frac{1}{8}$ | $\frac{70}{1}$ |
**Result:** 2 Khārīs, 7 <term>Droṇas</term>, 1 <term>Āḍhaka</term>, and 2 <term>Prasthas</term>.

->This is the Rule of Three.<-

**Expansion:** Here, the Argument is $\frac{32}{1}$, the Fruit is $1 \frac{1}{8}$, and the Requisition is $\frac{70}{1}$. <note>“In cases where the Argument or the Requisition are of different denominations, they must be converted to the same denomination. For this reason, the Argument of 2 Drammas has been converted into 32 Paṇas, so that the Argument and Requisition are of the same class. This is why 32 Paṇas is written in the place of 2 Drammas.”</note> Here, the Fruit ($1 \frac{1}{8}$ or $\frac{9}{8}$) is multiplied by the Requisition ($\frac{70}{1}$), which results in $\frac{630}{8}$. This is then divided by the Argument ($\frac{32}{1}$): $\frac{630}{8} \div \frac{32}{1} = \frac{630}{8} \times \frac{1}{32} = \frac{630}{256}$. By dividing the numerator by the denominator, we get a quotient of 2 Khārīs, with a remainder of 118. According to the rule <quote>“A Droṇa is indeed the sixteenth part of a Khārī,”</quote> <note>original: "द्रोणस्तु खार्य्याः खलु षोडशांशः"</note> we multiply 118 by 16 to convert to Droṇas, resulting in $\frac{1888}{256}$...

<meta>This page concludes the rice volume calculation from the previous page and introduces the "Inverse Rule of Three" (Vyasta-Trairāśika), where variables move in opposite directions. It transitions from direct proportion to more complex relational math used in commerce and animal husbandry.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>The Method of the Inverse Rule of Three</header>

After performing the reduction <note>the process of simplifying a fraction or ratio</note>, we have <gloss>572 / 64</gloss>. By dividing the numerator by the denominator, a quotient of <gloss>7</gloss> <term>Droṇas; a unit of dry measure</term> was obtained, with <gloss>24</gloss> Droṇas remaining. According to the rule, <quote>“An Āḍhaka is a fourth part of a Droṇa”</quote> <note>original: "स्यादाढको द्रोणचतुर्थभागः"</note>, we multiply the remainder by <gloss>4</gloss> to convert it into Āḍhakas, resulting in <gloss>96</gloss>. Dividing this by 64, we obtain a quotient of <gloss>1</gloss> <term>Āḍhaka; a unit equal to 1/4 of a Droṇa</term>, with <gloss>32</gloss> remaining. By the rule, <quote>“A Prastha is a fourth part of an Āḍhaka here”</quote> <note>original: "प्रस्थश्चतुर्थांश इहाढकस्य"</note>, we multiply by <gloss>4</gloss> to get 128 <term>Prasthas; a smaller unit of volume</term>. Dividing this by <gloss>64</gloss> gives a quotient of <gloss>2</gloss> Prasthas with no remainder. Thus, for <gloss>70</gloss> <term>Paṇas; copper coins</term>, one will receive <gloss>2</gloss> <term>Khārīs; the largest unit of volume mentioned</term>, <gloss>7</gloss> Droṇas, <gloss>1</gloss> Āḍhaka, and <gloss>2</gloss> Prasthas of husked rice.

->Thus ends the Rule of Three.<-

## Now, the Inverse Rule of Three—
->Now the Inverse Rule of Three is described:<-

> When the result decreases as the argument increases, or when the result increases as the argument decreases, the scholars of mathematics should understand that to be the Inverse Rule of Three. || 18 ||

**Syntax:** Where there is a decrease in the result upon an increase in the argument, and an increase in the result upon a decrease in the argument, there the Inverse Rule of Three should be known by those skilled in calculation. || 18 ||

**Meaning:** In cases where an increase in the "desired quantity" leads to a smaller "result," and a decrease in the "desired quantity" leads to a larger "result," those proficient in mathematics must apply the Inverse Rule of Three.

Specifically, the following are the situations where the Inverse Rule of Three occurs:

> The inverse rule of three occurs in the valuation of the age of living beings, in the weight and purity of gold, and in the division of quantities. || 1 ||

**Syntax:** The Inverse Rule of Three occurs in the price regarding the age of living beings, the weight and purity of gold, and in the division of sets of quantities. || 1 ||

**Meaning:** Generally, this inverse calculation is used to determine the price based on the age of animals or servants <note>historically, the value of a laborer or animal often decreased as they aged past their prime</note>, the weight of gold relative to its purity, and when dividing total sums among varying groups. || 1 ||

## Example—

> If a sixteen-year-old woman is worth thirty-two, what is a twenty-year-old woman worth? If an ox that carries two yokes receives four Niṣkas, what does an ox that carries six yokes receive?

**Syntax:** If a <gloss>16</gloss> sixteen-year-old woman is worth <gloss>32</gloss> thirty-two, then what does a <gloss>20</gloss> twenty-year-old woman fetch? If a <gloss>2</gloss> two-yoke-bearing ox receives <gloss>4</gloss> four Niṣkas, then what does a <gloss>6</gloss> six-yoke-bearing ox receive? || 1 ||

**Meaning:** If a woman aged sixteen years is valued at <gloss>32</gloss> thirty-two rupees, then what will be the value of a woman aged <gloss>20</gloss> twenty years? <note>This assumes that as the age increases beyond the peak of 16, the market value decreases</note>. If an ox capable of pulling a two-animal yoke is paid <gloss>4</gloss> <term>Niṣkas; high-value gold or silver coins</term>, what would an ox capable of pulling a six-animal yoke be paid? <note>In this mathematical logic, the "work" or "burden" is distributed, or the value of the individual unit changes inversely with the load capacity</note> || 1 ||

<summary>This page concludes the conversion of rice volume units and introduces the Inverse Rule of Three, explaining its application in commerce where values decrease as quantities or ages increase.</summary>
<keywords>Inverse Rule of Three (Vyasta-Trairāśika), currency conversion, volume units (Droṇa, Āḍhaka, Prastha), commodity valuation, animal husbandry math</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Inverse Rule of Three (Vyasta-Trairāśika), providing practical examples regarding the valuation of livestock/slaves based on age and the distribution of weight among oxen. It then introduces a more complex problem involving gold purity and currency weights.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>Līlāvatī</header>

Layout— 16 | 32 | 20. Result: 25 <gloss>3/5</gloss>
Second Layout— 2 | 4 | 6. Result: 1 <gloss>1/3</gloss>

**Elaboration—** Both of these questions concern the market value of a living being <note>likely referring to the valuation of livestock or domestic servants in a historical context</note>. For this reason, this is a case for the **Inverse Rule of Three**. Therefore, according to the rule mentioned above, since the **Demand** <term>Icchā; the third term in a ratio, or the "asked" quantity</term> of 20 has increased, the result must necessarily decrease. Here, following the method prescribed for the Rule of Three, we multiplied the **Standard** <term>Pramāṇa; the first term or the known base quantity</term> of 16 by the **Fruit** <term>Phala; the second term or the known result of the base</term> of 32. Thus, <insert>32 × 16 = 512</insert>. Having reached this, we divided the product 512 by the **Demand** of 20. This yielded a quotient of 25, and <gloss>3/5</gloss> (three over the divisor of five) remained. Consequently, the price of a twenty-year-old woman <note>medieval mathematical texts often used slaves, cattle, or grain as variables for commerce problems</note> is 25 <gloss>3/5</gloss>.

In the second example as well, as you continue to add oxen to the next two yokes, the load per bag will decrease; therefore, the value will also decrease. Since the result decreases as the **Demand** increases, we follow the method of the **Inverse Rule of Three**. We multiplied the **Standard** of 2 and the **Fruit** of 4, which equals 8. We divided this by the **Demand** (of 6), which gave a quotient of 1, and <gloss>1/3</gloss> (one over the divisor of three) remained. Therefore, the value for the one joined in the sixth yoke is 1 <gloss>1/3</gloss>.

### Example—

->If a Gadyāṇaka weight of gold of ten-karat purity is obtained,<-
->then tell me, how much gold of fifteen-karat purity is obtained for one Niṣka? || 2 ||<-
<note>original: "दशवर्णं सुवर्णं चेद्गद्याणकमवाप्यते ॥ निष्केण तिथिवर्णन्तु तदा वद कियन्मितम् ॥ २ ॥"</note>

**Prose Order:** If ten-purity gold is obtained for one Gadyāṇaka, then how much fifteen-purity gold is obtained for one Niṣka? || 2 ||

If one <term>Niṣka; a denomination of currency or weight</term> buys gold of ten <term>Varṇas; a measure of gold purity similar to karats</term> weighing one <term>Gadyāṇaka; a specific unit of weight</term>, then how much gold of 15 <note>original: "तिथि" (Tithi), a word-numeral for 15 representing the lunar days</note> purity will one receive for one Niṣka? || 2 ||

Layout— 10 | 1 | 15. Result: <gloss>2/3</gloss>

**Elaboration—** Since the price in both instances is one Niṣka, this appears to be a case for the **Rule of Five** <term>Pañcarāśika; a proportion involving five terms</term>. However, since the price is equal (one) on both sides, we discard them, leaving only three quantities. Thus, it remains a **Rule of Three** problem. This concerns the weight of gold, which is a subject of the **Inverse Rule of Three**. Following the previous rule, we performed the inverse operation: we multiplied the **Standard** of 10 and the **Fruit** of 1, which equals 10. Since 10 cannot be divided by the **Demand** of 15, we converted the 10 Gadyāṇakas into <term>Dharaṇas; a smaller unit of weight</term> by multiplying by 2 (following the rule "two Dharaṇas make a Gadyāṇaka"). This resulted in 20. We then divided this by the **Demand** of 15...

# The Method of the Rule of Five and Others

1 was obtained as the quotient in **Dharanas** <term>Dharana; a traditional unit of weight for precious metals</term> and 5 remained; to convert these into **Vallas** <term>Valla; a smaller unit of weight, where 8 Vallas equal 1 Dharana</term> according to the rule "there are eight vallas in a dharana," we multiplied by 8, which resulted in 40. This was divided by the **Demand** <term>Icchā; the third term in a proportion, representing the "asked" quantity</term>, which yielded a quotient of 2 **Vallas** and 10 remained. Following the rule "a valla is three **gunjas**," we multiplied these by 3 to convert them to **Gunjas** <term>Gunja; the smallest weight unit, based on the seed of the Abrus precatorius plant</term>, resulting in 30. This was divided by the **Demand**, yielding a quotient of 2, with no remainder. In this manner, for one **Nishka** <note>a gold coin or specific weight of gold</note> of fifteen-purity gold, the result will be 1 **Dharana**, 2 **Vallas**, and 3 **Gunjas**. <note>The text calculates 30 divided by the demand to get 2, but the final summary mentions 3 Gunjas; this may be a scribal error in the original manuscript's summary line.</note>

->**Example of Dividing a Heap—**<-

->Example regarding the measurement of a heap of grain, etc.—<-

> When a heap of grain was measured with a seven-**adhaka** measure,
> if a hundred measures were produced, then what would it be with a five-**adhaka** measure? || 3 ||
> <note>original Sanskrit: "saptāḍhakena mānena rāśau sasyasya māpite..."</note>

**Connection of words:** If a heap of grain is measured with a measure of seven **adhakas** <term>Adhaka; a dry measure used for grain</term> and results in a count of one hundred, then what would the count be if measured with a five-**adhaka** measure? || 3 ||

**Meaning:** A certain pile of grain was measured using a vessel that holds seven **adhakas**, and it took one hundred fillings to empty the pile. Now, if that same heap is measured with a vessel holding only five **adhakas**, how many fillings will there be? || 3 ||

Layout— 7 | 100 | 5 | Result: 140.

**Elaboration—** Here, the volume of a heap is being divided. Because this is a subject for the **Inverse Rule of Three** <term>Vyasta-Trairāśika</term>, we followed the previously mentioned rule for inverse operations. That is, we multiplied the **Standard** <term>Pramāṇa; the first term</term> of 7 by the **Fruit** <term>Phala; the second term</term> of 100, which equals 700. This was divided by the **Demand** of 5, which yielded a quotient of 140. This is the number of measures that will result from using the five-**adhaka** vessel. <note>As the size of the measuring cup decreases, the number of times you must scoop increases, which is why the "Inverse" rule is applied.</note>

---

->**Thus ends the Compound and Inverse Rule of Three.**<-

->**Now, the procedural verse for the Rule of Five, etc.—**<-

Now, the method for the **Rule of Five** <term>Pañcarāśika</term>, **Rule of Seven** <term>Saptarāśika</term>, **Rule of Nine** <term>Navarāśika</term>, and so on, is written in a single verse:

> In the Rule of Five, Seven, Nine, and so on, perform the exchange of the denominators of the results between the two sides. Having done this, the product of the side with many quantities is divided by the product of the side with fewer quantities; the result is the Fruit. || 19 ||
> <note>original Sanskrit: "pañcasaptanavarāśikādike’nyonyapakṣanayanaṃ phalacchidām..."</note>

**Connection of words:** In the Rule of Five, Seven, Nine, and so forth, after performing the mutual exchange of the denominators of the results between the sides, the product of the "many-quantity" side divided by the product of the "few-quantity" side yields the result. || 19 ||

<meta>This page continues the mathematical lessons from page 71. Having previously discussed the Rule of Three, the author now introduces compound proportions, specifically the "Rule of Five," using an example of calculating interest over time.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
# Lilavati

**Rule:** In the Rule of Five, Rule of Seven, Rule of Nine, and so on, exchange the **Fruit** <term>Phala; the result or benefit in a proportion</term> and the **Denominator** <term>Hara; the divisor</term>—that is, write those from one side onto the other side. Then, divide the product of the side containing more quantities by the product of the side containing fewer quantities. The quotient obtained is the result. || 19 ||

### Example—

> O mathematician, if the interest on one hundred is five for one month,
> tell me, what will it be for sixteen after one year has passed?
> Also, tell me the **Time** given the **Principal** and the **Interest**,
> and calculate the **Principal** amount if the **Time** and **Interest** are known. || 1 ||
> <note>original Sanskrit: "māse śatasya yadi pañca kalāntaraṃ syād..."</note>

**Prose Order:** O Calculator! If the interest <note>original: "kalāntaram"</note> on one hundred for one month is five, then tell me, what will be the result for sixteen after one year has passed? Similarly, calculate the time based on the principal and interest. Furthermore, tell me the principal amount once the time and interest are known. || 1 ||

**Explanation:** O expert in mathematics! If the interest on one hundred **Nishkas** <term>Nishka; a gold coin or unit of currency</term> in one month is 5 Nishkas, then what will be the interest on 16 Nishkas in 1 year (12 months)? This you must say. Furthermore, knowing the principal and the interest, determine the time. That is, if one hundred Nishkas earn 5 Nishkas of interest in 1 month, in how many days will an interest of $48/5$ <note>forty-eight divided by five, or 9.6</note> be earned? Finally, knowing the time and the interest, find the principal. That is, if 100 Nishkas earn 5 Nishkas interest in one month, what principal amount will yield an interest of $48/5$ in one year? || 1 ||

| **Setup:** | 1 (Month) | 12 (Months) | **Transposing Sides** | **Setup:** | 1 | 12 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| | 100 (Principal) | 16 (Principal) | | | 100 | 16 |
| | 5 (Interest) | | | | | 5 |

Product of the side with more quantities: 960 <note>calculated as 12 × 16 × 5</note>
Product of the side with fewer quantities: 100 <note>calculated as 1 × 100</note>
Dividing by this, the quotient is: 9
The remainder is $\frac{60}{100}$; reducing by twenty, we get $\frac{3}{5}$
The resulting interest is 9 $\frac{3}{5}$
<gloss>$\frac{48}{5}$</gloss>
By converting the mixed fraction into an improper fraction, we get $\frac{48}{5}$

**Elaboration—** Here there are 5 quantities; for this reason, this is a case for the **Rule of Five** <term>Pañcarāśika; a method for solving proportions involving five known values to find a sixth</term>. The standard setup here is: if in one month, for 100, the interest is five—this is the **First Side** <term>Pūrvapakṣa; the "argument" or known set of terms</term>...

<summary>This page introduces the "Rule of Five" (compound proportion) and provides a practical example involving the calculation of interest (Kalantara) on a principal amount (Nishkas) over a specific time period.</summary>
<keywords>Rule of Five, Pancharashika, interest calculation, Kalantara, Nishka, principal, mathematics, Lilavati, Bhaskara</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstrations from page 72, focusing on the practical application of the "Rule of Five" (Pancharashika). After establishing the rule for finding interest, the text now walks through two specific problems: first, calculating the interest amount, and second, determining the time required to earn a specific amount of interest.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>The Method of the Rule of Five, etc.</header>

# The Method of the Rule of Five, etc.

So, what is the interest for sixteen in one year? This is the second side of the problem. <gloss>1/100 12/16</gloss> Here, the **Fruit** <term>Phala; the result or interest</term> of 5 was written on the second side. It then took this form: <gloss>1/100 12/16 5/0</gloss>. Here, the **product** <term>Ghata; the result of multiplication</term> of the "many" quantities—which are the 3 quantities on one side—was calculated, resulting in 960 (nine hundred sixty). This was divided by the product of the "fewer" quantities, 100. The **quotient** <term>Labdhi</term> obtained was 9, and 60 remained over the **denominator** <term>Hara</term> of 100 <gloss>60/100</gloss>. Applying **reduction** <term>Apavartana; simplifying a fraction by a common divisor</term> of 20 to this, the fraction became three over five <gloss>3/5</gloss>. Thus, the interest is 9 <gloss>3/5</gloss>. Following the previously mentioned method of transposition, the interest on 16 **Nishkas** <term>Nishka; a standard gold coin</term> for one year is **9 3/5**.

### Now, the layout for determining Time:
<margin>
| 1 | 0 |
|---|---|
| 100 | 36 |
| | 48 |
| 6 | 6 |
| 1 | 0 |
</margin>
<note>This table shows the numerical arrangement for the calculation of time, where the unknown variable is the duration of the loan.</note>

### Arrangement after the mutual exchange of sides:
| 100 | 16 |
|---|---|
| | 5 |

**The product of the many quantities is 4800** | 48 | 6 |
|---|---|
**Dividing by 400 (the product of the few quantities), the months obtained are 12.** | | 5 |

**Elaboration:** In the second example, an interest of five is earned on one hundred in one month <gloss>1/100 | 5</gloss>. This is the first row. Then, in how many days will an interest of forty-eight fifths <gloss>16 | 48/5</gloss> be earned on sixteen? This is the second row. Such is the standard layout. Here, according to the rule mentioned previously, the **Fruit** 5 of the first row is written in the second row, and the value **48/5** from the second row is written in the first row. Then, the **denominator** <term>Hara</term> of 5 that was under the forty-eight in the first row is moved to the second row. Then 100, 16...

| 48 | 5 |
|---|---|
| | 6 |
| | 5 |

The product of the "many" quantities—that is, the quantities in the first row—was calculated, resulting in 4800 (forty-eight hundred). This was divided by 400 (four hundred), which is the product of the "fewer" quantities. The result <term>Labdhi</term> was twelve. This is the **Time** <term>Kala</term>. That is to say, the interest of forty-eight fifths <gloss>48/5</gloss> on sixteen (16) will be earned in 12 months, or one year. ॥

| **Arrangement for the Principal:** 100 | 0 |

## The principal 16 is obtained as before; the same applies everywhere.

**Explanation—** In the third example, five units of **Fruit** <term>Phala; interest</term> are obtained on a hundred in one month. This forms the first column. Then, in twelve months, how much **Principal** <term>Muldhan</term> will yield an interest of forty-eight fifths? <insert>| 1 | 12 |<br>| 100 | 0 |<br>| 5 | 48/5 |</insert> This forms the second column, creating a standard **Layout** <term>Nyasa; the formal arrangement of numbers for calculation</term>. 

Following the previously stated rule, the "Fruit" of the first column (5) is written in the second column, and the "Fruit" of the second column (forty-eight fifths) is written in the first column. Now, the **Denominator** <term>Hara</term> that appeared in the first column is moved to the second column. Then, the **Product** <term>Ghata</term> of the "many" quantities (4800) is divided by the product of the "fewer" quantities (300). The **Quotient** <term>Labdhi</term> obtained is 16. This is the Principal. This method should be understood for all such cases. <insert>| 12 | 0 / 5 |<br>| 4800 / 300 | 5 |</insert>

## Example—

> If the interest on one hundred for a month and one-third is five and one-fifth, tell me, what is the interest on sixty-two and a half for three months and one-fifth? ॥ 2 ॥

**Prose Order:** O friend! If the **Interest** <term>Kalantara</term> on one hundred for a month and one-third is five and one-fifth, then tell me, what would be the interest on sixty-two and a half for three months and one-fifth? ॥ 2 ॥

**Meaning:** O friend! If in one and one-third months (1 $\frac{1}{3}$) the interest on one hundred (100) is five and one-fifth (5 $\frac{1}{5}$), then what will be the interest on sixty-two and a half (62 $\frac{1}{2}$) in three and one-fifth months (3 $\frac{1}{5}$)?

| **Layout:** | 1 $\frac{1}{3}$ | 3 $\frac{1}{5}$ | <insert>Transforming the denominators</insert> | $\frac{4}{3}$ | $\frac{16}{5}$ | | $\frac{16}{5}$ |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| | 100 | 62 $\frac{1}{2}$ | <insert>The layout becomes:</insert> | 100 | $\frac{125}{2}$ | | $\frac{125}{2}$ |
| | 5 $\frac{1}{5}$ | 0 | | $\frac{26}{5}$ | 0 | | --- |
| | | | | | | | 0 |

<note>In this step, the author converts mixed fractions (like 1 1/3) into improper fractions (4/3) to simplify the multi-step multiplication required by the Rule of Five.</note>

# Method of the Rule of Five and Related Calculations.

Layout for bringing terms to their opposite sides: 4 | 16
| | |
|---|---|
| 5 | 3 |
| 100 | 125 |
| 2 | 26 |
| 5 | |

In this arrangement, the **Product of the many quantities** <term>Bahurāśivadha; the multiplication of the numbers in the larger group</term> is 156,000. The **Product of the fewer quantities** <term>Svalparāśivadha; the multiplication of the numbers in the smaller group</term> is 20,000.
Dividing by this, the **Quotient** <term>Labdha</term> obtained is 7 <gloss>$\frac{4}{5}$</gloss>. When the units are multiplied by the denominator, the **Interest** <term>Kalāntara</term> is produced as:
<margin>$\frac{39}{5}$</margin> To understand the calculation of time and other factors, proceed as shown previously.

**Explanation—** Here, according to the words of the requester, the **Layout** <term>Nyāsa</term> was 1 $\frac{1}{5}$ | 3 $\frac{1}{5}$,
| | |
|---|---|
| 100 | 62 $\frac{1}{2}$ |
| 5 $\frac{1}{5}$ | 0 |

By the method of **Association of fractions** <term>Bhāgānubandha; converting mixed numbers into improper fractions</term>, the quantities were made into fractions: $\frac{6}{5}$ | $\frac{16}{5}$. Then the layout became 100 | $\frac{125}{2}$
| | |
|---|---|
| $\frac{26}{5}$ | 0 |

According to the aforementioned rule, the **Result** <term>Phala; literally 'fruit'</term> and the **Denominators** <term>Hara</term> were swapped, resulting in this layout:

| $\frac{5}{6}$ | $\frac{16}{5}$ |
| :--- | :--- |
| 100 | $\frac{125}{2}$ |
| $\frac{5}{26}$ | |

<note>The original text contains a redundant layout table here which I have unified for clarity.</note>

This is the arrangement. Here, the larger set of quantities is in the second column. For that reason, by mutually multiplying them, the number 156,000 was obtained. In this, the number 20,000, which was obtained by multiplying the smaller set of quantities (the first column), was divided. This resulted in a quotient of 7 (seven), and a remainder fraction of $\frac{16000}{20000}$ was left. Now, by removing the three zeros from both the **Numerator** <term>Ansha</term> and the **Denominator** <term>Hara</term>, the number became $\frac{16}{20}$. This was **Simplified** <term>Apavartana; reducing a fraction by a common divisor</term> by four, leaving the fraction $\frac{4}{5}$. Then, by performing the fraction association on 7 $\frac{4}{5}$, the interest became $\frac{39}{5}$. If one wishes to create a layout to find the time or other factors for $\frac{125}{2}$ in $\frac{16}{5}$ months, it should be understood according to the method shown in the first example.

Here, by multiplying the whole numbers by the denominators and adding the numerators (as per the rule "the numerators are positive or negative" etc.) to make them **Uniform fractions** <term>Savarnī; having a common denominator</term>, they become:
<gloss>4</gloss> $\frac{6}{5}$ <gloss>100</gloss> $\frac{100}{1}$ <gloss>26</gloss> $\frac{26}{5}$ <gloss>16</gloss> $\frac{16}{5}$ <gloss>125</gloss> $\frac{125}{2}$. In bringing the terms to their opposite sides, of the many...

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the calculation of the "Rule of Five" (Pañcarāśika) problem introduced on the previous page. Having arranged the terms and swapped the "fruit" (result) and the denominators, the text now performs the final multiplication and division to find the interest. It then introduces the "Rule of Seven" (Saptarāśika) with a new practical example involving the pricing of silk cloth.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>Līlāvatī</header>

The product of the quantities <gloss>original: 26/5, 125/2, 16/5</gloss> $\frac{26}{5}$ $\frac{125}{2}$ $\frac{16}{5}$ is <gloss>original: 52000/50</gloss> $\frac{52000}{50}$.
The product of the two smaller quantities <gloss>original: 4/3, 100/1</gloss> $\frac{4}{3}$ $\frac{100}{1}$ is <gloss>original: 400/3</gloss> $\frac{400}{3}$.
For the purpose of division, the layout is made by inversion <note>The divisor is flipped to multiply</note>: <gloss>original: 52000/50</gloss> $\frac{52000}{50}$ <gloss>original: 3/400</gloss> $\frac{3}{400}$
The product of the numerators is 156,000. The product of the denominators is 20,000.
Dividing by this, the result is 7 <gloss>original: 16/20</gloss> $\frac{16}{20}$.
When converted into a single fraction <note>the integer is multiplied by the denominator and added to the numerator</note>, this interest is <gloss>original: 39/5</gloss> $\frac{39}{5}$.
An intelligent person should understand the process in this way everywhere.

### Or, another way of expanding this same example:

According to the requester's statement, the layout is 1 <gloss>?</gloss> $\frac{4}{3}$ <gloss>?</gloss> $\frac{100}{1}$ <gloss>?</gloss> $\frac{26}{5}$ $\frac{125}{2}$ $\frac{16}{5}$.
By performing the **association of fractions** <term>Bhāgānubandha; the process of resolving mixed fractions into improper ones</term>, it becomes $\frac{4}{3}$ $\frac{100}{1}$ $\frac{26}{5}$ $\frac{125}{2}$ $\frac{16}{5}$. Then, by transposing the result <note>the "fruit" or price/interest is moved to the opposite side to balance the equation</note>, the quantities in one row are $\frac{4}{3}$ and $\frac{100}{1}$. In the second row, the quantities are $\frac{26}{5}$, $\frac{125}{2}$, and $\frac{16}{5}$. 

Now, according to the aforementioned rule, by multiplying the numerators and denominators of the larger set of quantities, the form becomes $\frac{52000}{50}$. To divide this by the product of the numerators and denominators of the smaller quantities, which is $\frac{400}{3}$, the layout is inverted: <gloss>original: 52000/50</gloss> $\frac{52000}{50}$ <gloss>original: 3/400</gloss> $\frac{3}{400}$.
Now, multiplying the numerators together gives 156,000, and multiplying the denominators together gives 20,000. Dividing the product of the numerators by the product of the denominators, the **quotient** <term>Labdhi</term> is seven (7), and the fraction $\frac{16}{20}$ remains. 
When the fraction is resolved <note>7 + 16/20 simplifies to 7 + 4/5</note>, the interest becomes $\frac{39}{5}$. This is the same answer obtained by the first method. Thus, the wise should understand all such cases.

# Example of the Rule of Seven

->Now the example of the Rule of Seven is written:<-

> If eight beautiful silk scarves, which are three cubits in width and eight cubits in length, are obtained for one hundred;
> Then tell me quickly, O Merchant, if you understand trade: what would another scarf of the same kind, which is three and a half cubits long and half a cubit wide, be worth? || 3 ||

**Prose Connection:** O Merchant! If you understand trade, then tell me quickly: if eight silk scarves, measuring 3 **cubits** <term>Kara/Hasta; a unit of length approximately 18 inches</term> in width and 8 cubits in length, cost one hundred, then what is the value of another scarf of the same quality that is three and a half cubits long and half a cubit wide? || 3 ||

<keywords>Rule of Five, Rule of Seven, Bhāgānubandha, fractions, arithmetic, Līlāvatī, silk trade, cubits, Bhāskara</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstrations from Bhāskara's Līlāvatī, moving from the "Rule of Five" to the "Rule of Seven" (Saptarāśika). The previous page established the method for calculating interest using ratios; this page applies similar logic to commerce, specifically calculating the price of silk based on dimensions and quantity.</meta>

<header>The Method of the Rule of Seven</header>
<page-num>( 77 )</page-num>

# The Method of the Rule of Seven

**The Problem:** O worthy merchant! If you are skilled in trade, then tell me quickly: if 8 pieces of beautiful, multi-colored silk cloth—each being 3 cubits <note>original: "hatha," a unit of length from the elbow to the fingertip</note> wide and 8 cubits long—are obtained for 100 **Niṣkas** <term>Niṣka: a high-denomination gold coin</term>, what would be the price of another piece of the same beautiful silk that is 3 $\frac{1}{2}$ cubits long and $\frac{1}{2}$ cubit wide? ॥३॥

**The Layout:**
| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 3 | $\frac{1}{2}$ | Obtained: 0 **Niṣkas**, 14 **Drammas** |
| 8 | | |
| 8 | $\frac{1}{2}$ | 9 **Paṇas**, 1 **Kākini** |
| 100 | | |
| | $\frac{8}{3}$ | 6 $\frac{2}{3}$ **Varāṭakas** |

**The Expansion**—According to the questioner’s statement, the layout is as follows:
| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| 3 | $\frac{1}{2}$ |
| 8 | 3 $\frac{1}{2}$ |
| 8 | 1 |
| 100 | 0 |

This becomes:
| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 3 | $\frac{1}{2}$ | $\frac{1}{2}$ |
| 8 | $\frac{7}{2}$ | $\frac{7}{2}$ |
| 8 | $\frac{1}{1}$ | $\frac{1}{1}$ |
| 100 | $\frac{0}{1}$ | $\frac{0}{1}$ |
| | | --- |
| | | 100/1 |

This is the layout. Then, when the **association of fractions** <term>Bhāgānubandha: converting mixed numbers into improper fractions</term> was performed:
| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| 3 | $\frac{1}{2}$ |
| 8 | $\frac{7}{2}$ |
| 8 | $\frac{1}{1}$ |
| 100 | $\frac{0}{1}$ |

When the "fruit" <note>the result or price</note> and the denominators were transposed <note>swapped to the opposite side for calculation</note>:
| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| 3 | $\frac{1}{1}$ |
| 8 | $\frac{2}{7}$ |
| 8 | $\frac{1}{1}$ |
| 100 | $\frac{0}{1}$ |

It took this form. Here, the product of the larger set of quantities is 700. The smaller set is divided into it.
| $\frac{1}{2}$ | $\frac{1}{2}$ |
| :--- | :--- |
| $\frac{7}{2}$ | $\frac{7}{2}$ |
| $\frac{1}{1}$ | $\frac{1}{1}$ |
| $\frac{0}{1}$ | $\frac{0}{1}$ |
| $\frac{100}{1}$ | |
Since the product of the smaller set of quantities, 768, is greater than the dividend <note>700</note>, it cannot be divided directly. For this reason, the dividend of 700 **Niṣkas** is converted.

> "Sixteen **Drammas** make one **Niṣka**" <note>original: "drammaistathā ṣoḍaśabhiśca niṣkaḥ"</note>

Multiplying by 16 to create **Drammas**, we get 11,200. When divided by the product of the smaller set <note>768</note>, the result is 14 **Drammas**. A remainder of 448 remains. For these:

> "Know here that sixteen [**Paṇas** make a **Dramma**]" <note>original: "te ṣoḍaśa dramma ihāvagamyāḥ"</note>

Multiplying by 16 to create **Paṇas**, we get 7,168. Dividing this by the product of the smaller set, 768, the result is 9 **Paṇas**, and 256 remains. For these:

> "Four of those make a **Paṇa**" <note>original: "tāśca paṇaścatasraḥ"</note>

Multiplying by 4 to create **Kākinis**, we get 1,024. Dividing these by the product of the smaller set, 1,024 $\div$ 768 gives 1 **Kākini**, and 256 remains. For these:

> "Twenty cowries make one **Kākini**" <note>original: "varāṭakānāṃ daśakadvayaṃ yatsā kākiṇī"</note>

Multiplying by 20 to create **Varāṭakas** <note>cowrie shells</note>, we get 5,120. Dividing by the product of the smaller set, the result is 6 **Varāṭakas**, and the fraction $\frac{512}{768}$ remains. Reducing this by a factor of 256, the fraction $\frac{2}{3}$ remains. 

In this way, the price of that one piece of silk is 14 **Drammas**, 9 **Paṇas**, 1 **Kākini**, and 6 $\frac{2}{3}$ **Varāṭakas**.

<summary>This page explains the Rule of Seven (Saptarāśika) to calculate the price of a single piece of silk cloth based on the known price and dimensions of multiple pieces. It demonstrates complex division by converting gold Niṣkas into smaller denominations like silver Drammas, copper Paṇas, and cowrie shells.</summary>
<keywords>Rule of Seven, Saptarāśika, Bhāskara, Līlāvatī, Indian currency units, Niṣka, Dramma, Paṇa, Kākini, Varāṭaka, commerce, fractions</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the "Rule of Seven" (Saptarāśika) discussed on the previous page to the "Rule of Nine" (Navarāśika). While the previous example calculated the price of silk based on two dimensions and quantity, this page calculates the price of wooden planks (paṭṭa) based on three dimensions (thickness, width, length) and quantity, involving nine distinct terms.</meta>

<header>Līlāvatī</header>
<page-num>78</page-num>

# Now, the Example of the Rule of Nine
### An example of the Rule of Nine is written below:

> O friend! If thirty planks, which are twelve fingers <note>original: "arka-mita-angula"; 'arka' is a code for the number 12</note> in thickness, sixteen fingers <note>original: "chatur-varga-angula"; the square of four</note> in width, and fourteen cubits <note>original: "kara"</note> in length, are obtained for one hundred **Niṣkas** <term>Niṣka: a high-value gold or silver coin</term>. ॥
> Tell me, what price would fourteen such planks fetch if their dimensions of width, thickness, and length were each reduced by four? ॥ 4 ॥

**The Connection:** O friend! If thirty planks—which are 12 fingers thick, 16 fingers wide, and 14 cubits long—fetch 100 coins, then tell me the price for 14 planks where each dimension (width, thickness, and length) has been reduced by 4. ॥ 4 ॥

**The Meaning:** O friend! There are 30 wooden planks. Each is 12 fingers thick, 16 fingers wide, and 14 cubits long. These 30 planks are bought for 100 **Niṣkas**. Now, consider other planks where the width, thickness, and length are each 4 units less than the first ones. This means they are 8 fingers thick, 12 fingers wide, and 10 cubits long. Tell me, what will be the price for 14 such planks? ॥ 4 ॥

| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 12 | 8 | |
| 16 | 12 | |
| **Statement:** 14 | 10 | Obtained Price: 16 $^2/_3$ **Niṣkas** |
| 30 | 14 | |
| 100 | 0 | |

**The Expansion**—Following the requester's question, here is the **Statement** <term>Nyāsa: the formal layout of a mathematical problem</term>. According to the rules mentioned previously, even though there are no denominators here, we transpose the "fruit" <note>the result or price, 100</note> to the other side. The statement becomes:

<insert>
| 12 | 8 |
| :--- | :--- |
| 16 | 12 |
| 14 | 10 |
| 30 | 14 |
| 100 | |
</insert>

<insert>
$\frac{8}{12}$
$\frac{12}{16}$
$\frac{10}{14}$
$\frac{14}{30}$
$\frac{100}{ }$
</insert>

This is how it appears. We calculate the **Product** <term>Ghāta: the result of multiplication</term> of the many quantities on the right. That is, 8 multiplied by 12 equals 96; this multiplied by 10 equals 960; this multiplied by 14 equals 13,440 <note>The original Hindi text contains a typo '13340', but the calculation requires 13,440</note>. When this is multiplied by the "fruit" of 100, the product is 1,344,000.

Now, we calculate the product of the smaller set of quantities (the original dimensions): 12 times 16 times 14 times 30, which equals 80,640. When we divide the large product (1,344,000) by this number (80,640), the **Quotient** <term>Labdhi: the result of a division</term> is 16, with a **Fraction** <term>Bhinnāṅka</term> of $^2/_3$ remaining.

In this way, the price is 16 $^2/_3$ **Niṣkas**.

<vocab>Navarāśiko-dāharaṇam (Rule of Nine example), Nyāsa (Statement/Layout), Paṭṭa (Plank), Niṣka (Coin), Aṅgula (Finger-width), Labdhi (Quotient), Bhinnāṅka (Fraction), Ghāta (Product)</vocab>

# Method of the Rule of Eleven

### Now, an example of the Rule of Eleven—
->Now we write an example of the Rule of Eleven:<-

> If the freight for bringing those planks of the previously stated dimensions, located at a distance of one **league** <note>original: "gavyūti"; a gavyūti is approximately 2 miles or 4,000 yards</note>, is eight **Drammas** <term>Dramma: a medieval silver coin</term>... ॥
> ...then tell me, what would be the freight cost for a distance of six leagues for those other planks mentioned thereafter, whose dimensions were reduced by four? ॥ 5 ॥

**The Connection:** O friend! If the rental for bringing the previously mentioned planks across a distance of one league <note>original: "gavyūti-mātre"</note> is eight Drammas, then tell me: what is the rental charge for those other planks with dimensions reduced by four, transported over six leagues? ॥ 5 ॥

**The Meaning:** O friend! Consider the planks mentioned in the previous example: 12 fingers thick, 16 fingers wide, and 14 fingers long. These 30 planks are placed at a distance of two **Kosh** <term>Kosh: a traditional Indian measure of distance, where 2 Kosh equal 1 Gavyūti</term>. If the cart freight to bring them is 8 Drammas, then tell me the freight for 14 planks that are 4 fingers smaller in each dimension (meaning 8 fingers thick, 12 fingers wide, and 10 fingers long) across a distance of twelve **Kosh** (six leagues). ॥ 5 ॥

<margin>
12 | 8
16 | 12
14 | 10
30 | 14
 2 | 12
 8 | 0
</margin>

**Statement:**
| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 12 | 8 | |
| 16 | 12 | |
| 14 | 10 | Obtained freight: 8 Drammas |
| 30 | 14 | |
| 1 | 6 | |
| 8 | 0 | |

**The Expansion**—In this example, the **Statement** <term>Nyāsa: the formal mathematical layout</term> is arranged according to the instructions. Following the aforementioned method, there is no divisor; there is only the result.

**Transposition of the Statement:**
| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 12 | 8 | |
| 16 | 12 | This is the |
| 14 | 10 | resulting |
| 30 | 14 | layout. |
| 2 | 30 | |
| | 12 | |

->(Product of the many terms) 8 (Product of the fewer terms)<-

<summary>This page introduces the "Rule of Eleven" (Ekadash-rāśika), a complex proportion involving eleven terms. It calculates the freight cost of transporting wooden planks by considering five distinct variables: thickness, width, length, quantity, and distance.</summary>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>Līlāvatī <note>A famous 12th-century mathematical treatise by Bhāskara II</note></header>

| | |
| :---: | :---: |
| 8 | 12 |
| 12 | 16 |
| --- | --- |
| 96 | 192 |
| 10 | 14 |
| --- | --- |
| 960 | 2688 |
| 14 | 30 |
| --- | --- |
| 13440 | 80640 |

| 8 | |

When the product of the many quantities, 1,290,240, was divided by the product of the few quantities, 161,280, the quotient obtained was 8. Therefore, the freight <note>original: "bhāḍā"; rental or transport cost</note> will be 8 **Drammas** <term>Dramma: a standard silver coin of the period</term>.

### Now, the Rule for Barter/Exchange—a half-verse of the formula:

Now, the method for **Barter** (exchanging one item for another item of equal value) is described in half a verse—

**The rule for the exchange of goods is exactly the same; however, in that case, the prices must always be reversed.**
<note>original Sanskrit: "tathaiva bhāṇḍapratibhāṇḍake vidhirviparyyayastatra sadā hi mūlye"</note>

**Syntactic Connection:** The procedure for the exchange of goods should be performed in the same way <note>referring to the Rule of Five, Seven, etc.</note>. In that case, however, the prices must always be placed in a reversed position.

**The Meaning:** In the exchange of goods (Barter), the same method used for the Rule of Five <note>Pañcarāśika</note> is applied. The only difference is that the prices of the commodities must be swapped or transposed in the calculation.

### Example—

> O friend! If in this market three hundred mangoes are obtained for one **Dramma**, and thirty excellent pomegranates are obtained for one **Pana** <term>Pana: a copper coin; 16 Panas typically equal 1 Dramma</term>, then tell me quickly: how many pomegranates would be obtained in exchange for ten mangoes? ॥ 1 ॥

**Syntactic Connection:** O friend! If in this market <note>original: "vipaṇau"</note> three hundred mangoes are obtained for one Dramma, and thirty pomegranates are obtained for one Pana, then say quickly how many pomegranates will there be in exchange for ten mangoes? ॥ 1 ॥

**The Meaning:** O friend! If at this shop three hundred (300) mangoes are available for one Dramma, and thirty (30) pomegranates are available for one Pana, then how many pomegranates will you receive if you trade ten (10) mangoes for them? Tell me this soon. ॥ 1 ॥

<summary>This page completes a complex transport cost calculation using the Rule of Eleven and introduces the Rule of Barter, explaining that exchange calculations follow the Rule of Five but require swapping the prices of the goods.</summary>
<keywords>arithmetic, Rule of Eleven, barter, exchange of goods, Dramma, Pana, mangoes, pomegranates, Bhāskara II, Lilavati</keywords>

<header>Miscellaneous Calculations</header>

| 16 | 1 | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Setup:** 300 | 30 | **16 Pomegranates obtained** |
| 10 | 0 | |

**The Setup:** According to the questioner’s requirements, the setup <note>original: "nyāsa"; the formal arrangement of numerical data in a grid</note> was established as shown above. Here, following the previously mentioned rule, the "Fruit" <note>the quantity of goods</note> and the "Price" were swapped, resulting in the arrangement:
1, 16, 10
30, 300, 0
When the product of the "many" quantities (4800) was divided by the product of the "few" quantities (300), the quotient obtained was 16. These 16 pomegranates are what will be received in exchange for 10 mangoes.

->Thus ends the Miscellaneous section in the Līlāvatī.<-

### Now, the formula for the Practice of Mixtures in one and a half verses—

Now, the method for **Mixed Mathematics** (called "mixed" because it deals with quantities that have been combined or added together) is described in a verse and a half:

> Multiply the argument by the argument’s time, and multiply the fruit by the elapsed time. <note>Verse 20</note>
> Having placed these two results separately, multiply them by the mixed sum and divide each by their own sum; the results are the principal and the interest. 
> Or, find the principal using the Rule of Supposition; subtracting that from the mixed sum gives the interest. <note>Verse 21</note>

**Syntactic Connection:** One should multiply the **Argument** <term>Pramāṇa: the standard principal, e.g., 100</term> by the **Argument's Time** <term>Pramāṇa-kāla: the time unit for the interest rate, e.g., 1 month</term>. Then, multiply the **Fruit** <term>Phala: the interest earned on the argument</term> by the **Elapsed Time** <note>original: "vimiśra-kālena"; the actual time the money was lent</note>. Place these two results in separate positions. Multiply both by the **Mixed Sum** <note>principal plus interest</note> and then divide them by the sum of those two results. This produces the **Principal** and the **Interest**. Alternatively, find the principal via the **Rule of Supposition** <term>Iṣṭakarma: a method of trial and error or "working backward" from an assumed value</term>; when this is subtracted from the total mixed sum, the remainder is the interest.

**The Meaning:** Multiply the standard principal by its time. Multiply the interest rate by the actual time elapsed. Write these two products in two places. In one instance, multiply both by the total amount (principal + interest). Then, divide those figures by the sum of the two products; the results will reveal the original principal and the interest earned. Or, use the Rule of Supposition to find the principal and subtract it from the total to find the interest.

### Illustration:

#### Example—

> If the interest on one hundred is five per year, and the total of the principal and interest has become one thousand, tell me, what are the principal and the interest separately?

<meta>This page provides a practical example of the calculation rules introduced on the previous page regarding "Mixed Mathematics" (Mixtures). It specifically demonstrates how to separate a total sum (Principal + Interest) into its original components using both the standard proportional method and the Rule of Supposition.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>Līlāvatī</header>

**The Connection:** If the interest on one hundred is five per month, and the total sum of the principal and interest after one year is one thousand, tell me the principal and the interest separately. || 1 ||

**The Meaning:** If a sum of 100 <note>The "Argument" or standard principal</note> earns 5 <note>The "Fruit" or interest</note> in one month <note>The "Argument’s Time"</note>, and after one year <note>12 months; the "Elapsed Time"</note> the total amount of principal plus interest is 1000, then state what the original principal was and what the interest was within that thousand. || 1 ||

**The Setup:**
100 / 1 | 5 / 12 | 1000
The results obtained in order for the principal and interest are 625 and 375.

**Alternatively, by the Rule of Supposition:**
Assume 1 as the "Supposed" value <term>Iṣṭa-karman: the Rule of Supposition, where a simple number like 1 is used to test the ratio</term>. Following the Rule of Five <note>A calculation involving five known quantities to find a sixth</note>, the interest on the supposed amount of 1 is found:
1 | 5 / 100 | 12 / 1
The interest on the supposed 1 is 3/5. In this problem, the principal and interest are combined, so we add the supposed principal (1) to its interest (3/5), which gives 8/5. The total sum given in the problem (1000) is multiplied by the supposed principal (1) and divided by this sum (8/5), which results in 625 as the **Principal**. Subtracting this from the total sum (1000) gives 375 as the **Interest**.

### Expansion of the Method:
According to the rule previously stated:
1. Multiply the **Argument** (100) by the **Argument's Time** (1), which equals 100.
2. Multiply the **Fruit** (5) by the **Elapsed Time** (12), which equals 60.
3. These two quantities are written together: 100 | 60.
4. Their sum (160) is calculated. 
5. To find the principal: Multiply the first quantity (100) by the **Mixed Sum** (1000) and divide by the sum of the quantities (160). 100,000 ÷ 160 = **625** (the principal).
6. To find the interest: Multiply the second quantity (60) by the **Mixed Sum** (1000) and divide by the sum of the quantities (160). 60,000 ÷ 160 = **375** (the interest).

**Alternatively, using the Rule of Supposition:** 
Assume 1 as the principal. Using the Rule of Five to find the interest on 1:
Principal: 100 | Time: 1 | Interest: 5
New Principal: 1 | New Time: 12 | New Interest: ?
The interest on 1 is found to be 3/5. Since the problem provides the combined principal and interest, we add the supposed principal (1) to its interest (3/5) to get 8/5. We then divide the total given sum (1000) by this ratio (8/5), yielding 625 as the principal. Subtracting 625 from 1000 results in 375 as the interest.

### Formula for Different Portions of Mixture—
->Now the method for performing calculations on mixtures of multiple parts is written:<-

> The arguments, each multiplied by their respective times, are divided by the fruit multiplied by the elapsed time. Those results, divided by their own sum and then multiplied by the mixed sum, yield the individual portions of the principal. || 22 ||

<header>Mixed Calculations</header>

# Mixed Calculations

**The Connection:** Now, the standard times are multiplied by the standard principals, then divided by the products of the elapsed times and their respective interest rates. These results are then divided by their own sum and multiplied by the total mixed amount to find the individual portions of the principal. || 22 ||

**The Meaning:** Multiply each individual standard principal <note>usually 100</note> by its corresponding standard time <note>usually 1 month</note>. Divide these by the product of the actual time the money was lent and the interest rate. Set these values aside. Then, find the sum of these values. To find the specific parts of the total sum, multiply each individual value by the total mixed principal and divide by the total sum of those values. The resulting figures are the individual parts which, when added together, equal the total mixed amount. || 22 ||

->**The Problem**<-
->Example<-

O mathematician, a sum of one hundred minus six <note>94</note> <term>nishkas</term> <note>a gold coin or unit of currency</note> was lent out in three parts. These parts were lent at interest rates of five, three, and four percent. They remained for seven, ten, and five months respectively. If the interest earned on each of the three parts was exactly the same, tell me the size of each portion. || 1 ||

**The Connection:** O calculator! If 94 <note>one hundred minus six</note> *nishkas* were given in three parts at rates of five, three, and four per hundred, for durations of seven, ten, and five months, and the interest earned on all three parts was equal, then state the number of each part. || 1 ||

**The Meaning:** O expert in mathematics! A man has 94 <term>nishkas</term>. He divided this into three parts and lent them out. The first part was lent at a rate of 5 *nishkas* per hundred for 7 months. The second part was lent at 3 *nishkas* per hundred for 10 months. The third part was lent at 4 *nishkas* per hundred for 5 months. The interest earned on all three parts was equal. What are the values of those three parts? || 1 ||

**The Setup:**
| 1 / 7 | 1 / 10 | 1 / 5 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 100 | 100 | 100 |
| 5 | 3 | 4 |

<term>Miśradhanam</term> <note>Total mixed sum</note>: 94. The portions obtained in order are 24, 28, and 42. By using the <term>Pañcarāśivat-karaṇa</term> <note>the Rule of Five</note>, the equalized interest is found to be 8.

**The Expansion:** In this example, according to the rule stated above, the standard principals (100, 100, 100) are multiplied by their standard time (1), resulting in 100, 100, 100. These are then divided by the product of the elapsed times (7, 10, 5) and their respective interests (5, 3, 4). Thus:

<vocab>Miśravyavahāra (Mixed calculations), Anvaya (Logical connection/Prose order), Uddeśaka (Problem/Illustration), Niṣka (Currency unit), Nyāsa (Tabular setup), Pañcarāśivat-karaṇa (Calculation by the Rule of Five), Miśradhana (Mixed/Total capital), Labdhāni (Results obtained)</vocab>

It provides a specific example using 94 coins split into three parts at different interest rates (5%, 3%, 4%) and times (7, 10, 5 months).</summary>

became 35, 30, and 20. When the standard principal (100) was divided by these, it became $\frac{100}{35}$, $\frac{100}{30}$, and $\frac{100}{20}$. Here, by applying reduction <note>original: "apavartana," the process of simplifying fractions by a common factor</note> by 5, 10, and 20 respectively, the form became $\frac{20}{7}$, $\frac{10}{3}$, and $\frac{5}{1}$. By finding a common denominator <term>samaccheda</term> and adding them together, the sum became $\frac{235}{21}$. This sum was set aside. Then, the individual figures that were added—$\frac{20}{7}$, $\frac{10}{3}$, and $\frac{5}{1}$—were each multiplied by the total mixed capital of 94. This resulted in the forms $\frac{1880}{7}$, $\frac{940}{3}$, and $\frac{470}{1}$. Each of these was then divided by the sum $\frac{235}{21}$, which resulted in the three individual parts of the principal: 24, 28, and 42 (twenty-four, twenty-eight, and forty-two).

Now, the interest for each of these shares is calculated using the **Rule of Five** <term>pancharashika</term> <note>A method of proportion involving five known quantities to find a sixth</note>. For the first part: if the interest on 100 <term>nishkas</term> <note>a gold coin</note> for 1 month is 5, then what is the interest on 24 *nishkas* for 7 months?
$\begin{smallmatrix} 1 \\ 5 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 100 \\ 7 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 24 \\ \end{smallmatrix}$
The terms are rearranged for the calculation:
$\begin{smallmatrix} 100 \\ 7 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 1 \\ 5 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 24 \\ \end{smallmatrix}$
In this arrangement, the product of the larger set of numbers (840) is divided by the product of the smaller set (100). The resulting interest <term>labdhi</term> is $8 \frac{2}{5}$.

Similarly, if the interest on 100 is 3 for one month, what is the interest on 28 for 10 months?
$\begin{smallmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 100 \\ 10 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 28 \\ \end{smallmatrix}$
Rearranging the terms:
$\begin{smallmatrix} 100 \\ 10 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 28 \\ \end{smallmatrix}$
The product of the larger set (840) divided by the product of the smaller set results in the same interest: $8 \frac{2}{5}$.

Finally, if the interest on 100 is 4 for one month, what is the interest on 42 for 5 months?
$\begin{smallmatrix} 1 \\ 4 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 100 \\ 5 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 42 \\ \end{smallmatrix}$
Rearranging the terms:
$\begin{smallmatrix} 100 \\ 5 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 1 \\ 4 \end{smallmatrix}$ $\begin{smallmatrix} 42 \\ \end{smallmatrix}$
When the product of the larger set (840) is divided by the smaller product (100), the result is again $8 \frac{2}{5}$.

### Now, the Rule for Proportional Division —

Another method of mixed calculations is described in half a verse:

> **The individual shares, multiplied by the total mixture and divided by the sum of the shares, yield the individual results.**

**The Connection:** The individual contributions <term>prakṣepa</term>, multiplied by the total amount <note>profit or total mixed sum</note> and divided by the sum of all contributions, result in the individual shares of the profit.
**The Meaning:** When several people join together and invest capital into a business, that capital is called the **Contribution** <term>prakṣepa</term>. After conducting business, the total sum accumulated (including profit or loss) is called the **Mixed Amount** <term>mishradhana</term>.
Multiply each individual contribution by the total mixed amount and divide each by the sum of all contributions. This will reveal the individual share of the total for each person.

### An Illustration — An example on this subject:

**Three merchants contributed fifty plus one (51), sixty-eight (68), and ninety minus five (85) respectively. Through their trade, they gained a total mixed sum of three hundred. O calculator, tell me what the individual share of the wealth is for each of them after dividing it.** || 1 ||

<summary>This page concludes a complex interest problem using the "Rule of Five" and introduces the mathematical rule for "Proportional Division" (Prakṣepa), which explains how to distribute profits among business partners based on their initial investments.</summary>

<page-num>85</page-num>

# Miscellaneous Calculations

**Problem:** O Mathematician! There are three individuals whose initial capitals are fifty-one (51), sixty-eight (68), and eighty-five (85). From their combined capital used in trade, a total sum of three hundred (300) was obtained. Tell me, after dividing it, what are their respective shares?

**Explanation:** O master of mathematical skill! The invested capitals <term>prakṣepa-dhana</term> <note>original: "prakṣepa," meaning the amount thrown or put in; the individual's investment</note> are 51, 68, and 85. These three pooled their wealth to conduct business. The total wealth returned to them was 300. State clearly what each of the three received individually.

**Setup:** 
Investments: 51, 68, 85
Total Mixed Wealth: 300
Resulting Shares: 75, 100, 125
Profits (Shares minus initial capital): 24, 32, 40

**Alternatively:**
Total Mixed Wealth: 300
Sum of Initial Capitals: 204
Total Profit: 96
When this profit is multiplied by each individual investment and divided by the sum of investments (204), the individual profits are: 24, 32, 40.

**Detailed Breakdown:** 
There are three merchants here. Their individual capitals <term>prakṣepa-dhana</term> are 51, 68, and 85. The total mixed wealth <term>mishra-dhana</term> <note>original: "mishra-dhana," referring to the combined principal and profit</note> is 300. By multiplying each individual capital by this total wealth, we get 15,300, 20,400, and 25,500. Then, by dividing these by the sum of the capitals (204), we get the products 75, 100, and 125 respectively. These are the total amounts each merchant received. When the original investments (51, 68, 85) are subtracted from these shares, the profits are found to be 24, 32, and 40.

**Alternative Method:** 
Subtract the sum of the capitals from the total mixed wealth (300 - 204). The resulting total profit for everyone is 96. Multiply this profit by each individual investment and divide by the sum of investments (204). For example, 96 multiplied by the individual capitals results in 4,896, 6,528, and 8,160. Dividing these by the total capital (204) gives the individual profits: 24, 32, and 40. When you add these three profits together, they equal the total profit of 96.

### Rule for Filling a Stepped Well or Cistern — Half-Verse

Now, the method for calculating how a cistern <term>vāpī</term> is filled by various fountains or inlets is described in the following half-verse:

-> Divide one by the various fractions representing the fill-rates of the inlets; the result is the time required for completion. <-

<note>original: "bhajecchidraistairvimiśrai rūpaṃ bhajetsyātparipūrtikālaḥ" — literally: divide unity by the sum of the fractions of the openings.</note>

<summary>This page explains how to calculate individual profit shares in a partnership business based on proportional investment and introduces a new rule for determining how long it takes to fill a reservoir using multiple inlets.</summary>
<keywords>partnership business, proportional division, prakṣepa-dhana, profit calculation, cistern filling, vāpī, Līlāvatī, Bhāskara II</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical explorations from page 85, moving from partnership profits to problems involving rates of work—specifically, calculating the time required for multiple sources to fill a single reservoir.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>

->**Lilavati**<-

**Prose Order:** One should divide the denominators by their respective numerators. Then, divide the whole (one) by the sum of those results. That will be the time of completion. || 23 ||

**Meaning:** Divide the numerators into the denominators. Then, take the quotients obtained from these divisions and sum them together. Divide the number one (1) by this total sum. The resulting quotient is the time required to fill the vessel. || 23 ||

### Example

> O friend! There are streams which, when released individually, fill a reservoir in one day, half a day, a third of a day, and a sixth of a day respectively. If they are all opened together at once, tell me quickly: in what fraction of a day will the reservoir be filled? || 1 ||

**Prose Order:** O friend! These streams, when released individually, fill the reservoir in one day, half a day, a third, and a sixth. If they are released all at once, then tell me quickly: by what fraction of a day do they fill the reservoir? || 1 ||

**Meaning:** O friend! There are four streams <note>original: "nirjharāḥ," which can mean fountains, springs, or streams</note>. When opened separately, the first fills the reservoir <term>vāpī</term> <note>a stepped well or reservoir</note> in one full day; the second fills it in half a day; the third fills it in a third of a day; and the fourth fills it in a sixth of a day. If you open them all at the same time, tell me how long it will take for the four streams combined to fill the reservoir. || 1 ||

**Setup:** 1/1, 1/2, 1/3, 1/6

**Result:** The time to fill the reservoir is 1/12 of a day.

**Detailed Calculation:** Here, the four streams complete the task in 1/1, 1/2, 1/3, and 1/6 parts of a day. According to the rule stated above, we divide the numerators into the denominators <note>This effectively calculates the "rate" or how many reservoirs each stream fills per day</note>. This gives us the values 1/1, 2/1, 3/1, and 6/1. Summing these together results in 12/1. Now, divide the whole (number 1) by this sum. The result is 1/12 (one over twelve). This is the answer. That is to say, all the streams working together will fill the reservoir in the twelfth part of a day <note>the text equates this to one hour, likely assuming a 12-hour day cycle</note>.

### Rule for Buying and Selling in Verse

Now, the method for purchasing or selling goods is written in a single verse:

> Divide the quantities of goods by their respective prices. Multiply these by their individual shares and divide those by their sum. Then, multiply the shares by the total mixed capital to find the prices and the quantities of the goods in their proper order. || 24 ||

# The Practice of Buying and Selling

**Prose Order:** One should multiply the individual prices by their respective proportions and divide by the quantities of the goods. Multiply those results and the proportions by the total wealth spent, and then divide by the sum of those results. Then, the prices and the quantities of the goods will be found in order. || 24 ||

**Meaning:** Multiply the price of each item by its respective share (the desired ratio). Divide these products by the quantity of the item that is usually sold for that price. Take the resulting values and set them aside separately. Then, find the sum of these values. Next, take the individual values (the ones before they were summed), multiply them by the total "mixed wealth" <note>original: "mishradhana," referring to the total amount of money spent on all items combined</note>, and divide by the previously calculated sum. This will reveal the individual prices paid for each item. To find the specific weight or quantity of each item, multiply the shares (ratios) by the total wealth and divide by that same sum. || 24 ||

### Example:

> O Merchant! If three and a half measures of rice, and likewise eight measures of mung beans, are each sold for one dramma <note>a silver coin</note>, and I have thirteen kakinīs <note>a small copper coin; 64 kakinīs typically equal 1 dramma</note>—take these coins and quickly give me two parts of rice mixed with one part of mung beans. We must eat quickly and depart, for the caravan is moving ahead! || 1 ||

**Prose Order:** O merchant! If three and a half measures of rice and eight measures of mung beans are obtained for one dramma, then take these thirteen kakinīs and quickly provide two parts of rice mixed with one part of mung beans. We are fast-eaters and must go, as the caravan is moving forward. || 1 ||

**Meaning:** O best of merchants! If three and a half (3 ½) measures <term>māna</term> of rice and eight (8) measures of mung beans <term>mudga</term> cost one <term>dramma</term>, then take these thirteen (13) <term>kākiṇī</term> and give me both items. However, they must be in a specific proportion: one part mung beans to two parts rice. (Give them quickly because we need to cook and eat fast so we can leave, otherwise our companions in the caravan will get ahead of us.) Now, tell me, how much mung bean and how much rice did the merchant give, and what was the individual price for each? || 1 ||

**Setup:** 
Goods: 3 ½ and 8. 
Prices: 1 and 1. 
Respective shares: 2 and 1. 
Total wealth: 13/64. 

Here, the individual prices are multiplied by their respective shares and divided by the quantities of the goods, resulting in 4/7 and 1/8. Then, the shares 2 and 1 are multiplied by the total wealth 13/64 and divided by the sum... <note>The text concludes mid-calculation here, continuing onto the next page.</note>

$^7/_2$ $^7/_8$ Here, for the cost of the rice, the result is 2 panas, 2 kakinīs, and 13 $^1/_3$ varatikas. For the cost of the mung beans, the result is 2 kakinīs and 3 $^2/_3$ varatikas.

**Expansion:** The two prices, $^7/_2$ and $^7/_8$, were multiplied by their respective proportions, $^2/_1$ and $^1/_1$. That is to say, the price of the rice ($^7/_2$) was multiplied by the rice's proportion ($^2/_1$), resulting in the form $^7/_1$. The price of the mung beans ($^7/_8$) was multiplied by the mung's proportion ($^1/_1$), resulting in the form $^7/_8$. Thus, by multiplying each price by its respective proportion, we obtained the forms $^7/_1$ and $^7/_8$.

Now, these were divided by their respective weights. Specifically, the value for rice ($^7/_1$) was divided by the weight of the rice ($^3/_2$), which resulted in $^{14}/_3$. The value for mung beans ($^7/_8$) was divided by the weight of the mung beans ($^3/_1$), which resulted in $^7/_{24}$. After dividing in both instances, we have the forms $^{14}/_3$ and $^7/_{24}$.

These two values were written down in two places. In one place, the two quantities, $^{14}/_3$ and $^7/_{24}$, were added together; in the other place, they were left as they were. Where the addition was performed, the sum was $^{39}/_8$ <note>The sum of 14/3 and 7/24 is 119/24, which is approximately 4.95; 39/8 is 4.875, indicating a slight rounding or variation in the manuscript's fractional notation</note>.

These two quantities were then divided by the sum calculated previously ($^{39}/_8$). The resulting quotients were $^{28}/_9$ and $^7/_{36}$ respectively.

These represent the individual costs in <term>drammas</term> <note>a standard silver coin</note> for the rice and mung beans. Specifically:
* The cost for the 2 parts of rice is 2 panas, 2 kakinīs, 13 varatikas, and one-third ($^1/_3$) of a varatika.
* The cost for the 1 part of mung beans is 2 kakinīs, 6 varatikas, and two-thirds ($^2/_3$) of a varatika.

Next, according to the aforementioned rule, the proportions of rice and mung beans ($^2/_1$ and $^1/_1$) were multiplied by the mixed wealth ($^{13}/_4$), resulting in $^{26}/_4$ and $^{13}/_4$. These were divided by the sum calculated above ($^{39}/_8$), yielding the weights $^4/_3$ and $^2/_3$ for the rice and mung beans respectively.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical exercises from Bhaskara’s Lilavati regarding the "Rule of Three" and proportional bartering. While the previous page calculated costs for rice and mung beans, this page introduces a more fragrant problem: calculating the quantities and costs for ingredients used in making incense (camphor, sandalwood, and aloe wood).</meta>

89
Purchase and Sale Transactions

### Purchase and Sale Transactions

"Then give those to me in portions of one, sixteen, and eight for a single <term>nishka; a gold coin or unit of currency</term>. Because I wish to make incense." <note>original Sanskrit: तर्हि तान् एककषोडशाष्टकमितैः भागैः मे निष्केण देहि । यतः अहं धूप चिकीर्षाामि ॥ २ ॥</note>

**Meaning:—** O young merchant <term>vaishya-kumara; a son of the merchant caste</term> who brings joy to your mother! If one <term>pala; a traditional unit of weight</term> of beautiful camphor is available for two <term>nishkas</term>, and one <term>pala</term> of sandalwood is available for one-eighth part ($1/8$) of a <term>dramma; a silver coin</term>, and half a <term>pala</term> ($1/2$) of aloe wood is available for one-eighth of a <term>dramma</term>—then give me all these items (specifically: 1 part camphor, 16 parts sandalwood, and 8 parts aloe wood) for the total sum of one <term>nishka</term>, because I desire to make incense. (Task: Determine the weight of each of the three items obtained and their individual costs.)

**Statement of values:**
Merchandise weights: $\frac{1}{1}$ $\frac{1}{1}$ $\frac{1}{2}$
Prices: $\frac{32}{1}$ $\frac{1}{8}$ $\frac{1}{8}$ <note>The price of camphor is given as 2 nishkas; since 1 nishka equals 16 drammas, the price is written as 32 drammas to maintain a consistent currency unit.</note>
Portions: $\frac{1}{1}$ $\frac{16}{1}$ $\frac{8}{1}$ 
**Total wealth: 16 drammas** <note>One nishka</note>
Resulting costs of camphor, etc.: $14 \frac{2}{9}$ $\frac{8}{9}$ $\frac{8}{9}$
Resulting weights of merchandise: $\frac{4}{9}$ $7 \frac{1}{9}$ $3 \frac{5}{9}$

**Expansion—**

| Camphor | Sandalwood | Aloe wood | Total Wealth |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Price $\frac{32}{1}$ Portion $\frac{1}{1}$ | Price $\frac{1}{8}$ Portion $\frac{16}{1}$ | Price $\frac{1}{8}$ Portion $\frac{8}{1}$ | 16 |
| Weight (Pala) $\frac{1}{1}$ | Weight (Pala) $\frac{1}{1}$ | Weight (Pala) $\frac{1}{2}$ | |

Here, each individual price was multiplied by its respective portion according to the method described above. That is to say, the price of camphor ($\frac{32}{1}$) was multiplied by its portion ($\frac{1}{1}$), which resulted in the form $\frac{32}{1}$. Then the price of sandalwood ($\frac{1}{8}$) was multiplied by its portion ($\frac{16}{1}$), resulting in $\frac{2}{1}$, and the price of aloe wood ($\frac{1}{8}$) was multiplied by its portion ($\frac{8}{1}$), resulting in $\frac{1}{1}$. Thus, by multiplying the prices of the three items by their respective portions, the forms $\frac{32}{1}$, $\frac{2}{1}$, and $\frac{1}{1}$ were obtained.

From these, the division by their respective weights was performed. Specifically, $\frac{32}{1}$ was divided by its weight $\frac{1}{1}$, resulting in $\frac{32}{1}$. The value $\frac{2}{1}$ was divided by its weight $\frac{1}{1}$, resulting in $\frac{2}{1}$. Finally, the value $\frac{1}{1}$ was divided by its weight $\frac{1}{2}$, which resulted in $\frac{2}{1}$.

After dividing all three quantities by their respective weights, the values were $\frac{32}{1}$, $\frac{2}{1}$, and $\frac{2}{1}$. These were written down in two separate places. In one place, the sum of these three quantities was calculated, while in the other, they were left as they were. Where the sum was calculated, the result was $\frac{36}{1}$. Then, the individual quantities that were not summed ($\frac{32}{1}$, $\frac{2}{1}$, and $\frac{2}{1}$) were each multiplied by the "mixed wealth" <term>mishradhana; the total amount to be spent</term> of 16 drammas. This resulted in the forms $\frac{512}{1}$, $\frac{32}{1}$, and $\frac{32}{1}$. These were then individually divided by the sum of 36 that was calculated previously.

<header>LILAVATI</header>

Then the quotient became $\frac{128}{9}$, $\frac{6}{1}$, $\frac{8}{3}$. In this way, the costs for camphor, sandalwood, and aloe wood respectively became 14, $\frac{6}{1}$, and $\frac{2}{3}$ <term>drammas; silver coins</term>. Then, the portions of these three—camphor, sandalwood, and aloe wood—which were $\frac{9}{1}$, $\frac{1}{6}$, and $\frac{3}{8}$, were multiplied by the total wealth of $\frac{16}{1}$. This resulted in $\frac{144}{1}$, $\frac{16}{6}$, and $\frac{48}{8}$. These were then divided by the sum calculated previously, which was $\frac{36}{1}$. The resulting quotients were $\frac{4}{1}$, $\frac{16}{216}$, and $\frac{48}{288}$. In this manner, the weights for camphor, sandalwood, and aloe wood will be found to be 4, $\frac{2}{27}$, and $\frac{1}{6}$ <term>pals; a unit of weight</term> respectively.

## Rule for the Mixture of Jewels (Verse)

The method for calculating mixtures involving precious gems is written in the following verse:

*By dividing an assumed number by the remainders of the jewels—after subtracting the gift amount multiplied by the number of men—the values of the jewels are found. Or, the prices become integers if the product of all remainders is divided by each individual remainder.* ॥ 25 ॥ <note>original Sanskrit: नरघ्नदानोनितरत्नशेषैरिष्टे हृते स्युः खलु मूल्यसंख्याः ॥ शेषैर्हते शेषवधे पृथक्स्थैरभिन्नमूल्यान्यथवा भवन्ति॥ २५॥</note>

**Prose Order:** Truly, the prices of the jewels are found by dividing an assumed number by the remainders of the jewels minus the product of the number of men and the gift. Alternatively, integer values are obtained by taking the product of all remainders and dividing it separately by each remainder. ॥ 25 ॥

**Meaning:—** (In a situation where men exchange their items so that their total wealth becomes equal) multiply the number of men by the number of items given as a gift. Subtract this from the total quantity of each type of jewel to find the "remainders." Divide any chosen "assumed number" by each of these remainders; the resulting quotients will surely be the price per jewel for each type.

Alternatively—multiply all the remainders together to get a single product. Divide this product by each individual remainder; the resulting quotients will give the price of each jewel as a whole number. ॥ 25 ॥

### Example for this topic:

*Eight rubies, ten sapphires, one hundred pearls, and five fine diamonds are the wealth owned by four jewel merchants respectively. Out of the affection of their association, each gave one of their own jewels to every other merchant, and thus they all became possessed of equal wealth. O friend, tell me the prices of those jewels separately.* ॥ 1 ॥ <note>original Sanskrit: माणिक्याष्टकमिन्द्रनीलदशकं मुक्ताफलानां शतं सद्वज्राणि च पञ्च रत्नवणिजां येषां चतुर्णां धनम् ॥ सङ्गस्नेहवशेन ते निजधनाद्दत्त्वैकमेकं मिथो जातास्तुल्यधनाः पृथग्वद सखे तद्रत्नमूल्यानि मे ॥१॥</note>

**Prose Order:** O friend! Four jewel merchants possessed eight rubies, ten sapphires, one hundred pearls, and five fine diamonds respectively. Out of friendship, they each gave one jewel from their own stock to the others, after which they all had equal wealth. Therefore, tell me the prices of each of those jewels separately. ॥ 1 ॥
