<header>Practice of Mixed Jewels</header>

# Practice of Mixed Jewels

**Meaning:** O friend! There were four men trading in jewels whose stock consisted respectively of 8 rubies, 10 blue sapphires, 100 pearls, and 5 beautiful diamonds. Because of the friendship they developed while traveling together on the road, each gave one jewel from his own stock to each of the others <note>This means each man gives away 3 jewels in total—one to each of his three companions—and receives 3 in return</note>. After this exchange, they all possessed wealth of equal value. Tell me, what is the price of each ruby and the other gems? ॥ 1 ॥

**Statement of the problem:** Rubies 8, Sapphires 10, Pearls 100, Diamonds 5.
Gift 1, Men 4.

By multiplying the gift by the number of men <gloss>4</gloss> and subtracting that from the quantity of each jewel, the remainders are: Rubies 4; Sapphires 6; Pearls 96; Diamonds 1. By dividing an assumed quantity <note>The text uses the term "Ishta-rashi," a common Sanskrit mathematical technique where a convenient number is assumed to solve for unknowns</note> by these remainders, the prices of the jewels are found. If the assumed quantity is chosen arbitrarily, the results might be fractions. Here, we assume the quantity to be 96.
Therefore, the resulting prices are 24, 16, 1, and 96. The equal wealth of each man is 233. 
Alternatively, by taking the product of the remainders, which is 2304, and dividing it by each remainder individually, we get whole number prices: 576, 384, 24, and 2304. In this case, the equal wealth of the four men is 5592. These values are calculated in <term>drammas; a historical silver coin</term>.

---

**Detailed Explanation:** Here, the traders exchanged one jewel each, so the "gift" is 1 and the number of men is 4. For this reason, we multiply the number of men (4) by the gift amount (1), which equals 4. We subtract this from the total count of each jewel. The remainders are: Rubies <gloss>4</gloss>, Sapphires <gloss>6</gloss>, Pearls <gloss>96</gloss>, and Diamond <gloss>1</gloss>. 
We then assume an arbitrary number, 96, and divide it by each of these remainders to find the price of a single jewel of each type. 
Rubies: <gloss>24</gloss>; Sapphires: <gloss>16</gloss>; Pearls: <gloss>1</gloss>; Diamonds: <gloss>96</gloss>. 

By exchanging one gem with each other, everyone's total wealth becomes equal. For example, the man who originally had rubies now has 5 rubies, 1 sapphire, 1 pearl, and 1 diamond <note>He started with 8, gave 1 to each of the other 3 men (8 - 3 = 5), and received 1 of each of their gems in return</note>. Using the prices calculated above: 
The price of 5 rubies is 120 <term>drammas</term>. 
The price of 1 sapphire is 16 <term>drammas</term>. 
The price of 1 pearl is 1 <term>dramma</term>. 
The price of 1 diamond is 96 <term>drammas</term>. 
Adding these together, the total is 233 <term>drammas</term>. 

Similarly, the second man now has 1 ruby, 7 sapphires, 1 pearl, and 1 diamond. The third man has 1 ruby...

In this example, the application of "assumed choice" <term>Ishta-kalpana: a classic algebraic method in Indian mathematics where a convenient value is assumed for an unknown to find a solution without fractions</term> is written according to one's own intelligence. The method for this is as follows: from the total quantity of gems, subtract the number of men multiplied by the gift <note>As established on the previous page: (Gift 1 x 4 Men) - 2 = 2. This part of the text describes the logic of reducing the quantities to find the "remainders" used for calculations</note>. For the gems that remain, if a transformation <gloss>parivartan</gloss> can be applied to the first two quantities, one should apply it. After applying the transformation, calculate the product <gloss>ghat</gloss> of the resulting figures. Multiply the resulting product by the factor used for the transformation. Then, if the remaining gems in one quantity can be transformed by any factor, do so; find their mutual product and multiply by the factor used for that transformation. In this manner, for however many quantities there are, follow this same...

<meta>This page concludes the "Mixed Jewels" problem from page 92 and introduces a new section on "Gold Mathematics" (Suvarnaganita). It focuses on the arithmetic of mixtures, specifically calculating the average purity of combined gold alloys.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>Methods for Mixed Operations in Gold Mathematics</header>

...Perform the calculation in this manner. If a simplification factor <term>parivartana: reduction of numbers by a common divisor to simplify calculation</term> cannot be applied, then simply multiply the two quantities together and treat that as a single value. In this example, the number of men (4) was used to multiply the quantities, and after subtracting the gem counts, the remaining quantities are 4, 6, 1, and 96. 

Here, a simplification factor of 2 is applied to the first two quantities (4 and 6), which changes them to 2 and 3. The product of these two digits is 6. This is then multiplied by the simplification factor (2), resulting in 12. Now, 12 is treated as a single quantity. Comparing this with the next quantity from the refined gems, which is 1, we see that no simplification factor can be applied. Therefore, the product of these two (12) is taken as a single quantity and compared with the last quantity (96). Here, a simplification factor of 12 is applied, resulting in the ratio 1 and 8. The product of these two is 8. Multiplying this by the simplification factor (12) gives 96. Since the value remains consistent, 96 is the "assumed value" <term>ishta: an assumed or desired number used to solve algebraic problems</term>. By performing the actions described above using this 96, the answer will be found.

Alternatively—by multiplying the remaining numbers 4, 6, 1, and 96 together, we get 2304. Taking this as the assumed value and dividing it by each of the remainders separately, the price of each individual gem is found: 576, 384, 24, and 2304. Following this method, the equal total wealth for each of the five men becomes five thousand five hundred and ninety-two (5592).

# Now, the Rule for Gold Mathematics in Verse:

Now, the method for performing mixed calculations regarding gold is described in a single verse <note>original Sanskrit: "atha suvarṇagaṇite karaṇasūtraṃ vṛttam"</note>.

> Multiply the weight of each piece of gold by its own purity; sum these products and divide that total by the sum of the weights of the gold. The result is the purity of the combined gold. 
> If the sum of the products is divided by the weight of the refined gold, the purity is found. If divided by the purity, the weight of the refined gold is found. // 26 //

**Prose Order:** In the sum of the products of the gold quantities and their respective purity levels, when divided by the total weight of the gold, the purity of the combined gold is determined. When divided by the refined gold weight, the purity level is determined. When divided by the purity level, the quantity of refined gold is determined. // 26 //

**Meaning:** Multiply the weight of a piece of gold by its respective "varna" <term>varna: a measure of gold purity, similar to the modern 'karat'</term>. Then, add together all the results of these multiplications. Divide that total sum by the combined weight of all the gold pieces. The value you obtain is the average purity of the mixture. 

Furthermore, if you divide that same sum (of weight times purity) by the weight of the gold to be refined, you will find the purity level of that gold. Conversely, if you divide the sum by the purity level, you will find the total weight of the gold being refined.

<vocab>
**Gold Mathematics** (suvarṇagaṇita): Calculations involving the weight and purity of gold.
**Mixed Procedures** (mishraprakāra): Methods for calculating averages in mixtures.
**Simplification** (parivartana): Reducing numbers to their simplest terms for easier calculation.
**Product** (ghāta): The result of multiplication.
**Assumed Value** (iṣṭa): A chosen number used as a starting point to find an unknown.
**Refined Gold** (śodhitahema): Pure or processed gold.
**Combined Gold Purity** (kanakaikyavarṇa): The final purity level after different golds are melted together.
**Prose Order** (anvaya): The logical rearrangement of Sanskrit verse into a standard sentence structure.
</vocab>

## Examples

*Gold with purities of thirteen, twelve, eleven, and ten, weighing ten, four, two, and four units respectively—O merchant, if you are an expert in gold arithmetic, tell me quickly: what will be the fineness of the gold when these are melted together?* || 1 ||
<note>The original verse uses poetic synonyms for numbers: "Vishwa" (the All-gods) for 13, "Arka" (the Suns) for 12, "Rudra" (forms of Shiva) for 11, "Dik" (directions) for 10, "Veda" for 4, "Lochana" (eyes) for 2, and "Yuga" (ages) for 4.</note>

**Prose Order:** O merchant expert in gold mathematics! There is gold with purities of thirteen, twelve, eleven, and ten <term>varna: a measure of gold fineness, similar to the modern 'karat' system</term>, with weights of ten, four, two, and four <term>masha: a traditional unit of weight</term> respectively. If these are melted together, tell me quickly, what will the resulting fineness be? || 1 ||

**Meaning:** O merchant skilled in the calculations of gold! You have four pieces of gold. Their purities are 13, 12, 11, and 10 respectively. Their weights are 10, 4, 2, and 4 mashas. Specifically: gold of 13-fineness weighs 10 mashas; gold of 12-fineness weighs 4 mashas; gold of 11-fineness weighs 2 mashas; and gold of 10-fineness weighs 4 mashas. If all these golds are mixed and melted together, what will be the resulting fineness? Speak quickly. || 1 ||

*If those twenty mashas mentioned above, upon being refined, should weigh only sixteen, tell me quickly: what would the degree of fineness be then? Or, if that gold is refined to a fineness of sixteen, how many mashas would those twenty become?* || 2 ||

**Prose Order:** If those twenty mashas of weight previously mentioned should become sixteen upon refining, then what would the measure of fineness be? Tell this quickly. If those twenty mashas become refined gold of sixteen-fineness, then how many mashas will they be? || 2 ||

**Meaning:** If the twenty mashas of gold mentioned in the first problem are refined <term>shodhana: the process of purifying gold, which often results in loss of weight as impurities are removed</term> and only sixteen mashas remain, what will be the fineness of that gold? Tell me quickly. Furthermore, if those same twenty mashas of gold are melted and refined until the fineness reaches 16, how many mashas will the weight be reduced to? || 2 ||

**Statement of the problem:**
| 13 | 12 | 11 | 10 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 10 | 4 | 2 | 4 |

The fineness of the gold when melted together is 12.
<note>Calculation: [(13x10) + (12x4) + (11x2) + (10x4)] / [10+4+2+4] = 240 / 20 = 12.</note>

If these same pieces, after being refined, become 16 mashas in weight, then the fineness is 15.
<note>Calculation: 240 / 16 = 15.</note>

If that same gold is refined to a fineness of 16, then the weight becomes 15 mashas.
<note>Calculation: 240 / 16 = 15.</note>

<header>Method of Gold Mathematics</header>

**Elaboration:** Following the method stated above, the weights of the gold were multiplied by their respective <term>varna: a measure of gold fineness, similar to the modern 'karat' system</term> (market value/purity). The resulting products were 130, 48, 22, and 40 respectively. These were then

<insert>

| Fineness (Varna) | Weight (Masha) | Product |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 13 | 10 | 130 |
| 12 | 4 | 48 |
| 11 | 2 | 22 |
| 10 | 4 | 40 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Total Weight** | | **240** |
| resulted in | 20 | |
| this | | |

</insert>

summed together, reaching a total of two hundred and forty (240). When this sum was divided by the total weight (20 <term>masha: a traditional unit of weight</term>), the quotient was 12. Thus, if all the gold pieces are melted together, the resulting mixture will have a fineness of twelve.

Furthermore, if those same twenty (20) mashas of gold, upon being melted and refined, are reduced to sixteen (16) mashas in weight: following the aforementioned method, we take the sum of the products of weight and purity (240) and divide it by the remaining weight of gold (16). The result is a quotient of 15; this will be the fineness (varna) of the purified gold.

Alternatively, if those same twenty (20) mashas of gold, upon being melted, reach a fineness of sixteen (16), we take the sum of the products (240) and divide it by that fineness (16). The result is a quotient of 15; this will be the final weight of the purified gold.

->**Rule for Determining the Fineness**<-

The method for determining the unknown fineness of one of the gold pieces used in a mixture is described in the following verse:

> <note>original: "स्वर्णैक्यनिघ्नाद्युतिजातवर्णात्सुवर्णतद्वर्णवधैक्यहीनात् ॥ अज्ञातवर्णाभिजसंख्ययाप्तमज्ञातवर्णस्य भवेत्प्रमाणम् ॥२७॥"</note>
> *Multiply the resulting fineness of the mixture by the total weight of the gold. From this, subtract the sum of the products of the known weights and their respective purities. Divide the remainder by the weight of the gold whose purity is unknown; the result is the measure of that unknown purity.* || 27 ||

**Prose Order:** From the "resulting fineness" <note>the fineness of the final mixture</note> multiplied by the "total weight of gold," subtract the "sum of the products of the gold weights and their purities." Divide this by the "weight of the unknown gold." The result obtained is the measure of the unknown fineness. || 27 ||

**Meaning:** The fineness produced by mixing various types of gold is called the *yutijata varna* (resulting fineness). Multiply that resulting fineness by the total weight (sum) of the gold. From that product, subtract the sum of the products of the weight and fineness of the known gold pieces. Whatever remains should be divided by the weight of the gold whose purity is not known. The quotient resulting from this division is the numerical value of that unknown fineness. || 27 ||

->**Example**<-

> <note>original: "दशेशवर्णा वसुनेत्रमाषा अज्ञातवर्णस्य षडेतदैक्ये ॥ जातं सखे द्वादशकं सुवर्णमज्ञातवर्णस्य वद प्रमाणम् ॥ १ ॥"</note>
> *Friend, there is gold of ten and eleven fineness weighing eight and two mashas respectively. There are also six mashas of gold of an unknown fineness. When these are combined, the resulting gold has a fineness of twelve. Tell me, what is the measure of that unknown fineness?* || 1 ||

**Prose Order:** O friend! There are eight and two mashas <note>original: "vasu-netra," where Vasu=8 and Netra=2</note> of gold with purities of ten and eleven <note>original: "dasha-isha," where Isha=11</note> respectively. There are six mashas of gold of unknown purity. When these are all combined, the resulting gold is of twelve-fineness. Therefore, tell me the measure of the unknown fineness? || 1 ||

<summary>This page explains how to calculate the average fineness of a gold mixture and provides a specific formula for finding the unknown purity of a single component when the final mixture's properties are known.</summary>
<keywords>gold arithmetic, fineness, varna, masha, refining, alloy calculation, unknown purity formula</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the practical application of the mathematics of gold alloys. The previous page established the general method for finding average fineness; this page provides a specific practice problem to find an unknown fineness and introduces a new verse (Rule 28) for finding an unknown weight within a mixture.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

**Problem:** O friend! If there are eight (8) and two (2) <term>mashas: a traditional Indian unit of weight</term> of gold with a <term>varna: a measure of gold fineness or purity, similar to the karat system</term> of ten (10) and eleven (11) respectively, and there is a third piece of gold weighing six (6) mashas whose fineness is unknown—and if, upon melting them all together, the resulting fineness is twelve (12)—then tell me, what is the fineness of that unknown gold piece? ॥ 1 ॥

**Numerical Layout:**

| 10 | 11 | ? |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 8 | 2 | 6 |
| | 12 | |

### Result: The unknown fineness is 15. ॥

<margin>
16
Correct
</margin>

**Elaboration:** Here, the <term>yutijata-varna: the resulting fineness after melting all components together</term> is twelve (12). This is multiplied by the total weight of the gold, which is sixteen (16) <note>8 + 2 + 6 = 16</note>; the product is one hundred and ninety-two (192). From this, we subtract the sum of the products of the known gold weights and their respective finenesses <note>(8 × 10) + (2 × 11) = 80 + 22 = 102</note>. After subtracting 102 from 192, ninety (90) remains. This remainder is divided by the weight of the unknown gold, which is six (6). The quotient is fifteen (15). This is the fineness of the gold that was previously unknown.

This is verified because, according to the rule previously stated, if we multiply the weights by their finenesses, the products are 80, 22, and 90. Their sum is one hundred and ninety-two (192). Dividing this by the total weight (the sum of 8, 2, and 6, which is 16), we obtain the quotient of twelve (12), which matches the known resulting fineness. ॥

---

### A formulaic verse for determining the weight of gold:

For those mixtures where the resulting fineness is known, the method for finding the unknown weight of one of the components is described in the following verse:

> ->The resulting fineness of the mixture multiplied by the total weight of the gold, minus the sum of the weights of the known gold pieces multiplied by their respective finenesses, then divided by the difference between the fineness of the unknown gold and the resulting fineness of the mixture, gives the weight of the unknown gold. ॥ 28 ॥<-

<note>original Sanskrit: स्वर्णैक्यनिघ्नो युतिजातवर्णः स्वर्णघ्नवर्णैक्यवियोजितं च ॥ अहेमवर्णाग्रिजयो वर्णविश्लेषभक्तोऽविदिताग्निजां स्यात्॥ २८॥</note>

**Prose Order:** The resulting fineness (yutijata-varna) multiplied by the total weight of the gold (svarna-aikya), minus the sum of the known weights multiplied by their finenesses (svarna-ghna-varna-aikya), divided by the difference (vishlesha) between the fineness of the unknown gold and the resulting fineness, equals the unknown weight. ॥ 28 ॥

**Explanation:** Multiply the final fineness of the mixture (the result of melting everything together) by the total weight of all the gold. From this product, subtract the sum of the individual products (weight multiplied by fineness) of the gold pieces whose fineness is already known. Take the remaining value and divide it by the difference between the fineness of the piece whose weight is unknown and the final fineness of the mixture. The resulting quotient is the weight of the gold piece that was previously unknown. ॥ 28 ॥

<page-num>(97)</page-num>
<header>Various Methods for Mixed Gold Calculations</header>

### Example—
->The example is stated as follows:<-

**Gold pieces weighing three and one <term>mashas: a traditional Indian unit of weight</term> have a <term>varna: fineness or purity</term> of ten and fourteen respectively. There is also a portion of gold with sixteen fineness of unknown weight. When these are combined, the resulting mixture is gold of twelve fineness. How many mashas of that sixteen-fineness gold are there? ॥ 1 ॥**

<note>original: "दशेंद्रवर्णा गुणचंद्रभाषाः..." — The Sanskrit uses numerical code words: 'Guna' (3), 'Chandra' (1), and 'Indra' (14).</note>

**Syntax:** There are three and one mashas of ten and fourteen fineness respectively. Additionally, there is some gold of sixteen fineness. Their combination results in gold of twelve fineness. Therefore, in this case, how many mashas of that sixteen-fineness gold are there? ॥ 1 ॥

**Meaning:** Gold of three (3) and one (1) mashas are respectively of ten (10) and fourteen (14) fineness. There is another piece of sixteen (16) fineness whose weight is unknown. When all are mixed and melted together, the resulting gold is of the twelve (12) fineness grade. Tell me, what is the weight of that sixteen (16) fineness gold? ॥ 1 ॥

### Numerical Layout:
| 10 | 14 | 16 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 3 | 1 | 0 |
The resulting weight obtained is 1.

**Elaboration:** Here, the <term>yutijata-varna: the fineness produced by the mixture</term> is twelve (12). This is multiplied by the sum of the known weights, which is four (4) <note>3 + 1 = 4</note>, resulting in forty-eight (48). From this, we subtract the sum of the known gold pieces multiplied by their respective finenesses <note>(3 × 10) + (1 × 14) = 44</note>. Subtracting forty-four (44) leaves a remainder of four (4). Now, take the difference between the fineness of the unknown weight gold (16) and the mixture's fineness (12), which is four (4). Dividing the previous remainder (4) by this difference (4) gives a <term>labdhi: quotient</term> of one (1). This is the weight of that gold whose fineness was known but weight was not. This is verified because if we multiply the weights by their finenesses, we get 30, 14, and 16. Dividing their sum, sixty (60), by the total weight <margin>$\begin{vmatrix} 3 \\ 1 \\ 1 \\ \hline 5 \end{vmatrix}$</margin> of five (5), the quotient is twelve (12), which is the resulting fineness of the mixture. ॥ 1 2 5

### Another Rule for Knowing Gold Proportions (in Verse):
->Here is another method described in one verse for finding the weights of two types of gold when neither weight is known.<-

**Subtract the target fineness from the higher fineness; then subtract the lower fineness from the target fineness. These two remainders, when multiplied by an optional desired number, shall be the weights of the lower and higher fineness gold pieces respectively. ॥ 29 ॥**

**Syntax:** The higher fineness is to be diminished by the target; the target fineness is to be diminished by the lower fineness. Then, these two remainders, multiplied by a desired factor, become the weights of the lower and higher finenesses. ॥ 29 ॥

<meta>Catchword at bottom left: 7</meta>

<summary>This page provides a practical solution for finding the unknown weight of a gold component in a mixture to achieve a specific fineness and introduces Rule 29 for calculating the required proportions of two gold types to reach a desired purity level.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of gold-mixing calculations (suvarnaganita). While the previous page dealt with finding a single unknown weight in a three-part mixture, this page introduces the rule for determining the relative proportions of two types of gold needed to achieve a specific target fineness. This is essentially a medieval Indian version of the "Rule of Alligation."</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

**Rule:—** Subtract the target <term>varna: a unit of gold purity or fineness</term> from the higher varna, and subtract the lower varna from the target varna. If you then multiply these two remainders by any desired constant <term>ishta: an arbitrarily chosen number used to scale values</term>, you will find the respective weights of the gold pieces ॥ 29 ॥

## Example—

->There are two gold pellets, my friend, of sixteen and ten fineness. When they are combined,<-
->the resulting gold is of twelve fineness. Tell me the weights of those two gold pieces ॥ 1 ॥<-

**Syntax:** O friend! There are two gold pellets of sixteen and ten fineness. Their combination results in twelve fineness. Therefore, tell me the weights of those two gold pieces! ॥ 1 ॥

**Meaning:** O friend! There are two pieces of gold with a fineness (purity) of sixteen (16) and ten (10). By melting and mixing them together, gold of twelve (12) fineness is produced. Tell me, what are the weights of each of those two gold pieces? ॥ 2 ॥

### Numerical Layout:

| 16 | 10 | Target Fineness: 12 |
| :---: | :---: | :--- |
| 4 | 2 | |

**Weights found using a chosen constant of 1:**
| 16 | 10 |
| :---: | :---: |
| 4 | 2 |
<note>In this method, the difference between the target and one purity level gives the proportional weight of the *other* purity level. Here, 16 - 12 = 4 (assigned to the 10-fineness gold) and 12 - 10 = 2 (assigned to the 16-fineness gold).</note>

**Alternatively, using a constant of 2:**
| 16 | 10 |
| :---: | :---: |
| 8 | 4 |

**Or using a constant of one-half (1/2):**
| 16 | 10 |
| :---: | :---: |
| 2 | 1 |

**Elaboration:—** In this case, the target fineness (produced by the mixture) of twelve (12) was subtracted from the higher fineness of sixteen (16), leaving a remainder of 4. Then the lower fineness of ten (10) was subtracted from the target fineness of 12, leaving a remainder of 2. These two remainders, 4 and 2, were multiplied by the chosen constant <note>original: "इष्ट" (ishta)</note> of one (1). This results in the weights of the lower and higher fineness gold being 4 and 2 respectively. That is to say, the weight of the ten-fineness gold is 4, and the weight of the sixteen-fineness gold is 2. 

We can verify this: the sum of the products of the weights and their fineness is 72 <note>(10 × 4) + (16 × 2) = 40 + 32 = 72</note>. When this sum is divided by the total weight of 6 <note>4 + 2 = 6</note>, the result is 12, which matches our target fineness. 

Similarly, when 2 is chosen as the constant, the weight of the sixteen-fineness gold is 4 and the ten-fineness is 8. If one-half (1/2) is chosen as the constant, the weight of the sixteen-fineness gold is 1 and the ten-fineness is 2. In this manner, whatever constant you choose, you will find the corresponding weights required to reach the target.

<header>Method of Calculating Combinations of Meters (19)</header>

# Method of Calculating Combinations of Meters

## Now, three verses on the calculation rules for meter combinations and more—

Now, the method for understanding the types of meters and other variations is written in three verses:

> Beginning with one and increasing by one, the numbers are placed in reverse order and divided by the same numbers in their natural order. ॥ 1 ॥ 30 ॥
> Each preceding result is multiplied by the subsequent, and that result by the next, and so on. ॥ 1 ॥ 30 ॥
> This gives the variations for one, two, three, or more elements; this is known as a general rule. ॥ 2 ॥ 31 ॥
> Those who know the science of prosody use this in the calculations of meter combinations. ॥ 2 ॥ 31 ॥
> It is also used in the variations of furnace placements, in the <term>Khandameru: a tabular representation of combinations, similar to Pascal's Triangle</term>, in architecture, and in the classification of tastes in medicine; it is not detailed here for fear of being too lengthy. ॥ 3 ॥ 32 ॥

<note>original Sanskrit: एकाद्येकोत्तरा अङ्का व्यस्ता भाज्याः क्रमस्थितैः... (ekādyekottarā aṅkā vyastā bhājyāḥ kramasthitaiḥ...)</note>

**Syntax:** Numbers starting from one and increasing by one, placed in reverse, are to be divided by those placed in their natural order. The subsequent result is multiplied by the preceding one, and so on until the final digit. ॥ 1 ॥ In this way, variations for one, two, three, etc., are found. This is regarded as a general rule. Experts use this in the context of calculating meter combinations. ॥ 2 ॥ This is also used in variations of furnace placements, in the *Khandameru*, in architecture, and in the medical study of medicinal flavors; it is not stated here in detail for fear of expansion. ॥ 3 ॥

**Meaning:** Take the total number of items and write them in reverse order (starting from the total down to one). Beneath these, write the numbers in their natural order (starting from one up to the total). This creates two rows; the top row consists of the <term>bhajya: the dividend or number to be divided</term> and the bottom row consists of the <term>bhajaka: the divisor</term>. 

That is, place one beneath the first number as the divisor, and so on, writing each pair separately. Consider the first result as the "proven number." Multiply this proven number by the next dividend in the top row, then divide that result by the divisor directly beneath it. The resulting <term>labdhi: quotient</term> becomes the new "proven number." Multiply this new proven number by the next dividend and divide by its corresponding divisor. Continue this process for as many numbers as there are. This sequence yields the number of variations for choosing one item, two items, three items, and so on.

**Alternatively—** Multiply all the dividends together up to the desired point, then multiply all the corresponding divisors together. Dividing the product of the dividends by the product of the divisors will also yield the variations for one, two, three, and so on. This rule is recorded here as a general method. ॥

In the study of prosody (*Chhandashastra*), those who know the science use this to determine the possible variations of meters. It is also used to calculate the different ways air flows through doorways, in the <term>Khandameru</term> of prosody, in the science of architecture (*Shilpa-shastra*), and in the medical study of the combinations of the six tastes (*rasas*). It is not elaborated further here to keep the text concise. ॥ 1 ॥ 2 ॥ 3 ॥

<vocab>
**Chhandashchiti**: combinations of poetic meters; 
**Karanasutra**: a rule or formula for calculation; 
**Bhajya**: dividend; 
**Bhajaka**: divisor; 
**Labdhi**: quotient; 
**Khandameru**: "The Step-Pyramid," a diagram used to calculate combinations (Pascal’s Triangle); 
**Shilpashastra**: the science of arts, crafts, and architecture; 
**Vaidyaka**: the science of medicine (Ayurveda).
</vocab>

<header>The Lilavati</header>

# An Example Concerning the Calculations of Poetic Meters

## Here, some examples are shown regarding the permutations of meters:

> O friend! In the <term>Prastara: the systematic expansion or permutation of all possible forms of a meter</term> of the <term>Gayatri: a poetic meter where each quarter-verse (pada) consists of six syllables</term> meter, how many variations are there in a single foot? Tell me quickly and separately: how many variations have one <term>Guru: a "heavy" or long syllable (represented by the symbol ऽ)</term> and so on? ॥ 1 ॥

**Syntax:** O friend! If a permutation is made for a foot of the Gayatri meter, how many total variations would there be? And how many variations would there be having one, two, or more Gurus? Tell me those separately and quickly. ॥ 1 ॥

**Meaning:** O friend! In the fourth part (the 6-syllable foot) of the Gayatri meter, how many total variations (types) will there be if we calculate all permutations? Specifically, how many variations will have one, two, three, or more Gurus? Speak this clearly and quickly. ॥ 1 ॥

**The Layout:**

| 6 | 5 | 4 | 3 | 2 | 1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 |

<note>This table represents the "reverse order" digits over the "natural order" digits as prescribed by the previous rule.</note>

By following the stated method, we find:
* Variations with one Guru: 6
* Variations with two Gurus: 15
* Variations with three Gurus: 20
* Variations with four Gurus: 15
* Variations with five Gurus: 6
* Variations with six Gurus: 1
* Additionally, there is 1 variation where all syllables are <term>Laghu: a "light" or short syllable (represented by the symbol ।)</term>.

The sum of these variations for a single foot is 64. ॥ By arranging the numbers for the total syllable count of all four feet (24 syllables total) and calculating the variations for one or more Gurus in the same way, the total number of variations for the entire Gayatri meter is 16,777,216. In this way, the count of variations for all meters, up to the <term>Utkriti: a meter with 26 syllables per foot</term>, should be understood.

**The Expansion:** Following the previously mentioned method, since a foot of the Gayatri meter has 6 syllables, we write the numbers in reverse order starting from 6 down to 1. Below them, we write the numbers in natural order—one, two, and so on—resulting in these fractions: $\frac{6}{1}$ $\frac{5}{2}$ $\frac{4}{3}$ $\frac{3}{4}$ $\frac{2}{5}$ $\frac{1}{6}$.

Since there is no "previously established result" yet, we start with the first fraction:
1. Divide 6 by 1, which gives a result of **6**. This is our first established number (variations with 1 Guru). 
2. Take this result (6) and multiply it by the numerator of the next fraction (5) to get 30. Divide 30 by the denominator (2) to get **15**. This is the second established number (variations with 2 Gurus).
3. Take this result (15) and multiply it by the numerator of the next fraction (4) to get 60. Divide 60 by the denominator (3) to get **20**. This is the third established number (variations with 3 Gurus).
4. Take this result (20) and multiply it by the numerator of the next fraction (3) to get 60. Divide 60 by the denominator (4) to get **15**, which is the fourth established number.
5. Continuing this for the next...

<vocab>
**Chhandashchitti:** Calculation of meters.
**Prastara:** Permutation or expansion.
**Gayatri:** A meter with 6-syllable feet.
**Guru:** A long syllable.
**Laghu:** A short syllable.
**Vyakti:** Individual variation or type.
**Labdhi:** The quotient or result.
**Bhajya:** The dividend (number to be divided).
**Bhajaka:** The divisor.
**Nyasa:** The numerical layout or arrangement.
**Pada:** A quarter or "foot" of a verse.
</vocab>

<summary>This page demonstrates how to calculate the number of combinations of long and short syllables in a poetic meter. Using the 6-syllable Gayatri foot as an example, it shows that there are 64 possible variations for a single foot and over 16 million for a full four-line stanza.</summary>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>Miscellaneous Calculations</header>

### Miscellaneous Calculations

When the <term>Bhajya: the dividend or numerator</term> 2 from the number <gloss>2/5</gloss> was multiplied [by the previous result of 15], it became 30. This was then divided by the <term>Bhajaka: the divisor or denominator</term> 5, resulting in a <term>Labdhi: quotient or result</term> of 6. This 6 is the fifth established number. Next, this was multiplied by the dividend of the following number <gloss>1/6</gloss>, remaining 6. Dividing this by the divisor 6, the quotient 1 became the sixth established number. In this manner, the established numbers (representing the variations for one <term>Guru: a long syllable</term> and so on) are 6, 15, 20, 15, 6, and 1. When the single variation for "all <term>Laghu: short syllables</term>" is added to these, the total <term>Prastara: combinatorial expansion</term> for a single foot of the Gayatri meter results in 64 variations. <note>The calculation follows the formula for binomial coefficients (6 choose n). The results 6, 15, 20, 15, 6, 1 represent the ways to arrange 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6 long syllables respectively in a 6-syllable line.</note>

Alternatively, consider the series: <gloss>6/1</gloss> <gloss>5/2</gloss> <gloss>4/3</gloss> <gloss>3/4</gloss> <gloss>2/5</gloss> <gloss>1/6</gloss>. Here, take all the upper dividends and multiply the first by the second, then that result by the third, and so on. Similarly, multiply the lower divisors progressively and place them beneath. Then, divide the upper cumulative product by the lower cumulative product. 
For example, the first number is <gloss>6/1</gloss>. To find the second, multiply the numerator 6 by the next numerator 5, and multiply the first denominator 1 by the next denominator 2, resulting in <gloss>30/2</gloss>. For the third, multiply by the next numerator 4 and denominator 3, resulting in <gloss>120/6</gloss>. Continuing this to the end, the sequence becomes:
<gloss>6/1</gloss> <gloss>30/2</gloss> <gloss>120/6</gloss> <gloss>360/24</gloss> <gloss>720/120</gloss> <gloss>720/720</gloss>.
When the upper numbers are divided by the lower ones at each step, we obtain the same sequence: 6, 15, 20, 15, 6, and 1 variations for the respective counts of Gurus. Including the variation with no Gurus (all Laghu), the total is 64 variations. When all four feet of the meter are combined, the total variations for the entire Gayatri meter are 16,777,216. <note>This is calculated as 64 to the power of 4 ($64^4$).</note> The same method should be understood for the expansion of other meters.

->We now demonstrate how this method is applied within the <term>Khandameru: "The Fragmented Mountain," a triangular arrangement of numbers used to solve combinatorial problems, identical to Pascal's Triangle</term>.<-

---

->| 0 |<-
->| 1 | 1 |<-
->| 1 | 2 | 1 |<-
->| 1 | 3 | 3 | 1 |<-
->| 1 | 4 | 6 | 4 | 1 |<-
->| 1 | 5 | 10 | 10 | 5 | 1 |<-
->| 1 | 6 | 15 | 20 | 15 | 6 | 1 |<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of combinatorial mathematics from page 101, moving from the theoretical calculation of poetic meters to practical applications in architecture and culinary arts. It explains the "Prastara" method for listing permutations and begins a classic problem from Bhaskara's Lilavati regarding combinations of windows and tastes.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

In this <term>Khandameru: a tabular diagram used to calculate combinations, known in the West as Pascal's Triangle</term>, by performing the operations prescribed in the <term>Chhandashastra: the science of poetic meters</term>, the numbers that appear at the end represent the variations of one, two, three, and so on <term>Guru: long syllables</term> in sequence. By performing this calculation, one can determine whether a specific variation belongs to the sequence derived by the rules of prosody or not. 

The method for creating the <term>Prastara: a systematic table showing all possible permutations of long and short syllables</term> is as follows: first, write as many Guru syllables as there are total syllables in the meter. Then, place a <term>Laghu: short syllable</term> beneath the first Guru. For example:

S S S S S S                     S S S S S S
I S S S S S                     I S S S S S

->Then, write the subsequent characters exactly as they appear above, like this:<-

Here, a Laghu has been written beneath the first Guru, and the remaining characters following it are written exactly as they were in the line above. If the line is shorter than required, fill the remaining spaces with Guru syllables. For example:

I S S S S S  
I S S S S

In this instance, a Laghu was written under the first Guru, and the following characters were copied from the line above. Since one space remained at the beginning, it was filled with a Guru, resulting in I S S S S S. Continue this process until all syllables in the row become Laghu. 
S I S S S S <note>This explains the algorithm for generating binary-like sequences: to find the next row, find the first Guru, change it to Laghu, and change all preceding Laghus back to Gurus.</note>

By expanding the syllables of the fourth foot of the Gayatri meter in this manner, there are 64 sixty-four total variations.

# An Example in Architecture

->An example concerning the craft of building:<-

> O Mathematician, tell me, in the beautiful palace of the king—which is skillfully constructed, graced with elegant halls, and possesses eight windows—how many variations are there when opening the windows one, two, three, or more at a time? Also, tell me, in a single dish containing the six tastes—sweet, bitter, astringent, sour, salty, and pungent—how many different combinations of tastes can be formed by using one, two, three, and so on at once? ॥ 1 ॥
<note>original: "एकद्वित्र्यादिमूषावहनमिति महो ब्रूहि मे भूमिभर्तु- / हर्म्ये रम्येऽष्टमूषे चतुरविरचिते श्लक्ष्णशालाविशाले ॥ एकद्वित्र्यादियुक्ता मधुरकटुकषायाम्लकक्षारतिक्तै- / रेकस्मिन् षड्रसैः स्युर्गणक कति वद व्यञ्जने व्यक्तिभेदाः ॥ १ ॥"</note>

**Prose Order:** O <term>Ganaka: mathematician</term>! In the king’s beautiful <term>Harmya: palace</term>, which is skillfully built with spacious, elegant halls and eight <term>Musha: windows or vents</term>, tell me the number of combinations for opening one, two, or three windows. Furthermore, tell me how many distinct flavor combinations can be formed in a single <term>Vyanjana: dish or condiment</term> using the <term>Shad-rasa: six tastes</term>—sweet, bitter, astringent, sour, salty, and pungent—when they are combined one, two, or three at a time? ॥ 1 ॥

**Meaning:** O expert of mathematics! In a king's magnificent palace, adorned with wide, beautiful galleries built by clever craftsmen and featuring 8 eight windows, how many different ways can the wind flow through if one, two, three, four, five, six, seven, or eight windows are opened separately? Tell me this. Also, in a single preparation of food, using the six tastes—sweet, bitter, astringent, sour, salty, and pungent—how many variations result from using one at a time, two at a time...

It focuses on the "Mishraka-vyavahara" (calculations regarding mixtures), a standard chapter in Indian mathematics dealing with the number of ways to combine different items, such as architectural features or culinary flavors.</meta>

<header>Operations on Mixtures</header>

If meals are prepared using various combinations of three, four, five, or six distinct <term>Rasa: flavors or tastes; traditionally there are six: sweet, sour, salty, bitter, pungent, and astringent</term>, how many types of dishes will be created? Tell me this. ॥ 1 ॥

->Layout for the window problem: 8 7 6 5 4 3 2 1<-
->1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8<-

Results obtained for the number of <term>Mushavahana: window arrangements or openings</term> taken one at a time, two at a time, etc.:

| 8 | 28 | 56 | 70 | 56 | 28 | 8 | 1 |
|---|----|----|----|----|----|---|---|
| 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 |

->Thus, in a royal palace with eight windows, the variations of window arrangements are 255.<- <note>This total is the sum of all combinations: 8 + 28 + 56 + 70 + 56 + 28 + 8 + 1 = 255. This is equivalent to $2^n - 1$.</note>

### Now, the Second Example—

->Layout: $\frac{6}{1}$ $\frac{5}{2}$ $\frac{4}{3}$ $\frac{3}{4}$ $\frac{2}{5}$ $\frac{1}{6}$<-

The individual results obtained by combining flavors starting from one at a time:

| 6 | 15 | 20 | 15 | 6 | 1 |
|---|----|----|----|---|---|
| 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 |

->The sum of these is 63.<-

->Thus ends the section on Operations on Mixtures.<-

---

**Expansion**—In the first example, we need to find the variations for the air flowing through eight windows. For this reason, write the numbers starting from eight down to one in descending order, and below them, write the numbers from one to eight in ascending order:

$\frac{8}{1}$ $\frac{7}{2}$ $\frac{6}{3}$ $\frac{5}{4}$ $\frac{4}{5}$ $\frac{3}{6}$ $\frac{2}{7}$ $\frac{1}{8}$

Then, following the previously mentioned rule: since there is no preceding number to multiply with the first 8, we simply divide 8 by the 1 written below it, which gives a <term>Labdhi: quotient or result</term> of 8. Write this 8 in a separate place. Next, take the second upper number, 7, and multiply it by the previous result, 8, which equals 56. Divide this 56 by the number 2 written below the 7, which gives a result of 28. Write this 28 next to the 8. Now, multiply this 28 by the third number in the top row, which is 6, and divide the product by the number 3 written below the 6; this gives 56. Write this 56 after the 28. Following this method until the end, we find: 8 variations for 1 window at a time, 28 for 2 windows, 56 for 3 windows, 70 for 4 windows, and 56 for 5 windows...

For combinations of six at a time, there are 28; for seven at a time, there are 8; and for eight at a time, there is 1. When all these variations are added together, the total number of combinations is 255.

**Second Example**—To find the variations of the 6 <term>Rasas: the six traditional flavors or tastes</term>, write the numbers starting from six down to one in descending order, and below them, write the numbers from one to six in ascending order—

| 6 | 5 | 4 | 3 | 2 | 1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 |

Then, using the same method as before, take the first number of the top row (6) and divide it by the number written directly below it (1); the <term>Labdhi: the result or quotient</term> is 6. Write this 6 separately. Next, take the next number in the top row, which is 5, multiply it by the previous result (6), and divide by the number below the 5 (which is 2); the result is 15. Write this 15 next to the 6. Then, multiply 15 by the third number in the top row (4) and divide by the number below it (3); the result is 20. Write this 20 next to the 15. By continuing this process for all the numbers, you will find the variations for each combination: 6 (one at a time), 15 (two at a time), 20 (three at a time), 15 (four at a time), 6 (five at a time), and 1 (six at a time). Adding these all together, the total is 63.

# Now, the Operations on Series

Now we shall write the mathematics regarding <term>Shredhi-vyavahara: operations on mathematical series or progressions</term>. This is named "Shredhi" (Series) because the mathematics involved progresses in steps, much like a ladder or a flight of stairs.

### The Rule-Verse for the Sum of Summations:

First, we write the method for adding a series of already summed numbers (for instance, if you have ten sets of different numbers and you wish to find the total sum of all those sums quickly). In one verse:

> The number of terms plus one, multiplied by half the number of terms, is known as the Summation (the sum of numbers starting from one). That [Summation], when multiplied by the number of terms plus two and divided by three, is indeed the Sum of Summations. ॥ 63 ॥
<note>original: "सैकपदघ्नपदार्द्धमथैकाद्यङ्कयुतिः किल संकलिताख्या ॥ सा द्वियुतेन पदेन विनिघ्नी स्यात्त्रिहृता खलु संकलितैक्यम् ॥६३॥"</note>

**Prose Order:** The number of terms plus one multiplied by half the number of terms is called the <term>Sankalita: Summation</term> of numbers starting from one. Then, that sum multiplied by the number of terms plus two and divided by three is the <term>Sankalita-aikya: Sum of Summations</term>. ॥ 63 ॥

**Meaning:** (The final number in a sequence is called the <term>Pada: the number of terms or position</term>). Add one to the number of terms, then multiply by half the number of terms; the result will certainly be the sum of the numbers starting from one...

...the amount added, multiply that <term>Labdhi: result</term> by the <term>Pada: the number of terms</term> increased by two, and divide by three; then the sum of the added digits is determined. ॥ 33 ॥

## Example—

> <note>original Sanskrit: "एकादीनां नवान्तानां पृथक्संकलितानि मे ॥ तेषां संकलितैक्यानि प्रचक्ष्व गणक द्रुतम् ॥ १ ॥"</note>
> "O calculator, tell me the individual sums of the numbers starting from one and ending in nine. Then, O expert, quickly proclaim the sum of those sums!" ॥ 1 ॥

**Prose Order—** O calculator! Tell me the separate sums of the numbers from one to nine. And quickly proclaim the sum of those sums separately? ॥ 1 ॥

**Meaning—** O mathematician! State the sum of the digits written separately from one to nine. Then, state the sum of those sums (that is: the sum up to one, the sum up to two, the sum up to three, the sum up to four, the sum up to five, the sum up to six, the sum up to seven, the sum up to eight, and the sum up to nine—calculate the total of all these sums separately). ॥ 1 ॥

| | | | | | | | | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Setting:** | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 | 9 |
| **Sums** | 1 | 3 | 6 | 10 | 15 | 21 | 28 | 36 | 45 |
| **Sum of Sums** | 1 | 4 | 10 | 20 | 35 | 56 | 84 | 120 | 165 |

**Elaboration—** Here, the final digit is nine, which is why it is called the <term>Pada: the number of terms</term>. When one is added to the <term>Pada</term> (9), it becomes 10. These were multiplied by half the <term>Pada</term> <gloss>9/2</gloss>, which resulted in <gloss>90/2</gloss>. Here, the numerator was divided by the denominator, resulting in a <term>Labdhi</term> of 45. This is the sum of the digits from one to nine. In this same manner, the sums up to one, two, three, four, five, six, seven, eight, and nine are respectively: 1, 3, 6, 10, 15, 21, 28, 36, and 45. 

Next, to find the separate sums of these sums—up to the first quantity, the second, the third, the fourth, the fifth, the sixth, the seventh, the eighth, and the ninth—we use the rule mentioned above. We take the <term>Labdhi</term> (the previous sum) of 45 and multiply it by the <term>Pada</term> (9) increased by 2—which is to say, 11. This equals 495. This was divided by 3, resulting in one hundred sixty-five (165). This is the sum of the sums up to nine. By following this method, the sequence for the sum of sums up to one, two, three, four, five, six, seven, eight, and nine is respectively: 1, 4, 10, 20, 35, 56, 84, 120, and 165. In this way, the <term>Sankalana: summation</term> of any number of digits can be determined.

### Rule-verse for the Summation of Squares and Cubes —

A simple method for calculating the sum of the squares and the sum of the cubes of numbers in a sequence starting from one, presented in a single verse:

> <note>original Sanskrit: "द्विघ्नपदं कुयुतं त्रिविभक्तं सङ्कलितेन हतं कृतियोगः ॥ सङ्कलितस्य कृतेः सममेकाद्येकाद्यघनैक्यमुदीरितमाद्यैः ॥ ३४ ॥"</note>
> Twice the <term>Pada: number of terms</term> plus one, divided by three, and multiplied by the <term>Sankalita: sum of the sequence</term>, is the sum of the squares. The square of that same <term>Sankalita</term> is declared by the ancients to be the sum of the cubes of the numbers starting from one. ॥ 34 ॥

**Prose Order—** Twice the <term>Pada</term> plus one, divided by three, and multiplied by the sum of the sequence, results in the sum of the squares. The square of the sum of the sequence has been declared by the ancients as the sum of the cubes starting from one. ॥ 34 ॥

**Meaning—** Double the <term>Pada</term> (the last number in the sequence) and add one. Divide that result by three. Multiply the resulting figure by the <term>Sankalita</term> (the sum of the sequence up to that <term>Pada</term>). The result will be the sum of the squares of the numbers from one up to that point. <note>Formula: Σn² = [ (2n+1) / 3 ] × [ n(n+1) / 2 ]</note> To find the sum of the cubes, simply square the <term>Sankalita</term> (the sum of the sequence). ॥ 34 ॥

---

->Example-<-

> <note>original Sanskrit: "तेषामेव च वर्गैक्यं धनैक्यं च वद द्रुतम् ॥ कृतिसंकलनामार्गे नाकुला यदि ते मतिः ॥ १ ॥"</note>
> "Quickly tell me the sum of the squares and the sum of the cubes of those very same numbers (from one to nine). If your mind is not confused by the method of summing squares and cubes!" ॥ 1 ॥

**Prose Order—** Tell me quickly the sum of the squares and the sum of the cubes of those same numbers? If your mind is not confused in the path of summing squares. ॥ 1 ॥

**Meaning—** State the sum of the squares and the sum of the cubes of those same digits from one to nine? If your intellect is not overwhelmed by the calculation. ॥ 1 ॥

**Setting—**
| 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 | 9 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Sum of Squares** | 1 | 5 | 14 | 30 | 55 | 91 | 140 | 204 | 285 |
| **Sum of Cubes** | 1 | 9 | 36 | 100 | 225 | 441 | 784 | 1296 | 2025 |

**Elaboration—** The individual squares of these numbers should be understood by the method mentioned in the <term>Parikarmashtaka: the eight fundamental operations</term>. Then, using the rule mentioned above, we find the sum of those squares. For instance, here we wish to find the sum of the squares up to nine. Therefore, according to the rule, we double the <term>Pada</term> (9), which makes 18. We add one to this, making 19. We divide this by 3, which gives <gloss>19/3</gloss>. We multiply this by the <term>Sankalita</term> (sum) of the sequence, which is 45. This results in 285. This is the sum of the squares of the numbers from 1 to 9.

Now, to find the sum of the cubes of those same numbers: according to the rule mentioned above, we take the <term>Sankalita</term> of the <term>Pada</term> (9), which is 45, and square it. This results in 2025.

<note>The vocabulary listed in the source: Lilavati (the text), Krityadiyoga (summation of squares etc.), Karanasutra (rule-verse), Vargaikya (sum of squares), Ghanaikya (sum of cubes), Sankalita (sum of a sequence), Parikarmashtaka (eight basic operations), Bhaskaracharya (the author), Ganita (mathematics).</note>

<header>Operations with Progressions</header>

This is the sum of the cubes of the numbers from one to nine. In this same way, one can determine the sum of squares and the sum of cubes for as many numbers as one desires. ॥

## Rule-verse for finding the last term and other values in a progressive increase—

In a situation where an amount of wealth is given on the first day, and then a constant increase is added every subsequent day, the following verse describes the method for finding the <term>Madhyadhana: middle wealth or average</term>, the <term>Antyadhana: last wealth or final term</term>, and the <term>Sarvadhana: total wealth</term> (that is, how much was given on the middle day, how much on the final day, and the total sum given over the entire period). ॥

**The number of terms minus one, multiplied by the increase and added to the first term, is the last term. That [last term] added to the first term and halved is the middle wealth. That [middle wealth] multiplied by the number of terms is the total wealth; this is what is called "calculated" [the sum] by the wise. ॥ 35 ॥**

<note>original Sanskrit: "व्येकपदघ्नचयो मुखयुक्स्यादन्त्यधनं मुखयुग्दलितं तत् ॥ मध्यधनं पदसंगुणितं तत्सर्वधनं गणितञ्च तदुक्तम् ॥ ३५ ॥"</note>

**Prose Order—** The common difference multiplied by the number of terms minus one, added to the first term, results in the last term. That added to the first term and halved results in the middle wealth. That multiplied by the number of terms results in the total wealth. This is declared as the "Calculated Sum." ॥ 35 ॥

**Meaning—** (The amount by which the wealth is increased daily is called the <term>Chaya: common difference</term>.) Multiply the common difference by the number of terms (minus one), then add the wealth given on the first day (<term>Mukha: literally "face" or "start"</term>). This gives the amount of wealth given on the final day. To find the middle day's wealth, take the final day's wealth, add the first day's wealth to it, and divide the result by two. Finally, multiply this middle wealth by the total number of days (the <term>Pada: position or term-count</term>) to find the total wealth given over the entire duration. Mathematicians use the word <term>Ganita: the calculated</term> to refer to this total sum. ॥ 35 ॥

### Example—

**A man gave four <term>Drammas</term> on the first day to the Brahmins and then proceeded to give daily with an increase of five. Tell me quickly, friend, how many <term>Drammas</term> were given by him in a <term>Paksha</term>? ॥ 1 ॥**

<note>original Sanskrit: "आद्ये दिने द्रम्मचतुष्टयं यो दत्त्वा द्विजेभ्योऽनुदिनं प्रवृत्तः ॥ दातुं सखे पञ्चचयेन पक्षे द्रम्मा वद द्राक्कति तेन दत्ताः ॥ १ ॥"</note>

**Prose Order—** O friend! Tell me quickly: if a man, having given four <term>Drammas</term> on the first day to Brahmins, continued to give with an increase of five daily, how many <term>Drammas</term> were given by him in a <term>Paksha</term>? ॥ 1 ॥

**Meaning—** O friend! If a person gave 4 <term>Drammas</term> <note>Dramma: a silver coin used in ancient and medieval India</note> on the first day and intended to increase the gift by 5 <term>Drammas</term> every day, how many total coins did he distribute by the end of a <term>Paksha</term> <note>Paksha: a lunar fortnight, consisting of 15 days</note>? Answer this quickly! ॥ 1 ॥

->Statement for Calculation— First term (a): 4; Common difference (d): 5; Number of terms (n): 15.<-
->Middle Wealth (Average): 39<-
<note>Calculation: Last term = 4 + (15-1) * 5 = 74. Middle wealth = (4 + 74) / 2 = 39. Total wealth = 39 * 15 = 585.</note>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration from page 107. Previously, the author defined the rule for finding the last term, the average (middle term), and the sum of an arithmetic progression. Here, he applies those rules to specific examples, walking the reader through the calculations for wealth distributed over several days with a constant daily increase.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

# Final Wealth: 74
# Total Wealth: 585

**Explanation—** The amount given on the first day is called the <term>Adidhana: the first term or initial wealth</term>. The rate at which the wealth increases daily is called the <term>Chaya: the common difference or increase</term>. The number of days the wealth is given is called the <term>Gaccha: the period or number of terms</term>. 

In this specific example, the first term is 4, because four was given on the first day. The increase is 5, because the amount given grows by five each day. The period is 15, because the distribution lasted for fifteen days.

Now, to find the <term>Madhyadhana: middle wealth or average</term>, according to the previously stated rule, we take the <term>Pada: the number of terms</term>, which is 15, and subtract one (1). This leaves 14. We multiply this 14 by the increase of 5, which results in 70. To this, we add the "mouth" <note>the 'mouth' or 'mukha' is another term for the first term of a series</note> of 4, which equals 74. This is the <term>Antyadhana: final wealth</term>; meaning, on the final fifteenth day, 74 was given. 

Then, to this same final wealth of 74, we add the first term of 4, totaling 78. We halve this to get 39. This is the middle wealth. We then multiply this middle wealth of 39 by the period of 15 days, which results in 585. This is the <term>Sarvadhana: total wealth</term>. Thus, over the fifteen days, a total of 585 was given. In summary: the middle wealth is 39, the final wealth is 74, and the total sum is 585.

---

->**Another Example—**<-
->**Second Example—**<-

> Where the first term is seven, the increase is five, and the period is eight; tell me, what are the middle and final wealth amounts, and what is the total sum? ॥ 2 ॥

<note>original Sanskrit verse: "आदिः सप्त चयः पञ्च गच्छोऽष्टौ यत्र तत्र मे ॥ मध्यान्त्यधनसंख्ये के वद सर्वधनञ्च किम् ॥ २ ॥"</note>

**Prose Order—** Where the first term is seven, the increase is five, and the period is eight, tell me: what are the values of the middle and final wealth, and what is the total sum? ॥ 2 ॥

**Meaning—** In a case where the initial amount is 7, the daily increase is 5, and the duration is 8 days, what will be the values for the middle wealth and the final wealth? And what will the total sum be? Please tell me this. ॥ 2 ॥

**Statement of Values—** 
First term: 7 | Increase: 5 | Period: 8

Middle wealth: 49/2 <note>equivalent to 24.5</note> 
Final wealth: 42
Total sum: 196 ॥

<summary>This page provides a practical walkthrough of arithmetic progression formulas, demonstrating how to calculate the final term, average, and total sum using two specific numerical examples.</summary>
<keywords>arithmetic progression, first term, common difference, sum of a series, Bhaskara II, Lilavati, Indian mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical exploration of arithmetic progressions from page 108. Having established how to find the final term and the total sum when the first term is known, the author now addresses the logical problem of finding a "middle term" when the number of terms is even. He then introduces a new formula to work backward: finding the first term when the total sum, number of terms, and common difference are already known.</meta>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>Mathematics of Progressions</header>

In the case of an even number of days <note>or terms</note>, because there is no single middle day, one should understand that the average of the wealth of the days immediately before and after the center must be the wealth of the middle day.

**Expansion—** Here, the <term>Mukha: first term</term> is 7, the <term>Chaya: common increase</term> is 5, and the <term>Gaccha: number of terms</term> is 8. Following the rule stated previously: subtract one (1) from the eight terms, leaving 7. Multiply these 7 by the increase of 5, which equals 35. To this, add the first term of 7, which results in 42. This is the amount given on the final day, known as the <term>Antyadhana: final wealth</term>. 

Now, add this final wealth of 42 to the first term of 7, which equals 49. Halve this to get 49/2. This is the <term>Madhyadhana: middle wealth</term> given on the middle day. Multiply this middle wealth of 49/2 by the 8 days, which results in 196. This is the <term>Sarvadhana: total wealth</term>, meaning the total amount given over eight days. 

Even though eight is an even number and there is no specific single "middle day," one should establish conviction in the result by taking the wealth of the days surrounding the center and using their average as the middle wealth.

### A Half-Verse Formula for Finding the First Term

In cases where the middle wealth, the final wealth, or the total wealth are known, along with the common difference, but the initial wealth is unknown, the method for finding that initial wealth is described in the following half-verse:

> The total sum divided by the number of terms, minus half the product of the common difference and the number of terms less one, shall be the "mouth" <note>the first term</note>. 

**Word-for-word breakdown:** The <term>Ganita: the total sum</term> divided by the <term>Gaccha: number of terms</term>, then diminished by half of the <term>Chaya: common difference</term> multiplied by the <term>Pada: terms</term> minus one, results in the <term>Vadana: the mouth or first term</term>.

**Meaning:** Take the total sum of the progression and divide it by the number of terms. From the resulting quotient, subtract half of the value found by multiplying the common difference by the number of terms minus one. The remainder is the <term>Mukha: the first term or initial wealth</term>.

->**Example—**<-

> O dear son! If we know the total sum of the series is one hundred and five, the number of terms is seven, and the common difference is three, then tell me, what is the first term?

**Word-for-word breakdown:** O friend who gives great joy! Indeed, we know the <term>Shredhiphala: sum of the series</term> is 105, the terms are 7, and the increase is 3. In that case, tell me the first term.

**Meaning:** My dear friend, if the total sum is 105, the number of days is 7, and the daily increase is 3, what was the amount given on the very first day?

<summary>This page explains how to calculate the average (middle term) for an even number of terms and introduces the algebraic formula to find the first term of an arithmetic progression when the sum is known.</summary>
<keywords>arithmetic progression, first term (mukha), common difference (chaya), total sum (sarvadhana), middle term (madhyadhana), Lilavati, Bhaskara II</keywords>

<header>Lilavati</header>

**Setup**— First term: [Unknown]; Common Difference: 3; Number of terms: 7; Total sum: 105. Resulting first term: 6.

**Expansion**— In this example, the <term>Chaya: common difference</term> is 3, the <term>Gaccha: number of terms</term> is 7, and the <term>Sarvadhana: total sum</term> is 105. Only the <term>Adi-dhana: first term</term> is unknown. To find it, according to the rule stated above, we divide the total sum of 105 by the 7 terms, which gives a <term>Labdhi: quotient</term> of 15. From the number of terms (7), we subtract one to get the <term>Pada: reduced term</term>, which is 6. We multiply this by the common difference of 3, resulting in 18. We take half of this, which is 9. Subtracting these 9 from the quotient of 15, we are left with 6. This is the first term. We can verify this because if we calculate the total sum starting with 6, we get the same 105.

### Half-Verse Rule for Finding the Common Difference

The method for finding the common difference when the first term, total sum, and number of terms are known is described in the following half-verse:

> The sum divided by the number of terms, minus the first term, divided by half of the number of terms minus one, shall be the common difference. || 36 ||
<note>original: "गच्छहृतं धनमादि विहीनं व्येकपदार्द्धहृतं च चयः स्यात्"</note>

**Prose Order**— The sum divided by the number of terms, subtracted by the first term, and divided by half of the number of terms minus one, is the common difference.

**Meaning**— Divide the total sum by the number of terms. From the resulting quotient, subtract the first term. Divide the remaining value by half of the number of terms after one has been subtracted. The result should be understood as the common difference. || 36 ||

### Example

> O wise one, a king traveled eighty yojanas in seven nights to seize the elephants of his enemy. If he traveled two yojanas on the first day, tell me, by what constant daily increase did he travel? || 1 ||
<note>A yojana is a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8 to 9 miles.</note>

**Prose Order**— O wise one! A king who reached the enemy's city in seven nights, covering eighty yojanas to capture the enemy's elephants, traveled two yojanas on the first day. Tell me, by what daily increase in distance did he proceed? || 1 ||

**Meaning**— O friend, foremost in cleverness! A king reached the city of his enemy in seven days, covering a distance of 80 yojanas to slay the enemy's elephants. Here, the king traveled 2 yojanas on the first day; so, say with certainty, how much more distance did he cover each subsequent day? || 1 ||

**Setup**— First term: 2; Common difference: [Unknown]; Number of terms: 7; Total sum: 80. Resulting common difference: 22/7.
<note>The result 22/7 indicates a daily increase of 3 and 1/7 yojanas.</note>

**Expansion**— In this example, the <term>Adi-dhana: first term</term> is 2, because he traveled two yojanas on the first day. The <term>Gaccha: number of terms</term> is 7, because he arrived in seven days. The total sum is 80...

### Rule-Verse for Finding the Number of Terms

Where the first term, the middle term, the total sum, and the common difference are known, but the number of terms is unknown, the method for finding the number of terms is written in this verse:

> Multiply the total sum by twice the common difference; to this, add the square of the difference between half the common difference and the first term. Take the square root of this sum, subtract the first term, add half the common difference, and divide the result by the common difference. This, the teachers say, is the number of terms. || 37 ||
<note>original Sanskrit: श्रेढीफलादुत्तरलोचनघ्नाच्चयार्द्धवक्रान्तरवर्गयुक्तात् ॥ मूलं मुखोनं चयखण्डयुक्तं चयोद्धृतं गच्छमुदाहरन्ति ॥</note>

**Prose Order:** The teachers state that the square root of (the total sum multiplied by twice the common difference, plus the square of the difference between half the common difference and the first term), minus the first term, plus half the common difference, all divided by the common difference, is the <term>Gaccha: number of terms</term>. || 37 ||

**Meaning:** Multiply the total sum by the common difference, which has itself been multiplied by 2. Next, find the difference between half the common difference and the first term; square this difference and add it to the previous result. Take the square root of the resulting sum. From this square root, subtract the first term and add half the common difference. Finally, divide by the common difference. The mathematical teachers call the resulting quotient the "Gaccha" (number of terms or days). || 37 ||

### Example

> O friend! If a man gave three <term>Drammas: a type of silver coin</term> on the first day and continued to give with a common difference of two, and thus gave three hundred and sixty Drammas to the Brahmins, tell me quickly: in how many days was this given? || 1 ||
<note>original Sanskrit: द्रम्मत्रयं यः प्रथमेऽह्नि दत्त्वा दातुं प्रवृत्तो द्विचयेन तेन ॥ शतत्रयं षष्ट्यधिकं द्विजेभ्यो दत्तं कियद्भिर्दिवसैर्वदाशु ॥</note>

**Prose Order:** O friend! If someone, having given three Drammas on the first day to the Brahmins, proceeded to give with a common difference of two, then tell me quickly: in how many days were three hundred and sixty Drammas given? || 1 ||

**Meaning:** O dear friend! If a donor gave 3 Drammas to the Brahmins on the first day and then began giving with a daily increase of 2 Drammas, then tell me soon: in how many days did he give a total of 360 (three hundred and sixty) Drammas? || 1 ||

**Setup:** First term (a): 3 | Common diff (d): 2 | No. of terms (n): [Unknown] | Total sum (S): 360 | Resulting Gaccha: 18

**Expansion:** In this example, the first term is 3; the common difference is 2; and the total sum is 360. These are all known, but the number of terms (Gaccha) is unknown. Therefore, to find the Gaccha according to the rule stated above, we multiply the common difference of 2 by two, which gives 4. With this...

The <term>Sarvadhana: total sum</term> of 360 was multiplied <note>by twice the common difference, which is 4</note>, resulting in 1440 (one thousand four hundred and forty). Then, the difference between half of the <term>Chaya: common difference</term> <gloss>1</gloss> and the <term>Mukha: first term</term> <gloss>3</gloss> was taken, which left <gloss>2</gloss>. This was squared to get 4. This 4 was added to the 1440 (the total sum multiplied by twice the common difference), resulting in 1444 (one thousand four hundred and forty-four). The square root of this was taken, which gave 38 (thirty-eight). From this, the <term>Adi: first term</term> <gloss>3</gloss> was subtracted, leaving 35 (thirty-five). ### Rule-verse for finding the result when the ratio is doubled or otherwise

Now, the method for knowing the <term>Dvigunottara-phala: result of a doubling progression</term> (where the wealth given on the first day is doubled on the second, and the second day's amount is doubled on the third, and so on) is written in one and a half verses:

> ->When the number of terms is odd, subtract one and note the multiplier; when even, halve it and note the square.<-
> ->Continue until the terms are exhausted. Working backwards from the last, the result of the multiplier and the square,<- || 38 ||
> ->minus one, divided by the multiplier minus one, and multiplied by the first term, is the sum of a geometric progression.<-
<note>original Sanskrit: विषमे गच्छे व्येके गुणकः स्थाप्यः समेऽर्द्धिते वर्गः ॥ गच्छक्षयान्तमन्त्याद्वयस्तं गुणवर्गजं फलं यत्तत् ॥ ३८ ॥ व्येकं व्येकगुणोद्धृतमादिगुणं स्याद् द्विगुणोत्तरे गणितम् ॥</note>

**Prose explanation:** When the <term>Gaccha: number of terms</term> is odd, subtract one from it and set aside the "multiplier." If the number of terms is even, divide it by half and set aside the "square." Continue this process until the count reaches zero. This creates a sequence of "multipliers" and "squares." Then, starting from the last result, square the value if "square" was noted, or multiply it if "multiplier" was noted. In this way, work back to the top. From the final resulting value, subtract one. Divide that remainder by the multiplier decreased by one. Finally, multiply the quotient by the <term>Adi-dhana: first term</term>. The product will be the total sum (the result for doubling or other geometric progressions). || 38 ||

### Example

> ->A person pledged to give a pair of cowries to a beggar, doubling the amount every day.<-
> ->How many gold coins does he give in a month?<- || 1 ||
<note>original Sanskrit: पूर्वं वराटकयुगं येन द्विगुणोत्तरं प्रतिज्ञातम् ॥ प्रत्यहमर्थिजनाय स मासे निष्कान्ददाति कति ॥ १ ॥</note>

**Prose explanation:** A man promised a beggar a <term>Varataka-yugam: pair of cowrie shells</term> on the first day, and promised to double the amount every day thereafter. At the end of a month (30 days), how many <term>Nishka: gold coins</term> does he give? || 1 ||

# Mathematics of Series

**The Problem:**—A person promised a beggar two <term>Varatakas: cowrie shells used as currency</term> on the first day and agreed to give double that amount every day thereafter. Tell me, how many <term>Nishkas: high-value gold or silver coins</term> will he give in one month (30 days)? || 1 ||

**The Set-up:**—First term (a): 2; Multiplier/Ratio (r): 2; Number of terms/days (n): 30.

**Cowries obtained:** 2,147,483,646. **When divided by the value of a Nishka, the result is:** 104,857 **Nishkas, 9 Drammas, 9 Panas, 2 Kakinis, and 6 Varatakas.**

**The Expansion**—In this example, the <term>Adidhana: initial wealth</term> is two; the increase is a doubling; and the <term>Gaccha: number of terms</term> is one month, which is 30 days. Here, the total sum is to be found. According to the prescribed method: since the number of terms (30) is even, it is halved to 15, and the instruction for "Square" is noted. The remaining 15 is odd, so one is subtracted, leaving 14, and the instruction for "Multiplier" is noted. Since 14 is even, it is halved to 7, and "Square" is noted. The remaining 7 is odd, so one is subtracted, leaving 6, and "Multiplier" is noted. Since 6 is even, it is halved to 3, and "Square" is noted. The remaining 3 is odd, so one is subtracted, leaving 2, and "Multiplier" is noted. Since 2 is even, it is halved to 1, and "Square" is noted. Finally, 1 is odd, so one is subtracted, leaving zero, and the "Multiplier" is noted. By performing the operation in this manner, zero remains.

<note>This is a classic algorithm for exponentiation. By recording "square" for halving and "multiplier" for subtracting one, the mathematician builds a roadmap to calculate 2 to the 30th power efficiently.</note>

| Label | Value |
|:---|:---|
| Square—Square | 1,073,741,824 |
| Multiplier—2 Multiplier | 32,768 |
| Square—Square | 16,384 |
| Multiplier—2 Multiplier | 128 |
| Square—Square | 64 |
| Multiplier—2 Multiplier | 8 |
| Square—Square | 4 |
| Multiplier—2 Multiplier | 2 |

Now, working in reverse (from the bottom up), since the instruction is "Multiplier," the number 2 is written (as it was agreed to double the gift daily). Above the Multiplier is "Square," so that 2 is squared, and 4 is written next to "Square." Above that is "Multiplier," so this 4 is multiplied by 2, and 8 is written. Above that is "Square," so 8 is squared, and 64 is written. Above that is "Multiplier," so 64 is multiplied by 2, and this continues until the top is reached, resulting in 1,073,741,824. One is subtracted from this, leaving 1,073,741,823. This figure is then divided by the multiplier minus one <note>since the multiplier is 2, 2 - 1 = 1, the value remains the same</note>...

<note>The text continues the calculation of the total sum (S) using the formula $S = a \times (r^n - 1) / (r - 1)$. The table tracks the powers of 2 produced by the squaring/multiplying steps.</note>

<summary>This page demonstrates the calculation of a geometric progression where a gift of 2 cowries doubles daily for 30 days. It uses a specific power-of-two algorithm to find the sum, eventually converting the massive number of cowries into higher denominations of currency.</summary>
<keywords>geometric progression, cowries, Nishka, doubling sequence, Pingala's algorithm, mathematical series, currency conversion</keywords>

These <term>Varatakas: cowrie shells</term> were then converted into higher denominations, yielding 104,857 <term>Nishkas: gold or large silver coins</term>, 9 Drammas, 9 Panas, 2 Kakinis, and 6 Cowries.

->Example—<-
->Second Example—<-

### O friend! Where the first term is two, and the daily increase is triple the previous, ###
### and the period is seven days, tell me: what is the total sum there? || 2 || ###

**The Analysis:**—O friend! Where the <term>Adi: starting value</term> is two, the <term>Vriddhi: increase</term> is triple each day, and the <term>Gaccha: duration</term> is seven days—tell me, what does the <term>Ganitam: total sum of the series</term> become? || 2 ||

**The Meaning:**—O friend! In a case where the initial wealth is 2, the daily increase (the common ratio) is three times, and the duration is 7 days, what will be the <term>Sredhiphala: sum of the progression</term>? Speak that.

**The Setup:**—Initial (a): 2; Ratio (r): 3; Duration (n): 7. The calculated sum is 2186.

**The Expansion:**—In this example, the initial wealth is two (2), the ratio is three (3), and the duration is seven (7). We do not yet know the total wealth. To find it, following the rule previously mentioned: the duration 7 is an odd number, so 1 is subtracted (leaving 6), and "Multiplier" is noted. The remaining 6 is an even number; it is halved (leaving 3), and "Square" is noted. Then 3 is odd, so 1 is subtracted (leaving 2), and "Multiplier" is noted. The remaining 2 is even; it is halved (leaving 1), and "Square" is noted. Finally, 1 is odd; 1 is subtracted (leaving 0), and "Multiplier" is noted. Nothing remains.

| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Multiplier—3 Multiplier | .... | 2187 |
| Square—Square | .... | 729 |
| Multiplier—3 Multiplier | .... | 27 |
| Square—Square | .... | 9 |
| Multiplier—Multiplier | .... | 3 |

<note>This table shows the binary exponentiation method working from the bottom up: 3 (base), then squared is 9, then multiplied by the base (3) is 27, then squared is 729, then multiplied by the base (3) is 2187. This equals 3 to the power of 7 ($3^7$).</note>

Following the sequence of "Multiplier" and "Square" generated above, we begin from the bottom: where "Multiplier" is written, the ratio 3 is placed. Above that, "Square" is written, so the square of 3 (9) is written above it. Above that, "Multiplier" is written, so 9 is multiplied by 3 (27) and written above. Above that, "Square" is written, so the square of 27 (729) is written above. Above that, "Multiplier" is written, so 729 is multiplied by 3 (2187) and written above. This completes the sequence.

From this result, 1 is subtracted, leaving 2186. This is then divided by the ratio minus one <note>The text says "divided by the multiplier lessened by one," i.e., $3 - 1 = 2$</note> and multiplied by the initial wealth (2). The final quotient obtained is 2186. This is the total sum.
