<meta>This page continues the discussion on arithmetic and geometric progressions, specifically applying these mathematical principles to the field of prosody (the study of poetic meters). While the previous page calculated geometric growth in terms of currency and shells, this section uses the same "doubling" logic to determine how many different poetic meters can exist based on the number of syllables.</meta>

<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>Operations on Progressions</header>

The method for knowing the differences between the varieties of meters—Even, Semi-even, and Uneven—is written here in one and a half verses in the *Arya* meter:

> In the <term>Gaccha: duration</term> measured by the syllables of a quarter-verse, when the <term>Chaya: ratio</term> is two, the <term>Gunavarga-phalam: result of the geometric series</term>... || 39 ||
> ...is the total number of <term>Samavrittas: even meters</term>. The square of that, and the square of that square...
> ...each minus its respective base, shall be the count of the <term>Ardhasamas: semi-even meters</term> and the <term>Vishamas: uneven meters</term>. || 40 ||

**The Connection:**—In a <term>Gaccha: period/index</term> measured by the number of syllables in a quarter-verse, where the <term>Chaya: common ratio</term> is two, the resulting <term>Gunavarga-phalam: product of the power</term> is the total number of <term>Samavrittas: even meters</term>. The square of that value, and the square of that square, when each is reduced by its own base, provides the count of the <term>Ardhasamas: semi-even meters</term> and the <term>Vishamas: uneven meters</term> respectively. || 40 ||

**The Meaning:**—Consider the number of syllables in one quarter of a verse as the <term>Gaccha: duration</term> and double the ratio <note>original: "chaya"</note>. Then, following the previously mentioned rule for calculating geometric powers, the result will be the total number of <term>Samavrittas: even meters</term> <note>meters where all four lines are identical</note>. If you square that result and then subtract the original number of even meters from it, the remainder will be the count of <term>Ardhasama-vrittas: semi-even meters</term> <note>meters where the first and third lines match, and the second and fourth lines match</note>. Finally, if you take the square of that first squared result and subtract the first squared result from it, the remainder will be the number of <term>Vishama-vrittas: uneven meters</term> <note>meters where all four lines are different</note>. || 40 ||

### Example—

> Of the even, the semi-even, and the uneven meters—
> tell me quickly, my friend, their individual counts within the Anushtubh meter! || 1 ||

**The Connection:**—O friend! Tell me quickly the individual counts of the even, semi-even, and uneven variations found within the <term>Anushtubh: a meter of eight syllables per line</term>. || 1 ||

**The Meaning:**—O friend! Regarding the <term>Anushtubh meter</term>, calculate and tell me separately and quickly how many even, semi-even, and uneven varieties exist. || 1 ||

<term>Nyasa: The Setup</term>—The Multiplier/Ratio: 2. The <term>Gaccha: syllables</term>: 8.
Resulting count of Even meters: 256.
Count of Semi-even meters: 65,280.
Count of Uneven meters: 4,294,901,760.

**The Expansion:**—In this example, the question concerns the <term>Anushtubh</term> meter. For this reason, the eight syllables of a quarter of the Anushtubh meter are taken as the <term>Gaccha</term>, and the ratio 2 is used <note>representing the two choices for each syllable: light or heavy</note>. Then the geometric power calculation is performed. Here, the initial ratio is 2. Since 8 is an even number, it is halved, and the "Square" is noted. The remaining 4 is halved, "Square" is noted. The remaining 2 is halved, "Square" is noted. Finally, the remaining 1 is odd; 1 is subtracted and "Multiplier" is noted. Now, working from the bottom up...

<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

When 8 was squared, it became 64; then, because the instruction for multiplication is written, the square of 64 was multiplied by the doubled <term>Chaya: ratio</term> of 4, which resulted in 256. This is the total number of <term>Samavrittas: even meters</term> <note>meters where all four lines of the verse are identical in rhythm</note>. Then, 256 was squared, resulting in 65,536; its base of 256 was subtracted from this, leaving 65,280, which is the number of <term>Ardha-samavrittas: semi-even meters</term> <note>meters where the 1st/3rd lines and 2nd/4th lines match</note>. Then, the previous square of 65,536 was itself squared, resulting in 4,294,967,296; its base was subtracted from this, leaving a remainder of 4,294,901,760. This is the number of <term>Vishamavrittas: uneven meters</term> <note>meters where all four lines have different rhythms</note>.

An "even meter" is one in which the syllables of all four quarters <note>original: "charana"</note> are identical. A "semi-even meter" is one in which the first and third quarters are of one type, and the second and fourth quarters are of another type.

->An "uneven meter" is one in which all four quarters are different from one another.<-

## Here ends the Operations on Progressions in the Lilavati.

->This concludes the first section.<-

<vocab>Lilavati, Square, Even Meter, Semi-even Meter, Uneven Meter, Operations on Progressions</vocab>

It marks the formal conclusion of the chapter on mathematical progressions and the first major section of the treatise.</summary>

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>Calculations of Plane Figures</header>

# Now Begins the Second Section.

---

->First, the Calculations of Plane Figures.<-
->First, we explain the geometry of areas:<-

Regarding this, in the calculations of plane figures, there are three divisions: the base, the upright, and the hypotenuse. To find any one of these from the other two, here are two verses of procedural formulas—

In the study of geometry, there are three parts: the <term>Bhuja: Base</term>, the <term>Koti: Upright</term>, and the <term>Karna: Hypotenuse</term>. To understand the third when two are known, the method is written in these two verses:

> In a triangle or a quadrilateral, whichever side is chosen as the base, the other side that stands in the opposite <note>meaning perpendicular</note> direction is called the <term>Koti: Upright</term> by those who know this science. || 1 ||
> The square root of the sum of their squares is the <term>Karna: Hypotenuse</term>. From the difference between the squares of the base and the hypotenuse, the square root is the upright. From the difference between the squares of the upright and the hypotenuse, the square root is the base. || 2 ||

**Prose Arrangement:** In a triangle or a quadrilateral, whichever is the desired <term>Bhuja: Base</term>, the other side in the direction opposing it is called the <term>Koti: Upright</term> by experts. || 1 ||

The square root of the sum of their squares is the <term>Karna: Hypotenuse</term>. From the difference between the squares of the base and the hypotenuse, the root is the upright. From the difference between the squares of the upright and the hypotenuse, the root is the base. || 2 ||

**Meaning:** In a triangular or quadrilateral field, that which is considered the <term>Bhuja: Base</term> <note>original: "bhuj"</note>, the second side that intercepts it is called the <term>Koti: Upright</term> <note>original: "koti"</note> by those learned in the science of mathematics.

(The line that connects the tips of the upright and the base is called the <term>Karna: Hypotenuse</term>). In a right-angled triangle, by summing the squares of the base and the upright and taking the square root, the result is the measure of the hypotenuse. By squaring the base and the hypotenuse and taking their difference, the square root of the remainder is the measure of the upright. By squaring the upright and the hypotenuse and taking their difference, the square root of the remainder is the measure of the base. || 2 ||

## Example:

> Where the upright is four and the base is three, what is the hypotenuse? Given the upright, base, and hypotenuse, calculate the base from the upright and hypotenuse. || 1 ||

<header>Lilavati</header>

# Lilavati

**Prose Arrangement:** Where the <term>Upright (Koti)</term> is four and the <term>Base (Doh/Bhuja)</term> is three, what is the <term>Hypotenuse (Shruti)</term> there? Tell me the upright from the base and hypotenuse, and tell me the base from the upright and hypotenuse. || 1 ||

**Meaning:** Where the measure of the upright is 4 and the measure of the base is 3, what will be the measure of the hypotenuse? And knowing the base and hypotenuse, what will be the measure of the upright? And knowing the upright and hypotenuse, what will be the measure of the base? Tell me that. || 1 ||

**Mathematical Notation:**

<margin>
<note>A diagram of a right-angled triangle with the vertical side labeled 4 and the horizontal side labeled 3.</note>
</margin>
Upright: 4. Base: 3. Square of the base: 9. Square of the upright: 16.
From the sum of these: 25. The square root is 5, which is the <term>Hypotenuse (Karna)</term>.

->Now, finding the Upright from the Hypotenuse and Base:<-

<margin>
<note>A diagram of a right-angled triangle with the slanted hypotenuse labeled 5 and the horizontal base labeled 3.</note>
</margin>
Hypotenuse: 5. Base: 3. The difference of their squares: 16.
The square root of this is the Upright: 4.

->Now, finding the Base from the Upright and Hypotenuse:<-

<margin>
<note>A diagram of a right-angled triangle with the vertical side labeled 4 and the slanted hypotenuse labeled 5.</note>
</margin>
Upright: 4. Hypotenuse: 5. The difference of their squares: 9.
The square root of this is the Base: 3.

**Expansion:** Here, the horizontal line at the bottom is considered the <term>Base (Bhuja)</term> <note>original: "bhuj"</note>, and the straight line that intersects it vertically is the <term>Upright (Koti)</term> <note>original: "koti"</note>. The slanted line that connects both these lines is the <term>Hypotenuse (Karna)</term> <note>original: "karna"</note>. Now, here we know the measure of the base is 3 and the measure of the upright is 4, but we do not know what the measure of the hypotenuse is. For this reason, according to the formula mentioned above:
<margin>
<note>A triangle diagram labeled with '4 Upright', '3 Base', 'Hypotenuse 5', and the word 'Triangle' inside.</note>
</margin>
The square of base 3 was calculated, which became 9; and the square of upright 4 was calculated, which became 16. Their sum was calculated, which became 25; its square root was taken, and 5 was obtained. This is the measure of the hypotenuse in this figure.

<header>Measurement of Figures (119)</header>

## Now, an example of finding the perpendicular side (Koti) by knowing the hypotenuse and the base—

<margin>
<note>Diagram of a right-angled triangle labeled: Hypotenuse 5, Base 3, and the Perpendicular (Upright) which is being calculated.</note>
</margin>

In this example, we know the <term>hypotenuse (Karna)</term> measure is 5 and the <term>base (Bhuja)</term> measure is 3, but the measure of the <term>perpendicular (Koti)</term> is unknown. For this reason, following the method stated previously, we square the hypotenuse (5), which results in 25, and square the base (3), which results in 9. We then find the difference between them, leaving a remainder of 16. By taking the <term>square root (Mula)</term> of this, the result is 4. This is the measure of the perpendicular side.

## Now, an example of obtaining the base (Bhuja) by knowing the perpendicular and the hypotenuse—

<margin>
<note>Diagram of a right-angled triangle labeled: Perpendicular 4, Hypotenuse 5, and the Base which is being calculated.</note>
</margin>

In this example, we know the perpendicular measure is 4 and the hypotenuse measure is 5, but the measure of the base is unknown. Therefore, following the previous method, we square the perpendicular (4) to get 16 and square the hypotenuse (5) to get 25. Their difference is 9. Taking the square root of 9, the result is 3. This is the measure of the base side.

### A computational rule in one and a half verses for knowing this in another way—

The following one and a half verses describe another method for determining the base, perpendicular, and hypotenuse:

> The sum of the squares of two quantities is produced by adding the square of their difference to twice their product. || 3 ||
> Similarly, the product of the sum and the difference of two quantities results in the difference of their squares. This should be understood everywhere by the wise.
<note>original Sanskrit: राश्योरन्तरवर्गेण द्विघ्ने घाते युते तयोः ॥ वर्गयोगो भवेदेवं तयोर्योगान्तरहतिः ॥ ३ ॥ वर्गान्तरं भवेदेवं ज्ञेयं सर्वत्र धीमता ॥</note>

**Prose Connection:** If one must find the sum of the squares of two quantities, let them multiply the two quantities together, then multiply that product by 2. By adding the square of the difference of those same two quantities to that result, the sum of their squares is achieved. Similarly, if one must find the difference between the squares of two quantities, let them add the two quantities together and multiply that sum by the difference between the two quantities; this results in the difference of their squares. Let the wise person know this to be true in all cases. || 3 ||

<meta>CC-0. Late Pt. Manmohan Shastri Collection Jammu. Digitized by eGangotri</meta>

### Regarding the previously mentioned example, "the perpendicular side is four"—

Statement: The <term>perpendicular (Koti)</term> is 4. The <term>base (Bhuja)</term> is 3. Their <term>product (Ghata)</term> is 12. Doubled, it is 24. Added to the square of their difference (which is 1), the sum of the squares is 25. The <term>square root (Mula)</term> of this is the <term>hypotenuse (Karna)</term>, which is 5.
<note>The text uses the algebraic identity: 2ab + (a-b)² = a² + b² to find the square of the hypotenuse.</note>

### Now, calculating the perpendicular side from the hypotenuse and the base—

The hypotenuse is 5 and the base is 3. Their sum is 8. Again, multiplied by their difference (which is 2), the difference of their squares is 16. The square root of this is 4, which is the perpendicular side.
<note>The text uses the identity: (a+b)(a-b) = a² - b².</note>

### Now, finding the base—

->The perpendicular is 4 and the hypotenuse is 5; thus, the base becomes 3.<-

**Expansion**—In this example, we know the base and the perpendicular, but we do not know the measure of the hypotenuse. For this reason, following the method stated above, the two quantities 4 and 3 were multiplied, resulting in twelve; these were multiplied by 2, resulting in 24. To this, the square of the difference between those same two quantities (4 and 3), which is 1, was added, resulting in 25. According to the first method, this is the sum of the squares of the base and perpendicular. Taking its square root, we obtain five as the <term>result (Labdhi)</term>; this is the measure of the hypotenuse.

**Technical Terms:**
<term>Lilavati</term>: The title of the mathematical treatise, named after Bhaskara’s daughter.
<term>Koti</term>: The vertical or upright side of a right-angled triangle.
<term>Bhuja</term>: The horizontal base side of a right-angled triangle.
<term>Karna</term>: The hypotenuse; the side opposite the right angle.
<term>Ghata</term>: The product of multiplication.
<term>Vargayoga</term>: The sum of squares ($a^2 + b^2$).
<term>Mula</term>: The square root.
<term>Labdhi</term>: The quotient or the obtained result.
<term>Tribhuja</term>: A triangle (literally "three-arms").

## Example—
### Second Example—

> Where the side is measured as three and a quarter, and the perpendicular is of the same measure, O mathematician, tell me quickly, what is the measure of the hypotenuse? || 2 ||
<note>original: "सांघ्नित्रयमितो बाहुर्यत्र कोटिश्च तावती..."</note>

**Prose construction:**—O mathematician! Where the <term>base (bāhu)</term> is measured as three and a quarter, and the <term>perpendicular (koṭi)</term> is the same, what is the measure of the <term>hypotenuse (karṇa)</term> there? Tell me quickly. || 2 ||

**Meaning:**—O mathematician! Where the measure of the base is 3 ¼ (three and a quarter) and the perpendicular is also 3 ¼; what will be the measure of the hypotenuse there? Tell me this immediately. || 2 ||

**Statement:**—Base: 13/4. Perpendicular: 13/4. The sum of their squares is 169/8. Of this—

->Because it lacks a perfect square root, this hypotenuse remains <term>irrational (karṇī-gata)</term>.<-
->The method for knowing its approximate square root is as follows:<-

<margin>
13/4
Tri.
13/4
</margin>

**Expansion**—Here, the sum of the squares of the base 13/4 [and the perpendicular 13/4] became 338/16. <note>Calculation: (169/16) + (169/16) = 338/16</note>. When <term>reduction (apavartana)</term> by two was applied, it took the form 169/8. Now, according to the first method, its square root should be taken, but here a [perfect] square root is not found; for this reason, it is called an irrational root. In such cases, an exact root is not found, but a number close to the root can be known. Its method is written:

<margin>
13/4
Tri.
13/4
</margin>

> From the product of the numerator and denominator multiplied by a large desired square, the square root [of that product], divided by the denominator multiplied by the root of the [desired] square, becomes the approximate [root]. || 3 ||
<note>original: "वर्गेण महतेष्टेन हताच्छेदांशयोर्वधात्..." This describes a method for approximating the square root of a fraction by scaling the numbers.</note>

**Prose construction:**—The square root which is obtained from the product of the numerator and denominator multiplied by a large desired square—that root, divided by the denominator multiplied by the root of the multiplier, becomes the approximate [root]. || 3 ||

<summary>This page demonstrates how to find the base of a triangle when the hypotenuse and perpendicular are known, and introduces the concept of irrational numbers (karani) using an example with fractional sides (3 1/4). It begins explaining a rule for calculating approximate square roots for non-perfect squares.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagorean theorem, irrational roots, Karani, square root approximation, 13/4, geometry, Lilavati, Bhaskara, fractions</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the calculation from the previous page, where the sum of the squares was found to be 169/8, a value without a perfect square root. The text now explains the method for finding a decimal-like approximation and introduces a new rule for generating right-angled triangles.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

**Rule:—** Multiply the product of the numerator and the denominator by a large "chosen number" <note>An arbitrary large perfect square used to increase precision, similar to adding decimal places</note> that has a square root. Take the square root of this product. Divide this by the product of the denominator and the square root of the chosen number. The resulting quotient will be a value extremely close to the true square root. || 3 ||

**Calculation:—** This is the <term>irrational hypotenuse (karṇa-karaṇī)</term> <gloss>169/8</gloss>. The product of its denominator and numerator is 1,352. Multiplied by <term>ten thousand (ayuta)</term>, it becomes 13,520,000. Its approximate square root is 3,677. The root of the "chosen multiplier" <note>The square root of 10,000</note> is 100. This multiplied by the denominator (8) is 800. Dividing by this, the quotient is the approximate root: <gloss>4 477/800</gloss>. This is the hypotenuse. The same method applies everywhere.

**Explanation—** In the example mentioned above, <gloss>169/8</gloss> is the "square" of the hypotenuse <note>literally "the hypotenuse's surd"</note>. We multiplied its denominator and numerator together to get 1,352. This was then multiplied by a large square number, namely 10,000, resulting in 13,520,000. We took the square root of this to get 3,677. This was divided by 800 (which is the denominator 8 multiplied by 100, the square root of our chosen multiplier 10,000). The result of the division is 4 <gloss>477/800</gloss>. This is the closest possible number to the true root and is the measure of the hypotenuse. This process should be understood for all such cases.

---

->**Two verses on the formula for constructing right-angled triangles—**<-

The method for creating a right-angled triangle from a given base or perpendicular is written in two verses:

> Pick a number as the side. Multiply this by twice a chosen number. Divide that by the square of the chosen number minus one. The result is the perpendicular. To find the hypotenuse, multiply that perpendicular by the chosen number and subtract the original side. This creates a right-angled triangle. || 4 ||
<note>original: "इष्टो भुजोऽस्माद्विगुणैष्टनिघ्नादिष्टस्य कृत्यैकवियुक्तयाप्तम्..."</note>

**Prose Construction:—** Take a chosen number and a side. Multiply the side by twice the chosen number. Divide this by the square of the chosen number minus one; the result is the <term>perpendicular (koṭi)</term>. Set that aside, multiply it by the chosen number, and subtract the side to find the <term>hypotenuse (karṇa)</term>. This forms a <term>right-angled triangle (jātya-tryasra)</term>. || 4 ||

**Meaning:—** 1. Imagine a "chosen number" <note>a variable used for the calculation</note> and imagine a <term>side (bhuja)</term>. 2. Double the chosen number and multiply the side by it. 3. Divide this result by the square of the chosen number minus one. The quotient you get is the perpendicular. 4. Write that perpendicular down elsewhere, multiply it by the chosen number, and subtract the original side; the remainder is the hypotenuse. In this way, a right-angled triangle is formed. By imagining various "chosen numbers," many different types of right-angled triangles can be created. || 4 ||

<summary>This page explains a method for approximating square roots of fractions by using large multipliers and introduces a geometric formula for generating the side lengths of right-angled triangles from any given number.</summary>
<keywords>approximation of surds, square roots, Lilavati, Bhaskara, right-angled triangle construction, hypotenuse calculation, Indian mathematics</keywords>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>Practice of Geometry</header>

### Example—

> If the base is twelve, tell me quickly, O mathematician, the various pairs of perpendiculars and hypotenuses that are not irrational <note>meaning they result in whole numbers rather than square roots</note>, using the two methods. || 5 ||
<note>original: "भुजे द्वादशके यौ यौ कोटिकर्णौवनेकधा ॥ प्रकाराभ्यां वद् क्षिप्रं तौ तावकरणीगतौ ॥ ५ ॥"</note>

**Prose Order—** O mathematician! For a <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note> of twelve, tell me quickly and in various ways the pairs of <term>perpendiculars</term> <note>koṭi</note> and <term>hypotenuses</term> <note>karṇa</note> that are non-irrational, according to the two methods. || 5 ||

**Meaning—** O calculator! In a figure where the measure of the <term>base</term> <note>the horizontal side of a triangle</note> is assumed to be 12, tell us quickly the various measures of the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>the vertical side</note> and the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>the diagonal side</note> that result from choosing different <term>desired numbers</term> <note>iṣṭa; an arbitrary number used to generate the other sides</note>. Provide these using both the method previously mentioned and the one that follows, ensuring the results are whole numbers. || 5 ||

**Mathematical Layout—** The chosen base is 12. Let the chosen multiplier <note>iṣṭa</note> be 2. 
1. Multiply the base (12) by twice this multiplier (4), which equals 48. 
2. Square the chosen multiplier (2² = 4) and subtract one, leaving 3. 
3. Divide the first result by this: 48 divided by 3 gives the <term>perpendicular</term>, which is 16. 
4. To find the <term>hypotenuse</term>: Multiply the perpendicular (16) by the chosen multiplier (2) and subtract the base (12). Thus, 32 minus 12 equals 20.
<note>This generates the triangle sides 12, 16, and 20. This is a 3-4-5 triangle scaled by 4.</note>

Or, using 3 as the chosen multiplier: the perpendicular is 9 and the hypotenuse is 15.
<note>This generates the sides 12, 9, and 15. This is a 3-4-5 triangle scaled by 3.</note>

Or, using 5 as the chosen multiplier: the perpendicular is 5 and the hypotenuse is 13.
<note>This generates the sides 12, 5, and 13, which is a classic Pythagorean triple.</note>

**Elaboration—** Here, the measure of the base is assumed to be 12, and the measures of the perpendicular and hypotenuse are unknown. Therefore, following the rule mentioned above, we assume a "chosen number" of 2. Doubling this gives 4. Multiplying the assumed base (12) by this gives 48. Then, squaring the chosen number (2) gives 4; subtracting one from this leaves a remainder of 3. Dividing the 48 by this 3 results in 16...

Sixteen was obtained as the quotient; this is the measure of the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note>. This same perpendicular was multiplied by the <term>chosen number</term> <note>iṣṭa</note> 2, which equals 32. From this, the <term>assumed base</term> <note>bhuja</note> 12 was subtracted, leaving 20 as the remainder. This is the measure of the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note>.

<margin>9</margin>
<margin>15</margin>
<margin>Tri.</margin>
<margin>12</margin>
When 3 was taken as the chosen number, then the chosen number 3 was doubled to 6. The assumed base 12 was multiplied by this, resulting in 72. Then, the chosen number 3 was squared (9) and 1 was subtracted, leaving 8 as the remainder. Dividing by this, 9 was obtained as the quotient. This is the measure of the perpendicular. This same perpendicular was multiplied by the chosen number 3, resulting in 27. From this, the base 12 was subtracted, leaving 15 as the remainder. This is the measure of the hypotenuse.

<margin>5</margin>
<margin>13</margin>
<margin>Tri.</margin>
<margin>12</margin>
When 5 was taken as the chosen number, then by performing the operations according to the previously mentioned method, the measure of the perpendicular is 5 and the measure of the hypotenuse is 13. In this way, however many different numbers you choose, you will find that many various types of perpendiculars and hypotenuses.

## Second method for the same:
### The second method for this is shown:

> The base is chosen; its square is divided by a chosen number and the result is placed in two positions. It is then decreased and increased by the chosen number and then halved. These are the perpendicular and hypotenuse respectively. Or, starting from the perpendicular, the base and hypotenuse are found, and they are non-irrational. || 6 ||
<note>original: "इष्टो भुजस्तत्कृतिरिष्टभक्ता द्विःस्थापितेष्टोनयुतार्द्धिता वा ॥ तौ कोटिकर्णाविति कोटितो वा बाहुश्रुती चाकरणीगते स्तः ॥ ६ ॥"</note>

**Prose Order—** The <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note> is assumed. The square of that base is divided by a <term>chosen number</term> <note>iṣṭa</note> and placed in two positions. It is decreased and increased by the chosen number and then halved. These are the perpendicular and the hypotenuse. Or, from the perpendicular, the base and the hypotenuse are found, and they are <term>non-irrational</term> <note>akarṇīgata; meaning they are rational whole numbers</note>. || 5 ||

**Meaning—** First, assume a chosen number and assume a base. Divide the square of the assumed base by the chosen number. Write the resulting quotient in two places. In one place, add the assumed chosen number; in the other place, subtract it. Then, halve both results. In this way, the perpendicular and the hypotenuse are found. If one performs the aforementioned operation starting with the perpendicular, then the base and hypotenuse are proven to be non-irrational. || 5 ||

->The first example given should be understood here as well.<-

# Plane Geometry.

<margin>35, 37, 12, Tri. <note>original: "त्रि०" abbreviation for triangle</note></margin>
**Elaboration**—The <term>chosen multiplier</term> <note>iṣṭa</note> was assumed to be 2. The <term>chosen base</term> <note>iṣṭabhuja</note> was assumed to be 12. When the square of the assumed base was calculated, it became 144. This was divided by the chosen multiplier 2, and the <term>quotient</term> <note>labdhi</note> was 72. Writing this in two places, in one place the chosen multiplier was subtracted, resulting in 70; in the other place, the chosen multiplier was added, resulting in 74. When these numbers in both places, 70 and 74, were halved, they became 35 and 37. This is the measure of the perpendicular and hypotenuse; that is, the measure of the perpendicular is 35 and the measure of the hypotenuse is thirty-seven (37). At that point, the shape of the field <note>the triangle</note> becomes like this.

<margin>16, 20, 12, Tri.</margin>
When four (4) was assumed as the chosen multiplier, then according to the method mentioned above, the square of the chosen base 12 was calculated, which became 144. This was divided by the chosen multiplier 4, and 36 was obtained as the quotient. Writing these in two places, in one place the chosen multiplier 4 was subtracted and in one place it was added, resulting in 32 and 40. When these were halved, they became 16 and 20. This is the measure of the perpendicular and hypotenuse.

<margin>9, 15, 12, Tri.</margin>
When six (6) was assumed as the chosen multiplier, then the square of the base twelve (12), which is 144, was divided by the chosen multiplier 6, and 24 was obtained as the quotient. Writing these in two places, in one place the chosen multiplier was subtracted and in one place it was added, resulting in 18 and 30. When these were halved, they became 9 and 15. This is the measure of the perpendicular and the hypotenuse.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration from the previous page, shifting focus from finding the hypotenuse and perpendicular from a known base to a new rule: finding the perpendicular and base when only the hypotenuse is known.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>
<header>The Lilavati.</header>

In this manner, by assuming a measure for the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note>, various types of <term>bases</term> <note>bhuja</note> and <term>hypotenuses</term> <note>karṇa</note> can be produced, depending on the variety of the <term>chosen number</term> <note>iṣṭa</note> used in the calculation.

### Now, a verse on the rule for obtaining the perpendicular and the base from a chosen hypotenuse—

->A rule in one verse for bringing forth the perpendicular and the base from a given hypotenuse:<-
->**Double the hypotenuse, multiplied by a chosen number, and divided by the square of that chosen number plus one; what is obtained**<-
->**is the perpendicular. That, multiplied separately by the chosen number—the difference between that and the hypotenuse here is the base. || 6 ||**<-

**Prose Connection:** The quotient obtained from the doubled hypotenuse multiplied by a chosen number and divided by the square of that chosen number increased by one, shall be the perpendicular. That same perpendicular, multiplied by the chosen number, and the difference between that product and the hypotenuse, shall be the base. || 6 ||

**Meaning:** Double the hypotenuse and multiply it by a chosen number. Divide those digits by the square of the chosen number plus one. The resulting <term>quotient</term> <note>labdhi</note> is the perpendicular. In that same figure, multiply the perpendicular by the chosen number; the remainder left after finding the difference between that result and the hypotenuse is the measure of the base. || 6 ||

### Example—

->**If the hypotenuse measures eighty-five, what would the perpendicular**<-
->**and base be, such that they do not involve square roots? O learned one, tell me quickly! || 4 ||**<-

**Prose Connection:** O learned one! In a figure where the hypotenuse measures eighty-five, whatever the perpendicular and base may be that are <term>rational integers</term> <note>original: "akaraṇī gata", meaning not involving surds or irrational roots</note>, tell those to me quickly. || 4 ||

**Meaning:** O mathematician! In a field where there is a hypotenuse of 85 (eighty-five); in that field, whatever the numbers for the perpendicular and the base might be, state them quickly in rational form. || 4 ||

**Calculation:** The hypotenuse is 85. This doubled is 170. Multiplied by the chosen number two, it is 340. The square of the chosen number 2 is 4; with one added, it is 5.
Dividing [340 by 5], the perpendicular produced is 68. This [68] multiplied by the chosen number is 136. The hypotenuse 85 subtracted from this leaves the base as 51.

<note>This calculation produces a right-angled triangle with sides 51, 68, and hypotenuse 85. This is a multiple of the 3-4-5 triangle (17 × 3, 17 × 4, 17 × 5).</note>

Alternatively, with the chosen number as four: the perpendicular is 40, and the base is 75.

<note>This produces a right-angled triangle with sides 40, 75, and hypotenuse 85. This is a multiple of the 8-15-17 triangle (5 × 8, 5 × 15, 5 × 17).</note>

<summary>This page introduces a rule for deriving the two shorter sides of a right-angled triangle when only the hypotenuse is given, using a "chosen number" (iṣṭa) to generate rational integer solutions. It provides a worked example using a hypotenuse of 85, demonstrating two different sets of sides (51, 68 and 75, 40) that satisfy the Pythagorean theorem.</summary>
<keywords>hypotenuse, perpendicular, base, Lilavati, Bhaskara II, Pythagorean triples, rational sides, geometry, chosen number, iṣṭa</keywords>

# Practice of Plane Geometry (127)
<page-num>127</page-num>

**Explanation**—In this figure, the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note> is known to be 85. Now, to find the <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note> and the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note>, according to the previously stated rule, the hypotenuse 85 is multiplied by 2, which equals 170. This is then multiplied by a <term>chosen number</term> <note>iṣṭa</note>, 2, resulting in 340. This sum is divided by 5 (the square of the chosen number 2, which is 4, plus 1). The result is a <term>quotient</term> <note>labdhi</note> of 68; this is the measure of the perpendicular. Now, the perpendicular 68 is multiplied by the chosen number 2, equaling 136. When the hypotenuse 85 is subtracted from this, 51 remains; this is the measure of the base. 

<margin>

| \ 85
|__\
 51
</margin>

When 4 is taken as the chosen number, the hypotenuse 85 is multiplied by 2, again resulting in 170. This is multiplied by the chosen number 4, resulting in 680. This is divided by 17 (the square of the chosen number 4, which is 16, plus 1). The result is a quotient of 40; this is the measure of the perpendicular.

<margin>

     __
    / |
85/   | 75
 /____|
</margin>

Then, this same perpendicular 40 is multiplied by the chosen number 4, equaling 160. When the hypotenuse 85 is subtracted from this, 75 remains; this is the measure of the base. In this manner, whatever value is imagined as the chosen number, the shape of the figure will change accordingly. For this reason, through these variations, the figure can be of many different types.

### Again, the rule for that calculation in a different way, in verse—

Now, another method for knowing the perpendicular and the base when the measure of the hypotenuse is known is written in one verse:

->**The hypotenuse doubled and divided by the square of a chosen number plus one;**<-
->**the hypotenuse minus that result is the perpendicular. That same result multiplied by the chosen number is the base. || 7 ||**<-
<note>original: "iṣṭavargeṇa saikena dvighnaḥ karṇo'thavā hṛtaḥ | phalonah śravaṇaḥ koṭiḥ phalamiṣṭaguṇaṃ bhujaḥ || 7 ||"</note>

**Prose Connection:** The doubled hypotenuse should be divided by the square of a chosen number increased by one. Then, the hypotenuse diminished by that quotient is the perpendicular. Alternatively, the quotient multiplied by the chosen number is the base. || 7 ||

**Meaning:** Multiply the hypotenuse by 2. Divide those digits by the square of a chosen number plus one. Subtract the resulting quotient from the hypotenuse; the remainder is the measure of the perpendicular. Furthermore, multiply that same quotient by the chosen number; the resulting product is the measure of the base. || 7 ||

# Lilavati
<page-num>128</page-num>

**In the previous example**—this method should be understood in the context of the first example itself.

**Layout: Hypotenuse <note>karṇa</note> 85. Here, with 2 as the <term>chosen number</term> <note>iṣṭa: an arbitrary number selected by the mathematician to solve the problem</note>, the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note> and <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note> are produced as 51 and 68.**

**Or with 4 as the chosen number: Perpendicular 75, Base 40. Here <gloss>4</gloss> the difference between the base and perpendicular is only in name, and not in their actual nature.**

---

**Detailed Explanation**—In a figure where the measure of the hypotenuse is 85, to find the base and perpendicular using the second method: multiply the hypotenuse 85 by two, which equals 170. Divide this by 5 (which is the square of the chosen number 2, plus one). This gives a <term>quotient</term> <note>labdhi</note> of 34. Subtract this from the hypotenuse 85, and the remaining 51 is the measure of the perpendicular. Multiply that same quotient 34 by the chosen number 2, and the resulting 68 is known as the measure of the base. This determines the shape of the figure.

|__\
 68
</margin>

When 4 is taken as the chosen number, and the same calculation is performed, the measure of the perpendicular becomes 75 and the measure of the base becomes 40. Now, a doubt may arise here: according to the first method, using 4 as the chosen number with a hypotenuse of 85 resulted in a perpendicular of 40 and a base of 75. But by this second method, the perpendicular is 75 and the base is 40. This seems to be the exact opposite of the first method. The answer to this is that the difference between the "perpendicular" and the "base" is merely a matter of naming; there is no difference in their actual geometric form or relationship.

### Now, the rule for finding the Base, Perpendicular, and Hypotenuse from two chosen numbers, in verse—

The method for knowing all three—base, perpendicular, and hypotenuse—by choosing two numbers is described in this verse:

> **Twice the product of two chosen numbers is the perpendicular; the difference of their squares is the base.** 
> **The sum of their squares is the hypotenuse, which results in a rational number.** <note>original Sanskrit: iṣṭayorahatirdvighnī koṭirvargāntaraṃ bhujaḥ ॥ kṛtiyogastayorevaṃ karṇaścākaraṇīgataḥ ॥ 8 ॥</note> **(8)**

**Prose Construction:** Twice the product of two chosen numbers shall be the perpendicular. The difference of their squares shall be the base. Similarly, the sum of their squares shall be the hypotenuse, which is a <term>rational number</term> <note>akaraṇīgata: literally "not involving a surd," meaning a whole number or a perfect square</note>. (8)

Perp |               / Hypotenuse
(2ab)|              /  (a² + b²)
     |_____________/
</margin>

# Practice of Geometry <note>original: kṣetra-vyavahāra</note>

**Rule:** Multiply two <term>chosen numbers</term> <note>iṣṭa: arbitrary integers selected by the mathematician to initiate a calculation</note> by each other and then multiply that product by two; the result is the measure of the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note>. Square both chosen numbers and find the difference between them; the remainder is the measure of the <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note>. Sum the squares of both chosen numbers; the resulting sum is the measure of the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note>, which will be a rational number <note>akarṇī-gata: literally "not involving a surd," meaning the side is a whole number rather than a square root</note>. || 8 ||

### ->Example<-

->O wise friend! Tell me quickly those three unknown measures—<-
->the perpendicular, base, and hypotenuse—by which a right-angled triangle is formed. || 5 ||<-

**Prose Connection:** O wise friend! Quickly tell me all three of these unknown measures—the perpendicular, the base, and the hypotenuse—through which a right-angled triangle <note>jātya-tryasra: a "true" or "noble" triangle, referring to one with a perfect 90-degree angle</note> is produced. || 5 ||

**Meaning:** O clever friend! Tell me the measures of the <term>perpendicular</term>, <term>base</term>, and <term>hypotenuse</term> that form a right-angled triangle, even without being given any initial side. || 5 ||

**Layout:** Here, taking the <term>chosen numbers</term> as 2 and 1, the perpendicular, base, and hypotenuse are produced as:
->4, 3, 5<-

**Expansion—** Taking **2** and **1** as the <term>chosen numbers</term> <note>iṣṭa</note> to determine the perpendicular, base, and hypotenuse according to the previously mentioned rule:
First, the two chosen numbers are multiplied together, resulting in 2; this is then multiplied by 2, resulting in **4**. This is the measure of the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note>.

### Verse for the Rule of Separation when the Sum of Hypotenuse and Perpendicular and the Base are Known—

->The method for knowing the individual measures of the hypotenuse and perpendicular when their sum and the base are known, in one verse:<-

> The square of the distance between the tip and the root of the bamboo on the ground, divided by the height of the bamboo; the result is used to both add to and subtract from the bamboo's height. Half of those two results will be, in order, the two portions of the bamboo: the hypotenuse and the perpendicular. || 9 ||
> <note>original: vaṃśāgramūlāntarabhūmivargo vaṃśoddhatastena pṛthagyutonau ॥ vaṃśau tadarddhe bhavataḥ krameṇa vaṃśasya khaṇḍe śrutikoṭirūpe ॥ 9 ॥</note>

**Prose Construction:** The square of the ground distance between the tip and the root of the bamboo should be divided by the height of the bamboo <note>the total height, which represents the sum of the hypotenuse and perpendicular</note>. By that result, the height of the bamboo is separately increased and decreased. Half of those two results are, in order, the two parts of the bamboo in the form of the hypotenuse and the perpendicular. || 9 ||

**Meaning:** Whatever the measure of the earth <note>distance</note> between the tip of the bamboo and its root <note>base</note>, its square should be divided by the measure of the bamboo—that is, the sum of the hypotenuse and the perpendicular. Whatever the quotient obtained, it should be added to and subtracted from the sum of the hypotenuse and perpendicular—that is, the...

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general method of determining sides of a right-angled triangle to a specific applied problem known as the "Broken Bamboo." It concludes the instruction for Verse 9 and applies it to Verse 6 of the "Examples" section.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>Geometry of Plane Figures</header>

...add [the result] to the sum in one place and subtract it from the sum in another. Then, divide both of these by two to find the measure of the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note> and <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note> respectively. ॥ 9 ॥

->Example<-

### If on level ground a bamboo of thirty-two cubits' measure is standing, O Mathematician, 
### and by the force of the wind it is broken at a certain point; 
### its tip touches the ground at a distance of sixteen cubits. 
### Tell me, at what height from the base was it broken? ॥ 6 ॥

**Syntax:** O Mathematician! O Wise One! If a bamboo of thirty-two <term>cubits</term> <note>pāṇi/hasta: a measure of length from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note> is fixed in the ground and is broken by the force of the wind, and its tip touches the ground at a distance of sixteen cubits <note>nṛpa-mita: "measured by kings," a poetic code for the number 16</note>, then tell me: at how many cubits from the root was it broken? ॥ 6 ॥

**Meaning:** My dear mathematician! A bamboo thirty-two cubits long is fixed in the earth. If it breaks at some point due to the wind and its tip hits the ground 16 cubits away from the base, then say, at what height above the root did this bamboo break? ॥ 6 ॥

<margin>Statement</margin>

<note>A right-angled triangle diagram is positioned here to illustrate the mathematical problem of the broken bamboo. The vertical side (perpendicular) represents the standing portion of the bamboo, the hypotenuse represents the broken upper portion leaning to the ground, and the horizontal base represents the distance from the root to where the tip touches the soil.</note>

32 &emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; **Distance from root to tip: 16 &emsp; Total bamboo: 32**
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; **This is the sum of perpendicular and hypotenuse: 32. Base: 16**
&emsp;&emsp; 20 &emsp; **The upper and lower segments are found to be: 20 and 12.**
12
&emsp;&emsp; 16

**Expansion—** Here, the distance between the tip of the bamboo and the root on the ground is 16, which is the measure of the <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note>. The total measure of the bamboo, 32, is the sum of the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note> and the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note>. Now, to find the perpendicular and hypotenuse separately according to the aforementioned rule:
The square of the distance between the tip and the root (the base), which is 16, was calculated to be 256. 
This was then divided by the sum of the hypotenuse and perpendicular (the total length of the bamboo), which is 32. The result <note>labdhi</note> was 8. 
This [8] was added to the sum (32) in one place and subtracted from it in another, resulting in 40 and 24. 
These two were then halved separately, yielding 20 and 12 respectively. That is, the measure of the hypotenuse is 20 and the measure of the perpendicular is 12.

<summary>This page presents the famous "Broken Bamboo" problem, a classic application of the Pythagorean theorem to find the height of a break given the total length and the distance the tip falls from the base.</summary>

<page-num>132</page-num>
<header>Līlāvatī</header>

This means that the <term>bamboo</term> <note>vaṃśa</note> broke 12 cubits above the root. Specifically, the measurement of the ground between the tip of the bamboo and its root becomes the <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note>; the height from the root to the point of breaking becomes the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭikā</note>; and the portion from the point of breaking to the tip becomes the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note>.

## A verse providing the formula for separating the base and hypotenuse when their sum and the perpendicular are known—

The method for finding the individual measurements of the base and hypotenuse when the sum of the base and hypotenuse and the measure of the perpendicular are known:

> The square of the pillar is divided by the distance of the snake from its hole; the result should be subtracted from that distance between the snake and the hole. Half of that remaining measure in cubits is the distance from the hole where the meeting of the peacock and snake occurs. ॥ 10 ॥
<note>original Sanskrit: स्तम्भस्य वर्गोऽहिबिलान्तरेण भक्तः फलं व्यालबिला- / न्तरालात् ॥ शोध्यं तदर्द्धप्रमितैः करैः स्याद्विलाग्रतो / व्यालकलापियोगः ॥ १० ॥</note>

**Prose Order:** The square of the pillar is divided by the distance between the snake and the hole; the resulting figure is then subtracted from the distance between the snake and the hole. Half of that measurement in cubits <note>kara/hasta</note> is the distance from the hole where the union of the snake and peacock occurs. ॥ 10 ॥

**Meaning:** Take the square of the pillar's height and divide it by the distance to the snake's hole. Subtract the result from the distance between the snake and the hole. Calculate half of the remainder; that number of cubits represents the distance from the hole where the snake and peacock meet. ॥ 10 ॥

## Example—

> At the base of a pillar is a hole, and on top of it sits a pet peacock. The pillar is nine cubits high. Seeing a snake at a distance three times the pillar’s height crawling toward its hole, the peacock pounced obliquely upon it. Tell me quickly: at what distance from the hole do they meet, assuming their speeds are equal? ॥ 7 ॥
<note>original Sanskrit: अस्ति स्तम्भतले बिलं तदुपरि क्रीडाशिखण्डी स्थितः / स्तम्भे हस्तनवोच्छ्रिते त्रिगुणितस्तम्भप्रमाणान्तरे ॥ दृष्ट्वाऽहिं बिलमाव्रजन्तमपतत्तिर्य्यक्स तस्योपरि / क्षिप्रं ब्रूहि तयोर्बिलात्कतिमितैः साम्येन गत्योर्युतिः ॥ ७ ॥</note>

**Prose Order:** There is a hole at the base of a pillar. Atop that pillar, which is nine cubits high, sits a pet peacock. Seeing a snake approaching the hole from a distance three times the height of the pillar, the peacock pounced diagonally upon it. If their speeds are equal, tell me quickly: at what distance from the hole did their meeting occur? ॥ 7 ॥

<vocab>
**Bamboo** (vaṃśa), **Base** (bhuja), **Perpendicular** (koṭi), **Hypotenuse** (karṇa), **Pillar** (stambha), **Snake** (ahi), **Hole** (bila), **Pet peacock** (krīḍā-śikhaṇḍī), **Tripled** (triguṇita), **Separation/Analysis** (pṛthakkaraṇa)

It broke at a height of 12 (the perpendicular). The tip touched the ground 16 units away (the base). The leaning part (hypotenuse) is 20. Note that 12² + 16² = 144 + 256 = 400, which is 20².</note>

# Meaning:
There was a pillar, and at its base was a snake’s hole. A peacock was perched on top of the pillar. The pillar where the peacock was dancing was nine 9 <term>cubits</term> <note>hand-lengths or 'hastas'</note> high. At a distance of twenty-seven 27 cubits away from the pillar, a snake was seen rushing toward its hole. At that moment, the peacock spotted the snake coming; it flew down from the pillar and descended diagonally—that is, along the path of the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note>—to catch the snake. Tell me, at how many cubits from the hole did the peacock and the snake meet? ॥ 7 ॥

---

## Diagrammatic Layout:
<margin>
  9 <note>Pillar/Perpendicular</note>
   / \
  /   \ 15 <note>Peacock's path/Hypotenuse</note>
 /     \
/_______\ 27 <note>Total distance</note>
  12 <note>Distance from hole/Base</note>
</margin>
Pillar: 9. Distance from the snake's hole: 27.
The resulting cubits for the meeting point from the hole: 12.

---

## Explanation:
In this example, the pillar, which is 9 cubits high, is the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note>. The distance of 27 cubits to the snake’s hole is the sum of the <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note> and the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note>. Now, to find the individual measurements of the base and the hypotenuse, according to the rule mentioned above:

Square the measure of the pillar (the perpendicular), which is 9; the result is 81. Divide this by the distance between the snake and the hole (the sum of the hypotenuse and the base), which is 27. The <term>quotient</term> <note>labdhi</note> is 3. Subtract this 3 from the total distance of 27; the remainder is 24. Half of this is 12. This is the measure of the base. The remaining 15 is the measure of the hypotenuse. Thus, the meeting of the snake and the peacock occurred 12 cubits away from the hole.

<margin>
  9
   / \
  /   \ 15
 /     \
/_______\ 27
  12
</margin>

### A verse on the formula for separating the perpendicular and hypotenuse when their difference and the base are observed:
The method for determining the individual measurements of the perpendicular and the hypotenuse when the base and the difference between the perpendicular and hypotenuse are known is written in the following verse:

> Square the base and divide it by the difference between the perpendicular and the hypotenuse. Set this result down in two places. In one place, subtract the difference, and in the other, add the difference. Half of these results will be, in order, the perpendicular and the hypotenuse. This should be understood by the wise and applied everywhere. ॥ 11 ॥
<note>original Sanskrit: भुजाद्वर्गात्तत्कोटिकर्णान्तराप्तं द्विधा कोटिकर्णान्तरेणो- / नयुक्तम् ॥ तदद्धं क्रमात्कोटिकर्णौ भवेतामिदं धीमताऽऽ- / वेद्य सर्वत्र योज्यम् ॥ ११ ॥</note>

**Prose Order:** Square the base and divide it by the difference between the perpendicular and the hypotenuse. To that result, both subtract and add the difference in two separate steps. Half of those two results will be the perpendicular and the hypotenuse respectively. This should be known by the wise and applied in all such cases. ॥ 11 ॥

**Meaning:** Square the base and divide the result by the difference between the perpendicular and the hypotenuse. Take the result and write it in two places; in one place, subtract the difference of the perpendicular and hypotenuse, and in the other place, add it...

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

Then halve both values; these will then be, in order, the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note> and the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note>. The wise should apply this method thoughtfully across all similar examples. ॥ 11 ॥

> O friend, the distance between the lotus and the point where it disappears beneath the surface is the <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note>. The visible portion of the lotus is the difference between the perpendicular and the hypotenuse. The stalk is the perpendicular. Following this, calculate and tell me the measurement of the water. ॥ 12 ॥

**Analysis:**
O friend! In this example, the distance between the original position of the lotus and the spot where it submerges is the base. The visible part of the lotus <note>the part above water</note> represents the difference between the perpendicular and the hypotenuse. The stalk of the lotus is the perpendicular. Therefore, tell me the measurement of the water that corresponds to the perpendicular; how deep is it? ॥ 12 ॥

## Example—

> In a pond where the water is stirred by ruddy geese and cranes, a lotus bud was seen rising one <term>span</term> <note>vitasti; a measure of about 9 inches or half a cubit</note> above the surface. Gently moved by the wind, it became submerged at a distance of two <term>cubits</term> <note>hastas; the length of a forearm</note> from its original position. O mathematician, tell me quickly the depth of that water! ॥ 8 ॥

**Analysis:**
In a certain pond, the water was made beautiful by birds like the ruddy goose and the crane. In that pond, the tip of a lotus bud was seen standing one span above the water’s surface. A gentle breeze then blew, and because of that wind, the lotus submerged into the water at a distance of 2 cubits from where it started. O Knower of Mathematics! Tell me, how deep is the water in that pond? ॥ 8 ॥

## Calculation Layout:

<note>The diagram shows a right-angled triangle. The vertical side (the water depth) is the perpendicular. The horizontal side (the distance the lotus moved) is the base. The slanted line (the full length of the stalk) is the hypotenuse.</note>

1/2 is the difference between the perpendicular and the hypotenuse. <note>original: "कोटिकर्णान्तरम्"</note>

The base is 2. The resulting water depth is 15/4. <note>original: "जलगाम्भीर्यम्"</note>

This is the perpendicular. When the length of the lotus bud <note>the part that was above water</note> is added to it, the hypotenuse becomes 17/4.

<note>
**Technical Terms:**
- **Perpendicular (Koṭi):** The vertical side, here representing the water depth.
- **Hypotenuse (Karṇa):** The diagonal side, representing the full length of the lotus stalk.
- **Base (Bhuja):** The horizontal side, the distance the wind pushed the lotus.
- **Lilavati:** The title of this mathematical treatise, named after the author's daughter.
- **Lotus (Padma):** The subject of the geometric word problem.
- **Pond (Taḍāga):** The setting for the calculation.
- **Water Depth (Jalagāmbhīryam):** The unknown value being solved for.
- **Span (Vitasti):** A unit of length, approximately 9 inches.
- **Two Cubits (Hastayugma):** A unit of length, approximately 36 inches.
- **Layout (Nyāsa):** The formal setting down of the mathematical problem.
</note>

# Practical Geometry

**Calculation—** Here, the <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note> measurement of 2 was squared, resulting in 4. This was divided by the difference between the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note> and the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note>—which is the measurement of the <term>bud</term> <note>kalikā</note>, 1/2.
$4 \div 1/2 = 4/1 \times 2/1 = 8$.
The result obtained was 8; from this, the difference between the perpendicular and the hypotenuse was subtracted in one instance and added in another.

| Subtracted | Added |
| :--- | :--- |
| $8/1 - 1/2 = 16/2 - 1/2 = 15/2$ | $8/1 + 1/2 = 16/2 + 1/2 = 17/2$ |

Then the values 15/2 and 17/2 were produced in sequence. These were halved, resulting in 15/4 and 17/4, which are the measurements for the perpendicular and the hypotenuse respectively. Since the question asked for the depth of the water, the value of the perpendicular (15/4) is the depth. <note>15/4 cubits is 3.75 cubits.</note>

### A rule-verse for knowing the perpendicular and hypotenuse when the base and a portion of the perpendicular are observed:

<margin>The method for determining the form of the perpendicular and hypotenuse when the hypotenuse and base are known.</margin>

A rule in one verse regarding a portion of the perpendicular:

> Multiply the height of the tree by two and add the distance to the pond. Divide the product of the tree's height and the distance to the pond by that sum. The result obtained is indeed the measure of the leap. ॥ 13 ॥
<note>original Sanskrit: "dvinighnatālocchritisamyutaṃ yat..."</note>

**Prose Connection:** The distance to the pond is added to twice the height of the <term>palmyra tree</term> <note>tāla</note>. Divide the height of the tree, multiplied by the distance to the pond, by that sum. The result is the measure of the <term>leap</term> <note>uḍḍīnamāna; literally 'the measure of flying'</note>. ॥ 13 ॥

**Explanation:** Multiply the known height of the tree by two. To this product, add the distance between the tree and the pond. Now, take the height of the tree and multiply it by the distance to the pond. Divide this second product by the sum calculated first. The resulting quotient is the measure of the leap—which is the segment of the perpendicular that was combined with the hypotenuse. If you subtract this segment from the total sum <note>of the leap and the distance</note>, the measurement of the hypotenuse is revealed. ॥ 13 ॥

->Example<-

> From a tree one hundred <term>cubits</term> <note>hastas; a unit of measurement based on the forearm</note> high, a certain monkey leaped and descended toward a pond situated two hundred cubits away. Another monkey, however, moved quickly along the path of the hypotenuse, having first leaped some distance up from the tree...

<meta>This page transitions from the solution of the 'Lotus in the Pond' problem to a classic riddle from the Lilavati involving two monkeys. It applies the principles of the Pythagorean theorem (the relationship between the base, perpendicular, and hypotenuse) to a playful narrative scenario.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

> If their paths are equal in this way, how much was the height of the leap? O scholar, if you have laboured well in the science of calculation, tell it to me quickly! ॥ 9 ॥
<note>original Sanskrit: "jātaivaṃ samatā tayoryadi gatāvuḍḍīnamānaṃ kiyat..."</note>

### Prose Order
A certain monkey, descending from a tree one hundred cubits high, went to a pond located two hundred cubits away. Then, another monkey quickly leaped a certain distance up from the tree and went to the pond via the diagonal path <note>the hypotenuse</note>. If the distance traveled by both was equal, then, O scholar, if you have exerted yourself in mathematics, tell me quickly: how much was the measure of that leap? ॥ 9 ॥

### Meaning
A monkey, sitting atop a tree 100 <term>cubits</term> <note>hasta: a traditional unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note> high, climbed down and walked to a <term>stepped pond</term> <note>vāpī</note> 200 cubits away to drink water. At the same time, another monkey sitting on the tree leaped upwards and then dove straight along a diagonal path to the pond. In this way, both monkeys traveled exactly the same distance. O scholar! If you are clever in the science of mathematics and have practiced it, tell me quickly: how high did that second monkey leap before heading to the pond? ॥ 9 ॥

### Data Setup
Distance between tree and pond: 200. Height of the tree: 100.
The resulting measure of the leap: 50. <term>Perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note>: 150. <term>Hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note>: 250. <term>Base</term> <note>bhuja</note>: 200.

### Explanation
Here, the tree, which is 100 cubits tall, represents the known portion of the vertical side. The 200-cubit distance between the tree and the pond is the base. Both monkeys had to travel an equal distance. In this problem, the sum of the hypotenuse and the unknown portion of the vertical side is 300 cubits <note>Since the first monkey traveled 100 down + 200 across = 300 total, the second monkey's leap 'x' plus its diagonal path 'y' must also equal 300</note>. 

Following the previously stated rule:
1. Multiply the height of the tree (the known vertical part), 100, by two; this equals 200. 
2. Add the <term>base</term> (the distance to the pond), 200, to this; the sum is 400. 
3. Now, multiply the height of the tree, 100, by the distance to the pond, 200; the product is 20,000. 
4. Divide this 20,000 by the sum calculated earlier, 400. 
5. The result is 50. 

This 50 is the measure of that segment of the vertical side which was part of the diagonal path. Thus, the second monkey leaped 50 cubits high to reach the pond. By subtracting this leap (50) from the total distance (300), we find the <term>hypotenuse</term> is 250. By adding the leap (50) to the original tree height (100), we find the full <term>perpendicular</term> height is 150.

<note>Vocabulary: Lilavati (The Beautiful/Arithmetic), Mathematics, Measure of the leap, Perpendicular, Hypotenuse, Base, Pond, Monkey, Height of one hundred cubits, Data setup, Prose order, Explanation.</note>

### A verse on the formula for separating the base and perpendicular when their sum and the hypotenuse are known:
The method for knowing the <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note> and the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note> separately, having been given their sum and the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>karṇa</note>, in one verse:

> Subtract the square of the sum of the base and perpendicular from twice the square of the hypotenuse; take the square root of this. 
> This root should be both subtracted from and added to the sum; half of those results will be the measures of the base and perpendicular. ॥ 14 ॥
<note>original Sanskrit: "karṇasya vargāddviguṇādvishoḍhyo doḥkoṭiyogaḥ svagaṇo’sya mūlam..."</note>

**Prose Order:** One should subtract the square of the sum of the base and perpendicular from twice the square of the hypotenuse. The square root of this remainder should be taken. This root is then used in two ways: subtracted from and added to the sum. Half of these two results will be the measures of the base and the perpendicular. ॥ 14 ॥

**Meaning:** Multiply the square of the hypotenuse by two. From that number, subtract the square of the sum of the base and perpendicular. Take the square root of what remains. Write the sum of the base and perpendicular in two places; in one place, subtract the square root previously found, and in the other, add it. Then, halve the resulting numbers in both places. These will be the specific measures of the base and the perpendicular. ॥ 14 ॥

---

### Example:

> O friend! The hypotenuse is seventeen, and the sum of the base and perpendicular is twenty-three. 
> Tell me the individual measures of those two sides separately. ॥ 10 ॥
<note>original Sanskrit: "daśasaptādhikaḥ karṇastryadhikā viṃśatiḥ sakhe..."</note>

**Prose Order:** O friend! In a case where the hypotenuse is seventeen and the sum of the base and perpendicular is twenty-three, tell me those measures separately. ॥ 10 ॥

**Meaning:** O friend! Where the measure of the hypotenuse is 17 and the sum of the base and perpendicular is 23; there, state the measures of the base and perpendicular individually. ॥ 10 ॥

<margin>23</margin>
**Setup:**

8

->The hypotenuse is 17 and the sum of the base and perpendicular is 23.<-
->The base and perpendicular are 8 and 15.<-

**Expansion:** Here the hypotenuse is 17 and the sum of the base and perpendicular is 23. To find the separate measures of the base and perpendicular according to the above rule, the square of the hypotenuse (17) was calculated as 289. This was multiplied by two, resulting in 578. From this, the square of the sum of the base and perpendicular...

Subtracting the square of 23, which is 529, from [twice the square of the hypotenuse 578], 49 remains. Taking the <term>square root</term> <note>mūla</note> of these 49, we get 7. Then, the sum of the <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note> and the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>koṭi</note> [which is 23] was written in two places: in one place, the previously found root 7 was subtracted [23 - 7 = 16], and in the other, it was added [23 + 7 = 30]. 
These results were halved, giving the measures of the base and the perpendicular <insert>Right-angled triangle diagram with sides 15, 8 and hypotenuse 17</insert> as 8 and 15 respectively. That is, the measure of the base is 8 and the perpendicular <gloss>15</gloss> is 15. ॥ 10 ॥

### Example:

->Where the difference between the base and perpendicular is seven, and the hypotenuse is thirteen,<-
->O best of calculators, tell me quickly the base and perpendicular separately.<- ॥ 11 ॥
<note>original: "doḥkoṭyorantaraṃ śailāḥ karṇo yatra trayodaśa..." The word 'śailāḥ' (mountains) is a Sanskrit numerical symbol for the number 7.</note>

**Prose Order:** O best of calculators! Where seven is the difference between the base and the perpendicular, and thirteen is the hypotenuse, there, tell me the base and the perpendicular separately and quickly. ॥ 11 ॥

**Meaning:** O you who are well-versed in the science of mathematics! In a case where the difference between the base and perpendicular is 7 (seven) and the hypotenuse is 13 (thirteen), state the base and the perpendicular separately and without delay. ॥ 11 ॥

Statement
<insert>Right-angled triangle diagram with sides 12, 5 and hypotenuse 13</insert>
Hypotenuse: 13; Difference between base and perpendicular: 7

->Obtained base and perpendicular: 5 and 12<-

**Elaboration:** The square of the hypotenuse 13, which is 169, was doubled to get 338. From this, the square of the difference between the base and perpendicular (7), which is 49, was subtracted to get 289. Taking the square root <insert>Right-angled triangle diagram with sides 12, 5 and hypotenuse 13</insert> of this, we get 17. From this root, the difference [7] was subtracted in one place and added in another, resulting in 10 and 24. These were halved, giving the measures of the base and perpendicular as 5 and 12 respectively.

### A Verse on the Procedural Rule for Knowing the Perpendicular and the Base Segments:

The method for knowing the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>lamba</note> and the <term>base segments</term> <note>avabādhā</note> in one verse:

->The product of two poles divided by their sum gives the perpendicular formed by the intersection of strings stretched from the root of one to the tip of the other.<-
->The two poles, each multiplied by the distance between them and then divided by their sum, result in the two segments of the ground on either side of the perpendicular.<- ॥ 15 ॥
<note>original: "anyonyamūlāgragasūtrayogādveṇvorvadhe yogahate ca lambaḥ..." This describes a classic problem of two poles (bamboos) with criss-crossing wires.</note>

**Prose Order:** From the intersection of strings going from the base of one to the top of the other, the product of the two poles [bamboos] divided by their sum is the perpendicular. The two poles, multiplied by the desired ground [the distance between them] and divided by their sum, are the two base segments on either side of the perpendicular. ॥ 15 ॥

It also introduces a new geometric rule for calculating the height and ground segments created by the intersection of lines between two vertical poles.</summary>

( 139 )
# Practical Geometry

**Meaning:** Multiply the heights of the two bamboo poles together; then, divide this product by the sum of the heights of the two poles. The resulting <term>quotient</term> <note>labdhi</note> is the measure of the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>lamba</note> at the point where the lines cross. To find the segments of the ground, multiply the height of each bamboo individually by the total ground distance; by dividing these products by the sum of the heights, the resulting quotients provide the respective <term>base segments</term> <note>avabādhā; the segments of the ground created by the point directly below the intersection</note>. ॥ 15 ॥

## Example
-> Tell me the measure of the perpendicular formed by the intersection of strings tied from the base of one pole to the top of the other, <-
-> for two bamboo poles fifteen and ten cubits high, where the ground distance between them is unknown. <- ॥ 12 ॥
<note>original: "pañcadaśadaśakarocchrayaveṇorajñātamadhyabhūmikayoḥ..."</note>

**Prose Order:** O <term>Calculator</term> <note>gaṇaka</note>! Tell me the measure of the perpendicular formed by strings connecting the base and top of each pole, for two bamboo poles fifteen and ten cubits high, standing at an unknown distance from each other. ॥ 12 ॥

**Meaning:** O expert in mathematics! Two bamboo poles, one 15 <term>cubits</term> <note>kara; literally 'hand'</note> tall and the other 10 cubits tall, are standing in the ground. The distance between them is not known. Strings are tied to them such that one is tied to the base of the first and the top of the second, and another is tied to the base of the second and the top of the first. Tell me: if a perpendicular line is drawn to the ground from the point where the two strings meet, what will the measure of this perpendicular be? ॥ 12 ॥

**Statement:**

<margin>
15
6
3 2
5
10
</margin> 
The two bamboo poles are 15 and 10. The resulting perpendicular is 6. If the ground distance between the poles is 5, then the base segments are 3 and 2. Alternatively, if the ground distance is 10, the segments are 6 and 4; or if the ground distance is 20, the segments are 12 and 8. In all such cases, the perpendicular remains the same <note>The height of the intersection depends only on the heights of the poles, not the distance between them</note>. Here, the <term>Rule of Three</term> <note>trairāśika</note> is applied: if the height of the pole is the vertical side and the ground is the base, what will the segment be for a given height? This is how it is understood everywhere.

**Explanation:** The aforementioned perpendicular is the one that originates from the point where strings crossing from the base to the top of the poles intersect...

<insert>A geometric diagram illustrating the crossed-ladders or broken bamboo problem. It depicts two vertical lines representing bamboo poles of heights 15 and 10, with diagonal lines connecting the top of each to the base of the other. A central perpendicular line (lamba) of height 6 originates from the intersection point to the base, which is divided into segments of 3 and 2, totaling 5.</insert>

<summary>This page explains how to calculate the height of the intersection point of two lines crossing between two vertical poles (the "crossed ladders" problem). It demonstrates that the height of the intersection remains constant regardless of the distance between the poles.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, perpendicular, base segments, rule of three, bamboo problem, Bhāskara, intersection height, Kṣetravyavahāra</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page concludes the calculation for the "Two Poles" problem, demonstrating that the height of the intersection point remains constant regardless of the distance between the poles, and then introduces the definition of mathematically impossible geometric figures.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

When a string is tied from one pole to the other, a line is dropped to the earth from the point where the strings meet; this distance to the earth is the <term>perpendicular</term> <note>lamba</note>. The <term>base segments</term> <note>avabādhā</note> are the portions of the earth on both sides of that perpendicular. To determine that perpendicular and those base segments according to the previously stated rule, we first multiply the measures of the two bamboo poles, 15 and 10, which results in 150. We then divide this by the sum of the poles, 25, yielding a <term>quotient</term> <note>labdhi</note> of 6.

This 6 is the measure of the perpendicular dropped to the earth from the intersection of the strings. If we assume the ground distance between these bamboos to be 5, then we first multiply this ground distance by the first bamboo (15), which equals 75. Dividing this by the sum of both bamboos (25) gives a quotient of 3; this is the base segment on the side of the larger bamboo. Then, multiplying that same ground distance of 5 by the second bamboo (10) gives 50; dividing this by the sum of both bamboos (25) gives a quotient of 2; this is the base segment for the second, smaller bamboo. 

When 10 is assumed to be the middle ground distance, according to the stated method, the base segment on the side of the larger bamboo is 6 and the base segment on the side of the smaller bamboo is 4. Similarly, if 15 is assumed to be the middle ground distance, the two base segments are 12 and 8 respectively <note>Note: Mathematically, if the ground is 15, the segments should be 9 and 6. The text's "12 and 8" would correspond to a ground distance of 20.</note>. No matter what you assume the ground distance to be, the perpendicular will always remain 6. When the ground is treated as the <term>base</term> <note>bhuja</note> and the bamboo as the <term>upright</term> <note>koṭi</note>, the truth of this can be realized everywhere through the <term>Rule of Three</term> <note>trairāśika</note>. For example: if for a ground of 5, a certain bamboo upright is obtained, what upright will be obtained for the base segment? In this manner, the same perpendicular is arrived at from both sides.

# The Rule for Defining an Impossible Figure

->Now, the characteristics of a "non-field" (impossible figure) are described:<-

> That straight-sided figure in which the sum of the other sides is either less than or equal to one side should be known as an **akṣetra** <note>literally "non-field" or an impossible area</note>, boldly asserted [by the ignorant]. ॥ 16 ॥
> <note>original: "dhṛṣṭodiṣṭamṛjubhūjaṃ kṣetraṃ yatraikabāhutaḥ svalpā..."</note>

**Prose Order:** A straight-sided figure where the sum of the other sides is smaller than or equal to a single side is to be understood as an *akṣetra* <note>impossible figure</note> boldly proclaimed [by a fool]. ॥ 16 ॥

**Meaning:** In any triangular or quadrilateral figure, if the sum of the other sides is less than or equal to any one single side, then that figure—though described by a stubborn person—is actually a "non-field" <note>akṣetra</note> and cannot exist. ॥ 16 ॥

## Example—

> In a quadrilateral where the sides are three, six, two, and twelve; and in a triangle where they are three, six, and nine—if such are proposed by a defiant person, one should declare them a <term>non-field</term> <note>original: akṣetra; a geometric figure that cannot physically exist or close</note>. || 13 ||

**Prose Order:** Where a defiant person proposes sides of 3, 6, 2, and 12 in a quadrilateral, and 3, 6, and 9 in a triangle, one should designate that as a non-field. || 13 ||

**Meaning:** In a four-sided figure where the four sides are of the measures 3, 6, 2, and 12, and in a triangle where the three sides are of the measures 3, 6, and 9—if a stubborn person <note>original: dhṛṣṭa; someone posing a trick or impossible question</note> asks such a question, it should be called an <term>non-field</term>. || 13 ||

Setup: ->These are impossible figures.<-

---

->**Straight sticks of the measure of the sides should be placed in the positions of the sides—**<-
->**to demonstrate the impossibility.**<-

**Expansion:** Both of these are non-fields. To understand their impossibility, one should place straight sticks of the length of the sides in their respective places and show it; for this reason, we demonstrate this visually with lines.

---

<margin>
3
2 6
12
</margin>
<margin>

3
6
</margin>

In the quadrilateral figure, the sum of the three sides (2, 3, and 6) is 11, while the largest side is 12. Because the sum of the three sides (11) is smaller than the single large side (12), it is proper to call it a non-field. In such a figure, the <term>area</term> <note>original: kṣetraphala</note> cannot be found because the area depends on the <term>base</term> <note>bhūmi</note>, the <term>upright</term> <note>koṭi</note>, and the <term>calculated result</term> <note>labdhi</note>. In such a problem, all the sides simply collapse into the base. For this same reason, the triangle is also a non-field <note>In the triangle 3 + 6 = 9, the sum of two sides is exactly equal to the third side, meaning they lie flat against it and do not form an enclosed space</note>. The form of both figures is shown with lines below.
