### Two Arya Verses for the Formula to Find Base Segments and Other Properties

The method for knowing the <term>base segments</term> <note>original: abādhā; the two parts of the base created by dropping a perpendicular from the opposite vertex</note> and other properties is given in these two verses in the Arya meter:

> In a triangle, the sum of the two sides is multiplied by their difference and divided by the base. ||
> The base is written in two places: the result is subtracted from one and added to the other. These halved are the two base segments. || 17 ||
> The square root of the difference between the squares of a side and its corresponding segment is the <term>altitude</term> <note>original: lamba; the perpendicular height</note>. ||
> Half the base multiplied by the altitude is the accurate <term>area</term> <note>original: phala; literally "fruit" or result</note> of the triangle. || 18 ||

**Prose Order:** In a triangle, one should calculate the sum of the two sides. Then, multiply that by their difference. Then, divide by the base. The base should be placed in two positions; the result is subtracted from one and added to the other. Those halved are the two base segments of the triangle. || 17 || The square root of the difference between the squares of a base segment and its side is the altitude. Half the base multiplied by the altitude is the clear area in the triangle. || 18 ||

**Meaning:** In a triangular figure, add the two sides together; then multiply that total by the difference between those same two sides. Divide this product by the side designated as the base. Take the resulting quotient and place it in two spots. In one spot, subtract it from the base value, and in the other spot, add it to the base value. Divide both these results by two. The numbers obtained are the two <term>base segments</term> <note>original: abādhā</note>. || 17 || Now, square your side and square its corresponding base segment. Subtract the smaller square from the larger square and find the square root. The resulting number is the measure of the <term>altitude</term> <note>original: lamba</note>. To find the area, take half of the base and multiply it by the altitude; this gives the exact <term>area</term> <note>original: phala</note> of the triangle. || 18 ||

### Example—

> In a triangle where the base is measured as <term>fourteen</term> <note>original: manu-mitā; Manu represents the number 14 in Indian numerical symbolism</note>, and the two sides are <term>thirteen and fifteen</term> <note>original: trayodaśa-tithi-pramitau; Tithi represents the 15 days of the lunar fortnight</note>—quickly tell me the altitude, the two base segments, and the area in terms of <term>equal square units</term> <note>original: samakoṣṭham</note>. || 14 ||

**Prose Order:** Where in a triangular field the base is 14 and the sides are 13 and 15, tell me quickly the altitude, the base segments, and the result called the area in square units. || 14 ||

<header>Geometry of Fields</header>

# Geometry of Fields

**Verse Meaning:** In a triangular field where the base is 14 units and the two sides are 13 and 15 units, quickly state the perpendicular height, the two base segments, and the area of the figure. || 14 ||

**Data Setup:** Base: 14; Sides: 13, 15. The results are:

-> <term>Base Segments</term> <note>original: abādhā; the segments of the base created by the point where the perpendicular height meets it</note>: 5 and 9. Perpendicular Height: 12. <-

-> Area: 84. <-

<margin>
13    15
   12
 5    9
   14
</margin>
**Explanation:** In this triangular field, the base is 14 and the two sides are 13 and 15. To find the base segments according to the previously stated rule, first add the two sides (13 + 15), which equals 28. Then, find the difference between these two sides (15 - 13), which is 2. Multiply these together (28 × 2) to get 56. Divide this by the known base of 14, resulting in a quotient of 4. This result is both subtracted from and added to the base of 14 in two different places, giving 10 and 18. Halving these values gives the base segments of 5 and 9 respectively. Specifically, the segment corresponding to the first side is 5, and the segment for the second side is 9.

Next, to find the <term>perpendicular height</term> <note>original: lamba</note>, square the side and its corresponding base segment and find the difference between those squares. Then, take the square root of that difference. For example, the square of the first side (13) is 169, and the square of its base segment (5) is 25. Their difference is 144. The square root of 144 is 12, which is the measure of the height. Similarly, for the second side (15), the square is 225, and the square of its segment (9) is 81. Their difference is also 144, the square root of which yields the same height of 12. Finally, to find the area, multiply half of the base (7) by the height (12), which equals 84. This is the area of the field.

**Example of Negative Base Segments:** An example for understanding when a base segment is "negative" <note>This occurs in obtuse triangles where the perpendicular height falls outside the triangle, causing one segment calculation to result in a value that must be interpreted geometrically as being outside the base.</note>—

> Where the two sides of a triangle are 10 and 17, and the base is 9—
> O mathematician! Quickly tell me the base segments, the height, and the calculated area. || 15 ||

**Verse Connection:** In a triangle where the sides are 10 and 17 and the base is 9, O calculator! Tell me the base segments, the height, and the area immediately. || 15 ||

**Meaning:** In a triangular field where the two sides are measured as 10 and 17, and the base is 9...

<summary>This page demonstrates practical applications of geometric formulas to calculate the internal segments, height, and area of a triangle, transitioning into complex cases involving obtuse triangles.</summary>
<keywords>triangle geometry, base segments, perpendicular height, area calculation, Bhaskara, Lilavati, Sanskrit mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion from the previous page regarding the calculation of triangular properties, specifically focusing on cases where the altitude falls outside the triangle's base.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>The Lilavati</header>

...is the earth <note>original: pṛthvī; here referring to the base of the triangle</note>, O you who are knowledgeable in mathematics! In that field, tell me both the base segments, the perpendicular height, and state the area quickly. || 16 ||

**Data Setup:** Sides: 10, 17. Base: 9. In this triangle, by using the rule "the sum of the sides" and so on, the result obtained is 21. Because of this, the result cannot be subtracted from the base <note>Since 9 minus 21 would be negative</note>. Therefore, the base is subtracted from the result and halved, or it is considered a negative direction <note>original: digviparītyena; indicating the altitude falls outside the triangle's footprint</note>. Thus:

-> The two <term>base segments</term> <note>original: abādha; the horizontal distances from the vertices to the point where the altitude meets the extended base line</note> are 6 and 15. <-
-> From both of these, the <term>perpendicular height</term> <note>original: lamba</note> is 8 and the area is 36. <-

**Detailed Explanation—** Here, the perpendicular height falls outside the base; for this reason, this is called an example of a **negative segment** <note>original: ṛṇābādhā</note>. Here, following the previously mentioned rule, the sum of the two sides (10 and 17) was calculated to be 27. This was multiplied by the difference of those same sides (7), resulting in 189. This was divided by the base (9), yielding a quotient of 21. This quotient (21) was added to the base (9), making 30; half of this is 15, which is the segment corresponding to the side of 17. 

Now, to find the segment for the first side, the quotient (21) should be subtracted from the base; however, it cannot be subtracted <note>as 9 is smaller than 21</note>. For this reason, the direction was reversed—meaning, instead of subtracting the quotient from the base, the base was subtracted from the quotient. This left 12. Half of this is 6, which is the **negative segment**. 

In this way, the two base segments are 6 and 15. To find the height from these segments, the first side (10) was squared, resulting in 100. The square of its corresponding segment (6) is 36. Their difference is 64. Taking the square root of this gives the perpendicular height of 8. 

Similarly, the second side (17) was squared, resulting in 289. The square of its corresponding segment (15) is 225. Their difference is 64. Taking the square root gives the same height measurement of 8. Thus, one consistent height was found from both segments. Now, to find the area, half of the base (4 ½) was multiplied by the height (8), resulting in 36. This is the area.

# **Verse on the rule for calculating the approximate and accurate areas of quadrilaterals and triangles—**
The method for knowing the <term>approximate area</term> <note>original: aspaṣṭa phala</note> in quadrilaterals and the <term>accurate area</term> <note>original: spaṣṭa phala</note> in triangles in one verse:

<vocab>Triangle (Tribhuja), Base Segment (Abādhā), Perpendicular Height (Lamba), Area (Kṣetraphala), Data Setup (Nyāsa), Side (Bhuja), Base (Bhūmi), Negative Segment (Ṛṇābādhā), Quotient (Labdhi), Directional Reversal (Digviparītya)</vocab>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion from the previous page regarding the calculation of triangular properties, specifically focusing on cases where the altitude falls outside the triangle's base. This page shifts focus to general area calculations for four-sided figures.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>
<header>Practice of Mensuration</header>

> Half the sum of all the sides, placed in four positions and diminished by the sides; the square root of the product of those values is the **approximate area** in a quadrilateral, but the **precise area** in a triangle. || 19 ||

**Word-for-word connection—** The sum of all the sides is to be halved and placed in four positions; then, it is to be diminished by the sides respectively. From the product of those, the square root is the **approximate area** <note>original: asphuṭa-phalam</note> in a quadrilateral. In the same way, the **precise area** <note>original: spaṣṭa-phalam</note> is declared for a triangle (a three-sided figure). || 19 ||

**Meaning—** Add all the sides of the figure together and take half of that sum. Write that resulting number down in four separate places. From each of these four numbers, subtract one of the sides of the figure. Multiply the four remainders together and take the square root of the result. This gives the **approximate area** (not strictly accurate for all shapes) for a <term>quadrilateral</term> <note>a four-sided polygon</note>. By this same method, the **precise area** is found for a triangle. || 19 ||

## Example—

> Where the base is measured as fourteen, the face is nine, and the two sides are thirteen and twelve, and where the perpendicular height is also twelve—tell me the area of that field as stated by the ancients. || 16 ||

**Word-for-word connection—** In a field where the base <note>original: bhūmi</note> is measured at fourteen, the face <note>original: mukham; the top side parallel to the base</note> is the number nine, and the two sides <note>original: bāhū</note> are measured as thirteen and twelve, and where the perpendicular height <note>original: lamba</note> is also twelve—tell me that area which was stated by the ancient masters. || 16 ||

**Meaning—** In a field where the base is 14, the top side is 9, the two remaining sides are 13 and 12, and the perpendicular height is also 12; calculate the area of that field according to the traditional teachings. || 16 ||

**Data setup—** Base: 14. Face: 9. Sides: 13 and 12. Perpendicular height: 12.

<margin>
Face: 9  
Side: 13  
12 Height Quadrilateral  
Side: 12  
14 [Base]
</margin>

By performing the operation as described above, the product is 19,800. The square root of this is slightly less than one hundred forty-one (141). This is not the **true area** <note>original: vāstava-phalam</note> in this specific field. Rather, the true area is 138, which is found by the rule to be mentioned later: "The height multiplied by half the sum of the base and the face." <note>This refers to the standard formula for the area of a trapezoid: $Area = h \times \frac{a+b}{2}$</note>

<meta>Catchword: 10 at bottom center. Image quality is clear.</meta>

<summary>This page introduces a method (equivalent to Heron's formula) for calculating the area of triangles and quadrilaterals, noting that while accurate for triangles, it only provides an approximation for quadrilaterals unless they meet specific geometric criteria. An example of a trapezoid is used to demonstrate the difference between this "gross" approximation and the "true" area.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, mensuration, area calculation, quadrilateral, triangle, Bhaskara II, Lilavati, trapezoid, perpendicular height</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific mathematical example started on the previous page, applying various area calculation methods to a quadrilateral with sides 9, 12, 14, and 13, and a height of 12.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>
<header>Lilavati</header>

**Elaboration—** According to the method mentioned above, to find the area, take the sum of all the sides 9, 12, 14, and 13, which is 48. Half of this is 24. Write this 24 in four places; then, subtract each side in sequence from each place. The remainders are 15, 12, 10, and 11. Multiply these together, which results in 19,800. The square root of this is the area; however, because a perfect square root cannot be found, this is called a <term>surd result</term> <note>original: karaṇīgata phala; a result expressed as an irrational square root</note>. Taking its <term>approximate root</term> <note>original: āsanna mūla</note>, we get slightly less than 141. However, this area is not precise. Instead, we shall use the method for finding the area of a <term>trapezoid</term> <note>original: samalamba-caturbhuja; literally a "four-sided figure with equal perpendiculars," referring here to a shape with parallel top and bottom</note> which will be described next;

| Half of the Sum | Side | Remainder |
|-----------------|------|-----------|
| 24              | 9    | 15        |
| 24              | 12   | 12        |
| 24              | 14   | 10        |
| 24              | 13   | 11        |

-> “Add the base and the face together and take half; then multiply by the perpendicular height.” <-

According to that rule, here too, add the base (14) and the face (9) and halve the sum, which gives <gloss>23</gloss>/<gloss>2</gloss> <note>11.5</note>. Multiply this by the perpendicular height (12), which results in 138. This is the correct area. ||

Now, we shall find the area by another method by dividing that same figure into two parts. In the aforementioned quadrilateral, by dropping a perpendicular, a <term>rectangle</term> <note>original: samacaturbhuja</note> is formed along with a triangle. Because the quadrilateral is uniform, the base of the rectangle becomes 9, equal to the face (9). The remaining part of the total base, 5, becomes the base of the triangle. Thus, in the triangle, the side is 5, the height <note>original: koṭi</note> is 12, and the hypotenuse <note>original: karṇa</note> is 13. By multiplying this side and height (5 × 12), we get 60; half of that is 30. This is the area of the triangle. Then, multiplying the side (9) and height (12) of the rectangle, we get 108. Adding these two together gives 138, which is the same correct area. ||

<vocab>Lilavati (The treatise), Area (kshetraphala), Side (bhuja), Altitude/Vertical side (koti), Hypotenuse (karna), Perpendicular (lamba), Trapezoid (samalambacaturbhuja), Approximate root (asanna mula), Surd/Irrational result (karanigata phala), Half-sum (yogardha)</vocab>

<summary>This page demonstrates that the general formula for quadrilateral area provides only an approximation, whereas the specific trapezoid formula and geometric decomposition (into a rectangle and triangle) yield the precise area of 138.</summary>

This page provides the mathematical proof for a triangle and introduces a verse explaining why quadrilaterals are more complex.</meta>

# Practice of Measurement

By the rule "half the sum of all sides" <note>original: sarva-dau-ryuti-dala; referring to the formula for the area of a triangle using its semi-perimeter</note> and so on, we calculate the <term>precise area</term> <note>original: spaṣṭa-phala</note> for the triangle previously illustrated. Here is the <term>layout</term> <note>original: nyāsa; the setup of the problem</note> for that triangle: the base is 14 and the two sides are 13 and 15. By this method, the result for a three-sided figure is the true area: 84. It is noted here that for a quadrilateral, this method yields an <term>imprecise result</term> <note>original: aspaṣṭa-phala</note>.

**Elaboration—** To bring about the precise area in a <term>triangle</term> <note>original: tribhuja</note> according to the rule "half the sum of all sides" stated above, we calculate using the same triangle from the previous example. Here, we sum the three sides 13, 15, and 14, which equals 42. We take half of this, 21, and write it in four places. From these, we subtract each side individually, which leaves remainders of 8, 6, and 7, plus the original 21. We multiply these together <note>21 × 8 × 6 × 7</note>, which equals 7056. Taking the square root of this gives 84. 

| Half-Sum | Side | Remainder |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 21 | 13 | 8 |
| 21 | 15 | 6 |
| 21 | 14 | 7 |
| 21 | 00 | 21 |
| **63** | **42** | **42** |

This is the area, and it is equal to the area we calculated previously; therefore, this is the precise area. For a quadrilateral, we have already shown that this method produces an imprecise result.

### A verse and a half regarding the determination of approximate values

The method by which the <term>approximate area</term> <note>original: sthūla; "gross" or "rough" value</note> of a quadrilateral is found has been stated previously. Now, the rules are written to demonstrate why that value is considered approximate:

> Since the two diagonals of a quadrilateral are indefinite, how then can the area in this case be definite? Even when those diagonals are calculated by the ancient masters for their own envisioned shapes, those same diagonals do not exist in other cases. || 20 ||

**Connection of Words:** Indeed, because the diagonals of a quadrilateral are indefinite, how can the area in this case be definite? When the diagonals are calculated by the ancients for their own specific constructions, they do not apply elsewhere. || 20 ||

**Meaning:** It is certain that in a quadrilateral, the <term>diagonals</term> <note>original: karṇa</note> are indefinite—meaning that in a single figure, there can be many types of diagonals. For this reason, how can there be a fixed area here? Furthermore, the diagonals that the ancient mathematicians calculated for their own specific quadrilaterals cannot be applied to all situations. || 20 ||

> With those same sides, there can be other diagonals in many ways, and therefore, the area can also be of many kinds.

**Connection of Words:** With those very same sides, diagonals can be of many types. Therefore, the area also becomes of many types.

<vocab>Practice of Measurement, Triangle, Quadrilateral, Area, Diagonal, Layout, Side, Approximate Value, Connection of Words</vocab>

<summary>This page demonstrates that the semi-perimeter method for calculating area is precise for triangles but explains through Verse 20 that it is imprecise for quadrilaterals because their diagonals, and thus their areas, are not fixed by the side lengths alone.</summary>
<keywords>Heron's Formula, triangle area, quadrilateral area, diagonals, Lilavati, Bhaskara II, geometry, precise vs approximate area</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 147 regarding the calculation of areas. While the previous page proved that the "half-sum of sides" method works for triangles, it now explains why this method is insufficient for quadrilaterals due to their shifting geometry.</meta>

# Lilavati

**Explanation:** With those same sides, the diagonals can be of many types; because of this, the area is also of many types.

In a quadrilateral, if one pushes the opposite corners inward, the sides connected to them contract the diagonal. Meanwhile, the other corners spread outward, increasing their respective diagonal. Therefore, it is said: "With those same sides, there are other diagonals."

**Explanation:** In a four-sided figure <note>original: *caturbhuja-kṣetra*</note>, by bypassing one corner and pulling the two opposite corners toward the center, the sides contract the diagonal connecting them. Conversely, the sides that spread outward by this pulling action increase their diagonal. For this reason, it was stated above that as the diagonals take many forms, the resulting area also takes many forms, even though the sides remain the same. Because by pulling the corners, one diagonal increases and the other becomes smaller, the diagonals will vary; consequently, the area of that same field will have many different values.

->How can another ask for a fixed area, when the area is indeterminate,<-
->without first specifying either the perpendiculars or one of the diagonals? || 1 ||<-
->That questioner is a ghost, or the teacher is even more of one,<-
->if he does not understand the indeterminate nature of a four-sided figure. || 2 ||<-
<note>The author, Bhaskara, uses the term *pishacha* (ghost or demon) to humorously mock students or teachers who try to find a single "correct" area for a quadrilateral without knowing its internal angles or diagonals. Without a fixed diagonal, a quadrilateral is "unstable" and its area can change.</note>

**Word-for-word connection:** How can another ask for a fixed area <note>original: *niyata-phalam*</note> when it is indeterminate <note>original: *aniyatatve’pi*</note>, without specifying one of the two <term>perpendiculars</term> <note>original: *lamba*</note> or <term>diagonals</term> <note>original: *karna*</note>? || 1 ||

That questioner is a ghost, or the speaker is even more of one, who does not know the unstable nature of a four-sided figure. || 2 ||

### The rule for finding the area of a rhombus and a rectangle in two and a half verses:

**Explanation:** The method for bringing about the area of an <term>equilateral quadrilateral</term> <note>original: *sama-caturbhuja*; a rhombus</note> and an <term>equidistant quadrilateral</term> <note>original: *āyata-caturbhuja*; a rectangle</note> in two and a half verses—

For a quadrilateral with equal sides, assume one diagonal as desired. Subtract the square of that diagonal from four times the square of a side; the square root of the remainder is the measure of the second <term>diagonal</term> <note>original: *shravana-pramana*</note>. The product of the unequal diagonals divided by two is the <term>precise area</term>... <note>original: *sphuta-phalam*</note>

<summary>Bhaskara explains that a quadrilateral's area is not fixed by its sides alone but depends on its diagonals, mockingly calling those who ignore this "ghosts." He then begins the rule for calculating the area of a rhombus using its diagonals.</summary>
<keywords>quadrilateral area, diagonals, pishacha, rhombus, rectangle, Bhaskara, geometry, Lilavati</keywords>
